Abstract

We apply the strategy to study of diffeomorphisms via block diffeomorphisms to the world of positive scalar curvature (psc) metrics.
For each closed psc manifold
M
, we construct the cubical set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
of all psc block metrics, which only encodes concordance information of psc metrics within its homotopy type.
We show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a cubical Kan set, give a geometric description for the group structure of the combinatorial homotopy groups, and construct a comparison map from the cubical model
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
of the space of psc metrics on
M
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
Next, we build a concordance-themed model for real
K
-theory based on the notion of invertible block Dirac operators and use it to factor the index difference through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
In the final part of this thesis, we construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence, which is a non-index-theoretic tool to get information about the difference of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

Chapter 1
Introduction

The overall goal of this thesis is to systematically study the space of positive scalar curvature (psc) metrics on closed manifolds from a concordance view point.

Background

The scalar curvature
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)
of a Riemannian metric
g
is a geometric quantity of the Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
that measures how far this Riemannian metric deviates from being flat.
Among the commonly considered curvatures (sectional, Ricci, and scalar) in Riemannian geometry, scalar curvature is the weakest one.
It is a scalar-valued function
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\colon M\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
that measures on small scales how fast the volume of a geodesic ball grows compared to a ball in euclidean space.
More precisely, the scalar curvature at
p\in M
appears in the Taylor series expansion
[
gray1974volume
]
*Theorem 3.1

\frac{\mathrm{vol}(B_{\varepsilon}(p)\subseteq M^{d})}{\mathrm{vol}\bigl(B_{\varepsilon}(0)\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\bigr)}=1-\frac{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)(p)}{6(d+2)}\varepsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4}).

In particular, if the scalar curvature is positive, then the volume grows slower on those scales where the second order term dominates the higher order terms.

For surfaces, i.e.,
\dim\,M=2
, the scalar curvature is twice the Gauss curvature, so assuming positivity is a strict condition.
Indeed, the Gauss-Bonnet theorem

\int_{M}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\mathrm{d}\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=4\pi\chi(M)=4\pi\bigl(2-2\mathrm{genus}(M)\bigr)

together with the classification theorem for closed surfaces imply that only the sphere
S^{2}
and the real projective plane
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}P^{2}
can carry a positive scalar curvature metric, which they do!

In higher dimensions, the curvature notions differ, and it is a bit surprising that there are obstructions against the existence of positive scalar curvature metrics at all.
Lichnerowicz
[
lichnerowicz1963spineurs
]
observed that on spin manifolds, i.e., orientable Riemannian manifolds with vanishing second Stiefel-Whitney class, the (Clifford-linear) Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is closely related to the scalar curvature of its underlying Riemannian metric:

{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}^{2}=\nabla^{\ast}\nabla+\frac{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)}{4}.

This formula implies that if the scalar curvature is positive, then the Dirac operator is invertible.
On closed manifolds, the Dirac operator is an (unbounded) Fredholm operator and its kernel generates a class in real
K
-theory.
By Hitchin’s extension of the Atiyah-Singer index theorem, this class is a purely topological quantity of the
d
-dimensional manifold
M
, the so called
\alpha
-invariant
or
topological index

\alpha(M)\in KO^{-d}(\mathrm{pt})
.
In particular, this class is independent of the metric that was used to define the Dirac operator and one infers that the non-vanishing of
\alpha(M)
is an obstruction against positive scalar curvature.

On the constructive side, the surgery result of Gromov-Lawson
[
gromov1980classification
]
is an important tool to produce examples of manifolds that carry a positive scalar curvature metric.
It says, in its simplest form, that if
M_{2}
is obtained from
M_{1}
by a surgery of codimension
\geq 3
and if
M_{1}
carries a psc metric, then
M_{2}
also carries a psc metric.
The surgery result implies that a spin manifold of dimension
\geq 5
carries a psc metric if and only if its singular bordism class
[M,f]\in\Omega^{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}}(B\pi_{1}(M))
has a representative that carries a psc metric.
Here,
f\colon M\rightarrow B\pi_{1}(M)
is a continuous map to the classifying space of the fundamental group of
M
that induces the identity between the fundamental groups.

Stephan Stolz used in
[
stolz1992simply
]
the surgery result to prove that, for simply connected spin manifolds of dimension
\geq 5
, the vanishing of the
\alpha
-invariant is not only necessary, but also sufficient by showing that all elements in the kernel of the
\alpha
-invariant

\alpha\colon\Omega_{\ast}^{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}}(\mathrm{pt})\rightarrow KO^{-\ast}(\mathrm{pt})

are represented by psc-manifolds.
For other fundamental groups, the analogous question whether the (higher)
\alpha
-invariant, which we are not going to define here, is also a sufficient condition for existence became known as the Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture and was proven to be false in general by the counterexample of Thomas Schick
[
schick1998counterexample
]
.

The stable Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture claims that if the higher
\alpha
-invariant vanishes, then
M\times\mathrm{Bott}^{N}
carries a positive scalar curvature metric for all sufficiently large
N\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
, see
[
rosenberg2006manifolds
]
*p.19 ff for details.
Here,
\mathrm{Bott}
is an eight-dimensional, simply-connected, closed manifold with
\alpha(\mathrm{Bott})=1\in KO^{-8}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
, in particular it cannot carry a positive scalar curvature metric.
This manifold is referred to as
Bott-manifold
and can be chosen to be Ricci-flat.
The stable Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture, formulated in the 90s, is a consequence of the Novikov conjecture and is state of the art for the exisitence question.

Although not completely solved, the question whether
M
carries a psc metric or not is reasonably well understood: it is governed by the stable Gromov-Lawson-Rosenberg conjecture.
Thus, the focus has turned toward the question:

How many essentially different psc metrics can a manifold carry if it carries at least one psc metric?

More precisely, on a closed manifold
M
, we consider the set
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)

of all Riemannian metrics on
M
with positive scalar curvature.
This is a subset of sections on
M
with values in bilinear forms on tangent spaces, so we equip it with the smooth Whitney topology - the usual topology on the space of sections.
We are interested in the homotopy type of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.

If
\dim M=2,3
, then the space
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is either empty or contractible, see
[
rosenberg149metrics
]
*Theorem 3.4 and
[
bamler2019ricci
]
*Theorem 1.1.
If
\dim M\geq 4
and
M
is spin, the situation changes dramatically.
Using a secondary version of the
\alpha
-invariant, the so called
index difference
, many non-trivial elements in the homotopy groups of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
were discovered, see for example
[
hitchin1974harmonic
]
,
[
carr1988construction
]
,
[
hanke2014space
]
,
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
, and
[
frenck2021SphericalKappa
]
.
A priori, there are two versions of the index difference, one is due to Hitchin
[
hitchin1974harmonic
]
, the other one is due to Gromov-Lawson
[
gromov1983positive
]
.
Let us now describe these in turn.

Recall that
\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(H)
, the space of
Cl_{d,0}
-linear Fredholm operators on a separable Hilbert space with a
Cl_{d,0}
-module structure, is a classifying space for real
K
-theory.
This means that there is a natural isomorphism

[X;\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(H)]\cong KO^{-d}(X)

for all finite CW-complexes
X
.
Here,
Cl_{d,0}
denotes the real Clifford algebra, the universal algebra generated by
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
under the relations
e_{i}e_{j}+e_{j}e_{i}=-2\delta_{ij}
.

Fix a base point
g_{0}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
For each psc metric
g\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
, convex combination gives a path of Riemannian metrics
g_{t}
(possibly without positive scalar curvature).
This path gives rise to a path of Dirac operators
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{t}}
that is invertible near the end points.
Its bounded transform is a path of Fredholm operators with invertible ends
1
1

1

We ignore for this moment that the Hilbert space itself depend on the metric, too.
.
The space of invertible operators is contractible by Kuiper’s theorem, see
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Lemma 2.8 for the Clifford-linear version, so we can close this path to a loop.
This procedure yields a continuous map

\mathrm{inddif}_{H}(\text{--},g_{0})\colon\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\rightarrow\Omega\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(H).

The Gromov-Lawson version is constructed differently.
For a psc metric
g\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
, we consider the metric
G
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
that is given by

G=\begin{cases}g_{0}+\mathrm{d}t^{2},&\text{on }M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq-1},\\
\frac{1-t}{2}g_{0}+\frac{(1+t)}{2}g+\mathrm{d}t^{2},&\text{on }M\times[-1,1],\\
g+\mathrm{d}t^{2},&\text{on }M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}.\end{cases}

Its Dirac operator is still an unbounded Fredholm operator, so its bounded transform is an element in
\mathrm{Fred}^{d+1,0}(H)
.
This gives a continous map

\mathrm{inddif}_{GL}(\text{--},g_{0})\colon\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{Fred}^{d+1,0}(H).

The main result of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
shows that the two versions essentially agree, that means, composed with the weak homotopy equivalence
\mathrm{Fred}^{d+1,0}(H)\rightarrow\Omega\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(H)
, the Gromov-Lawson version of the index difference map is homotopic to the Hitchin version.

The important feature of the Gromov-Lawson version is its invariance under
concordance
and not just
isotopy
.
Let us recall these concepts.
An isotopy between two psc metrics is nothing but a smooth path between them, so the term isotopy is just a synonym for smooth homotopy.
A concordance between two psc metrics on the other hand is a psc metric
G
on
M\times[-1,1]
that agrees near the end points
M\times\{j\}
with the metrics
g_{j}+\mathrm{d}t^{2}
.
We call two psc metrics
isotopic/concordant
if there is an isotopy/concordance between them.
Being isotopic implies being concordant.
It is an open conjecture whether the converse is also true.

Conjecture

(Concordance-Implies-Isotopy)
.

If
g_{0},g_{1}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
are concordant, then they are also isotopic.

There are counterexamples to this conjecture in dimension 4, see
[
ruberman2002positive
]
, but this conjecture might still be true in higher dimension.

We observe a huge discrepancy:
Although we are interested in the homotopy type of the space
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
(its isotopy type), our invariants at hand are invariant under concordance.
Thus, it makes sense to have a systematic look at
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
from a concordance perspective.
However, results in this direction are rather scarce.
Stolz
[
stolz1998concordance
]
considered the set of concordance classes of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and showed that it only depends on the first Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{1}(M)
and the group extension

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hat{\pi}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

that is classified by the second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(M)
, see
[
stolz1998concordance
]
for details.

Motivated by this discrepancy and the results of Stolz, we systematically study the space of psc metrics from a concordance view point in this thesis.
Our ansatz is inspired from the study of diffeomorphism groups of high-dimensional manifolds started in the late 60’s of the last century, which we are going to recall.

The theory of block diffeomorphisms as blue print

The key idea to study the space of diffeomorphism groups on a compact manifold
M
is to “approximate” the diffeomorphism group
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
by the larger group of
block diffeomorphisms

\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
.
This group of block diffeomorphism cannot be written down in a closed form but needs to be constructed with tools from combinatorial topology, like simplicial or cubical sets.
We use cubical sets here because we will also use them in the main body of this thesis.
The group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
is thus the geometric realisation of the cubical group whose
n
-cubes are given by diffeomorphisms on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
that map each face
M\times\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
, where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, to itself.
It contains
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
, or rather its combinatorial counterpart, as the cubical subgroup whose
n
-cubes are diffeorphisms
\varphi
on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
that make the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{M\times[-1,1]^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}}
\textstyle{M\times[-1,1]^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}}
\textstyle{[-1,1]^{n}.}

The advantage of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
over
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
is that it can be better handled with tools from algebraic topology like homotopy theory or surgery theory.
To explain this, we first need to introduce the topological monoid of
homotopy automorphisms

\mathrm{hAut}(M)
, which consists of all continuous self-maps that are homotopy equivalences.
There is also a block version
\widetilde{\mathrm{hAut}}(M)
that arises out of
\mathrm{hAut}(M)
in the same way as
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
arises out of
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
; but by
[
dold1963partitions
]
*Theorem 6.1 the canonical inclusion
\mathrm{hAut}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{hAut}}(M)
is a homotopy equivalence.
The inclusion
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{hAut}}(M)\simeq\mathrm{hAut}(M)
gives rise to a homotopy fibration

\textstyle{\mathrm{hAut}(M)/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathrm{hAut}(M).}

The classifying space
B\mathrm{hAut}(M)
is a purely homotopy-theoretic object and can be approached with tools from algebraic topology, like obstruction theory
[
rutter1997spaces
]
.
The fibre
\mathrm{hAut}(M)/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
has a close connection to surgery theory as it is weakly homotopy equivalent to the fibre in Quinn’s surgery fibration

\textstyle{\mathrm{hAut}(M)/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Maps}(M,G/O)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}[\pi_{1}M]),}

see
[
berglund2012homological
]
*Section 3 for a clear and concise discussion on the Surgery fibration and the weak equivalence statement.
The long exact sequence of homotopy groups of this fibration yields that
\pi_{k}(\mathrm{hAut}(M)/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M))
appear in the long exact surgery sequence

\textstyle{L_{k+d+1}(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}[\pi_{1}M])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{\partial}(M\times D^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{N}_{\partial}(M\times D^{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L_{k+d}(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}[\pi_{1}M])}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\pi_{k}\bigl(\mathrm{hAut}(M)/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)\bigr).}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

We refer to
[
Lueck2020surgery
]
for details on the surgery sequence.

The
L
-groups
L_{\ast}(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}[\pi_{1}(M)])
have a completely algebraic description, while the
normal bordism groups

\mathcal{N}_{\partial}(M\times D^{k})
can be studied via (stable) homotopy theory.
The upshot is that, despite its differential-topological origin of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
, one can extract a lot information out of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
using only algebraic and homotopy-theoretic tools, which reduces the difficulty of the problem in practice.

To get information about the difference between
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
, the object of interest, and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
, the object that is easier to study, one uses the fibration

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)/\mathrm{Diff}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathrm{Diff}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Diff}(M).}

In
[
hatcher1978concordance
]
, Hatcher examines the fibre
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)/\mathrm{Diff}(M)
with the help of the group of
pseudo isotopies

C(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Diff}(M\times[0,1])\,:\,\varphi=\mathrm{id}\text{ near }M\times\{0\}\cup\partial M\times[0,1]\}.

More precisely, he constructs a first quadrant spectral sequence that starts with
E^{1}_{p,q}=\pi_{q}(C(M\times[0,1]^{p}))
and converges to
\pi_{p+q+1}(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)/\mathrm{Diff}(M))
.

If
q\ll\dim M
, then one can actually compute the differentials
d^{1}_{p,q}
in the Hatcher spectral sequence and relate the results to Waldhausen’s algebraic
K
-theory.
Farell and Hsiang
[
farrell1978rational
]
used these tools to compute the rational homotopy groups
\pi_{k}(B\mathrm{Diff}(S^{n}))\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}
for
k\ll n
.
For completeness, the precise results is the following:

\pi_{k}(B\mathrm{Diff}(S^{n}))\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}=\begin{cases}\mathord{\mathbb{Q}},&\text{if }k\equiv 0\mod 4,n\text{ even},\\
\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}\oplus\mathord{\mathbb{Q}},&\text{if }k\equiv 0\mod 4,n\text{ odd},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

in the range
0<k<n/6-7
.

Statement of the results

The overall aim of this thesis is to develop the theoretical foundations to carry over the strategy of the study of diffeomorphism groups described in the last section to the world of psc metrics.
We will introduce the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
of
psc block metrics
, which should be thought of as an analog of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
in the psc realm.

As before, the space
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
cannot be written down in a closed form but needs to be constructed.
Our construction tool is cubical set theory because we believe that cubes are better suited to study curvature related questions than simplices due to the canonical identification
I^{n}\times I^{m}=I^{n+m}
.
In fact, we will mostly work in the category of cubical sets, instead of the category of topological spaces.
This is unproblematic because, in this thesis, we are only interested in homotopy-theoretic questions and the two categories have the same homotopy category.

To keep this thesis self-contained, we recall the foundations of cubical set theory we are going to use.
We first give a general introduction to cubical set theory and survey the existing literature before we discuss examples and variations of general concepts that are specifically adapted to this thesis.

We then model the approximation space
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
by the cubical set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
whose
n
-cubes can be thought of as psc metrics on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
that agree near each face
M\times\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
with the product metric
g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}}\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}
, where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.

Usually, cubical set theory only works well for so called
Kan-sets
, which should be thought of as cubical sets with “enough cubes”.
The first main result of this thesis can be considered as a door-opener to the combinatorial world:

Theorem A
.

If
M
is a closed psc manifold, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan set.

An important consequence of this result is that the homotopy groups of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
have a purely combinatorial description.
For example, the set of path components
\pi_{0}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M))
is precisely the set of concordance classes of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M),g_{0})
is the set of concordance classes of self-concordances classes of
g_{0}
.
We know from Stolz’ results in
[
stolz1998concordance
]
that
\pi_{0}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
only depends on the fundamental group of
M
if
M
is spin, and we expect a similar result for the higher homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
.

Cubical set theory also provides an abstract group structure on the combinatorial versions of the homotopy groups.
However, this group structure is implicit and, in practice, not really useful for calculations.
We therefore present a more geometric and explicit description.
In the case of the fundamental group, the geometric product of two self-concordances
G_{1},G_{2}
of
g_{0}
on
M\times[-1,1]
can be thought as the union
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
on
M\times[-1,3]
, which we re-identify with
M\times[-1,1]
.

Of course, we would like to relate the original space
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
.
A cubical model for
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is the cubical set
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
whose
n
-cubes are smooth maps
[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
that near each hyperface
\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
are independent of
x_{i}
.
The cubical map
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
that assigns to each map of psc metrics its adjoint metric

\bigl(g\colon[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\bigr)\mapsto g(\text{--})+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M\times[-1,1]^{n})

is not well defined because the adjoint metric can have non-positive scalar curvature.
However, if we restrict this map to a homotopy equivalent cubical subset of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
whose cubes are sufficiently slowly parametrised, then the adjoint metric has positive scalar curvature as well so that we end up with a well defined map on a weakly equivalent subset
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

The next task is of course to find out whether the index difference (or rather its cubical model) factors through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
If this is true, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
would have many non-trivial homotopy groups whenever
\dim M\geq 6
because the index difference induces non-trivial homomorphisms

\pi_{n}(\mathrm{inddif}(\text{--},g_{0}))\colon\pi_{k}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M^{d}),g_{0})\rightarrow KO^{-(d+k+1)}(\mathrm{pt})

whenever its target group is non-trivial; see
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
.

The starting point is the observation made in
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
that the space of
invertible pseudo Dirac operators

\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
on a closed, spin, psc manifold
M
of dimension
d
is a classifying space for
KO^{-d}
.
We prefer this model over
\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(H)
, because, in this picture, the index difference is the continuous map
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
that assigns to a psc metric
g
its Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
.
As for the space of psc metrics, a cubical model for
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
is given by the cubical set whose
n
-cubes are smooth maps
[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
that near each hyperface
\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
are independent of
x_{i}
and the continuous map
g\mapsto{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
induces a cubical map between the cubical models so that we end up with two cubical maps

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)}

that we would like to complete with a cubical map
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
to a homotopy commutative diagram.

If such a map exists, then it must have a complicated description because an element in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}}(M)
is a metric on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
while an element in
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{n}(M)
is a map
[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
.
To overcome this problem, we apply the same procedure we used to construct the cubical set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
out of the space
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
to the space
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
.
This yields the cubical set
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
of
invertible block Dirac operators
, whose
n
-cubes can be thought of as pseudo Dirac operators on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
that decompose near the boundary in the same manner as Dirac operators of block metrics would do.

In complete analogy to the map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
there is a cubical map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
, well-defined only on a homotopy equivalent subset of
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
, that assigns to a map of operators
[-1,1]^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
its “adjoint operator” on
M\times[-1,1]^{n}
.

We can now formulate the next main theorem of this thesis.

Theorem B
.

The map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

The important consquence of this theorem is that
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is another model for the classifying space of real
K
-theory that is big enough to host the Dirac operator of a general psc block metric.
We therefore get a cubical map
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
that assigns to a psc block metric its Dirac operator.

Theorem C
.

The diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)}

commutes up to homotopy.

Due to the nature of the construction, we interpret
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
as the cubical model of the Hitchin index difference and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
as cubical model for the Gromov-Lawson index difference.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
C
is the main result of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
that the Hitchin and the Gromov-Lawson version of the index difference agree.
However, Theorem
C
shows even more, because the index difference factors through
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
, which we believe to depend only on the fundamental group of the spin manifold
M
.
The image of the index difference in the real
K
-theory therefore only depends on the fundamental group.

Of course, as in the case of the diffeomorphism groups, we would like to know how much
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M)
or, equivalently, their cubical models,
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, differ from each other.
The concordance-implies-isotopy conjecture translates to the statement that
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
induces an injection (and hence a bijection) on the set of path components.
More daringly, we could conjecture:

Conjecture

(Strong Concordance-Implies-Isotopy)
.

The cubical map

\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Although the concordance-implies-isotopy conjecture is true if
\dim M\leq 3
(because
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is contractible), the stronger version is false.
We will present counterexamples in Chapter
5
.
In fact, we show even more:

Theorem D
.

If
M
is a psc manifold of dimension
2
or
3
, then the suspension map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is not a weak homotopy equivalence.

To get information about the difference between
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, in particular for high-dimensional manifolds, for which we have no counterexamples, we mimick the approach from the study of diffeomorphism groups and consider the homotopy fibre
\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
of the comparision map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
Inspired by the result of Hatcher
[
hatcher1978concordance
]
, we construct a psc-analog of the Hatcher spectral sequence, that approximates the homotopy groups of the homotopy fibre by the homotopy groups of the space of
psc pseudo isotopies

C^{\mathrm{psc}}(M\times I^{p})
.
This set consists of all psc block metrics on
M\times[-1,1]^{p+1}
that agree near each hyperface
M\times\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
, where
\varepsilon=\pm 1
, with the product metric
g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p}}
of the base point
g_{0}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and the euclidean metric, except near the last front face
M\times\{x_{p+1}=1\}
.

Theorem E
.

There is a first quadrant spectral sequence
E^{r}_{p,q}
starting with the homotopy groups
E^{1}_{pq}=\pi_{q}\bigl(C^{\mathrm{psc}}(M\times I^{p})\bigr)
and converging to
\pi_{p+q}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
if
p+q\geq 2
.

Outline of this thesis

A short outline of this thesis is the following one (for a more detailed description, see the introduction of each individual chapter).
Chapter
2
discusses the required material on cubical set theory.
Section
2.1
summarises the required general theory and surveys the existing literature, while Section
2.2
presents suitable modifications of the general theory and examples needed for this thesis.
Chapter
3
focuses on the construction of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, its Kan property, i.e. Theorem
A
, and the geometric addition.
Chapter
4
is the operator-theoretic analog of Chapter
3
. There we construct
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
, show that it is a Kan set, and that it is a model for real
K
-theory.
In particular, Theorem
B
is the main result of Section
4.9
.
In Chapter
5
, we prove that the index difference factors through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, i.e. Theorem
C
.
As an application, we have a more in-depth discussion of how this thesis and the cubical set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
relate to previous works in the field of positive scalar curvature metrics.
In particular, we provide with the proof of Theorem
D
counterexamples to the strong concordance-implies-isotopy conjecture.
Finally, in Chapter
6
, we construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence, which proves Theorem
E
.
To keep this thesis self-contained, we give a thorough discussion of Sobolev spaces and pseudo differential operators in the Appendix.
At the end of this thesis we present a list of symbols that are used in more than one section.

Chapter 2
Cubical Set Theory

Cubical set theory is the foundational tool from combinatorial topology we use to construct the main objects of this thesis, the concordance set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
in Chapter
3
and the operator concordance set
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
in Chapter
4
.
These “approximation spaces” of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and
KO^{-(\dim M+1)}
cannot be written in a closed form but need to be constructed; and cubical sets are the tool of our choice.
Although simplicial sets are more popular, cubical sets fit our geometric setup better and this is the reason why we work with them.

We will lay out in Section
2.1
the foundation of cubical sets needed in this thesis.
The main result is Theorem
2.1.32
.
Roughly speaking, it says that the category of topological spaces and the category of cubical set carry the same information that are preserved under homotopy.
In other words, if one is interested in homotopy-theoretical questions, then one can work, in theory, equally well in both categories
1
1

1

In practice, which category is more convenient depends on the question. In this thesis, it will be more convenient to work in the category of cubical sets.
.

In Section
2.2
, we will present conceptual examples that will be applied later in this thesis.

2.1
Foundations of Cubical Sets

The presentation of this section mostly follows the articles of Rosa Antolini
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
and John Jardine
[
jardine2002cubical
]
,
[
jardine2006categorical
]
.
Different authors use different conventions for cubical sets.
The cubical sets we will use in this thesis have
no connections
.
We made the effort to collect everything we need, because the literature on cubical sets is quite scattered and, in contrast to simplicial sets, there is no standard reference.
Although the original article of Kan
[
KanAbstractHomotopyI
]
contains most of the results presented here, most theorems are not proven.
We do not claim any originality for this section; our contribution is merely to bring everything in consistent notation, and present proof-sketches when needed or hint at analogous statements in simplicial set theory from which the proof can be modified straightforwardly.

Throughout this section, we use the convention
I=[-1,1]
and
\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=\{\pm 1\}
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

The
box category

\Box
is the category whose objects are
I^{n}

for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and whose morphisms are generated by the following ones:

•

The morphism
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\colon I^{n-1}\hookrightarrow I^{n}
includes
\varepsilon
in the
i
-th position of the tuple.

Here,
(i,\varepsilon)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

•

The morphism
p_{i}\colon I^{n}\rightarrow I^{n-1}
projects the
i
-th component away.

Here,
i
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}
if
n>1
.
If
n=1
, then
p_{1}\colon I^{1}\rightarrow I^{0}=\{0\}
is the zero map.

The geometric idea is that
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}
identifies
I^{n-1}
with the face
\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}
of
I^{n}
and that
p_{i}
projects the
n
-dimensional cube to the
(n-1)
-dimensional cube along the
x_{i}
-axis.
All
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}
are the restrictions of affine maps
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and all
p_{i}
are restrictions of linear maps.
We will also denote restriction of these maps to other subsets by
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}
and
p_{i}
.
More generally, we will denote the insertion of
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
into the
i
-th position by
{\delta^{s}_{i}}
.

Combinatorial calculations imply the next lemma.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

The maps
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}
and
p_{i}
satisfy the
co-cubical identities

\displaystyle{\delta^{\omega}_{j}}{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}

\displaystyle={\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}{\delta^{\omega}_{j-1}},\quad\ \text{ if }i<j,

\displaystyle p_{j}p_{i}

\displaystyle=p_{i}p_{j+1},\quad\ \text{ if }i\leq j,

\displaystyle p_{j}{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}p_{j-1},&\text{if }i<j,\\
\mathrm{id},&\text{if }i=j,\\
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i-1}}p_{j},&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

An important consequence of these identities is the following characterisation of morphisms in the box category.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

For every morphism
\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\Box}(I^{n},I^{m})
exists an
r\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, natural numbers
n\geq j_{1}\geq\dots\geq j_{n-r}
, and pairs
(i_{1},\varepsilon_{i_{1}}),\dots,(i_{m-r},\varepsilon_{m-r})
with
i_{1}\leq i_{2}\dots\leq i_{m-r}
such that

\varphi={\delta^{\varepsilon_{i_{m-r}}}_{i_{m-r}}}\circ\dots\circ{\delta^{\varepsilon_{i_{1}}}_{i_{1}}}\circ p_{j_{1}}\circ\dots\circ p_{j_{n-r}}.

Definition 2.1.4
.

A
cubical set

X_{\bullet}
is a contravariant functor
X\colon\Box\rightarrow\mathbf{Set}
.
A
cubical map

f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
is a natural transformation between the contravariant functors
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
.

Unpacked, Definition
2.1.4
reads as follows.

Definition 2.1.5
.

A cubical set
X_{\bullet}
is a sequence of sets
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
together with
connecting maps

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=X({\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}})\colon X_{n}\rightarrow X_{n-1}

for all
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
with
n\geq 1
, and
\sigma_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=X(p_{i})\colon X_{n}\rightarrow X_{n+1}

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}
with
n\geq 0
that satisfies the
cubical identities

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\partial^{\omega}_{j}

\displaystyle=\partial^{\omega}_{j-1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i},\quad\ \text{ if }i<j,

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j+1}\sigma_{i},\quad\ \text{ if }i\leq j,

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\sigma_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j-1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i},&\text{if }i<j,\\
\mathrm{id},&\text{if }i=j,\\
\sigma_{j}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-1},&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

A
cubical map

f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
is a sequence of maps
f_{n}\colon X_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
that commute with all connecting maps.

We refer to an element of
X_{n}
as an
n
-cube of
X_{\bullet}
and sometimes call
X_{n}
the set of all
n
-cubes (of
X_{\bullet}
).
All cubical sets together with cubical maps form a category, which we denote by
\mathbf{cSet}
.

Example 2.1.6
.

Let
X
be a topological space. The
singular set

S_{\bullet}(X)

is the cubical set consisting of

S_{n}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{s\colon I^{n}\rightarrow X\text{ continuous }\}

and whose connecting maps are

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}s=s\circ{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sigma_{i}s=s\circ p_{i}.

In other words, the connecting maps are given by pre-composing a singular cube with a morphism of the box category.

A continuous map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
induces a map of cubical sets by post-composing it to singular cubes.
In this way, the singular set is a functor
S_{\bullet}\colon\mathbf{Top}\rightarrow\mathbf{cSet}
.

Example 2.1.7
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a cubical set and
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
a sequence of subsets of
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
to which the connecting maps restrict.
These restrictions turn the sequence of subsets into a cubical set
A_{\bullet}
such that the level-wise inclusion is a cubical map.
We call
A_{\bullet}
a cubical subset of
X_{\bullet}
.
For example, if
Y\subseteq X
is a topological subspace, then
S_{\bullet}(Y)
is a cubical subset of
S_{\bullet}(X)
.

Let
f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
be a cubical map and let
A_{\bullet}\subseteq X_{\bullet}
and
B_{\bullet}\subseteq Y_{\bullet}
be cubical subsets. The preimage
f_{\bullet}^{-1}(B_{\bullet})
and the image
f_{\bullet}(A_{\bullet})
are cubical subsets of
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
, respectively.
Furthermore, intersections of cubical subsets are cubical subsets and unions of cubical subsets are cubical subsets.

In this thesis, we use cubical sets as a blueprint to construct topological spaces by gluing cubes together.
This interpretation begs the question how to model the standard cubes in this manner.

Example 2.1.8
.

The
(cubical)

n
-cube

\Box[n]_{\bullet}

is the cubical set given by the functor
that sends an object
I^{m}
to
\mathrm{Hom}_{\Box}(I^{m},I^{n})
and a morphism
\varphi\colon I^{k}\rightarrow I^{m}
to its pre-composition
\varphi^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\Box}(I^{m},I^{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\Box}(I^{k},I^{n})
.
We will often just write
\ast
for
\Box[0]_{\bullet}
.
A morphism
\psi\colon I^{n}\rightarrow I^{r}
in
\Box
induces a cubical map
\psi_{\ast}\colon\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow\Box[r]_{\bullet}
by post-composition.
In this way,
\Box[\text{--}]_{\bullet}\colon\Box\rightarrow\mathbf{cSet}
is a covariant functor.

The cubical cubes can be considered as the building blocks of cubical sets.
The next lemma, which is an immediate application of the Yoneda lemma, makes this statement more precise.

Lemma 2.1.9
.

The evaluation at
I^{n}
is a natural bijection

\mathrm{ev}_{I^{n}}\colon\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(\Box[n]_{\bullet},X_{\bullet})\rightarrow X_{n}

for every cubical set
X_{\bullet}
.

Example 2.1.10
.

A base point
x_{0}
of a cubical set
X_{0}
is a cubical map
x_{0}\colon\Box[0]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.
Using Lemma
2.1.9
, we may identify the cubical map with its image
x_{0}\in X_{0}
.
The image of the map is the cubical subset
\{x_{0}\}_{\bullet}
given by
\{x_{0}\}_{n}=\{\sigma_{n-1}\dots\sigma_{1}x_{0}\}
.

For the sake of readability, we will denote
\sigma_{n-1}\dots\sigma_{0}x_{0}
again by
x_{0}
if
x_{0}
serves as a base point.

Example 2.1.11
.

For
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, the
cubical
n
-horn

\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
is the cubical subset of
\Box[n]_{\bullet}
given by

\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigcup\Bigl\{{\delta^{\omega}_{j}}(\Box[n-1]_{\bullet})\,:\,(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\setminus\{(i,\varepsilon)\}\Bigr\}.

It is the combinatorial model of the boundary of
I^{n}
with the open face
{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}(I^{n-1})^{\circ}
removed.
In view of Lemma
2.1.9
, every cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
uniquely determines a set of elements

\{x_{(j,\omega)}\in X_{n-1}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}x_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}x_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k,\,(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\},

where
(j,\omega)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\setminus\{(i,\varepsilon)\}
.
Conversely, each collection of this type uniquely determines a cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.

Example 2.1.12
.

The
cubical sphere

\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}
is the cubical set given by

\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigcup\Bigl\{{\delta^{\omega}_{j}}(\Box[n-1]_{\bullet})\,:\,(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\Bigr\}.

It is a combinatorial model for the boundary
\partial I^{n}=S^{n-1}
.
In view of Lemma
2.1.9
, every cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
uniquely determines a set of elements

\{x_{(j,\omega)}\in X_{n-1}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}x_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}x_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k\},

where
(j,\omega)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
Conversely, each collection of this type uniquely determines a cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.

If we think of a cubical set as a blueprint to construct a topological space, then we eventually want to construct this topological space from it.
This is what the geometric realisation functor does.

Definition 2.1.13
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be a cubical set. Equip each set
X_{n}
with the discrete topology.
Its
geometric realisation
is the topological space obtained by endowing the set

|X_{\bullet}|=\left(\bigsqcup_{n=0}I^{n}\times X_{n}\right)\biggl/\penalty\sim

with the quotient topology.
Here,
\sim
is the equivalence relation that is generated by
(\varphi(v),x)\sim(v,X(\varphi)(x))
for all
v\in I^{m}
,
x\in X_{n}
, and
\varphi\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\Box}(I^{m},I^{n})
.

The geometric realisation of a cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
is the continuous map
|f_{\bullet}|\colon|X_{\bullet}|\rightarrow|Y_{\bullet}|
that is induced by the sequence of continuous maps
\mathrm{id}\times f_{n}\colon I^{n}\times X_{n}\rightarrow I^{n}\times Y_{n}
.

The proof of the following lemma can be found in
[
jardine2006categorical
]
*p.89.

Lemma 2.1.14
.

The geometric realisation is a functor
|\text{--}|\colon\mathbf{cSet}\rightarrow\mathbf{Top}
.
It is the left adjoint to the singular set functor

|\text{--}|\colon\mathbf{cSet}\rightleftarrows\mathbf{Top}:\!S_{\bullet}.

Example 2.1.15
.

As expected, we have
|\Box[n]_{\bullet}|=I^{n}
and
\partial|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|=I^{n}\setminus{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}(I^{n-1})^{\circ}
.
However, only few continuous maps
I^{n}\rightarrow I^{m}
are geometric realisations of cubical maps
\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow\Box[m]_{\bullet}
, namely, the morphisms of the box category.

Let us now discuss products of cubical sets.
The (categorical) product of two cubical set
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
is the cubical set
(X\times Y)_{\bullet}
whose set of
n
-cubes is given by

(X\times Y)_{n}=X_{n}\times Y_{n}

and whose connecting maps are applied component-wise.
In contrast to simplicial sets, the categorical product does not commute with the geometric realisation.
Indeed, Jardine shows in
[
jardine2002cubical
]
that
|\Box[1]_{\bullet}\times\Box[1]_{\bullet}|\simeq S^{1}
instead of
I^{1}\times I^{1}
.
This is the reason why Kan introduced in
[
KanAbstractHomotopyI
]
a different product that fits the geometric expectations.

Definition 2.1.16
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
be cubical sets.
Define the
reduced product

(X\otimes Y)_{\bullet}
to be the cubical set whose set of
n
-cubes is the set of equivalence classes

(X\otimes Y)_{\bullet}=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}X_{k}\times Y_{n-k}\biggl/\sim,

of the equivalence relation generated by
(\sigma_{k+1}x,y)\sim(x,\sigma_{1}y)
for all
x\in X_{k}
,
y\in Y_{n-k}
, and
0\leq k\leq n
.
The connecting maps are given by

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}(x,y)=\begin{cases}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}x,y),&\text{if }i\leq k,\\
(x,\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-k}y),&\text{if }i>k,\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma_{i}(x,y)=\begin{cases}(\sigma_{i}x,y),&\text{if }i\leq k,\\
(x,\sigma_{i-k}y),&\text{if }i>k.\end{cases}

Example 2.1.17
.

As expected, the cubical sets
(\Box[m]\otimes\Box[n])_{\bullet}
and
\Box[m+n]_{\bullet}
are isomorphic.
An isomorphism is given by the unique cubical map that sends the element
\mathrm{id}_{I^{m+n}}\in\Box[m+n]_{\bullet}
to the equivalence class of
(\mathrm{id}_{I^{m}},\mathrm{id}_{I^{n}})
.
Its inverse is the cubical map induced by

\displaystyle\Box[m]_{k}\times\Box[n]_{l}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Box[m+n]_{k+l},

\displaystyle(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\varphi\circ p_{k+1}\circ\dots\circ p_{k+l},\psi\circ p_{1}^{\circ k}).

To check that both maps are indeed inverse to each other, it is enough to check that they send
\mathrm{id}_{I^{m+n}}
and
(\mathrm{id}_{I^{m}},\mathrm{id}_{I^{n}})
to each other, as all other elements in those cubical sets can be obtained either from
\mathrm{id}_{I^{m+n}}
or
(\mathrm{id}_{I^{m}},\mathrm{id}_{I^{n}})
using a composition of connecting maps.

Lemma 2.1.18
.

For all cubical sets
X_{\bullet}
,
Y_{\bullet}
, the geometric realisation
|(X\otimes Y)_{\bullet}|
is homeomorphic to
|X_{\bullet}|\times|Y_{\bullet}|
if the latter is equipped with the CW-topology.

Proof.

For the proof we refer to Jardine
[
jardine2006categorical
]
*p.92,
who introduced
\otimes
in a different but equivalent manner.
∎

We can use the reduced product to model homotopies combinatorially.

Definition 2.1.19
.

Let
f_{\bullet},g_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
be two cubical maps and let
A_{\bullet}\subseteq X_{\bullet}
be a cubical subset.
A
homotopy
between
f_{\bullet}
and
g_{\bullet}
that is
stationary
on
A_{\bullet}
is a cubical map
H_{\bullet}\colon(\Box[1]\otimes X)_{\bullet}\rightarrow Y_{\bullet}
such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(\Box[0]\otimes X)_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\delta^{1}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{{\delta^{-1}_{0}}}
\textstyle{X_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{Y_{\bullet}\,}
\textstyle{(\Box[1]\otimes X)_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{H_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{(\Box[1]\otimes A)_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\scriptstyle{{p_{0}}_{\ast}}
\textstyle{(\Box[0]\otimes A)_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>A_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{H_{\bullet}|_{A_{\bullet}}}

The maps
f_{\bullet}
and
g_{\bullet}
are called
homotopic relative to

A_{\bullet}
, if there is a homotopy between them that is stationary on
A_{\bullet}
.

The diagram in the previous definitons are, in fact, two diagrams in one.
The upper triangle describes a homotopy between two given cubical maps, the lower square describes what it means to be stationary.

In light of Lemma
2.1.9
, we may say that two elements
x_{-1},x_{1}\in X_{n}
are homotopic if and only if their representing maps
f_{x_{i}}\colon\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
agree on
\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}
and are homotopic relative to
\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}
.

For concrete calculations, the following equivalent description in terms of elements turns out to be more convenient.

Definition 2.1.20
.

Two elements
x_{-1},x_{1}\in X_{n}
are
homotopic
if
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}x_{-1}=\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}x_{1}
for all
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and if there is an
h\in X_{n+1}
that satisfies

\partial^{\pm 1}_{1}h=x_{\pm 1}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}h=\sigma_{1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-1}x_{\pm 1}.

We call such elements
h
a
homotopy
between
x_{-1}
and
x_{1}
.

Being homotopic is not an equivalence relation on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
in general.
However, it is an equivalence relation if the target satisfy an extension condition that was introduced by Kan in
[
KanAbstractHomotopyI
]
.

Definition 2.1.21
.

A cubical set
K_{\bullet}
is a
Kan set
or satisfies the
Kan extension property
if every commutative outer square has a dotted filler

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>\ast.}

More generally, a cubical map
p_{\bullet}\colon E_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
is a
Kan fibration
if every commutative outer square has a dotted filler

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>B_{\bullet}.}

A cubical set
X_{\bullet}
is
combinatorially contractible
if every commutative outer square has a dotted filler

\textstyle{\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>\ast.}

The name ’combinatorially contractible’ is motivated by Proposition
2.1.33
.
In light of the Yoneda lemma (see also Example
2.1.11
and Example
2.1.8
) we can give an element-wise description of Kan sets and Kan fibrations.

Lemma 2.1.22
.

A cubical set
K_{\bullet}
is a Kan set if and only if, for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
and for each collection

\bigl\{x_{(j,\omega)}\in K_{n-1}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}x_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}x_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\bigr\},

there is an element
x\in K_{n}
such that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}x=x_{(j,\omega)}
.
Here,
(j,\omega)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\setminus\{(i,\varepsilon)\}
.

More generally, a map
p_{\bullet}\colon E_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
is a
Kan fibration
if and only if, for each collection

\bigl\{e_{(j,\omega)}\in E_{n-1}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}e_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}e_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\bigr\}

and each
b\in B_{n}
with
p_{n-1}(e_{(j,\omega)})=\partial^{\omega}_{j}b
, there is an
e\in E_{n}
that satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}e=e_{(j,\omega)}
and
p_{n}(e)=b
.
Again,
(j,\omega)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\setminus\{(i,\varepsilon)\}
.

A cubical set
X_{\bullet}
is combinatorially contractible if and only if, for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
and each collection

\bigl\{x_{(j,\omega)}\in X_{n-1}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}x_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}x_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k\bigr\},

there is an element
x\in X_{n}
such that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}x=x_{(j,\omega)}
.
Here,
(j,\omega)
ranges over
\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

There are further formal consequences that can be proven analogously to their simplicial counterparts.

Lemma 2.1.23
.

Let
p_{\bullet}\colon E_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
be a Kan fibration and let
b_{0}
be a base point of
B_{\bullet}
.

(i)

If the base
B_{\bullet}
is a Kan set, then the total set
E_{\bullet}
is a Kan set.

(ii)

The fibre
F_{\bullet}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=p^{-1}_{\bullet}(\{b_{0}\})
is a Kan set.

Proof.

To prove the first claim, we consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>b_{0}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The dashed arrow exists because
B_{\bullet}
is Kan. Since
p_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration, we can lift the dashed arrow, which gives the dotted arrow.

The proof of the second claim is captured in the diagram

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>B_{\bullet}.}

The dashed arrow exists because
p_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration.
By construction, it takes values in the fibre, so the dotted arrow exists.
∎

Example 2.1.24
.

The singular set
S_{\bullet}(X)
of a topological space
X
is a Kan set.
Indeed, each singular cubical
n
-horn
f_{\bullet}\colon\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(X)
yields a continuous map
f\colon|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|\rightarrow X
. Clearly,
|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|=\partial I^{n}\setminus({\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}(I^{n-1}))^{\circ}
is a deformation retract of
I^{n}
. If
r\colon I^{n}\rightarrow|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|
is a retract, then
f\circ r\in S_{n}(X)
is an extension of the given singular
n
-horn
f_{\bullet}
.

With a similar argument, one can show that
S_{\bullet}(p)\colon S_{\bullet}(E)\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(B)
is a Kan fibration if
p\colon E\rightarrow B
is a Serre fibration.

Lemma 2.1.25
.

For all Kan sets
K_{\bullet}
and all cubical sets
X_{\bullet}
, being homotopic is an equivalence relations on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(X_{\bullet},K_{\bullet})
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof for the simplicial counterpart sketched in
[
curtis1971simplicial
]
*Lemma 1.15ff.
∎

We define the
n
-th homotopy group of a cubical set to be the
n
-th homotopy group of its geometric realisation.
However, if the cubical set is a Kan set, then there is a purely combinatorial description of the homotopy groups.
The following notation is taken from
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
.

Definition 2.1.26
.

Let
k_{0}
be a base point of the Kan set
K_{\bullet}
.
Recall the convention that we abbreviate
\sigma_{n-1}\dots\sigma_{0}k_{0}\in K_{n}
to
k_{0}
.
For
n\geq 1
, define the set of
n
-fold loops as

\Omega_{k_{0}}^{n}K_{\bullet}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigl\{k\in K_{n}\,:\,\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}k=k_{0}\in K_{n-1}\text{ for all }(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\bigr\}.

There is also a version for pairs of Kan sets, see
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
.

Definition 2.1.27
.

For every cubical set
X_{\bullet}
, define
\pi_{0}(X_{\bullet})
to be the set of all (combinatorial) path components

\pi_{0}(X_{\bullet})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=X_{0}/\sim_{\text{homotopy}}\!.

If
K_{\bullet}
is a Kan set with base point
k_{0}
, then define the higher (combinatorial) homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet},k_{0})
via

\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet},k_{0})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Omega^{n}_{k_{0}}(K_{\bullet})/\sim_{\text{homotopy}},

where
\sim_{\text{homotopy}}
denotes the element-wise homotopy relation from Definition .

We can use the Kan condition to put a purely combinatorial group structure on
\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet})
for all
n>0
.
For two given elements
[a],[b]\in\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet},k_{0})
, we pick representatives
a,b\in\Omega_{k_{0}}^{n}K_{\bullet}
, respectively.
These two elements form an
\sqcap^{n+1}_{(1,1)}
-horn with
k_{(1,-1)}=a
,
k_{(2,1)}=b
, and
k_{(j,\omega)}=k_{0}
for all other
(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\setminus(1,1)
.
By the Kan extension property, this cubical horn has a filler
x\in K_{n+1}
, and the cubical identities imply that
\partial^{1}_{1}x\in\Omega^{n}_{k_{0}}K_{\bullet}
.
We define
[a]+[b]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[\partial^{1}_{1}x]
.

Lemma 2.1.28
.

[
KanAbstractHomotopyI
]
*Theorem 7
This operation is independent of all choices and turns
\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet},k_{0})
into a group for all
n\geq 1
.

There are also relative homotopy groups and connecting maps
\partial_{n}\colon\pi_{n}(K_{\bullet},L_{\bullet})\rightarrow\pi_{n-1}(L_{\bullet})
that fit into a long exact sequence, see
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
for details.
Furthermore, different choices of base points within the same path component yield isomorphic groups, see
[
KanAbstractHomotopyI
]
*Theorem 8 (the isomorphisms are not canonical, though).

Adapting the straightforward (but cumbersome) proof of
[
curtis1971simplicial
]
*Theorem 2.8
to the cubical setup, we get the following fibration result.

Lemma 2.1.29
.

Let
E_{\bullet}\rightarrow B_{\bullet}
be a Kan fibration, let
b_{0}
be a base point of the Kan set
B_{\bullet}
, and let
F_{\bullet}=p_{\bullet}^{-1}(b_{0})
be the fibre.
Then, for all
n\geq 1
, there are maps of pointed sets
\partial_{n}\colon\pi_{n}(E_{\bullet})\rightarrow\pi_{n-1}(F_{\bullet})
that are group homomorphisms if
n\geq 2
, and that give rise to a long exact sequence of homotopy groups

\textstyle{\dotsb\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(F_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(E_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(B_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n-1}(F_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\dotsb.}

Lastly, we would like to know that cubical sets carry the same homotopy information within them as topological spaces.

Theorem 2.1.30
.

[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
*Theorem 2.9
Let
K_{\bullet}
be a Kan set. The unit of the adjunction in Lemma
2.1.14
,

\psi_{\bullet}\colon K_{\bullet}\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(|K_{\bullet}|)\quad\text{given by}\quad k\mapsto\bigl(x\mapsto[x,k]\bigr),

induces isomorphisms on all homotopy groups.

Although it is easy to prove that the homotopy groups
\pi_{n}(S_{\bullet}(X))
and
\pi_{n}(X)
are isomorphic, the theorem itself is quite deep.
For example, it implies that every cubical map
\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(|K_{\bullet}|)
that is constant on the boundary is homotopic relative to its boundary to a cubical map
\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow K_{\bullet}
.
In
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
, the theorem is not proven.
Instead, the source refers to the book of May
[
may1992simplicial
]
by analogy.
We will also not prove this theorem in detail, but guide through the existent literature that can be used as a blue-print to prove this theorem following the strategy of Milnor
[
milnor1957geometric
]
.

Proof.

We first show that
K_{\bullet}
and
S_{\bullet}(|K_{\bullet}|)
have the same homology groups.
The proof of
[
milnor1957geometric
]
*Lemma 5 carries over without change
if we use homology groups for cubical sets whose definition is along the lines in
[
dubrovin1990modern
]
.

The next step is to prove that
K_{\bullet}
and
S_{\bullet}(|K_{\bullet}|)
have isomorphic fundamental groups.
Following
[
milnor1957geometric
]
, first observe that each cubical Kan set
K_{\bullet}
has a minimal subcomplex
M_{\bullet}
, which is, by definition, a cubical subset that satisfies the Kan extension property, that is a (combinatorial) strong deformation retract of
K_{\bullet}
, and in that every element
x\in M_{n}
is the unique representative of its homotopy class
2
2

2

homotopy class in the sense of Definition
2.1.27
for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
.
The existence proof can be carried out, for example, along the line of
[
curtis1971simplicial
]
*Prop 1.23.
By minimality,
M_{\bullet}
consists of only one vertex per path component, so the geometric realisation
|M_{\bullet}|
is a CW complex with a single zero cell per path component.
The fundamental group of such a CW complexes has one generator per edge and one relation per attaching map.
But the combinatorial group structure of
\pi_{1}(M_{\bullet})
is a just a reformulation of these relations, so the unit induces an isomorphism
\pi_{1}(M_{\bullet})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(S_{\bullet}(|M_{\bullet}|))=\pi_{1}(|M_{\bullet}|)
because it sends each element of the combinatorial fundamental group to an edge.

Since the unit
\psi_{\bullet}
is natural with respect to cubical maps and since the inclusion
M_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow K_{\bullet}
is a homotopy equivalence, the unit for
K_{\bullet}
induces an isomorphism on the fundamental group because of the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{1}(K_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}(\psi_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(S_{\bullet}(|K_{\bullet}|))}
\textstyle{\pi_{1}(M_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}(\mathrm{incl}_{\bullet})}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}(\psi_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\>\pi_{1}(S_{\bullet}(|M_{\bullet}|))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}(S_{\bullet}(|\mathrm{incl}_{\bullet}|))}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

We prove that the unit induces an isomorphism on higher homotopy groups by applying a relative Hurewicz theorem in the category of cubical sets to the pair of universal coverings
(S_{\bullet}(|\tilde{K}_{\bullet}|),\tilde{K}_{\bullet})
to show that all relative homotopy groups vanish.
Note that the homology of this pair of cubical sets vanish by an argument analogous to the one that simplicial and singular homology of a simplicial set
3
3

3

Hatcher speaks of
\Delta
-sets or semisimplicial complexes. However, these are nothing but the geometric realisation of a simplicial set.
agree, see
[
hatcher2001algebraic
]
*Chapter 2.1.

The relative Hurewicz theorem for cubical sets can be proven as follows.
First, one establishes a cubical version of the homotopy addition theorem as in
[
curtis1971simplicial
]
*Theorem 2.4
and uses it to prove an absolute Hurewicz theorem for cubical sets as in
[
curtis1971simplicial
]
*Theorem 3.12.
Then the relative Hurewicz theorem can be derived by modifying the proof of the relative Hurewicz theorem in
[
stocker2013algebraische
]
.
∎

The main theorem of this chapter is the cubical extension theorem, which was proven in
[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
.
The key ingredient of its proof is Theorem
2.1.30
.

Theorem 2.1.31

(Cubical Approximation Theorem)
.

[
antolini_cubical_2000
]
*Theorem 2.11

Let
(X_{\bullet},A_{\bullet})
be a pair of cubical sets and let
K_{\bullet}
be a Kan set.
For every continuous map
g\colon|X_{\bullet}|\rightarrow|K_{\bullet}|
whose restriction to
|A_{\bullet}|
is the geometric realisation of a cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon A_{\bullet}\rightarrow K_{\bullet}
, there is a cubical map
F_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow K_{\bullet}
such that
F_{\bullet}|_{A_{\bullet}}=f_{\bullet}
and
|F_{\bullet}|
is homotopic to
g
relative to
|A_{\bullet}|
.

A formal consequence of this (relative) homotopy extension theorem is that the (naive) homotopy categories of cubical sets and CW complexes are isomorphic.
Informally speaking, this means that if we are only interested in homotopy-theoretic question, then it does not matter in which category we work.

Theorem 2.1.32
.

1.

For all CW complexes
X
, the co-unit of the adjunction between topological spaces and cubical sets

\phi\colon|S_{\bullet}X|\rightarrow X,\quad\text{given by }\quad[t,\sigma]\mapsto\sigma(t),

is a homotopy equivalence.

2.

If
K_{\bullet}
is a Kan set and
X_{\bullet}
is an arbitrary cubical set, then the geometric realisation induces a bijection between the homotopy classes of maps

|\text{--}|\colon[X_{\bullet};K_{\bullet}]\rightarrow[|X_{\bullet}|;|K_{\bullet}|].

3.

The singular set functor induces a bijection between the homotopy classes of maps

S_{\bullet}\colon[X,Y]\rightarrow[S_{\bullet}(X),S_{\bullet}(Y)]

for all CW complexes.

Proof.

To prove the first claim, observe that, by definition, the co-unit
\phi
is a weak homotopy equivalence if and only if
S_{\bullet}(\phi)\colon S_{\bullet}(|S_{\bullet}X|)\rightarrow S_{\bullet}X
induces an isomorphism on all (combinatorial) homotopy groups.
From the decomoposition

\textstyle{S_{\bullet}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}(|S_{\bullet}X|)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{\bullet}\phi}
\textstyle{S_{\bullet}X,}

we deduce that
S_{\bullet}(\phi)
induces an isomorphism on all (combinatorial) homotopy groups because
\psi_{\bullet}
and the identity do it.
By Whitehead’s theorem,
\phi
is an homotopy equivalence.

The second claim follows immediately from the Cubical Approximation Theorem:
To prove surjectivity, we apply it to a given continuous map
g\colon|X_{\bullet}|\rightarrow|Y_{\bullet}|
.
To prove injectivity, we apply the Cubical Approximation Theorem to a homotopy
H
relating
|f_{\bullet}|
and
|g_{\bullet}|
so that we deform
H
relative to
|X_{\bullet}|\times\partial I=|(X\otimes\partial\Box[1])_{\bullet}|
to a cubical homotopy
|H_{\bullet}|
.

The third claim follows from the first claim by adjunction

\textstyle{[X,Y]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{\bullet}}
\scriptstyle{\text{--}\circ\phi}
\textstyle{[S_{\bullet}X,S_{\bullet}Y]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Ad}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{[|S_{\bullet}X|,Y].}

As
\phi
is a homotopy equivalence, it induces a bijection between the homotopy classes and so does
S_{\bullet}
.
∎

As an application, we show that the geometric realisation of a combinatorially contractible cubical set is contractible.

Proposition 2.1.33
.

Let
X_{\bullet}
be combinatorially contractible. Then
X_{\bullet}
is Kan and its geometric realisation is contractible.

Proof.

For a given a cubical
n
-horn

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>\ast,}

the cubical map
f
can be extended to
\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}
as follows:
The cubical subset
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\cap{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\left(\Box[n-1]_{\bullet}\right)
can be identified with
\partial\Box[n-1]_{\bullet}
.
Since
X_{\bullet}
is combinatorially contractible, the restriction of
f
to
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\cap{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\left(\Box[n-1]_{\bullet}\right)
can be extended to a map
f_{(i,\varepsilon)}\colon{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}\left(\Box[n-1]_{\bullet}\right)\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.
Using again that
X_{\bullet}
is combinatorially contractible, we get the extension

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\cup f_{(i,\varepsilon)}}
\textstyle{X_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\>\ast,}

which shows that
X_{\bullet}
is Kan.

To show that
X_{\bullet}
is contractible, we show that all homotopy groups are trivial.
This is clear for
\pi_{0}
because the definition of combinatrial contractibility for
n=1
precisely states that every two points can be joined by a path.
For the higher homotopy groups, let
x\in\Omega^{n}_{x_{0}}(X_{\bullet})
be given.
It can be represented by a map
f_{x}\colon\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
that restricts on
\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}
to the constant map with value
k_{0}
.
If we identify
\Box[n]_{\bullet}
with
{\delta^{-1}_{1}}(\Box[n]_{\bullet})\subseteq\partial\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}
, then we can extend
f_{x}
by the constant map with value
x_{0}
to the cubical set
\partial\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}
.
As
X_{\bullet}
is combinatorially contractible, the extension has a filler
h\colon\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.
Under the identification
\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}\cong\left(\Box[1]\otimes\Box[n]\right)_{\bullet}
, the filler corresponds to a homotopy between the constant map
x_{0}
and
f
.
Thus,
x
represents the zero element and
\pi_{n}(X,x_{0})
is therefore trivial.

Theorem
2.1.30
and the remark below it implies that
|X_{\bullet}|
is a CW complex with vanishing homotopy groups, so it is contractible by Whitehead’s theorem.
∎

Theorem
2.1.32
can actually be improved.
Cisinski proved in
[
cisinski2006prefaisceaux
]
, see also Jardine
[
jardine2006categorical
]
, that the category of cubical sets carries a model structure.
The weak equivalences are cubical maps whose geometric realisations induce isomorphisms on all (topological) homotopy groups.
The fibrations are given by Kan fibrations.
The cofibrations are just cubical maps that are level-wise injective.
The geometric realisation and the singular set functor form a Quillen equivalence between
\mathbf{cSet}
, equipped with the just mentioned model structure, and the category
\mathbf{Top}
of topological space endowed with the Quillen model structure.
4
4

4

Oversimplifying: The Quillen model structure favours the CW approximation of a topological space over the space itself.

2.2
Examples of Cubical Sets

In this subsection we discuss some conceptual examples that we will need later in this thesis.
We begin with the construction of a different model for the singular set of topological spaces.

2.2.1
Block Versions of the Singular Set

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
X
be a topological space. A continuous map
f\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X
is a
block map
if there is a
\rho>0
such that, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, the map
f
satisfies

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{n})=f(x_{1},\dots,\mathrm{sgn}(x_{i})\rho,\dots,x_{n})

if
|x_{i}|\geq\rho
.

We think of block maps as singular cubes with unknown side length
2\rho
.

Definition 2.2.2
.

For a topological space
X
, let
S_{\bullet}^{bl}(X)
be the cubical set whose set of
n
-cubes is given by

S_{n}^{bl}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{f\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X\,:\,f\text{ block map}\}

and whose connecting maps are given by

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}f=\lim_{R\to\infty}f\circ{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma_{i}f=f\circ p_{i}.

There is a canonical inclusion
\iota_{\bullet}\colon S_{\bullet}(X)\hookrightarrow S_{\bullet}^{bl}(X)
given by elongation.

Lemma 2.2.3
.

The cubical set
S_{\bullet}^{bl}(X)
is a Kan set and the canonical inclusion
\iota_{\bullet}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

It is straightforward to verify that the inclusion is a cubical map.

For a given singular cubical
n
-horn
f_{\bullet}\colon\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(X)
that is represented by the finite collection of block maps
\{f_{(j,\omega)}\}_{(j,\omega)\neq(i,\varepsilon)}
, we find a uniform
\rho
such that each
f_{(j,\omega)}
is the elongation of its restriction to
\rho I^{n-1}
.
The compatibility
\partial^{\omega}_{j}f_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}f_{(j,\omega)}
implies that we can unify these maps to a continuous map
f\colon\rho|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|\rightarrow X
.
Pick a retract
r\colon\rho I^{n}\rightarrow\rho|\!\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\!|
.
The constant extension of
f\circ r
in normal direction to each hypersurface
\{\varepsilon x_{i}=\rho\}
yields a filler for the given cubical
n
-horn.
Thus,
S_{\bullet}^{bl}(X)
is a Kan set.

We will use the combinatorial homotopy groups to show that the inclusion
\iota_{\bullet}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Clearly, the inclusion induces a surjective map on the set of path components for
S_{0}(X)=S_{0}^{bl}(X)=X
.
It is injective because for each block path
y\in S_{1}^{bl}(X)
joining
x_{-1}
and
x_{1}
there is a
\rho>0
such that
y
is the elongation of
y|_{\rho I}
. Then
y(\rho\cdot)\colon I\rightarrow X
is an element in
S_{1}(X)
that joins
x_{-1}
with
x_{1}
.

For the higher degree case, the proof is similar.
We first prove surjectivity.
Every element in
\pi_{n}(X_{\bullet},x_{0})
is represented by a block map
f\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X
that maps each point outside some
\rho I^{n}
to
x_{0}
.
Abbreviate the image of
f|_{\rho I^{n}}(\rho\cdot\text{--})\colon I^{n}\rightarrow X
under
\iota_{n}
to
\hat{f}
.
A joining homotopy
h\in S_{n+1}^{bl}(X)
can be constructed as follows.
Pick a smooth function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
that is identically
1
on
\{t\leq-1\}
and identically
\rho
on
\{t\geq 1\}
.
Then
h(t,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=f(\chi(t)x)
is a block map that satisfies
\partial^{-1}_{1}h=f
,
\partial^{1}_{1}(h)=\hat{f}
, and
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{j}h=x_{0}
if
j>1
.

We prove injectivity by applying the previous argument to homotopies.
Let
h\in S_{n+1}^{bl}(X)
be a homotopy that relates the extended singular cubes
\iota_{n}(f_{1})
and
\iota_{n}(f_{-1})
.
There is a
\rho>0
such that
h
is the elongation of
h|_{\rho I^{n}}
.
Thus,
h(\rho\cdot\text{--})\colon I^{n+1}\rightarrow X
is a homotopy between
\iota_{n}(f_{1})|_{\rho I^{n}}(\rho\cdot\text{--})
and
\iota_{n}(f_{-1})|_{\rho I^{n}}(\rho\cdot\text{--})
.
But
\iota(f)_{\rho I^{n}}(\rho\cdot\text{--})
is homotopic to
f
relative
\partial I^{n}
for all continuous maps
f\in S_{n}(X)
.
An example homotopy is provided by
\iota(f)|_{\rho_{t}I^{n}}(\rho_{t}\cdot\text{--})
, where
\rho_{t}=t\rho+(1-t)\cdot 1
.
Thus,
f_{1}
and
f_{-1}
already represent the same element in
\pi_{n}(S_{\bullet}(X))
and injectivity is proven.
∎

If the reference space
X
is a smooth object, which means in our context that
X
is a finite dimensional smooth manifold or an open subset of an affine Fréchet space
5
5

5

All affine Fréchet spaces we encounter are closed affine subspaces of an ambient Fréchet space, so the ready may safely only think of those.
, then we can also speak about smooth block maps.
A map
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X
into a Fréchet space
X
is smooth if all partial derivatives of it exists.
We denote the cubical subset of
S_{\bullet}^{bl}(X)
consisting of all smooth block maps by
\mathcal{S}_{\bullet}^{\infty}\left(X\right)
.

Lemma 2.2.4
.

The cubical set
\mathcal{S}_{\bullet}^{\infty}\left(X\right)
is Kan and the inclusion
