\mathcal{S}_{\bullet}^{\infty}\left(X\right)\hookrightarrow S^{bl}_{\bullet}(X)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

The proof of this lemma requires to generalise Whitney’s Approximation Theorem to Fréchet space valued functions.
We follow the formulation of
[
lee2013smooth
]
*Theorem 6.21 and Theorem 6.26.

Proposition 2.2.5

(Whitney’s Approximation Theorem)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold with corners,
X
be an open subset of an affine Fréchet space with Fréchet metric
d
, and
f\colon M\rightarrow X
a continuous map. Then the following two assertions hold true:

1.

For each continuous function
\delta\colon M\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, there is a smooth map
g\colon M\rightarrow X
satisfying
d(f,g)<\delta
.
If
f
was already smooth near a closed subset
A\subset M
, then
g
can be chosen such that
f|_{A}=g|_{A}
.

2.

The function
f
is homotopic to a smooth function
g
. If
f
is already smooth near
A
, then
g
can be chosen to be equal to
f
on
A
and the homotopy can be chosen to be stationary on
A
.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem 6.21 in
[
lee2013smooth
]
carries over without too much effort.
However, we need to be more careful because we have to deal with an unbounded number of different semi-norms, which is why we present the argument in full detail.

Recall that countably many semi-norms of a Fréchet space give rise to a metric
d
via the following construction:

d(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sum_{k=1}\frac{1}{2^{k}}\cdot\frac{||x-y||_{k}}{1+||x-y||_{k}}.

It follows that for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
there is a
N_{n}\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
such that if

||x-y||_{k}<\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\text{ for all }k\leq N_{n}\Longrightarrow d(x,y)<\frac{1}{2n}.

Note that
d(x,y)<1
and that we may assume without restriction that
\delta<1
, because we may replace
\delta
with
\delta\cdot(1+\delta)^{-1}
otherwise.
For all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
, we set
M_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\delta^{-1}([1/(n+1),1/n))
so that
M
is a disjoint union of all
M_{n}
’s.

Let
V
be an open neighbourhood of
A
on which
f
is already smooth and set
V_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in V\,:\,1/n>\delta(x)>1/(2n)\}
.
For a given
x\in M\setminus A
, we find a small open neighbourhood
U_{x}
in
M\setminus A
such that all
y\in U_{x}
satisfy

\frac{1}{n}\delta(y)>\frac{1}{2n}\quad\text{ and }\quad||f(y)-f(x)||_{k}<\frac{1}{2(n+1)}\text{ for all }k\leq N_{n}.

Pick a locally finite, countable subcover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
of
\{U_{x}\,:\,x\in M\setminus A\}
and extend it with
\{V_{n}\}_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
to a locally finite, countable cover of
M
.
Rename
V_{i}
into
U_{-(i-1)}
so that the open cover is
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

Pick a partition of unity
(\varphi_{i})_{i\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}
that is subordinated to that cover and define

g(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(x_{i})+\sum_{i\leq 0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(y).

Of course, we have
f|_{A}=g|_{A}
by construction.

If
y\in M\setminus A
, then we observe that

d\left(g(y),f(y)\right)=d\left(\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(x_{i}),\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(y)\right).

By assumption,
\varphi_{i}(y)\neq 0
for only finitely many
i\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\bar{i}
be the minimal number with that property.
This implies that
1/\bar{i}>\delta(y)>1/(2\bar{i})
.
Thus, it suffices to show that

\left|\left|\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(x_{i})-\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(y)\right|\right|_{k}<\frac{1}{2(\bar{i}+1)}

for all
k\leq N_{\bar{i}}
.
But this follows straightforwardly from

\displaystyle\left|\left|\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(x_{i})-\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(y)\right|\right|_{k}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)(f(x_{i})-f(y))\right|\right|_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)||f(x_{i})-f(y)||_{k}

\displaystyle<\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)\frac{1}{2(i+1)}

\displaystyle<\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)\frac{1}{2(\bar{i}+1)}=\frac{1}{2(\bar{i}+1)}

This now implies

d(g(y),f(y))=d\left(\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(x_{i}),\sum_{i>0}\varphi_{i}(y)f(y)\right)<\frac{1}{2\bar{i}}<\delta(y).

The second statement follows immediately from the first.
Since
X
is an open subset of an affine Fréchet space there is a positive function
\delta\colon M\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
such that all functions
\tilde{f}
with values in this affine Fréchet space with
d(\tilde{f},f)<\delta
still take values in
X
.
If
g
is a function as in the first statement, then a homotopy between
f
and
g
is given by convex combination:

H\colon M\times[0,1]\rightarrow X,\qquad(m,t)\mapsto(1-t)f(m)+tg(m).

(The proof of the first statement can be easily adapted to show that

d(H(m,s),f(m))<\delta(m),

so that
H
indeed takes values in
X
).
If
f
and
g
agree on
A
, then
H
is stationary on
A
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2.4
.

It is straightforward to verify that the inclusion is a cubical map.

For a given a singular cubical
n
-horn
f_{\bullet}\colon\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow S_{\bullet}(X)
that is represented by the finite collection of block maps
\{f_{(j,\omega)}\}_{(j,\omega)\neq(i,\varepsilon)}
, we find a
\rho
such that each
f_{(j,\omega)}
is the elongation of its restriction to
\rho I^{n-1}
.
Let
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho,x_{j}\in\rho I^{n}\text{ if }j\neq i\}
be the infinite cuboid that is open towards
\varepsilon\infty
in the
i
-th coordinate.
The compatibility
\partial^{\omega}_{j}f_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}f_{(j,\omega)}
implies that we can unify these maps to a continuous map
f\colon\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\right)^{c}\rightarrow X
.
Pick an open subset
U\subset\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\right)^{c}
that contains
\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{i,\epsilon}^{R}}\right)^{c}
for some
R>\rho
and that is diffeomorphic to
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
relative
\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{i,\epsilon}^{R}}\right)^{c}
.
If we denote the diffeomorphism by
\phi
, then
f\circ\phi^{-1}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X
yields a filler for the given cubical
n
-horn.

We will use the combinatorial homotopy groups to show that the inclusion is a weak equivalence.

For surjectivity, we use Proposition
2.2.5
to find a smooth block map that is sufficiently
\mathcal{C}^{0}
-close to any chosen representative of a given element of
\pi_{n}(X)
.
A homotopy between the given representative and its approximation can be given by convex combination.

For injectivity, we apply the previous argument to homotopies.
∎

The following criterion is useful for constructing cubical subsets of singular sets.
Recall that
p_{j}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
are the linear maps that project the
j
-th component away.

Definition 2.2.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold or an open subset of an affine Fréchet space.
For
0\leq k\leq\infty
, let
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},X)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
be a sequence of maps.
This sequence is called
stable
if
f_{n}(\psi\circ p_{j})=f_{n-1}(\psi)\circ p_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n

This sequence is called
local
if, for any open
U\subset\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the equation
f_{n}(\psi|_{U})=f_{n}(\psi)|_{U}
holds.

We formulate the next Lemma only in the smooth case for definiteness.
Of course, it also holds for any other degree of differentialbility.

Lemma 2.2.7
.

Let
X
be an open subset of an affine Fréchet space.
Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
be a local and stable sequence of maps
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},X)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
.
Then the preimage
f_{n}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},U))\cap\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\infty}\left(X\right)
forms a cubical subset of
\mathcal{S}_{\bullet}^{\infty}\left(X\right)
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow X
be a block map such that
f_{n}(\varphi)\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow U
.
We need to show that the images of
f_{n-1}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi)
and
f_{n+1}(\sigma_{i}\varphi)
also lie in
U
.
From stability we get

f_{n+1}(\sigma_{i}\varphi)(y)=f_{n+1}(\varphi\circ p_{i})(y)=f_{n}(\varphi)(p_{i}(y))\in U

for all
y\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
Since
\varphi
is a block map, there is a sufficiently large
R>0
such that
\varphi|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}}=\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi\circ p_{i}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}}
.
Because
f_{n}
is local and stable, we have

\displaystyle f_{n-1}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi)(y)

\displaystyle=f_{n-1}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi)(p_{i}{\delta^{2R\varepsilon}_{i}}y)\overset{\text{stab}}{=}f_{n}((\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi)\circ p_{i})({\delta^{2R\varepsilon}_{i}}y)

\displaystyle=f_{n}(\varphi|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}})({\delta^{2R\varepsilon}_{i}}y)\overset{\text{loc.}}{=}f_{n}(\varphi)({\delta^{2R\varepsilon}_{i}}(y))\in U

for all
y\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
∎

2.2.2
The Topological Space of Block Maps

When we construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence, we need to consider the set of block maps as a topological space (with a topology distinct from the discrete one).
The main result in this subsection is that the inclusion of the space of smooth block loops into the loop space (modeled by continuous block maps) is a weak homotopy equivalence.
In this subsection,
X
denotes either a smooth manifold or an open subset of an affine Fréchet space.

Definition 2.2.8
.

For each
R>0
set

{}_{R}\Omega^{q}X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{f\colon RI^{q}\rightarrow X\,:\,f=x_{0}\text{ near }\partial RI^{q}\}

and

{}_{R}\Omega^{q,\infty}X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{f\colon RI^{q}\rightarrow X\,:\,f\text{ smooth, }f=x_{0}\text{ near }\partial RI^{q}\}

endowed with the smooth Whitney topology.

For all
R<S
, we have embeddings
{}_{R}\Omega^{p,(\infty)}X\hookrightarrow{}_{S}\Omega^{p,(\infty)}X
given by elongation.
We use these spaces to define a block version of (smooth) loop spaces.

Definition 2.2.9
.

Let

\Omega^{q}X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{f\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\rightarrow X\,:\,f\text{ block map},f=x_{0}\text{ near }\infty\}

and

\Omega^{q,\infty}X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{f\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\rightarrow X\,:\,f\text{ smooth block map},f=x_{0}\text{ near }\infty\}

be equipped with the colimit topology induced by the embeddings
{}_{R}\Omega^{p,(\infty)}X\hookrightarrow{}_{S}\Omega^{p,(\infty)}X
.

We wish to show that the canonical inclusion
\Omega^{q,\infty}X\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}X
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
The proof is based on the following strengthening of the exponential law.

Lemma 2.2.10
.

Let
X
be an open subset of an affine Fréchet space or a finite dimensional manifold and let
M
,
N
be smooth, compact manifolds, possibly with faces.
Then the adjoint map

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(N,X))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M\times N;X)

is a diffeomorphism between the smooth spaces.

Proof.

We first consider the case, where
X
is an open subset of an affine Fréchet space for this is the case of interest.
We may assume that
X
is an Fréchet space because smoothness is a local property and preserved by translation with a fixed element.
Every Fréchet space is convenient in the sense of Kriegl-Michor
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*Theorem 2.14(7).
It thus becomes a Frölicher space whose smooth curves are precisely those that are infinitely often differentiable
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*Section 23.1.
If
M
is a compact manifold, then
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*Lemma 42.5 yields that the set-theoretic identity between the set of smooth maps with a priory different smooth structures

\mathfrak{C}^{\infty}(M,X)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\"{o}}}^{\infty}(M,X)

is an isomorphism in the category of Frölicher spaces.
Furthermore, the set-theoretic identity

\mathfrak{C}^{\infty}(M,X)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\text{Fr\'{e}chet}}(M,X),

where the right-hand side carries the smooth weak topology,
is an isomorphism of locally convex vector space by the remark above Lemma 42.5 in
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
.
The claim follows now from
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*Theorem 23.2, which states that the exponential map

\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\"{o}}}^{\infty}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2},Z)\cong\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\"{o}}}^{\infty}(Y_{1},\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\"{o}}}^{\infty}(Y_{2},Z))

is an isomorphism of Frölicher spaces for all Frölicher spaces
X,Y,
and
Z
.
Thus, we have

\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\'{e}chet}}^{\infty}(M,\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\'{e}chet}}^{\infty}(N,X))\cong\mathcal{C}_{\text{Fr\'{e}chet}}^{\infty}(M\times N,X).\qed

For completeness, if
X
is a finite dimensional smooth manifold, then the corresponding statement of Lemma
2.2.10
still holds, provided
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(N,X)
is given an appropriate smooth structure.
We refer to
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*§42 for more details, in particular
[
kriegl1997convenient
]
*Theorem 42.14.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

If
X
is a finite-dimensional smooth manifold or an open subset of an affine Fréchet space, then
the canonical inclusion
\Omega^{q,\infty}X\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}X
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

For the proof, we face the following nuisance of the category of topoligcal spaces: It is in general not true that a continuous map
f\colon K\rightarrow\mathrm{colim}_{i\in\mathcal{I}}X_{i}
from a compact space into a colimit factors through an element
X_{i}
of the diagram.
However, it is true in most cases of interest by the following result of
[
dugger2004topological
]
*Lemma A3.

Lemma 2.2.12
.

Let
(\varphi_{ij}\colon X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j})
be a sequential diagram of inclusions that are
relatively
T_{1}
,
i.e.
, for all
U\subseteq X_{i}
open and all
y\in X_{j}\setminus\varphi_{ij}(U)
there is an open
V\subseteq X_{j}
such that
\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(V)\subseteq U
and
y\notin V
.

Then, for each continuous map
f\colon K\rightarrow\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{colim}}}X_{i}
from a compact domain
K
, there is an
X_{i}
such that
f
factors through it.

The lemma can be applied in our setup because of the following elementary observation.

Lemma 2.2.13
.

Let
X_{1}\hookrightarrow X_{2}\hookrightarrow X_{3}\hookrightarrow\dotsb
be a sequence of embeddings of
T_{1}
-spaces.
Then each embedding is relative
T_{1}
.

Proof.

Fix a composition
\varphi_{ij}\colon X_{i}\hookrightarrow X_{j}
.
It is again an embedding.
Let
U\subseteq X_{i}
be open and
y\in X_{j}\setminus\varphi_{ij}(U)
be given.
The set
\tilde{V}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=X_{j}\setminus\{y\}
is open as
X_{j}
is
T_{1}
.
Since
\varphi_{ij}
is an embedding, we can find an open subset
\tilde{U}\subseteq X_{j}
such that
\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(\tilde{U})=U
.
Thus,
V\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\tilde{U}\cap\tilde{V}
is still open, satisfies
y\notin V
, and

\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(V)=\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(\tilde{U})\cap\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(\tilde{V})=U\cap\varphi_{ij}^{-1}(\tilde{V})\subseteq U.\qed

Proof of Lemma
2.2.11
.

It suffices to check that each continuous map
\varphi\colon M\rightarrow\Omega^{q}X
, where
M
is a compact, finite-dimensional manifold, can be deformed into a map with values in
\Omega^{q,\infty}X
relative to each closed subset on which
\varphi
already takes values in
\Omega^{q,\infty}X
.

Since
\Omega^{q}X
is a sequential colimit of embeddings between Hausdorff spaces, there is an
R>0
such that
\varphi
factors through
{}_{R}\Omega^{q}X
by Lemma
2.2.13
and
2.2.12
.
Under the exponential law, it corresponds to a continuous map
\mathrm{Ad}(\varphi)\colon M\times RI^{n}\rightarrow X
that is constant near the (topological) boundary
\partial RI^{n}
.
Approximation theory, see Proposition
2.2.5
, yields a homotopy
H\colon M\times RI^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow X
between
\mathrm{Ad}(\varphi)
and a smooth map
\psi\colon M\times RI^{n}\rightarrow X
that is still constant near the (topological) boundary.
If
\psi
is smooth on
A
, then
H
can be chosen to be stationary there.
Now,
\mathrm{Ad}^{-1}(H)
is a homotopy between
\varphi
and
\mathrm{Ad}^{-1}(\psi)
by Lemma
2.2.10
.
It is relative to the given closed subspace on which
\varphi
already takes values in
\Omega^{q,\infty}X
by the choice of
H
.
∎

2.2.3
Morphism Set and Path Sets

For two cubical sets
X_{\bullet}
and
Y_{\bullet}
, we will construct a cubical set
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(X,Y)
that has
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(X_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})
as
0
-cubes.
Define
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(X,Y)
to the cubical set with

\mathbf{Hom}_{n}(X,Y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}((\Box[n]\otimes X)_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})

and whose connecting maps are given by

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\text{--}\circ({{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{i}}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{i}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\text{--}\circ({p_{i}}_{\ast}\otimes\mathrm{id}).

It is the right adjoint functor to the reduced product

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}((X\otimes Z)_{\bullet},Y_{\bullet})\overset{1:1}{\rightleftarrows}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{cSet}}(X_{\bullet},\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(Z,Y)),

see
[
jardine2006categorical
]
*p.96, which is different compared to simplicial sets, where the
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}
is the right adjoint to the (categorical) product.

Our primary interest lies in the
cubical path set

P_{\bullet}X\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(\Box[1],X).

It comes with two cubical maps

\mathrm{ev}_{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\text{--}\circ{{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{1}}}_{\ast}\colon P_{\bullet}X=\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(\Box[1],X)\rightarrow\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(\Box[0],X)\cong X_{\bullet}.

The latter identification comes from the Yoneda Lemma.
These two cubical maps have a section

s\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\text{--}\circ{p_{1}}_{\ast}\colon X_{\bullet}\cong\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(\Box[0],X)\rightarrow P_{\bullet}X,

which can be thought of as mapping an element to the constant path with that element as its single value.

Lemma 2.2.14
.

If
X_{\bullet}
is a Kan set, then

(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{-1})\colon P_{\bullet}X\rightarrow(X\times X)_{\bullet}

is a Kan fibration.

Proof.

Let a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{P_{\bullet}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f,g)}
\textstyle{(X\times X)_{\bullet}}

be given.
Via the adjunction between
\otimes
and
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}
, the map
y
corresponds to a cubical map
\mathrm{Ad}(y)\colon(\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\otimes\Box[1])_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.
We use the decomposition

\sqcap^{n+1}_{(i,\varepsilon)}=\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\otimes\Box[1]_{\bullet}\cup\Box[n]_{\bullet}\otimes{\delta^{1}_{1}}(\Box[0]_{\bullet})\cup\Box[n]_{\bullet}\otimes{\delta^{-1}_{1}}(\Box[0]_{\bullet})

to extend
\mathrm{Ad}(y)
to
\sqcap^{n+1}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
as follows:

\mathrm{Ad}(y)\cup f\cup g\colon\sqcap^{n+1}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}.

As
X_{\bullet}
is Kan, this map extends to a filler
F\colon\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}
.
It is not hard to verify that under the isomorphism
\Box[n+1]_{\bullet}\cong(\Box[n]\otimes\Box[1])_{\bullet}
and the adjunction with
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}
the filler
F
yields a filler
\mathrm{Ad}^{-1}(F)\colon\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow P_{\bullet}X
for the initial diagram.
∎

As for topological spaces, we will use the cubical path set to turn every cubical map between Kan sets into a Kan fibration by replacing its domain with a homotopy equivalent cubical set.

For each cubical map
f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow K_{\bullet}
between Kan sets, we define the
mapping path space

P_{\bullet}(f)
as the following pullback

\textstyle{P_{\bullet}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\bullet}K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{-1})}
\textstyle{X_{\bullet}\times K_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\bullet}\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{K_{\bullet}\times K_{\bullet}.}

The universal property of pullbacks implies that
P_{\bullet}(f)\rightarrow X_{\bullet}\times K_{\bullet}
is a Kan fibration.
Composing it with the projection to
X_{\bullet}
and
K_{\bullet}
yields the maps
p_{\bullet}
and
q_{\bullet}
, respectively.
It is easy to see that the projection from a product of Kan sets to one of its factors is a Kan fibration and that fibrations are closed under compositions.
Thus, the maps
p_{\bullet}
and
q_{\bullet}
are also Kan fibrations.
The map
p_{\bullet}
has a section
s_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow P_{\bullet}(f)
, which is induced by
(\mathrm{id},f_{\bullet},s\circ f_{\bullet})\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow X_{\bullet}\times K_{\bullet}\times P_{\bullet}K
.

In view of the Yoneda Lemma
2.1.9
, we have the identification of cubical sets

P_{\bullet}X\cong X_{\bullet+1},

where the connecting maps of the latter cubical sets agree with the one of
X_{\bullet}
with index shifted by
1
, for example,
{}_{X_{\bullet+1}}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}={}_{X_{\bullet}}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i+1}
.
Under this identification and
\mathbf{Hom}_{\bullet}(\Box[0],X)\cong X_{\bullet}
, the fibration
\mathrm{ev}_{\varepsilon}
corresponds to
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{1}
.
Thus, the mapping path space can be identified with

P_{n}(f)\cong\{(x,k)\in X_{n}\times K_{n+1}\,:\,f_{n}(x)=\partial^{1}_{1}(k)\}.

Lemma 2.2.15
.

The map
s_{\bullet}\circ p_{\bullet}\colon P_{\bullet}(f)\rightarrow P_{\bullet}(f)
is homotopic to the identity.

Proof.

Let
F_{\bullet}=p_{\bullet}^{-1}(\{x_{0}\})
be the fibre of
p_{\bullet}
.
It is explicitly given by

F_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{(x_{0},k)\in X_{n}\times K_{n+1}\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}k=f_{n}(x_{0})\},

so it agrees with the pointed path space
P_{f(x_{0})}K_{\bullet}
.
The latter is combinatorially contractible by an argument similar to the proof Lemma
2.2.14
that
(\mathrm{ev}_{1},\mathrm{ev}_{-1})
is a Kan fibration.
The long exact sequence for Kan fibrations between Kan sets implies that
p_{\bullet}
must be a weak homotopy equivalence.
It follows from Whitehead’s Theorem that
|p_{\bullet}|\colon|P_{\bullet}(f)|\rightarrow|X_{\bullet}|
is a homotopy equivalence.
The cubical approximation theorem implies that
p_{\bullet}
must be a homotopy equivalence, too.
This, together with the equation
p_{\bullet}\circ s_{\bullet}=\mathrm{id}
, implies that
s_{\bullet}
is the homotopy inverse of
p_{\bullet}
.
∎

Definition 2.2.16
.

Let
f_{\bullet}\colon X_{\bullet}\rightarrow K_{\bullet}
be a cubical map between Kan sets.
The
homotopy fibre
of
f_{\bullet}
at the point
k_{0}\in K_{0}
is the Kan set

\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}(f_{\bullet};k_{0})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=q_{\bullet}^{-1}(\{k_{0}\})=\{(x,k)\in X_{n}\times K_{n+1}\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}k=f_{n}(x),\,\partial^{-1}_{1}k=k_{0}\}.

Remark 2.2.17
.

In contrast to simplicial sets, it is unknown to the author, whether the geometric realisation of a Kan fibration
F_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\iota}E_{\bullet}\xrightarrow{p}B_{\bullet}
is a Serre fibration.
However, Lemma
2.1.29
and Theorem
2.1.32
imply that it is a homotopy fibration, which means that the composition
|p|\circ|\iota|
is homotopic to a constant map
\mathrm{const}_{b_{0}}
and, for some null-homotopy
h
, the induced fibre comparison map

|F_{\bullet}|\rightarrow\mathrm{hofib}(|p_{\bullet}|)\quad\text{given by}\quad f\mapsto\bigl(h(b_{0},\text{--}),f,b_{0}\bigr)

is a weak homotopy equivalence.
In particular, the canonical map

|\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}(f_{\bullet};k_{0})|\rightarrow\mathrm{hofib}(|f_{\bullet}|;|k_{0}|)

is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Informally speaking, the cubical homotopy fibre models the correct thing!

Chapter 3
Cubical Versions of Positive Scalar Curvature

We will apply the theory of cubical sets developed in Chapter
2
to positive scalar curvature.
Cubical set theory allows us to construct the central object of this thesis, the concordance set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
of positive scalar curvature metrics, which cannot be written in a closed form.
This cubical set comes with a comparison map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
defined on a weakly equivalent cubical subset of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
, the cubical set analog of the space of psc metrics
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
Although we already describe the mapping rule of
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}
in this section, the construction of its domain, the weakly equivalent subset, and the proof that
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}
takes values in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
will be carried out in Chapter
5
after we have developed the index theoretical counterpart (because the index theoretical counter also influences the choices made in the construction of the weakly equivalent subset).

The main part of this chapter is devoted to show that the concordance set satisfy the Kan extension property.
This is an important property, as it allows us to apply the tools from cubical set theory to the concordance set.
For example, the Kan property gives us a combinatorial description of the homotopy groups of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, which is an inevitable tool for the construction of the psc Hatcher spectral sequence in Chapter
6
.

In Section
3.1
we introduce the concept of
block metrics
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and present some of their elementary properties.
We describe the cubical analog
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
and construct the concordance set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
as the cubical set of all block metrics with positive scalar curvature.
Section
3.2
serves as a preparatory section and is quite technical.
There, we will construct, for each block metric, a certain folation of hyperplanes in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that bend well adapted to the chosen block metric.
This foliation will then be used in Section
3.3
to modify a block metric with positive scalar curvature so that the resulting block metric still has positive scalar curvature.
This modification is the key ingredient to prove the Kan property, what we will do at the end of Section
3.3
.
We finish this chapter by giving a different and geometric motivated description of the groups structures on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
in Section
3.4
.

In the entire section, let
M
be a smooth closed, manifold of dimension
d
.

3.1
Foundations

We first translate the space of positive scalar curvature metrics into the combinatorial world by considering the cubical set of block maps into the space of Riemannian metrics
\mathrm{Riem}(M)

and the space of psc metrics
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)

instead of the spaces themselves.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}(M)

denote the cubical set whose
n
-cubes consists of smooth block maps
g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
.
The connecting maps are given by

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g=\lim_{R\to\infty}g\circ{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sigma_{i}g=g\circ p_{i}.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)

denote the cubical subset consisting of all smooth block maps with values in
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.

These cubical sets are indeed the correct models because by Lemma
2.2.3
and
2.2.4
and Theorem
2.1.32
(1) give the following zig-zag of weak equivalences

\textstyle{|\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}^{(+)}(M)|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\bigl|S_{\bullet}^{bl}\bigl(\mathrm{Riem}^{(+)}(M)\bigr)\bigr|}
\textstyle{\bigl|S_{\bullet}\bigl(\mathrm{Riem}^{(+)}(M)\bigr)\bigr|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Riem}^{(+)}(M),}

where the first map is the inclusion of smooth block maps into continuous block maps, the second map is the inclusion of singular cubes into continuous block maps described before Lemma
2.2.3
, and the last map is the co-unit of the adjunction between
|\text{--}|
and
S_{\bullet}(\text{--})
described in Theorem
2.1.30
.

Now, we are going to construct the combinatorial reference space.
First we have to make some organisational definitions.
For a visualisation, we refer to figure
3.1
.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Set
\mathbf{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
We define for
{\bm{\varepsilon}}\colon{\mathrm{dom}\,}\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}\subseteq\mathbf{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
,

the sets

\displaystyle U_{\rho}({\bm{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=M\times\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i)x_{i}>\rho\text{ for }i\in{\mathrm{dom}\,}{\bm{\varepsilon}}\},

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}({\bm{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Map}(\mathbf{n}\setminus{\mathrm{dom}\,}{\bm{\varepsilon}},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\cong\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i)x_{i}=\rho\},

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\rho}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\mathrm{dom}\,}{\bm{\varepsilon}}|}\,:\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i_{j})x_{j}>\rho;\,j\mapsto i_{j}\text{ strictly monotone}\}.

U_{\rho}(2,1)
U_{\rho}(1,-1)
U_{\rho}(1,2;-1,1)
x_{2}=\rho
x_{1}=-\rho

Figure 3.1:
An example for case
{\bm{\varepsilon}}=1\mapsto-1
, denoted by
(1,-1)
, for the case
{\bm{\varepsilon}}=2\mapsto 1
, denoted by
(2,1)
, and the union of these two maps, denoted by
(1,2;1,-1)
,

Note that the isomorphism is canonical once
\rho
is fixed.
Using permutation of coordinates we can identify
U_{\rho}({\bm{\varepsilon}})
with
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}({\bm{\varepsilon}})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\rho}
.
To be precise, all of these identification are of the form
\mathrm{id}_{M}\times A
, where
A\in GL_{n}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
is a permutation matrix.

For each
{\bm{\varepsilon}}
, and all
\rho
we set
{\delta^{\rho{\bm{\varepsilon}}}}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-|{\mathrm{dom}\,}{\bm{\varepsilon}}|}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that includes
\rho{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i)
into the
i
-th position of the tuple for all
i\in{\mathrm{dom}\,}{\bm{\varepsilon}}
.
The image is the affine hyperplane
\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i)x_{i}=\rho\}
.

Definition 3.1.3
.

A Riemannian metric
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a
block metric
if there is a
\rho>0
such that
g
restricts on all
U_{\rho}({\bm{\varepsilon}})
to the following product metric

g|_{U_{\rho}({\bm{\varepsilon}})}={\delta^{\rho{\bm{\varepsilon}}}}^{\ast}g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle=:g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}({\bm{\varepsilon}})}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle.

We say that
g

decomposes outside of

M\times\rho I^{n}
.

In fact, we can weaken this definition.

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
g
be a Riemannian metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
If there is a
\rho>0
such that

g|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}}={\delta^{\rho\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}g\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}=g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{i}=\varepsilon\}}\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}

for all
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, then
g
is a block metric.

Informally, we think of a block metric as a metric on
M\times\rho I^{n}
that is a product metric near each hyperface
\{x_{i}=\rho\varepsilon\}
.

Since
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an open manifolds, we have to make sure that block metrics are not covered by Gromov’s h-principle.
From this perspective, the next proposition tells us that block metrics are indeed interesting.

Proposition 3.1.5
.

Every block metric
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
has the following properties:

1.

The metric
g
is complete.

2.

If
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)>0
, then there is a constant
c>0
such that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\geq c
.

Proof.

For the first statement, we will prove that
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is complete with respect to the path-length metric induced by
g
.
Consider a Cauchy sequence
\{(m_{j},p_{j})\}
of points in
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Assume that
g
decomposes outside the cube
M\times\rho I^{n}
.
Then, either infinitely many points lie in one of the sets
M\times Q^{\varepsilon}_{k}
, where
Q^{\varepsilon}_{k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,|\,\varepsilon\cdot x_{k}\geq 1.2\rho\}
,
or infinitely many points lie in the cube
M\times 2\rho I^{n}
.
In the latter case, there exists a limit due to compactness.
In the first case, we use that
g
is a product metric of a block metric on
M\times{\delta^{2\rho\varepsilon}_{k}}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1})
and the euclidean metric on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.
Since a product metric of two complete metrics is again complete, we conclude the existence of a limit point.
Thus, the given block metric is complete.

For the second statement, we make an induction over the dimension.
For a block metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{0}=M
the statement is clear because
M
is compact.
Now let
g
be a block metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that it decomposes outside of
M\times\rho I^{n}
and has positive scalar curvature.
Due to compactness,
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\geq\tilde{c}
on
M\times 2\rho I^{n}
for some
\tilde{c}>0
.
By the induction hypothesis, the same is true for
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)
on
\{\varepsilon x_{j}>\rho\}
.
If
c
the minimum of the infima of
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)
over all these domains, then
c
is positive and satisfies
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\geq c
.
∎

Definition 3.1.6
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)

be the cubical set whose set of
n
-cubes is given by

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{g\in\mathrm{Riem}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\,:\,g\text{ block metric }\}

and whose connecting maps are

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g=\lim_{R\to\infty}{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}g\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sigma_{i}g=p_{j}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}.

The concordance set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)

is the sequence of subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
whose
n
-cubes have
positive scalar curvature
.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

The connecting maps of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
are well defined and satisfy the cubical identities.
The connecting maps restrict to
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a cubical subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

Proof.

If
g
is a block metric that decomposes outside of
M\times\rho I^{n}
, then so do all of its faces
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\partial^{\omega}_{j}g=\left({\delta^{\rho\omega}_{j}}{\delta^{\rho\varepsilon}_{i}}\right)^{\ast}g=\left({\delta^{\rho\omega}_{i}}{\delta^{\rho\varepsilon}_{j-1}}\right)^{\ast}g=\partial^{\omega}_{j-1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g

for all
i<j
.
The identity

\displaystyle{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{j}=\mathrm{d}x_{j}\circ D{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}x_{j-1},&\text{if }i<j\\
0,&\text{if }i=j,\\
\mathrm{d}x_{j},&\text{if }i>j\end{cases}

implies

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\sigma_{j}g

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\left(p_{j}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}\right)=\lim_{R\to\infty}{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}p_{j}^{\ast}g+{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lim_{R\to\infty}p_{j-1}^{\ast}{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{j-1}^{2},&\text{if }i<j,\\
g,&\text{if }i=j,\\
\lim_{R\to\infty}p_{j}{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i-1}}}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sigma_{j-1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g,&\text{if }i<j,\\
g,&\text{if }i=j,\\
\sigma_{j}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-1}g,&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

Lastly, the equation

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{j}=\mathrm{d}x_{j}\circ p_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}x_{j+1},&\text{if }i\leq j,\\
\mathrm{d}x_{j},&\text{if }i>j,\end{cases}

gives, for
i\leq j
, the equation

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}g

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{\ast}(p_{j}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{\ast}p_{j}^{\ast}g+p_{i}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=p_{j+1}^{\ast}(p_{i}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x_{j+1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j+1}\sigma_{i}g,

so the first claim is proven.

The connecting maps send positive scalar curvature metrics to positive scalar curvature metrics because scalar curvature is additive under taking products, see Lemma
C.1.1
below.
In more detail,

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\sigma_{i}g)

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(p_{i}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2})=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)>0,

and, since
g|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}}
is the product metric of
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g
and the euclidean metric, we have

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g)

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(p_{i}^{\ast}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2})|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}}=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}}>0,

which implies the second claim.
∎

We would like to know whether
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
are Kan sets.
For
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
even more is true.

Proposition 3.1.8
.

The cubical set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
is combinatorially contractible.

The proposition follows immediately from the following two elementary lemmas.

Lemma 3.1.9
.

Let
U,V
be an open cover of a not necessarily compact manifold
N
.
Assume there is a submanifold
H
such that
U\cap V
is diffeomorphic to
H\times(-1,1)
.
If
g_{U}
and
g_{V}
are Riemannian metrics on
U
and
V
, respectively, then there is a Riemannian metric
g
on
N
such that

g|_{U\setminus\overline{V}}=g_{U}|_{U\setminus\overline{V}}\quad\text{ and }\quad g|_{V\setminus\overline{U}}=g_{V}|_{V\setminus\overline{U}}.

Proof.

Pick a smooth function
\chi\colon(-1,1)\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically zero near
-1
and identically
1
near
1
.
Let
\phi\colon U\cap V\rightarrow H\times(-1,1)
be a diffeomorphism.
Define on
U\cap V
the Riemannian metric

g|_{U\cap V}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left(\chi\circ\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}\circ\phi\right)\cdot g_{U}+\left(1-\chi\circ\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}\circ\phi\right)\cdot g_{V}

It can be extended via
g_{U}
and
g_{V}
to the rest of
M
and the result
g
is the desired metric.
∎

Lemma 3.1.10
.

For
(j,\omega)
and
(k,\eta)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, pick two block metrics
g_{(j,\omega)}
and
g_{(k,\eta)}
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that decompose outside of
M\times\rho I^{n}
.
Assume that
j\leq k
and that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}g_{(j,\omega)}
.
Then, the metric
\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}
and
\sigma_{k}g_{(k,\eta)}
agree on the set
\{\omega x_{j}>\rho,\eta x_{k}>\rho\}
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\{\omega x_{j}>\rho,\eta x_{k}>\rho\}
to
U
.
If
j=k
there is nothing to prove for either
U
is empty or
(j,\omega)=(k,\eta)
.
For
j<k
, the calculation

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{U}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}\partial^{\eta}_{k}\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{U}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k-1}\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{U}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\sigma_{k-1}\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{(k,\eta)}|_{U}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\partial^{\omega}_{j}\sigma_{k}g_{(k,\eta)}|_{U}=\sigma_{k}g_{(k,\eta)}|_{U}

shows the claim.
Here, we used the block form of
g_{(j,\omega)}
in the first line, cubical identities in the second line, and the assumed compatibility in the third line.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1.8
.

Let
\partial\Box[n]_{\bullet}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
be given by the family of block metrics

\{g_{(j,\omega)}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n-1}}(M)\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}g_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k\},

where
(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
Since we are given only finitely many block metrics, we can pick a sufficient large
\rho>0
such that all
g_{(j,\omega)}
decompose outside of
M\times\rho I^{n-1}
.
The compatibility assumption yields that the metrics
\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{\{\omega x_{j}>\rho\}}
agree on the intersection of their domain by Lemma
3.1.10
.
Thus, these metrics form a Riemannian metric
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\rho I^{n}
.
Pick some Riemannian metric
g_{0}
on
M\times(\rho+2)I^{n}
and apply Lemma
3.1.9
.
The result
g_{fill}
is obviously a block metric because it agrees with
g
outside a compact subset and therefore satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{fill}=g_{(j,\omega)}
.
Thus, it is a desired filler for the initially given cubical sphere, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
is combinatorially contractible.
∎

To prove that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan set is more complicated.
In the next section, we will do some preparations and finish off the proof in Section
3.3
.

We close this section by introducing the comparison map between
\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

Definition 3.1.11
.

For each smooth block map
g\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(M)
, we define
\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)
as the adjoint metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
More precisely, we set

\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)_{(m,x)}=g(x)_{m}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}

and call it the
suspension
of
g
.

Lemma 3.1.12
.

The suspension of every
g\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(M)
is a block metric and all suspension maps assemble to a cubical map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

Proof.

Since
g
is a block map, there is a
\rho>0
such
g|_{\{\pm x_{i}>\rho\}}
does not depend on
x_{i}
anymore.
Thus, on
\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
, the suspension
\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)
is the product of
{\delta^{\rho\varepsilon}_{i}}\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)
and
\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}
.
Lemma
3.1.4
.
implies that
\mathrm{susp}_{n}g
is a block metric.

The second statement follows from the calculations

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)_{(m,x)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\left(g(\text{--})_{(\text{--})}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}\right)_{(m,x)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}g({\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}(x))_{m}+{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g)_{m}(x)+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mathrm{d}x_{n-1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{susp}_{n-1}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g)_{(m,x)}

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)_{(m,x)}

\displaystyle=p_{i}^{\ast}\left(g(\text{--})_{(\text{--})}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}\right)_{(m,x)}+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=g(p_{i}(x))_{m}+p_{i}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\dots+p_{i}^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{n}^{2}+\mathrm{d}x_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{susp}_{n+1}(\sigma_{i}g)_{(m,x)}.

∎

Unfortunately, it is not true that the suspension restricts to a cubical map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
If the parameter of the block map
g
’runs too fast’, the scalar curvature of
\mathrm{susp}_{n}(g)
might be non-positive at some points.
This can be already seen in the following example: The euclidean metric on
2D^{n}\setminus\overset{\circ}{D^{n}}
expressed in polar coordinates is the suspension of the curve
[1,2]\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(S^{n-1})
that is given by
r\mapsto r^{2}\cdot g_{S^{n-1}}
,
where
g_{S^{n-1}}
is the standard round metric.

Later we will construct a weakly equivalent Kan subset
A_{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
so that
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}|_{A_{\bullet}}
takes values in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
But we would like this cubical subset
{A_{\bullet}}
to be adapted to the index theory we will develop Chapter
4
.
Therefore, we postpone the construction of
{A_{\bullet}}
to Chapter
5
.

3.2
Construction of Special Submanifolds

The main result of this section, Theorem
3.2.2
, is an embedding result.
It allows us to find, for each given block metric, a family of neatly embedded hyperplanes in the sense that the planes bend only in those direction in which the block metric is euclidean.
More precisely, the unit normal vector field of these hyperplanes always lies in a subspace on which the given block metric restricts to the euclidean metric.

The application of this theorem in the proof of the Kan property are two-fold.
The existence of these hyperplanes provide an open, cylindrical neighbourhood in which we can deform the block metric in a way such that the result has product structure on one end of the cylinder.
Secondly, the theorem provides a diffeomorphism that we will use to “push” the modified metric on (an open neighbourhood) the given initial
n
-horn onto the entire cube.

We start with definitions of certain subsets of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that we will need in the proof of the embedding theorem and throughout the thesis overall.

Definition 3.2.1
.

We define, for each
{\bm{\alpha}}\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}=\{0,\pm 1\}

and each
\rho>0
, the open subsets

V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,{\bm{\alpha}}(i)x_{i}>\rho-1\text{ if }i\in\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}},x_{i}\in(-\rho,\rho)\text{ if }{\bm{\alpha}}(i)=0\}.

For
\rho>0
and
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, define the
infinite half cuboid open towards

(i,\varepsilon)
as

{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho,\ x_{j}\in[-\rho,\rho]\text{ if }j\neq i\}.

The open subsets
V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})
should be thought of as an open partition of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with only one relative compact set
V_{\rho}(\mathbf{0})
, see figure
3.2
for a visualisation in which we chose
\rho
so large that the difference between
\rho
and
\rho-1
is not visible.

For each
{\bm{\alpha}}\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
, let
\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}={\bm{\alpha}}|_{\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}}}
.
The tupel
\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}
parameterises the coordinates in those directions
V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})
is unbounded and, more importantly for our purpose, in which coordinate-direction a block metric decomposes.
Note also that
U_{\rho-1}({\bm{\alpha}})
is the union of all
V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime})
with
\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}}
.

Theorem 3.2.2

(Embedding Theorem)
.

For all
\rho>5
, there is a smooth hypersurface
H\subset\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
and an embedding
\Phi\colon H\times[1,2]\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
with the following properties for all
{\bm{\alpha}}
:

1.

(i)

The permutation that identifies
V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
with
(-\rho+4,\rho-4)^{\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{\rho-5}
identifies
H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
with
(-\rho+4,\rho-4)^{|\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})|}\times H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}
for some hypersurface
H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{\rho-5}
.

(ii)

Under this identification, the embedding
\Phi
corresponds to
\mathrm{id}\times\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}
.

(iii)

Outside a compact set,
\Phi_{t}(x)=x-(t-1.9)e_{1}
.

2.

The hypersurface
H
separates
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
into two unbounded open subsets.
If
U_{left}
denotes the component that contains
\Phi(H\times\{2\})
, then there is a diffeomorphism

\Psi_{n}\colon U_{left}\cup\Phi(H\times[1,2])\rightarrow\{x_{1}\leq\rho+1\}

that is the identity on
\Phi(H\times[1.8,2])
and outside a compact subset of
\Phi(H\times[1,2])
.

3.

There are smooth maps
h\colon[1,2]\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(H)
and
f\colon[1,2]\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
that are constant outside a compact set
K
with values
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}
and
1
, respectively, such that

\left(\Phi^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\right)_{(p,t)}=h(t)_{p}+f^{2}(p,t)\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

In fact, on
\Phi^{-1}(H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}}))
, the curve of metrics
h
decomposes into

t\mapsto\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})}\oplus h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(t)
and the map
f
factors through
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{\rho-5}
.

V_{\rho-4}(0,1)
V_{\rho-4}(0,1)
V_{\rho-4}(1,1)
V_{\rho-4}(1,-1)
V_{\rho-4}(-1,0)
V_{\rho-4}(-1,1)
V_{\rho-4}(-1,-1)
V_{\rho}(0,0)
V_{\rho}(1,0)
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,(1,1)}^{\rho-4}}
\Phi(H\times[1,2])
H=H\times\{1.9\}
x_{2}=\rho-4
x_{2}=-(\rho-4)
x_{1}=\rho-4
x_{1}=-(\rho-4)
U_{left}

Figure 3.2:
The shape of
\Phi(H\times[1,2])
in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, here for
n=2
. The set
H
is canonically identified with
\Phi(H\times\{1.9\})
.

Unfortunately, the proof of this theorem is quite technical and the entire section is devoted to it.
The theorem is therefore formulated in a manner so that the reader can safely take it for granted during his or her first reading.
We will not need the ingredients of its proof later, with the following definition as the only exception.
However, it might be helpful to have a look at the pictures presented here.

Definition 3.2.3
.

For
\rho>2
, let
\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
be a smooth, convex function that satisfies

\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{0\})=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho-2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}|_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho}}=\mathrm{id}-(\rho-1).

Define the
dice function
\mathrm{dice}_{n,\rho}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
via

\mathrm{dice}_{n,\rho}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|x_{j}|).

The level set of
\mathrm{dice}_{n,\rho}
of values around
1
look like a cube with smoothed corners, see Figure
3.3
, hence the name.

\mathrm{dice}^{-1}_{\rho,2}(\{0\})
x_{2}=\rho-2
x_{2}=\rho-1
x_{2}=\rho
x_{1}=\rho
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho,2}^{-1}(\{\mathrm{dice}_{\rho,2}(0,\rho-1)\})
\mathrm{dice}^{-1}_{\rho,2}(\{1\})

Figure 3.3:
The level sets of a dice function. Recall that
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho,2}(0,\rho)=1
by construction.

In the following, we will abbreviate
\mathrm{dice}_{2,\rho}
to
\mathrm{dice}
.
Fix
0<\varepsilon<(\rho-2)/2
and set

\mathrm{dice}^{l}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{dice}|_{\{x_{1}<\varepsilon\}}.

Furthermore, we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=-\mathrm{dice}(x_{1},x_{2}-(2\rho+1))+3\quad\text{ on }(-\varepsilon,\rho+1]\times[\rho,\rho+4],

\displaystyle\mathrm{dice}^{u}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=-\mathrm{dice}(x_{1},x_{2}+(2\rho+1))+3\quad\text{ on }(-\varepsilon,\rho+1]\times[-\rho,-(\rho+4)]

\displaystyle\,=\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},-x_{2}).

Lemma 3.2.4
.

The functions
\mathrm{dice}^{l}
and
\mathrm{dice}^{o}
agree on
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times[\rho,\rho+1]
, while
\mathrm{dice}^{l}
and
\mathrm{dice}^{u}
agree on
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times[-(\rho+1),-\rho]
.

Proof.

For
(x_{1},x_{2})\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times[\rho,\rho+1]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{dice}(x_{1},x_{2}-(2\rho+1))+3=-\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|x_{2}-(2\rho+1)|)+3

\displaystyle=-\left(|x_{2}-(2\rho+1)|-(\rho-1)\right)+3

\displaystyle=-\left(2\rho+1-x_{2}-(\rho-1)\right)+3

\displaystyle=x_{2}-(\rho-1)=\mathrm{dice}^{l}(x_{1},x_{2}),

where we used
|x_{2}-(2\rho+1)|\in[\rho,\rho+1]
to go from the first to the second line.
The second statement follows from the first because
\mathrm{dice}^{l}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathrm{dice}^{l}(x_{1},-x_{2})
and
\mathrm{dice}^{u}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},-x_{2})
.
∎

On
[\rho,\rho+1]\times[\rho+3,\rho+4]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=-\left(\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|x_{1}|)+\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|x_{2}-(2\rho+1)|)\right)+3

\displaystyle=-\left(x_{1}-(\rho-1)\right)+0+3

\displaystyle=-(x_{1}-\rho)+2

and, similarly, on
[\rho,\rho+1]\times[-(\rho+4),-(\rho+3)]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dice}^{u}(x_{1},x_{2})=-(x_{1}-\rho)+2.

This allows us to merge these functions.
For

\displaystyle Q_{1}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigl([-\varepsilon,\rho]\times[\rho,\rho+1]\bigr)\cup\bigl([\rho-2,\rho+1]\times[\rho,\rho+4]\bigr),

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigl([-\varepsilon,\rho]\times[-(\rho+1),-\rho]\bigr)\cup\bigl([\rho-2,\rho+1]\times[-(\rho+4),\rho]\bigr),

we define

\displaystyle\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\begin{cases}\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x),&\text{if }x\in Q_{1},\\
\mathrm{dice}^{u}(x),&\text{if }x\in Q_{2},\\
\mathrm{dice}^{l},&\text{if }x\in\{x_{1}<\varepsilon\},\\
-(\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}-\rho)+2,&\text{if }x\in[\rho,\rho+1]\times[-(\rho+3),\rho+3]^{c}.\end{cases}

Lemma 3.2.5
.

The set of regular values for
\mathrm{dice}_{n,\rho}
is
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
The interval
[1,2]
is a set of regular values for
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}
is a smooth and convex function, its derivative is monotonically increasing.
As its null-set is
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\rho-2}
, the derivative
\mathrm{aux}^{\prime}_{\rho}
must be positive on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>\rho-2}
.
The first claim now follows from

\mathrm{d}\,\mathrm{dice}_{n,\rho}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathrm{sgn}(x_{j})\mathrm{aux}^{\prime}_{\rho}(|x_{j}|)\mathrm{d}x_{j}.

It suffices to prove the second claim for each summand.
For

-(\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}-\rho)+2\colon[\rho,\rho+1]\times[-(\rho+3),\rho+3]^{c}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}},

there is nothing to prove.
The first claim implies that
\mathrm{dice}^{o}
and
\mathrm{dice}^{u}
have only one singular value, namely
3
.
Since
\mathrm{dice}^{l}
is the restriction of
\mathrm{dice}
to
\{x_{1}<\varepsilon\}
, it only has one singular value, namely
0
.
Thus,
[1,2]
is indeed a set of regular values for
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}
.
∎

Similar arguments also yield the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2.6
.

If
\partial_{j}\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x)\neq 0
, then
|x_{j}|>\rho-2
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the statement for each summand.
For
-(\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}-\rho)+2
there is nothing to prove as all points in its domain satisfy the claimed inequalities.

All
x\in\mathrm{dom}(\mathrm{dice}^{o})
satisfy
|x_{2}|>\rho-2
, so we have to check the statement only for
\partial_{1}\mathrm{dice}^{o}
.
Since
\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}^{\prime}
is monotonically increasing and
\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{0\})=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\rho-2}
the condition

(\partial_{1}\mathrm{dice}^{o})(x)=\partial_{1}\mathrm{aux}(|x_{1}|)=\mathrm{sgn}(x_{1})\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}^{\prime}(|x_{1}|)\neq 0

is equivalent to
|x_{1}|>\rho-2
.

Since
\mathrm{dice}^{u}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathrm{dice}^{o}(x_{1},-x_{2})
the statement holds true also for
\mathrm{dice}^{u}
.

For
\mathrm{dice}^{l}
, we deduce from

\partial_{j}\mathrm{dice}^{l}(x)=\mathrm{sgn}(x_{j})\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}^{\prime}(|x_{j}|)\neq 0,

that
|x_{j}|>\rho-2
.
∎

We would like to generalise this construction to arbitrary dimension in a ’dice-rotational’ manner.
To this end, we need an abstract extension result.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

For all
\rho>5
, there is a function
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
that satisfies:

(i)

The preimages of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2}
under
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}
and
\mathrm{dice}_{n-1,\rho-2}+\rho-3
agree.
Furthermore, the two functions agree on
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2})
.

(ii)

\mathrm{d}\,\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}\neq 0
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
.

(iii)

The origin is the unique local minimum of
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}
with value
0
.

Proof.

Pick a smooth function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
that is monotonically increasing, satisfies
\chi^{\prime}\leq 1.2
, and that has the following preimages:
\chi^{-1}(\{0\})=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho-3}
and
\chi^{-1}(\{1\})=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2}
.
Pick further
\mathtt{C}^{-1}>(n-1)\cdot(\rho-2)^{2}/(\rho-3)
and abbreviate
\mathrm{dice}_{n-1,\rho-2}+(\rho-3)
to
\varphi
.
Define

\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\varphi(x)\cdot\chi(\varphi(x))+\mathtt{C}(1-\chi(\varphi(x)))\cdot||x||^{2}.

The two functions
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}
and
\varphi
agree on
\varphi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2})
.
To prove
(i)
it is enough to show that
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}<\rho-2
on the complement
\varphi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{<\rho-2})
so that the preimage of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2}
under the two functions agree.
By the choice of
\mathtt{C}
, we have
\mathtt{C}||x||^{2}<\rho-3
on
\varphi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho-2})
so that the second summand satisfies

\mathtt{C}(1-\chi(\varphi(x)))\cdot||x||^{2}<\rho-3.

Since
\varphi\geq\rho-3
, we conclude
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}<\varphi
, whenever
\chi\circ\varphi\neq 0
and
(i)
follows.

Clearly,
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}\geq 0
and the origin gets mapped to zero. Thus,
(iii)
follows from
(ii)
.

To prove
(ii)
, we calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\qquad\mathrm{d}_{x}\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}&=\bigl(\chi(\varphi(x))+(\varphi(x)-\mathtt{C}||x||^{2})\chi^{\prime}(\varphi(x))\bigr)\mathrm{d}_{x}\varphi\\
&\qquad+2\mathtt{C}(1-\chi(\varphi(x)))\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}x_{j}\cdot\mathrm{d}x_{j}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\Bigl(\bigl(\chi(\varphi(x))+(\varphi(x)-\mathtt{C}||x||^{2})\chi^{\prime}(\varphi(x))\bigr)\mathrm{aux}_{\rho-2}^{\prime}(|x_{j}|)\mathrm{sgn}(x_{j})\\
&\qquad\qquad+2\mathtt{C}x_{j}(1-\chi(\varphi(x)))\Bigr)\mathrm{d}x_{j}.\end{split}

Since
(\varphi-\mathtt{C}||x||^{2})\geq 0
on
\varphi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho-2})
, in particular on
\mathrm{supp}\,(\chi^{\prime}\circ\varphi)
, the two summands have the same parity.
The second summand
\mathtt{C}x_{j}(1-\chi(\varphi(x))
vanishes only if
x_{j}=0
or
\varphi(x)\geq\rho-2
.
In the latter case, the first summand does not vanish.
Thus,
\mathrm{d}_{x}\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}
only vanishes at the origin.
∎

We use this function to define

\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))

as real valued map with domain

\mathrm{dom}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})=\bigl\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))\in\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2])\bigr\}.

Observe that
\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}=\mathtt{d}_{\rho}
because
\mathrm{dice}_{1,\rho-2}(x_{2})+\rho-3=|x_{2}|
on
\mathrm{dice}_{1,\rho-2}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho-2})
because
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x_{1},x_{2})=\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x_{1},|x_{2}|)
and
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}
is independent of
x_{2}
on
\mathrm{dom}\,\mathtt{d}_{\rho}\cap\{|x_{2}|<\rho-2\}
.

Lemma 3.2.8
.

Restricted to
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
, the map
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
satisfies:

(i)

If
\partial_{j}\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)\neq 0
, then
|x_{j}|>\rho-4
.

(ii)

Every point in
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
is a regular point.

Proof.

We start to prove the first statement.
For
j=1
, the assumption

(\partial_{1}\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})(x)=(\partial_{1}\mathtt{d}_{\rho})(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))\neq 0,

implies
|x_{1}|>\rho-2
by Lemma
3.2.6
.

For
j\geq 2
, the assumption

\partial_{j}\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)=(\partial_{2}\mathtt{d}_{\rho})(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(\dots))\cdot\partial_{j}\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\neq 0

implies that the first factors do not vanish, which concludes
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})>\rho-2
.
Since
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}=\mathrm{dice}_{n-1,\rho-2}+(\rho-3)
on this domain, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}\mathrm{dice}_{n-1,\rho-2}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(x_{j})\mathrm{aux}_{\rho-2}^{\prime}(|x_{j}|)\neq 0,

which implies
|x_{j}|>\rho-4
.

Assume that
x\in\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
were a singular point.
We know from Lemma
3.2.5
that
(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))
is a regular point of
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}
.
If
\partial_{1}\mathtt{d}
does not vanish at this point, then
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)\neq 0
and
x
would be a regular point for
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
.
Thus,
\partial_{j}\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x)=0
for all
2\leq j\leq n
, which implies that
(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})
is the origin of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
From the equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial_{1}\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)=(\partial_{1}\mathtt{d}_{\rho})(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))=(\partial_{1}\mathtt{d}_{\rho})(x_{1},0)

follows
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)=\mathtt{d}_{\rho}(x_{1},0)\notin[1,2]
, which contradicts our domain assumption.
∎

Lemma 3.2.9
.

The set
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
does not intersect the closure of the cuboid

{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,x_{1}\geq-(\rho-4),\,x_{j}\in[-(\rho-4),\rho-4]\}
.

Proof.

Each point
x\in{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
satisfies
\mathrm{dice}_{n-1,\rho-2}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=0
.
Condition
(i)
of
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}
yields
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x)<\rho-2
.
Thus,
x
is either not in the domain of
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
or

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{dice}^{l}(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|x_{1}|)+\mathrm{aux}_{\rho}(|\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})|)

\displaystyle=0.

∎

Definition 3.2.10
.

Let
H\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}(\{1.9\})
and let
\Phi\colon H\times[1,2]\rightarrow\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
be the restriction of the (shifted) gradient flow of
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
so that
\Phi_{1.9}=\mathrm{id}_{H}
.

Recall that the (shifted) gradient flow is the unique smooth map

\Phi\colon\mathrm{dom}\,\Phi\subset\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

that solves the initial value problem

(\partial_{t}\Phi)_{t_{0}}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})}{||\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}||^{2}}(\Phi_{t_{0}}(x))\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Phi_{1.9}(x)=x.

Proof of the Embedding Theorem.

Since
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
is non degenerate on
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
there is a diffeomorphism

\textstyle{\Phi\colon H\times[1,2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}}
\textstyle{[1,2],}

see for example
[
hirsch1997differential
]
*p.153ff for a proof.
By Lemma
3.2.9
, the image of
\Phi
lies in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
.
In particular,
H
is a hypersurface of this open manifold.

We can describe
\Phi
even in more details:
Recall from Definition
3.1.2
that
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}({\bm{\varepsilon}})=\mathrm{Abb}(\mathbf{n}\setminus\mathrm{dom}\,{\bm{\varepsilon}},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
, and that

\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\rho-5}=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{|\mathrm{dom}{\bm{\varepsilon}}|}\,:\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}(i_{j})x_{j}>\rho-5\}
.
By permuting coordinates, we identify
V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
with
(-(\rho-4),\rho-4)^{|\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})|}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{\rho-5}
.
Lemma
3.2.8
implies that
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}
does not depend on the points parametrised by
\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})
, so under this identification,
H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
corresponds to
(-\rho+4,\rho-4)^{|\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})|}\times H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}
, where
H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}=\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{\rho-5}}(\{1.9\})
.
Furthermore, under this identification, the gradient flow
\Phi
corresponds to
\mathrm{id}\times\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}
.
Note also that
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}(x)=2-(x_{1}-\rho)
if
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})>\rho+3
, so
\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})=-e_{1}
outside the compact subset
K\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho+3})\cap\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
.
Thus,
\Phi_{t}(x)=x+(t-1.9)e_{1}
outside this compact subset.
Statement 1. is therefore proven.

Next we prove statement 3.
The gradient of a function is always perpendicular to its level sets, so

\Phi^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{(x,t)}=h(t)_{x}+f^{2}(x,t)\mathrm{d}t^{2},

where
h(t)
is the induced metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\Phi_{t}(H)}
and
f^{2}=||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})||^{-2}
.
On
H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
, we can use the decomposition of the gradient flow to get the finer decomposition

\Phi^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})}+h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}+(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2},

where
h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(t)
is the induced metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}_{t}(H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})}
and
(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}=||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})||^{-2}
, which depends only on those coordinates that are parametrised by
\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}}
.
Since
\Phi
is a translation outside of
K
, the maps
h
and
f
are constant with values
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}
and
1
, respectively.
Thus, statement 3. is proven.

To prove statement 2., we will study the shape of
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}([1,2])
and how it lies in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
We will consider the case
n=2
first.

Since
\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])=\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
it is easy to see that this set separates
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
into two unbounded regions
\hat{U}_{left}
and
\hat{U}_{right}
, see Figure
3.4
.
We set

U_{left}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\hat{U}_{left}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>1.9})\qquad\text{and}\qquad U_{right}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\hat{U}_{right}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{<1.9})

so that
H=\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}^{-1}(\{1.9\})
.

x_{1}=\rho-1
x_{1}=\rho
x_{1}=\rho+1
x_{2}=\rho+1
x_{2}=\rho+3
x_{2}=-(\rho+1)
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho,2}^{-1}([1,2])
U_{left}
U_{right}

Figure 3.4:
The separation of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by
\mathfrak{d}_{2,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
.

Visual reasons imply the existence of a diffeomorphism
\Psi\colon\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2])\rightarrow\Psi(\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2]))
that is the identity near the
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1.8,2])
and outside a compact set, that is of the form
\Psi(x_{1},x_{2})=(\Psi^{(1)}(x_{1}),x_{2})
near the line
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}e_{1}
, and those extension to
U_{left}
via the identity maps
U_{left}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
to
\{x_{1}\leq\rho+1\}
, see Figure
3.5
.

{x_{1}=\rho+1}
{x_{1}=\rho+1}
x_{1}=\rho
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
U_{left}
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{1\})
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{2\})
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{2\})
\Psi
pull it straight!

Figure 3.5:
The diffeomorphism
\Psi
“stretches”
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1,2])
such that
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{1\})
becomes a straight line and
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}(\{2\})
remains unchanged.

For higher dimensions, we extend the diffeomorphism in a ’dice-symmetrical’ manner.
Let
\Xi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}

be the map that sends
(x,t)
to
\Xi_{t}(x)
, the image of
x
under the (shifted) gradient flow that lies in the levelset
\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}^{-1}(\{t\})
.
Define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{n}(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Bigl(&\Psi^{(1)}(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})),\\
&\ \Xi_{\Psi^{(2)}(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\Bigr),\end{split}

where
\Psi^{(j)}
denotes
\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{j}\circ\Psi
.
Its inverse is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{n}^{-1}(y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Bigl(&(\Psi^{-1})^{(1)}(y_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(y_{2},\dots,y_{n}),\\
&\ \Xi_{(\Psi^{-1})^{(2)}(y_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(y_{2},\dots,y_{n}))}(y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\Bigr).\end{split}

The two maps are smooth and inverse to each other.
As

\Xi_{\Psi^{(2)}(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=(x_{2},\dots,x_{n})

in a neighbourhood of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}e_{1}
,
this assignment extends to a smooth diffeomorphism

\Psi_{n}\colon\mathrm{dom}(\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho})\rightarrow\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))\in\mathrm{im}\Psi(\mathrm{dom}(\mathtt{d}_{\rho}))\}.

Since
\Psi
is the identity on
\mathtt{d}_{\rho}^{-1}([1.8,2])
and outside a compact set, the diffeomorphism
\Psi_{n}
is the identity on
\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1.8,2])
and outside a compact set.
We extend it via the identity ’to the left’ to
U_{left}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,(x_{1},\widetilde{\mathrm{dice}}_{n-1,\rho}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n}))\in U_{left}\}
.
This gives a diffeomorphism

\Psi_{n}\colon U_{left}\cup\mathfrak{d}_{n,\rho}^{-1}([1,2])\rightarrow\{x_{1}\leq\rho+1\}

with the same properties as
\Psi=\Psi_{2}
.
Note that this particularly implies that
H
is diffeomorphic to
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
.
∎

3.3
Kan Property of the Concordance Set

The aim of this section is to prove Theorem
A
, which can be thought of as a can opener to the combinatorial world.
The precise formulation is the following one:

Theorem 3.3.1
.

The concordance set
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan set for every closed psc manifold
M
.

The proof requires the following technical ingredient.

Lemma 3.3.2

(Metric Modification)
.

Let
g
be a block metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that decomposes outside of
M\times\rho/2I^{n}
for
\rho>20
. Assume further that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g
is a psc metric for all
(j,\omega)\neq(1,1)
.
Let
H
and
\Phi
be as in the Embedding Theorem
3.2.2
.
and let
{}_{R}\Phi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=R\cdot\Phi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times R^{-1})\colon H\times[R,2R]\rightarrow M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Then, for all
R\geq 1
, there is a metric on
G_{R}
on
M\times R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
that satisfies:

(o)

There is an
R_{0}>0
such that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(G_{R})>0
if
R>R_{0}
.

(i)

The pull back
({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}G_{R}
and
({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}g
agree on
M\times H\times[1.8R,2R]
and on the complement of a compact subset of
M\times H\times[R,2R]
.

(ii)

The pull back
({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}G_{R}
is a product metric on
M\times H\times[R,1.2R]
. More precisely,

({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}G_{R}=({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}G_{R}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times H\times\{R\}}\oplus\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

Proof.

Recall the open subsets

V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,{\bm{\alpha}}(i)x_{i}>\rho-1\text{ if }i\in\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}};x_{i}\in(-\rho,\rho)\text{ otherwise}\},

for all
{\bm{\alpha}}\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{3}
.
Also recall the notation
{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}={\bm{\alpha}}|_{\mathrm{supp}\,{\bm{\alpha}}}
.
We have the following finite decomposition

\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho}}=\bigcup\{V_{\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\,:\,{\bm{\alpha}}\neq\mathbf{0},(1,0,\dots,0)\}.

Let
H
and
\Phi
be as in the Embedding Theorem.
Then the subset
R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
is still in the complement of
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho}}
for all
R\geq 1
.
The block metric
g
decomposes on
M\times V_{R\rho}({{\bm{\alpha}}})
into
\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Together with Embedding Theorem
3.2.2

this implies

\displaystyle{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}(g|_{V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}}))

\displaystyle=((\mathrm{id}\times(\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times R^{-1}))))^{\ast}R^{\ast}(\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus R^{2}(\mathrm{id}\times R^{-1})^{\ast}{\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus R^{2}h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(\text{--},R^{-1}\cdot)+(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}(\text{--},R^{-1}\cdot)\mathrm{d}t^{2}

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus R^{2}{}_{R}h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}+{}_{R}(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}

\displaystyle=:h_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}+f_{R}^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}

These decompositions assemble to the (global) decomposition

({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}g=h_{R}+f^{2}_{R}\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

Define the auxiliary metric
k_{\mathrm{aux},R}\in\mathrm{Riem}(M\times H\times[R,2R])
via

k_{\mathrm{aux},R}(m,x,t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=h_{R}(m,x,1.1\cdot R)+\mathrm{d}t^{2}

and pick a smooth function
\chi\colon[1,2]\rightarrow[0,1]
that vanishes identically on
[1,1.2]
and that is identically
1
on
[1.8,2]
.
Set further
{}_{R}\chi(t)=\chi(R^{-1}t)
.

We define
k_{R}\in\mathrm{Riem}(M\times H\times[R,2R])
via

k_{R}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1-{}_{R}\chi)\left(k_{\mathrm{aux},R}+\mathrm{d}t^{2}\right)+{}_{R}\chi\left(h_{R}+f^{2}_{R}\mathrm{d}t^{2}\right)

and define

G_{R}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=({}_{R}\Phi)_{\ast}(k_{R}).

In the following, we drop the lower
R
if
R=1
.
To prove statement
(i)
, we claim that metric agrees with
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}(g)
on
M\times H\times[1.8R,2R]
and on the complement of some compact set so that
G_{R}
agrees with
g
on
M\times{}_{R}\Phi(H\times[R,1.2\cdot 2])
and away from a compact subset.
The first claim follows immediately from
\chi\equiv 1
on
[1.8,2]
.
The second claim follows from the existence of a compact set
C
such that
H\setminus C
is an open submanifold of
\{x_{1}=\rho+1\}
and such that
\Phi
restricts to
(x,t)\mapsto x-(t-1.9)e_{1}
on
H\setminus C\times[1,2]
, so that, on
H\setminus C\times[R,2R]
, the pull back
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}g
decomposes into

({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}g=(R\Phi\circ\mathrm{id}\times R^{-1})^{\ast}(\partial^{1}_{1}g\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2})=R^{\ast}(\partial^{1}_{1}g)+\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

Thus, on
H\setminus C\times[R,2R]
, the curve
h_{R}\equiv R^{\ast}(\partial^{1}_{1}g)
and the map
f_{R}\equiv 1
are constant so that
k_{R}=({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}g
there.

To prove
(o)
, we claim that
k_{R}
(and hence
G_{R}
) is a psc metric, provided that
R
is sufficiently large.
Indeed, on
V_{R\rho}({{\bm{\alpha}}})
with
{\bm{\alpha}}\notin\{\mathbf{0},(1,0,\dots,0)\}
the metric
k_{R}
decomposes into

\displaystyle k_{R}

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus\left((1-{}_{R}\chi)(h_{R}(\cdot,1.1R)+\mathrm{d}t^{2})+{}_{R}\chi(h_{R}+f_{R}^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})\right)|_{V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\times[R,2R]}

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus\left((1-{}_{R}\chi)(R^{2}{}_{R}h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(\cdot,1.1R)+\mathrm{d}t^{2})+{}_{R}\chi(R^{2}{}_{R}h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}+({}_{R}f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus R^{2}{}_{R}\left((1-\chi)h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(\cdot,1.1R)+\chi h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\right)+{}_{R}\left((1-\chi)+\chi(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t^{2}

Since
\Phi_{t}(x)=x-(t-1.9)e_{1}
outside a compact set
C
, the metrics

k_{1}
and
\Phi^{\ast}g
agree outside of
C
and
h_{R}
is a psc metric there.
Thus, the metrics
k_{\mathrm{aux},R}
and
h_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}
agree outside
C
for all
R\geq 1
and have positive scalar curvature there.
Furthermore, for
R=1
, we find positive constants
\mathrm{Const}({{\bm{\alpha}}})
such that

\left|\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}\left((1-\chi)(h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(\cdot,1.1)+\mathrm{d}t^{2})+\chi(h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}+(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})\right)\right|\leq\mathrm{Const}({\bm{\alpha}})

because the domain where the two metrics disagree is compact.

Corollary
C.1.3
implies that

\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}\left(R^{2}{}_{R}\left((1-\chi)h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(\cdot,1.1)+\chi h^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\right)+{}_{R}\left((1-\chi)+(f^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}t^{2}\right)\xrightarrow{R\to\infty}0.

Furthermore,
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g)=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g)\circ R
is bounded from below by a positive constant that is independent of
R
, for
\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g
