is a block metric with positive scalar curvature.
Putting these facts together, we can choose
R
sufficiently large such that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(k_{R})
is positive on
(H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}}))\times[R,2R]
.
Since all
H\cap V_{\rho-4}({\bm{\alpha}})
form a finite cover of
H
, we find an
R_{0}>0
such such that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(k_{R})>0
for all
R>R_{0}
.

A choice for the desired metric is given by the unique metric
G_{R}
on
M\times{}_{R}\Phi(H\times[R,2R])
that satisfies
({}_{R}\Phi)^{\ast}G_{R}=k_{R}
.
∎

We can now prove the Kan property.

Proof of Theorem
3.3.1
.

Assume we are given a cubical
n
-horn
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
that is represented by the following set of block metrics

\{g_{(j,\omega)}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n-1}}(M)\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}g_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }\,j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\}.

The problem is symmetric in
(i,\varepsilon)
so we may assume that
(i,\varepsilon)=(1,1)
.
We pick a sufficient large
\rho>20
such that all given block metrics
g_{(j,\omega)}
decompose outside of
M\times\rho/2I^{n-1}
.
By assumption, the block metrics
g_{(j,\omega)}
have matching faces, so the Riemannian metrics
\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{\{\omega x_{j}>\rho-4\}}
agree on the intersection on their domains, see Lemma
3.1.9
.
Thus, the union of all
\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}|_{\{\omega x_{j}>\rho-4\}}
with
(j,\omega)\neq(1,1)
forms a smooth psc metric
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
.

By the proof of Proposition
3.1.8
, we can find a block metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that agrees with
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-2}}
as follows:
First, the proof shows that we can extend
g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=c\}}
to a block metric on
\{x_{1}=c\}
that agrees with
g
on
M\times\{c\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\setminus(\rho-2)I^{n-1}
for all
c>\rho-4
.
As
g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=c\}}
does not depend on
c
, the extension can be chosen to be independent of
c
.
These extensions assemble to a smooth metric
g_{\mathrm{aux}}
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(\rho-4)I^{n}
.
Applying the proof of Proposition
3.1.8
one more time, yields a block metric that agrees with
g_{\mathrm{aux}}
outside of
(\rho-2)I^{n}
, in particular, it agrees with
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-2}}
.
We denote this extension again with
g
.
Of course, we do not claim that the scalar curvature of this extension of
g
is positive on the whole manifold
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Let
H
,
\Phi
, and
U_{left}\subset\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho-4}}
be as in the Embedding Theorem
3.2.2
.
By the Metric Modification Lemma
3.3.2
,
we find, for all sufficient large
R
, a psc metric
G_{R}
on
M\times R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
that agrees with
g
outside a compact subset of
M\times R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
and that can be extended on
M\times\left(R\cdot U_{left}\setminus R\Phi(H\times[1,2])\right)
to a smooth psc metric via
g
.
This extension is again denoted by
G_{R}
.
The set
U_{left}\cup\Phi(H\times[1,2])
is diffeomorphic to
\{x_{1}\leq\rho+1\}
via
\Psi_{n}
, by Embedding Theorem
3.2.2
, 2.,
the set
R\cdot U_{left}\cup R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
is diffeomorphic to
\{x_{1}\leq(\rho+1)R\}
via
{}_{R}\Psi_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=R\Psi_{n}(R^{-1}\cdot)
.
Since
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}
is a product metric on
H\times[R,1.2R]
, the metric
({}_{R}\Psi_{n}^{-1})^{\ast}G_{R}
is also collared with collar length
0.2R
.
This means that there is a (necessarily unique) collar map
{}_{R}\kappa\colon M\times\{x_{1}=(\rho+1)R\}\times(-0.2R,0]\rightarrow M\times\{x_{1}\leq(\rho+1)R\}
such that the
{}_{R}\kappa^{\ast}({}_{R}\Psi_{n}^{-1})^{\ast}G_{R}
is a product metric.

The collar
{}_{R}\kappa
might not be the standard one
(m,x,t)\mapsto(m,x+te_{1})
but they agree outside a compact set.
This follows from the fact that
{}_{R}\Psi_{n}
is the identity away from a compact set, so
({}_{R}\Psi_{n}^{-1})^{\ast}G_{R}
agrees with
g
away from a compact set.
Since the set of collars that agree outside a fixed compact set is contractible we find a diffeomorphism
{}_{R}\Theta\colon M\times\{x_{1}\leq(\rho+1)R\}\rightarrow M\times\{x_{1}\leq(\rho+1)R\}
that is the identity outside a compact set and that maps the standard collar to
{}_{R}\kappa
.
In fact, the proof of this statement allows us to assume that
{}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\kappa
agrees with the standard collar on
M\times\{x_{1}=(\rho+1)R\}\times(-R/10,0]
.

We can now extend the metric
{}_{R}\Theta^{\ast}({}_{R}\Psi_{n}^{-1})^{\ast}G_{R}
from
\{x_{1}\leq(\rho+1)R\}
to
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with the product metric
({}_{R}\Psi_{n}^{-1})^{\ast}G_{R}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=(\rho+1)R\}}\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}
.
The resulting metric
g_{\mathrm{fill}}
is a block metric that has positive scalar curvature.
It further satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{\mathrm{fill}}=g_{(j,\omega)}
for all
(j,\omega)
because it agrees with
g
on the complement of a sufficient large cuboid
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{?\cdot R}}
.

Thus,
g_{\mathrm{fill}}
is a filler for given
n
-horn, so
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan set.
∎

3.4
Geometric Addition

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan set, the (combinatorial) homotopy groups are well defined and carry a (combinatorical) group structure.
These group structures are formally defined through the Kan condition and, as we do not keep track of the fillers, they are only defined on homotopy classes.
This raises the question, whether there is a geometrical meaningful representative for it.

Theorem
3.4.4
gives answers this question.
Informally, the theorem says that the addition on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
is given by gluing two block metrics together along one of their matching faces.

Angel Rotation

We start with describing a construction that extends a metric on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
to
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
by “rotating it around the origin”.
In the following, let
N
be a smooth, not necessarily closed manifold.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}

the shift diffeomorphism on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
given by
x\mapsto x+Re_{j}
.
If
g
is a Riemannian metric on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then we define

\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}}_{\ast}(g)=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-R}^{\ast}(g).

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Let
g
be a Riemannian metric on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
that has a product structure away from
N\times[r_{1},r_{2}]
.
We can construct a metric
g_{R}^{\angle}

on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
for all
R\geq 1
with the following properties:

(o)

The metric
g^{\angle}_{R}
is a product metric away from some compact subset.

(i)

If
g
is a psc metric, then there is a
R(g)>0
such that
g^{\angle}_{R}
is a psc metric for all
R>R(g)
.

(ii)

g^{\angle}_{R}
restricts to
\mathrm{Sh}_{R(g)}
on
N\times\{0\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
and on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\{0\}
under the canonical identification with
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
.

(iii)

g^{\angle}_{R}
is a product metric on
\{x_{j}\leq R/2\}
and the product structures are compatible on the intersection.

(v)

If
g
is the suspension of
h\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{(+)}(N)
, then
g^{\angle}_{R}
is the suspension of a function
\mathfrak{h}_{R}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{(+)}(N)
.
Furthermore, if
h
is constant so is
\mathfrak{h}_{R}
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a compact, convex, point symmetric body inside
I^{2}
with smooth boundary
\partial K
.
We further assume
K
agrees with
I^{2}
inside
\{|x_{j}|\leq 1/2\}
.
Let
\gamma\colon[0,l]\rightarrow\partial K\cap(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}
be the surjective curve that is parametrised by arclength and satisfies
\gamma(0)=e_{1}
.
This particularly implies that

\displaystyle\gamma(\varphi)=\begin{cases}e_{1}+\varphi e_{2},&\text{on }[0,1/2],\\
e_{2}+(l-\varphi)e_{2},&\text{on }[l-1/2,l].\end{cases}

Let
v\colon[0,l]\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the unique normalised vector field that is perpendicular to
\gamma^{\prime}
and satisfies
\det(v,\gamma^{\prime})>0
.
In particular,
v(0)=e_{1}
.
Furthermore, let
\kappa
be the curvature of
\gamma
.
Recall that the curvature
1
1

1

The sign appears because we chose
v
such that
(v,\gamma^{\prime})
is positively oriented.
of
\gamma
is defined by
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)=-\kappa(\varphi)v(\varphi)
.
The curvature is non-negative as
\gamma
parametrises the boundary of a convex body.

If we replace
K
by
R\cdot K
, then the corresponding objects
\gamma_{R}
,
v_{R}
, and
\kappa_{R}
have
[0,R\cdot l]
as domain and satisfy

\gamma_{R}(\varphi)=R\gamma(R^{-1}\varphi),\quad v_{R}(\varphi)=v(R^{-1}\varphi),\quad\kappa_{R}(\varphi)=R^{-1}\kappa(R^{-1}\varphi).

For all
R\geq 1
, this datum defines a diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\Xi\colon(0,\infty)\times[0,R\cdot l]

\displaystyle\rightarrow(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}\setminus R\cdot K

\displaystyle(r,\varphi)

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{R}(\varphi)+r\cdot v_{R}(\varphi),

which can be thought as a variant of polar coordinates on the quadrant
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{\geq 0}
.
Pulling back the euclidean metric yields

\bigl(\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\bigr)_{(r,\varphi)}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}.

This motivates the following construction, which is visualised in Figure
3.6
.

Figure 3.6:
The metric
g
gets rotates around the smooth, convex body
R\cdot K
.

Given
g\in\mathrm{Riem}(N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
that is a product metric on
N\times[0,\varepsilon)
, for some
\epsilon>0
.
We extend
g
via the product structure to
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.
Define
g_{R}^{\angle}
to be unique Riemannian metric on
N\times(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}\setminus R\cdot K^{\circ}
that satisfies

\displaystyle\bigl((\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}\bigr)_{(n,r,\varphi)}=g_{(n,r)}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}.

Since
g=g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{N}\oplus\mathrm{d}r^{2}
away from
M\times[r_{1},r_{2}]
, we conclude that

(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}=g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{N}\oplus(\mathrm{d}r^{2}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})=g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{N}\oplus\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle

(3.1)

away from
M\times[r_{1},r_{2}]\times[0,R\cdot l]
.

As a consequence,
(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}
has positive scalar curvature away from
M\times[r_{1},r_{2}]\times[0,R\cdot l])
, if
g
has positive scalar curvature.
Since the
r
-coordinate is bounded on
M\times[r_{1},r_{2}]\times[0,R\cdot l]
, we can choose
R
sufficiently large such that the 2-jets of
(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}
and the product metric
g\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi
are arbitrarily close on
M\times[r_{1},r_{2}]\times[0,R\cdot l]

This implies that
g_{R}^{\angle}
is a psc metric if
R
is sufficient large.

The metric
g_{R}^{\angle}
is a product metric on
\{x_{j}\leq R/2\}
.
Indeed, on
\Xi_{R}^{-1}(\{x_{1}\leq R/2\})
we have
\Xi_{R}(r,\varphi)=(R+r)e_{2}-\varphi e_{1}
, so that
p_{1}\circ(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})=\mathrm{Sh}_{R}\circ p_{1}
, where
p_{1}
projects the first component away.
Thus, the identity

g_{R}^{\angle}|_{\{x_{1}\leq R/2\}}=\mathrm{Sh}_{R\,\ast}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}=\mathrm{Sh}_{R\,\ast}(p_{1}^{\ast}g)+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}

follows from the calculation

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}(\mathrm{Sh}_{R\,\ast}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}(p_{1}^{\ast}\mathrm{Sh}_{-R}^{\ast}(g)+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2})

\displaystyle=p_{1}^{\ast}g+(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=g\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}=(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}.

We use Equation (
3.1
) to extend
g_{R}^{\angle}
from
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus RK^{\circ}
to
N\times(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}
via
g\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{N}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2}+\mathrm{d}x_{2}^{2}
and denote the result again with
g_{R}^{\angle}
.
The extension satisfies
(o)
,
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iii)
by construction.

Lastly, if
g=\mathrm{susp}(h)
is the suspension of a curve of metrics, then
g_{R}^{\angle}
is the suspension of

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}_{R}\colon(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto\begin{cases}h(\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}(\Xi_{R}^{-1}(x_{1},x_{2}))),&\text{if }(x_{1},x_{2})\in(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}\setminus R\cdot K,\\
h(0),&\text{if }(x_{1},x_{2})\in(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{2}\cap R\cdot K.\end{cases}

The map is well defined and smooth, as
h
is constant near zero and thus
\mathrm{Sh}_{R}(h)=h(\cdot-R)
can be constantly extended to
[0,R]
.
As before, one checks that
(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{R})=(\mathrm{id}\times\Xi_{R})^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle}=h+\mathrm{d}r^{2}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}
and claim
(iv)
follows.
∎

Geometric Addition on the Concordance Set

We will use the previous extension construction to give a geometrical description of the group structures.

Definition 3.4.3
.

For
1\leq j\leq n
, let
g,h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
be two block metrics with matching faces,
\mathrm{i.e.}
,
\partial^{1}_{j}g=\partial^{-1}_{j}h
.
If
g
and
h
decompose away from
M\times RI^{n}
, then we set

g+_{j,R}h=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-2R}(g)|_{\{x_{j}\leq 0\}}\cup\mathrm{Sh}_{j,2R}(h)|_{\{x_{j}\geq 0\}}.

It is easy to see that
g+_{j,R}h
is a block metric whose faces are given by

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}(g+_{j,R}h)=\begin{cases}\partial^{-1}_{j}g,&\text{if }i=j,\varepsilon=-1,\\
\partial^{1}_{j}h,&\text{if }i=j,\varepsilon=1,\\
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g+_{j-1,R}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}h,&\text{if }i<j,\\
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g+_{j,R}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}h,&\text{if }i>j.\end{cases}

Furthermore,
g+_{j,R}h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}}(M)
has positive scalar curvature, if
g
and
h
have positive scalar curvature.
Note further, that if
g=\mathrm{susp}(\bar{g})
and
h=\mathrm{susp}(\bar{h})
are suspension of block maps, then

g+_{j,R}h=\mathrm{susp}\bigl(\bar{g}(\cdot+2Re_{j})|_{\{x_{j}\leq 0\}}\cup\bar{h}(\cdot-2Re_{j})|_{\{x_{j}\geq 0\}}\bigr)

is again a suspension of a block map.

These operations represent the addition on the combinatorial homotopy groups.

Theorem 3.4.4
.

There are
n
-many group structures
+_{j}
on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M),g_{0})
that are defined as follows:
If
g,h
are representatives of
[g],[h]\in\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
that decompose away from
M\times RI^{n}
, then

[g]+_{j}[h]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[g+_{j,R}h].

If
r_{j}
denotes the reflection at the hyperplane
\{x_{j}=0\}
, then
[r_{j}^{\ast}g]
is the inverse element of
[g]
with respect to
+_{j}
.
The structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to each other.
Furthermore,
+_{1}
agrees with the group structure provided by cubical set theory.

Proof.

We start with the proof that the operations are well defined.
Two different choices
R_{0}
,
R_{1}
yield isotopic metrics.
Indeed, an isotopy between
g+_{j,R_{0}}h
and
g+_{j,R_{1}}h
is given by
s\mapsto g+_{j,R_{s}}h
, where
R_{s}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1-2)R_{0}+sR_{1}
.
Since isotopy implies concordance (after a reparametrisation), the two block metrics represent the same element in
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
.

Let
G\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n+1}}(M)
be a homotopy between
g_{-1}
and
g_{1}
and let
H\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n+1}}(M)
be a homotopy between
h_{-1}
and
h_{1}
.
Assume that
G
and
H
decompose outside of
M\times RI^{n}
.
Then
G+_{j+1,R}H\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n+1}}(M)
is a homotopy bewteen
g_{-1}+_{j,R}g_{1}
and
h_{-1}+_{j,R}h_{1}
.

The two previous observations imply that
+_{j}
is a well defined operation on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M),g_{0})
.

The proof of associativity agrees with the proof that the group structure of homotopy groups is associative.
The neutral element is given by the base point
g_{0}
.

If
g
decomposes outside of
M\times\rho I^{n}
, so does
r_{j}^{\ast}g
.
The homotopy between
r_{j}^{\ast}g+_{j,\rho}g
is indicated in Figure
3.7
.

x_{j}
x_{1}
g
r_{j}^{\ast}g
g_{0}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}+\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}
g_{0}+\mathrm{d}x_{1}+\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}
r_{j}^{\ast}g+_{j}g

Figure 3.7:
A homotopy between
r_{j}^{\ast}g+_{j}g
and the base point
g_{0}
. The lower quadrants are not shown. Each line segment can be identified with the lower right one and under this identification, the metric on the line segment corresponds to
g
.

In formulas, if
g_{R}^{\angle,j}
is the metric obtained from Proposition
3.4.2
applied to the coordiantes
x_{1},x_{j}
, then, provided
R
is sufficient large, the metric

\displaystyle\begin{split}G\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=&g_{R}^{\angle,j}|_{\{x_{1}\geq 0,\,x_{j}\geq 0\}}\,\cup\,(g+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2})|_{\{x_{1}\leq 0,\,x_{j}\geq 0\}}\,\cup\\
&r_{j}^{\ast}g_{R}^{\angle,j}|_{\{x_{1}\geq 0,\,x_{j}\leq 0\}}\,\cup\,(r_{j}^{\ast}g+\mathrm{d}x_{1}^{2})|_{\{x_{1}\leq 0,\,x_{j}\leq 0\}}\end{split}

is a homotopy in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n+1}}(M)
between
g_{0}
and
r_{j}^{\ast}g+_{j,R}g
for all
R\geq\rho
sufficiently large.

The proof that two of these group structures are Eckmann-Hilton related is the same as for homotopy groups.

The proof that
+_{1}
agrees with
+_{top}
, the group structure coming from cubical set theory, is given by a picture.
Recall that we need to find an element
H\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n+1}}(M)
that satisfies
[\partial^{-1}_{1}H]=a
,
[\partial^{1}_{1}H]=a+_{1}b
, and
[\partial^{1}_{2}H]=b
, while
[\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}H]=[g_{0}]
for all other faces.
Such a filler is given indicated in Figure
3.8
.

Figure 3.8:
A homotopy between
+_{1}
and
+_{top}
. In the blank space, the metric
H
agrees with
g_{0}
.

∎

Chapter 4
The Operator Concordance Set

Recall that one of our main goals is to factor the index difference through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
The naive approach would be to translate the index difference
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\rightarrow KO^{-d}
into the category of cubical sets via the singular set functor (or a weakly equivalent version, like the cubical set of (smooth) block maps) and then try to factor the corresponding cubical map
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow KO_{\bullet}^{-d}
through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
However, the resulting map
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\rightarrow KO^{-d}_{\bullet}
would be highly non-canonical and difficult to describe because, roughly speaking,
KO_{\bullet}^{-d}
is modelled using the notion of
isotopy
while
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is build on the notion of
concordances
.

Instead, we construct a new model for real
K
-theory in this section.
It should be the geometric realisation of a cubical set that is the target of a ’nice’ cubical map from
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
so that the proof of the factorisation result carried out in Chapter
5
is of mild difficulty.

The starting point is the observation made in
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
that the space
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
of invertible
pseudo Dirac operators
on a closed,
d
-dimensional, spin manifold
M
, provided it is non-empty, is a classifying space for real
K
-theory.
In this model, the index difference is the map
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
that assigns to a psc metric
g
its Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
.
Our new model for real
K
-theory emerges in the same manner as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
emerges out of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
The cubical set
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
of all invertible
block Dirac operators
comes with a comparison map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
defined on a weakly equivalent subset of
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
.
The main result of this chapter is that this operator suspension map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

In Section
4.1
, we recall the foundations of spin geometry like the construction of the Clifford-linear spinor bundle and the Clifford linear Dirac operator and study how these geometric objects depend on the underlying metric.
We also translate the results of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
into our language and establish the space of invertible block Dirac operators as a classifying space for real
K
-theory and give a description of the (Hitchin) index difference.

Section
4.2
presents the foundations of block operators on spinor bundles. In particular, we lay the focus on how local these pseudo differential operators are and how much they increase the support of sections.

In Section
4.3
we study the analytic properties of these operators and discuss several cut-and-paste arguments.

Section
4.4
is the operator theoretic analog of Section
3.1
.
There we construct the operator concordance set
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
and define the operator suspension map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}
.
We will construct, in Section
4.5
, a weakly homotopy equivalent subset
B_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
so that the restriction
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}|_{B_{\bullet}}
takes values in
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
.

In Section
4.6
and
4.7
, we show that
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan set followed by the even stronger statement that the cubical map
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
that assigns to a block Dirac operator its underlying Riemannian metric is a cubical Kan fibration.

Section
4.8
is the operator theoretic analog of Section
3.4
.
It introduces an operator theoretic addition which agrees with the group structure on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
coming from cubical set theory.
As an application, we show that the Gromov-Lawson index difference is a group homomorphism
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))\rightarrow KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})
.

Finally, in Section
4.9
, we use all previous acquired results to show that the operator suspension
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a weak equivalence.
We show that
\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
and
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
can be identified with certain
KK
-theory groups and that, under this identification, the homomorphism
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
corresponds to the Kasparov-product with the fundamental class
\alpha_{n+1}
, which is known to be an isomorphism.

4.1
Foundations on Spin Geometry

We recall and summarise the necessary material on which we will base our results.
First, we start with the construction of the Clifford-right linear spinor bundle and examine the role of the underlying Riemannian metrics.
In particular, our focus lies on the identification of the two spinor bundles with different underlying metrics.
This allows us to define the universal spinor bundle over
M\times\mathrm{Riem}(M)
that considers all Riemannian metric at once.

The main task of this section is the construction of the space of invertible pseudo Dirac operators
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
of a closed spin manifold
M
of dimension
d>0
.
Results of Johannes Ebert
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
imply that if
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
is not empty, then it is a classifying space for
KO^{-(\dim M+1)}
.

The Spin Package

We recall the foundations of classical spin geometry and set up notational conventions.
Everything that is covered here can be found in the book
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
except for Lemma
4.1.15
and
4.1.17
.
Although these results are probably known to experts, the author has not found them in the literature.

Definition 4.1.1
.

Let
(V,q)
be a vector space with a quadratic form.
The
Clifford algebra

Cl(V,q)

is the unital algebra that contains
V
and satisfies the following universal property:
Every injective linear map
f\colon V\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
into a unital algebra
\mathcal{A}
that satisfies
f(v)^{2}=-q(v)\cdot 1

extends uniquely to a unital algebra homomorphism
Cl(f)\colon Cl(V,q)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 4.1.2
.

Every Clifford algebra has a canonical grading, which is given by the parity operator
Cl(-\mathrm{id})
.
We call this grading the
even/odd
-grading and it is denoted by
\mathrm{e/o}
.

If
V
is a real vector space, we can consider it as Real vector space
(V\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{C}},\mathrm{id}\otimes\overline{\cdot})
.
Then the Clifford algebra is Real as well.
The Real structure
\overline{\cdot}
commutes with the parity operator.

Remark 4.1.3
.

Every Real vector space or Real vector bundle that we consider in this thesis comes from a real vector space or bundle via the above construction.
We will not notationally distinguish them.
The reason why we actually need to work with Real vector bundles is that we consider pseudo differential operators on these bundles, which are locally defined with the help of Fourier transformation, see Appendix
B
for details, which is only defined on complex vector spaces and that the principal symbol of pseudo differential operator does not in general preserve the real bundle.

Example 4.1.4
.

We denote the vector space
V=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
endowed with the quadratic form
q(x,y)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=||x||^{2}_{\mathrm{eucl}}-||y||^{2}_{\mathrm{eucl}}
with
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p,q}
.
The standard basis is denoted by
e_{1}\dots,e_{p},\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{q}
.
Its Clifford algebra is multiplicatively generated by the standard basis of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p,q}
. The generators satisfy the following relations

\displaystyle e_{i}e_{j}+e_{j}e_{i}=-2\delta_{ij},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{i}=2\delta_{ij},

and
Cl(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p,q})
is the universal algebra with these generators and relations.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}
be strictly monotonically increasing ordered tupels with entries between
1
and
p
, and
1
and
q
, respectively.
If
e_{I}
denotes
e_{i_{1}}\cdot\dots\cdot e_{i_{|I|}}
(with the convention
e_{\emptyset}=1
), then the set of all
e_{I}\cdot\varepsilon_{I^{\prime}}
form a basis of
Cl(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p,q})
.
There is a unique quadratic form
||\cdot||^{2}
that turns that basis into a (signed) orthonormal basis.
The Clifford algebra endowed with all this data, is denoted by
Cl_{p,q}
.

The Clifford algebra carries a structure of a
C^{\ast}
-algebra.
Indeed, the left multiplication map
l\colon Cl_{p,q}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(Cl_{p,q})
is an injective algebra homomorphism whose image is closed under taking adjoints.
It suffices to show this for the generators
e_{1},\dots,e_{p},\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{q}
.
The linear maps
l(e_{i})
and
l(\varepsilon_{j})
are isometries as they map the canonical orthonormal basis of
Cl_{p,q}
to itself up to sign.
Together with the Clifford relations this implies

l(e_{i})^{\ast}=l(-e_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad l(\varepsilon_{j})^{\ast}=l(\varepsilon_{j}).

In the following we only write
e_{i}\cdot
and
\varepsilon_{j}\cdot
for
l(e_{i})
and
l(\varepsilon_{j})
unless we want to emphasise the representation
l
.

Usually we will denote the
C^{\ast}
-norm on
Cl_{p,q}
with
||\text{--}||_{\mathrm{op}}
.
Note that
||v||_{\mathrm{op}}=||v||
for vectors
v\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p,0}
but
||v||_{\mathrm{op}}\neq||v||
for general elements
v\in Cl_{p,0}
.

The most important case for us is
q=0
. We will restrict our discussions of spin geometry to this case, although it holds in larger generality.

Definition 4.1.5
.

We denote by
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)

the subgroup of
Cl_{d,0}
that is multiplicative generated by
\{v\cdot w\,:\,v,w\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subseteq Cl_{d,0},\,||v||=||w||=1\}
and we denote by
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)

the subgroup of
Cl_{d,0}
that is multiplicatively generated by
\{v\,:\,v\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subseteq Cl_{d,0},\,||v||=1\}
.

Note that left multiplication with elements in
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)
is an isometry on
Cl_{d,0}
.
The conjugation with elements of
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)
is another isometric action on
Cl_{d,0}
.
It preserves the subspace
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subset Cl_{d,0}
and thus yields a homomorphism
\lambda\colon\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\rightarrow\mathrm{O}(d)
.
One can show that it is a two-sheeted covering, see for example
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem I.2.10.
Restricted to
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
, this yields a two-sheeted cover
\lambda\colon\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(d)
.

To generalise these concepts to manifolds, the best approach seems to be via principal frame bundles and representations.
A basic reference for principal bundles, their associated bundles and their differential geometry is
[
baum2009eichfeldtheorie
]
.
In the following, let
M^{d}
be a smooth manifold of dimension
d
(it may have corners and does not need to be compact).

Definition 4.1.6
.

For each manifold
M^{d}
, the
bundle of frames
is given by

\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\xrightarrow{\cong}TM_{x}\,:\,\text{isomorphisms}\}\rightarrow M.

It is a principal
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)
-bundle whose
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)
-right action is given by pre-composition.
If
M^{d}
is additionally orientable, we can define, for each orientation, the subbundle

\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\xrightarrow{\cong}T_{x}M\,:\,\text{orientation preserving isomorphisms}\}\rightarrow M.

It is a principal
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(d)
-bundle.

Conversely, a choice of a
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(d)
-reduction of
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)
is a choice of an orientation.

Definition 4.1.7
.

For a Riemannian metric
g
, we define
bundle of orthonormal frames
to be

\mathrm{O}(M,g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)\xrightarrow{\cong}(T_{x}M,g_{x})\,:\,\text{isometries}\}\rightarrow M.

If
M^{d}
is additionally orientable, we can define, for each orientation, the subbundle

\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{O}(M,g)\cap\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M).

A choice of a Riemannian metric on
M^{d}
is equivalent to the choice of an
\mathrm{O}(d)
-reduction of
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)
.
Conversely, a choice of an
\mathrm{O}(d)
-reduction of
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)
gives a Riemannian metric because an inner product is uniquely determined by one of its orthonormal bases.

A
spin structure
on
M
is the choice of a
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
-reduction of
\mathrm{SO}(M,g)
, that is a choice of a principal
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
bundle
P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}
and a
\lambda
-equivariant map
\xi\colon P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(M,g)
over
M
; in other words, the following diagram, in which the horizontal arrows denote the right action, commutes:

\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi\times}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\times\mathrm{SO}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(M,g).}

Not every orientable manifold carries a spin structure.
The existence of a spin structure is obstructed by the first and second Stiefel-Whitney classes of
M

[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem II.1.7.
Note that the choice of a spin structure involves the choice of a metric.
Since manifolds can carry several inequivalent spin structures
1
1

1

Indeed, the set of (equivalence classes of) spin-structure is parameterised by
H^{1}(M,\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem II.1.7
, we have to make sure to pick spin structures consistently when varying the metric.
This can be done as follows:
Let
\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)}^{+}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)^{+}
the unique two-sheeted cover that extends the canonical group homomorphism
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(d)
. The vanishing of the first and second Stiefel-Whitney classes implies the existence of a
\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)}^{+}
-reduction
\rho\colon P_{\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)}^{+}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M)
.
We fix such a structure.
For each metric and each choice of
\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(d)^{+}}
-structure, the
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
-reduction of our choice is given by the following pull-back
2
2

2

It can be shown that any spin structure can be obtained in that manner

\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M).}

With the help of principal bundles we can generalise the Clifford algebra construction to vector bundles over manifolds.
Note that this construction does not require
M
to be spin.

Definition 4.1.8
.

Let
M^{d}
be a manifold with Riemannian metric
g
.
The
Clifford bundle
is given by

Cl(TM,g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\times_{Cl(\mathrm{taut})}Cl_{d,0}=P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathrm{conj}}Cl_{d,0},

where
\mathrm{taut}
is the tautological representation of
\mathrm{SO}(d)
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\mathrm{conj}
is the representation that is given by conjugations of elements of
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(n)
in
Cl_{d,0}
.

The two representations,
Cl(\mathrm{taut})
and
l
, are isometric.
The bundle
Cl(TM,g)
is a bundle of algebras with fibres
Cl(T_{x}M,g_{x})
.
It canonically includes
TM
.
The Riemannian metric
g
on
TM
canonically extends to a metric on
Cl(TM,g)
so that all products
e_{I}
form an orthonormal basis.
Since
Cl(\mathrm{taut})
preserves the grading,
Cl(TM,g)
is a graded algebra bundle.
The Levi-Cevita connection
\nabla^{L.C.}
on
(TM,g)
extends uniquely to a metric connection
\nabla^{L.C.}
on
Cl(TM,g)
that is a derivation with respect to the algebra structure
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Proposition II.4.8, meaning that, for all smooth sections
\sigma,\tau
, we have

\nabla^{L.C.}(\sigma\tau)=(\nabla^{L.C.}\sigma)\tau+\sigma\nabla^{L.C.}(\tau).

The grading and the Real structure are parallel with respect to that connection.

If we use the left-multiplication as representation, we obtain the spinor bundle.
In contrast to the conjugation, this representation does not factor through
\mathrm{SO}(d)
, which is the reason why this construction only works over spin manifolds.

Definition 4.1.9
.

Let
M^{d}
be a spin manifold with Riemannian metric
g
.
The associated
spinor bundle
is given by

\mathfrak{S}_{g}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d),l}Cl_{d,0}.

It is a
Cl(TM,g)
-left module bundle and the module structure is denoted with
\mathbf{c}
.

The spinor bundle also has a canonical
Cl_{d,0}
-right action
\mathbf{r}

induced by right multiplication.
It is well defined because left and right multiplication commute.
The left-multiplication
l
acts by isometries, so
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
carries a canonical Riemannian metric induced by the inner product on
Cl_{d,0}
.
Multiplication with unit vectors induces isometries with respect to this metric.
Since
\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
consists only of even elements in
Cl_{d,0}
, the left multiplication preserves the grading of
Cl_{d,0}
, so the grading on
Cl_{d,0}
carries over to
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
3
3

3

This is the very reason why we restrict to spin structures.
The construction still makes sense, if we consider only Pin structures, but we would then loose the grading, on which index theory relies.
.
We call this grading again
\mathrm{e/o}
.

Right multiplication with fixed odd elements are odd linear maps.
The Levi-Cevita connection extends canonically to a metric connection
\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
.

The resulting metric connection is uniquely characterised by the following properties:

(i)

\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}}
is a
Cl(TM,g)
module derivation,

(ii)

\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}}
is even,

(iii)

\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}}
is linear with respect to the
Cl_{d,0}
-right action.

Thus,
\mathfrak{S}
is a Dirac-bundle in the sense of
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Def. II.5.2.

Dirac bundles are precisely those bundles that allow the definition of a Dirac operator.
We give definition in the case of the spinor bundle.

Definition 4.1.10
.

The
Dirac operator

of a Riemannian metric
g
on
M
is defined by the composition

\textstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}\colon\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla^{\mathrm{\mathfrak{S}}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}(T^{\vee}M\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}(TM\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{c}(\cdot)}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g}),}

where
g_{\sharp}\colon T^{\vee}M\rightarrow TM
is defined by
g(g_{\sharp}(\xi),\text{--})=\xi
.

The inner product on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
, denoted by
h
, and the volume form
\mathrm{vol}_{g}

on
M
induced by the Riemannian metric
g\in\mathrm{Riem}(M)
allow us to equip
\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
with an inner product, see Appendix
A
for the general construction.
It is given by

(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{M}h_{x}(\sigma_{1}(x),\sigma_{2}(x))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x).

The completion of
\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
is denoted by
L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
and is called the space of
square integrable spinors

\mathfrak{S}_{g}
.
The
Cl_{d,0}
-right action
\mathbf{r}
and the
\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading
\mathrm{e/o}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
carry over to a
Cl_{d,0}
-right action and a
\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading on
L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
, which we denote with the same symbols.
The Dirac operator acts as an unbounded operator on this space.
More precisely, we have the following theorem, which summarises results from
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Chapter II.

Theorem 4.1.11
.

The Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is an elliptic, odd, symmetric,
first-order,
Cl_{d,0}
-right linear differential operator with principal symbol
i\mathbf{c}(g_{\sharp}(\cdot))
.
If
(M^{d},g)
is complete, then
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is self-adjoint.
If
M
is closed, then
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is an (unbounded) Fredholm operator, that is, it has a finite dimensional kernel.

The relation between the Dirac operator and the scalar curvature of a Riemannian metric is established by the Lichnerowicz-formula, which is of outstanding importance.

Theorem 4.1.12

(Lichnerowicz-Formula)
.

For every Riemannian metric
g
, the formula

{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}^{2}\sigma=\left({\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}}^{\ast}\circ\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}+\frac{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)}{4}\right)\sigma,

(4.1)

holds true for all
\sigma\in\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
.
Here
{\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}}^{\ast}\colon\Gamma_{c}(T^{\vee}M\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g})\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
is the formal adjoint of the spinor connection.

Since
\nabla^{\ast}\nabla
is positive semi-definite, we immediately deduce that a psc-metric contradicts the existence of smooth harmonic spinors that can be approximated by compactly supported spinors.

Corollary 4.1.13
.

Let
g\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
, then the kernel of
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
in the minimal domain is trivial.

The Lichnerowicz-formula combined with the Atiyah-Singer index theorem gives a powerful topological obstruction to positive scalar curvature metrics on closed spin manifolds.
If the underlying manifold is closed, then the kernel of the Dirac operator is a finite dimensional graded
Cl_{d,0}
-module and hence represents an element in real
K
-theory
KO^{-d}(\mathrm{pt})
, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem I.9.21, which we call the
analytical index
.
The Clifford-linear Atiyah-Singer index theorem, proved in
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Chapter III.16, relates this
KO
-theory class with the
\alpha
-invariant, or
topological index
, which is homomorphism
\alpha\colon\Omega_{\ast}^{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}}(\mathrm{pt})\rightarrow KO^{-\ast}(\mathrm{pt})
, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem II.7.14.

Theorem 4.1.14
.

For a closed spin manifold
(M^{d},g)
, the analytic index and the topological index agree:

[\ker{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}]=\alpha(M)\in KO^{-d}(\mathrm{pt}).

The Atiyah-Singer index formula implies that if we change the orientation on
M^{d}
, then the analytical index of
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
needs to change the sign.
The effect of the chosen orientation is less transparent in the Clifford-linear setup in contrast to the setup that uses the irreducible spinor bundle, where a change of orientation results in the change of the grading.

Lemma 4.1.15
.

Let
M
be a connected spin manifold of dimension
d
.
If
M^{\mathrm{op}}
is the same manifold equipped with the opposite orientation, then there is an
odd
isomorphism of
Cl(M,g)
-left module bundles

\mathrm{Sw}\colon\mathfrak{S}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathfrak{S}(M^{\mathrm{op}}).

The map
\mathrm{Sw}
is not necessarily right-linear but
\mathrm{Sw}^{\ast}(\mathbf{r})
and
\mathbf{r}
are homotopic through
Cl_{d,0}
-right actions on
\mathfrak{S}(M)
.

Proof.

If a Riemannian manifold
(M^{d},g)
is orientable, then the bundle of orthonormal frames has an
SO(d)
-reduction

\mathrm{O}(M,g)\cong\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\times_{\mathrm{SO}(d)}\mathrm{O}(d),

and a choice of an orientation agrees with a choice of a connected component.
Every linear map
A\in\mathrm{O}(d)\setminus\mathrm{SO}(d)
allows us to change the orientation by right multiplication
4
4

4

Warning! This map is not
\mathrm{O}(d)
-equivariant.

\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(M,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\times_{\mathrm{SO}(d)}\mathrm{O}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\cdot A}
\textstyle{\mathrm{SO}(M,g)\times_{\mathrm{SO}(d)}\mathrm{O}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(M,g)}

Since
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\rightarrow\mathrm{O}(d)
is a two-sheeted cover, there is an element
\phi\in\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\setminus\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
, unique up to sign, such that
\lambda(\phi)=A
.

If
\xi\colon P_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-},g}\rightarrow\mathrm{O}(M,g)
is the
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}
structure that restricts to the chosen spin structure, then the chosen element
\phi
induces the change of orientation

\textstyle{P_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-},g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)}\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\cdot\phi}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)}\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces P_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-},g}}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(M,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\cdot A}
\textstyle{\mathrm{O}(M,g).}

The identity

\mathfrak{S}(M)=P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d),l}Cl_{d,0}\cong P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)}\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)\times_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d),l}Cl_{d,0}

shows that the choice of the embedding of
P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d),g}
into
P_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d),g}=P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d),g}\times_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)}\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)
does not affect the isomorphism type as we can extend the right multiplication with
\phi
from
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)
to
Cl_{d,0}
.
Thus, we deduce that
\mathfrak{S}(M)\cong\mathfrak{S}(M^{\mathrm{op}})
and that the isomorphism
\mathrm{Sw}
between them is given by right multiplication with
\phi
, which is odd as
\phi\notin\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}(d)
.

If
d
is odd, then
-\mathrm{id}\notin\mathrm{SO}(d)
and we can choose
\phi=e_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot e_{d}
, which is in the centre of the Clifford algebra.
In this case,
\mathrm{Sw}
is
Cl_{d,0}
-right linear.

If
d
is even, then we need to argue differently.
Since
\mathrm{Sw}^{\ast}(\mathbf{r})=\mathbf{r}(\phi^{-1}(\text{--})\phi)
, it suffices to show that the conjugation with
\phi^{-1}
is homotopic through algebra endomorphisms to
\mathrm{id}_{Cl_{d,0}}
.
By a theorem of Cartan-Dieudonne, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem I.2.7, every orthogonal matrix can be decomposed into a sequence of reflections along hyperplanes, so it suffices to assume that
\phi=v\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subseteq Cl_{d,0}
is a single vector with length
1
.
By the universal property of
Cl_{d,0}
, algebra homomorphisms correspond to injective linear maps
A\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\rightarrow Cl_{d,0}
such that
A(x)^{2}=-||x||^{2}
.
It thus suffices to find a homotopy between the reflection at the hyperplane perpendicular to
v
and the identity through such maps.
If
w
is another vector with length
1
that is perpendicular to
v
, we can define such a homotopy
A_{s}
as the linear extension of the following map

A_{s}(v)=\cos(s)v+\sin(s)v\cdot w,\qquad A_{s}(x)=x,\text{ if }x\in(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}v)^{\perp}.

Anti-commutativity guarantees that
A_{s}(x)^{2}=-||x||^{2}
for all
x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Clearly,
A_{0}=\mathrm{id}
and
A_{\pi}
is the reflection at the hyperplane perpendicular to
v
, which finishes the proof.
∎

The observation from the previous proof offers the opportunity to lift isometries, which are not necessarily orientation preserving, to homomorphisms between spinor bundles.
However, we cannot expect that every isometry
\varphi\colon(M,g_{1})\rightarrow(M,{g_{2}})
lifts to an isometry of the associated spinor bundles.
The following criterion is sufficient.

Definition 4.1.16
.

Let
\xi_{j}\colon P_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-},g_{j}}\rightarrow\mathrm{O}(M,g_{j})
be
\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)
-reductions of
\mathrm{O}(M,g_{j})
.
An isometry
F\colon(TM,g_{1})\rightarrow(TM,g_{2})
is
Pin-structure preserving
if the induced map between the principal bundles of frames lifts to an equivariant map between the Pin-structures.
The isometry
F
is
spin structure preserving
, if it is orientation preserving and Pin-structure preserving.

Lemma 4.1.17
.

A Pin-structure preserving isometry
F\colon(TM,g_{1})\rightarrow(TM,g_{2})
induces an isometry
\mathfrak{S}(F)\colon\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}}\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}}
that is
Cl_{d,0}
-right linear and
Cl(M,g_{1})
-
Cl(M,g_{2})
equivariant.
If
F
additionally preserves the orientation, then
\mathfrak{S}(F)
is even.

Proof.

A lift
\tilde{F}\colon P_{\mathrm{Pin^{-}},g_{1}}\rightarrow P_{\mathrm{Pin^{-}},g_{2}}
of
F
to the Pin structures induces a map on spinor bundles via

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.40959pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.40959pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}(F)\colon\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.40959pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\mathrm{Pin^{-},g_{1}}}\times_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)}{Cl_{d,0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 157.10855pt\raise 7.02779pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.19444pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tilde{F}\times\mathrm{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 196.35164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 166.35164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 196.35164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\mathrm{Pin^{-},g_{2}}}\times_{\mathrm{Pin}^{-}(d)}{Cl_{d,0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 315.29369pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The desired properties follow immediately.

If
F
is orientation preserving, then
\tilde{F}
restricts to an equivariant map on
P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{1}}\rightarrow P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{2}}
, so that
\mathfrak{S}(F)
preserves the grading.
∎

We close this section by discussing the spinor bundle and the Dirac operator of a product manifold.

Example 4.1.18
.

Recall from
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
that, for two quadratic spaces
(V_{i},q_{i})
, the Clifford algebra decomposes as

Cl(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},q_{1}\oplus q_{2})\cong Cl(V_{1},q_{1})\hat{\otimes}Cl(V_{2},q_{2}).

The symbol
\hat{\otimes}
indicates that we have a graded tensor product and a graded algebra structure.
That means that the algebra structure on the right hand side is given by the linear extension of

(v_{1}{\otimes}v_{2})\cdot(w_{1}{\otimes}w_{2})=(-1)^{|v_{2}|\cdot|w_{1}|}(v_{1}\cdot w_{1}){\otimes}(v_{2}\cdot w_{2}),

where
v_{1},w_{1}\in Cl(V_{1},q_{1})
and
v_{2},w_{2}\in Cl(V_{2},q_{2})
are elements of pure degree, i.e., eigenvectors of the grading.
The symbol
|v_{j}|\in\{0,1\}
denotes the degree of
v_{j}
; it is zero if and only if
v_{j}
is even.
A similar formula holds for the left representation
l\colon Cl(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},q_{1}\oplus q_{2})\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(Cl(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},q_{1}\oplus q_{2}))
.

In the case that
(M,g)=(M_{1}\times M_{2},g_{1}\oplus g_{2})
is a Riemannian product, we have the reductions

SO(M_{1},g_{1})\times SO(M_{2},g_{2})\subseteq SO(M,g)\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{1}}\times_{\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}}P_{Spin,g_{2}}\subset P_{Spin,g}

so that the decomposition of Clifford algebras generalises to bundles

Cl(M_{1}\times M_{2},g_{1}\oplus g_{2})\cong Cl(M_{1},g_{1})\hat{\boxtimes}Cl(M_{2},g_{2})

and

\mathfrak{S}(M_{1}\times M_{2},g_{1}\oplus g_{2})\cong\mathfrak{S}(M_{1},g_{1})\hat{\boxtimes}\mathfrak{S}(M_{2},g_{2}),

where
\hat{\boxtimes}
denotes the graded exterior tensor product of two graded vector bundles.
Recall that the exterior tensor product

of two vector bundles
E\rightarrow X
and
F\rightarrow Y
is the vector bundle on
E\boxtimes F\rightarrow X\times Y
whose fibre at
(x,y)
is
E_{x}\otimes F_{y}
.
If the bundles are graded with gradings
\iota_{E}
and
\iota_{F}
, then the grading on
E\hat{\boxtimes}F
is given by
\iota_{E\hat{\boxtimes}F}=\iota_{E}\otimes\iota_{F}
.

These isomorphisms are isomorphisms of algebras and left-modules, respectively.
They also preserve the grading, are isometric, and, in case of spinor bundles, respect also the right action from the Clifford algebra under the identification
Cl_{d_{1}+d_{2},0}\cong Cl_{d_{1},0}\hat{\otimes}Cl_{d_{2},0}
.

The
Cl(M_{1}\times M_{2})
-left action restricted to
TM_{1}\oplus TM_{2}
corresponds to

\mathbf{c}(v_{1}\oplus v_{2})(\sigma_{1}\otimes\sigma_{2})=\mathbf{c}_{1}(v_{1})(\sigma_{1})\otimes\sigma_{2}+\mathrm{e/o}(\sigma_{1})\otimes\mathbf{c}_{2}(v_{2})(\sigma_{2}).

In the product case, the musical isomorphism
(g_{1}\oplus g_{2})_{\sharp}\colon T^{\vee}M_{1}\oplus T^{\vee}M_{2}\rightarrow TM\oplus TM
is given by

(g_{1}\oplus g_{2})_{\sharp}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=(g_{1})_{\sharp}(\xi_{1})+(g_{2})_{\sharp}(\xi_{2}).

By uniqueness, the spinor-connection on
\mathfrak{S}(M_{1}\times M_{2})
corresponds to

\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}(M_{1})}\otimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{id}\otimes\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}(M_{2})}

under the decomposition.
In conclusion, the Dirac operator of a product metric is given by

{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{1}\oplus g_{2}}={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\otimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{2}}.

However, we often write
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{1}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{2}}
instead, to emphasise the product structure of the underlying metric.

The Universal Spinor Bundle

We would like to consider all spinor bundles at once,
\mathrm{i.e.}
, we would like to construct a bundle
\mathfrak{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)\times M
with fibre
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{m}}
. In order to do that we have to address how the change of the underlying metric affects the constructions.
The ansatz is taken from
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
, but we will give a less abstract presentation and provide more details.

First, we recall the
Gram endomorphism
.
A bilinear form
b
on a finite dimensional vector space
V
defines a canonical map
b^{\flat}\colon V\rightarrow V^{\vee}
via
v\mapsto b(v,\text{--})
.
The bilinear form
b
is non-degenerate if and only if
b^{\flat}
is an isomorphism.
If
g
is an inner product on
V
, we denote the inverse of
g^{\flat}
with
g_{\sharp}
and define the
Gram endomorphism
of
b
(with respect to
g
) as
\mathrm{Gram}_{g}(b)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=g_{\sharp}\circ b^{\flat}
.

Lemma 4.1.19
.

If
g
and
g_{0}
are inner products on
V
, then
\mathrm{Gram}_{g}(g_{0})
is self-adjoint with respect to
g
and
g_{0}
.
The Gram endomorphism is furthermore positive definite and satisfies
g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v,w)=g(v,w)
for all
v,w\in V
.

For each invertible linear map
A\colon W\rightarrow V
, the Gram endomorphism satisfies

\mathrm{Gram}_{A^{\ast}g_{0}}(A^{\ast}g)=A^{-1}\circ\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)\circ A.

Proof.

We first prove that
\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)
is self-adjoint with respect to
g_{0}
.
This follows from the calculation

\displaystyle g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=g_{0}({g_{0}}_{\sharp}\circ g^{\flat}v_{1},v_{2})=g(v_{1},v_{2})=g(v_{2},v_{1})

\displaystyle=\dots=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{2},v_{1})

\displaystyle=g_{0}(v_{1},\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{2}).

This calculation shows further that

g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{1},v_{2})=g(v_{1},v_{2}).

In fact, since
g_{0}
is non-degenerate, this equation uniquely determines
\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)
.

Self-adjointness with respect to
g
is now easily verified.
Using the previous identity in the first equation and self-adjointness with respect to
g_{0}
in the second equation, we derive

\displaystyle g(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}^{2}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{1},v_{2})=g_{0}(v_{1},\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)^{2}v_{2})

\displaystyle=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}^{2}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{2},v_{1})=g(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v_{2},v_{1}).

To prove that
\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)
is positive definite, it suffices to prove that all eigenvalues are positive.
Let
v
be an eigenvector corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda
.
Then

\displaystyle\lambda g_{0}(v,v)=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v,v)=g(v,v)>0

implies that
\lambda
must be positive.

The final identity follows from

\displaystyle A^{\ast}g_{0}(A^{-1}\circ\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)\circ Aw_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)Aw_{1},Aw_{2})

\displaystyle=g(Aw_{1},Aw_{2})

\displaystyle=A^{\ast}g(w_{1},w_{2})

and the fact that
A^{\ast}g_{0}
is non-degenerate.
∎

Definition 4.1.20
.

Let
g
and
g_{0}
be two inner products on
V
.
We define the
pre-gauge
map
\tau_{g,g_{0}}\in\mathrm{End}(V)

to be the positive definite square root of
\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)
.

The pre-gauge map inherits many properties of the Gram endomorphism.

Lemma 4.1.21
.

The pre-gauge map
\tau_{g,g_{0}}
is self-adjoint with respect to
g
and
g_{0}
.
It is an isometry
\tau_{g,g_{0}}\colon(V,g)\rightarrow(V,g_{0})
and positive definite.
Furthermore,
\tau_{g,g_{0}}
is uniquely determined by these properties.
In particular,
\tau_{g_{0},g}=\tau_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}
and
\tau_{A^{\ast}g,A^{\ast}g_{0}}=A^{-1}\circ\tau_{g,g_{0}}\circ A
.

Proof.

The root of a self-adjoint endomorphism is self-adjoint.
The pre-gauge map
\tau_{g,g_{0}}
is positive definite by definition.
It is an isometry because

g_{0}(\tau_{g,g_{0}}v,\tau_{g,g_{0}}w)=g_{0}(\tau^{2}_{g,g_{0}}v,w)=g_{0}(\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)v,w)=g(v,w).

An endomorphism
A
that is self-adjoint with respect to
g_{0}
, positive definite, and an isometry
(V,g)\rightarrow(V,g_{0})
necessarily squares to the Gram endomorphism
\mathrm{Gram}_{g_{0}}(g)
.
Since the square root is unique on the set of positive definite self-adjoint operators, the operators
A
and
\tau_{g,g_{0}}
agree.

The equations

\tau_{g_{0},g}=\tau_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau_{A^{\ast}g,A^{\ast}g_{0}}=A^{-1}\circ\tau_{g,g_{0}}\circ A

hold because the operators on the right hand sides satisfy the properties of the operators on the left hand side.
∎

In the most important example
(V,g)=(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)
, the Gram endomorphism
\mathrm{Gram}_{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}(g)
represented in the standard basis of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
gives the more commonly known
Gram matrix
of the metric
g
.
This coordinate expression is useful for the proof that the pre-gauge map depends smoothly on the inner products.

Proposition 4.1.22
.

The map
A\mapsto\sqrt{A}
that assigns a positive definite, self-adjoint matrix its positive definite square root is smooth.

Proof.

The exponential map

\exp\colon\{A\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}\,:\,A^{\ast}=A\}\rightarrow\{A\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}\,:\,A^{\ast}=A,\,A>0\}

is surjective because for each self-adjoint, positive definite
A
there is a
U\in\mathrm{O}(n)
and positive numbers
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
such that
U^{\ast}AU=\mathrm{dia}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})=:T
.
If
B=U\log(T)U^{\ast}
, then
\exp(B)=A
.

We claim that, for all self-adjoint, positive definite
A
, the differential

D_{A}\exp\colon\{A\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}\,:\,A^{\ast}=A\}\rightarrow\{A\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}\,:\,A^{\ast}=A\}

is surjective and therefore an isomorphism.
Indeed, from

(D_{A}\exp)(H)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{j=1}^{k}A^{j-1}HA^{k-j}

we conclude

U^{\ast}D_{A}\exp(H)U=D_{U^{\ast}AU}(U^{\ast}HU).

Since
H
is arbitrary, we may replace
H
by
UHU^{\ast}
and assume that
A=T
is diagonal with entries
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
.
If
H=(h_{ij})
, then
(T^{k}HT^{l})_{ij}=\lambda_{i}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{l}h_{ij}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle(D_{T}\exp)(H)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}\lambda^{\alpha-1}_{i}\lambda_{j}^{k-\alpha}\right)h_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=:(r_{ij}h_{ij}),

where
r_{ij}>0
.
If
B=(b_{ij})
is given, then, for
H=(h_{ij})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(r_{ij}^{-1}b_{ij})
, we have
(D_{T}\exp)(H)=B
.

By the open mapping theorem,
\exp
is locally invertible around each positive definite, self-adjoint matrix
A
.
Call the local inverse
\mathrm{log}
. Then
\sqrt{A}=\exp(1/2\mathrm{log}(A))
is smooth.
∎

Corollary 4.1.23
.

For a fixed but arbitrary inner product
g_{0}
, the maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pos}(V)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(V),

\displaystyle g\mapsto\tau_{g,g_{0}}\quad\text{ and }\quad g\mapsto\tau_{g_{0},g}

are smooth.

Proof.

From
\tau_{g,g_{0}}=\tau_{g_{0},g}^{-1}
follows that one map is smooth if and only if the other one is.

Fix an isometry
A\colon(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)\rightarrow(V,g_{0})
.
Clearly,
g\mapsto\tau_{g,g_{0}}
is smooth if and only if
g\mapsto A^{-1}\circ\tau_{g_{0},g}\circ A
is smooth.
By Lemma
4.1.21
, the latter map agrees with
g\mapsto\tau_{A^{\ast}g,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
, which is smooth by Proposition
4.1.22
.
∎

We wish to extend the pre-gauge map to bundles.
This is done, most transparently, in terms of frame bundles and their associated bundles.
Let
\mathrm{Pos}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be the set of all positive definite, symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
It is a
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
space with left action
A.b\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(A^{-1})^{\ast}b
.
Let
\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be the set of all orientation preserving isomorphisms on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
It is a
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
space with left action
A.\varphi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=A^{-1}\varphi A
.

Let
\mathrm{Pos}(M)

be the bundle of all positive definite, symmetric bilinear forms on
TM
and
\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(TM)
be the bundle of all bundle orientation preserving automorphisms of
TM
.
These bundles can be obtained from
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)
by using the described group actions in the Borel construction.
Lemma
4.1.21
implies that the pre-gauge is a
\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
equivariant map between

\mathrm{Pos}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{2}
and
\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
.
It gives rise to bundle maps

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{\text{--}\,,\,\text{--}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(M)}
\textstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)\tilde{\times}\mathrm{Pos}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\tau_{\text{--}\,,\,\text{--}}}
\textstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)\tilde{\times}\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

Precomposing the pre-gauge map yields a map

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 72.25142pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-72.25142pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.92822pt\raise 5.49306pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.52084pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\tau_{\text{--},\text{--}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.25137pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 115.25137pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(M)\times_{M}\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 227.00975pt\raise 4.62848pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.62848pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circ}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 253.55833pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 253.55833pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}(M)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Convex combination yields a homotopy equivalence
\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}(M)\simeq M\times\{g_{0},g_{0}\}
, where
g_{0}
is a section of
\mathrm{Pos}(M)\rightarrow M
, in other words, a Riemannian metric on
M
.
Covering theory now provides a unique lift

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}}^{+}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\tau}_{\text{--},\text{--}}\circ\,\text{--}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}}^{+}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}(M)\times_{M}\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{\text{--},\text{--}}\circ\,\text{--}}
\textstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(M)}

determined by
\tilde{\tau}_{g_{0},g_{0}}=\mathrm{id}
.

Let
\mathrm{SO}(M,g)
and
\mathrm{SO}(M,g_{0})
be two orthonormal frame bundles corresponding to the Riemannian metrics
g,g_{0}
.
The pre-gauge map
\tau_{g,g_{0}}
maps
\mathrm{SO}(M,g)
to
\mathrm{SO}(M,g_{0})
and the lift
\tilde{\tau}_{g,g_{0}}
therefore maps
P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}
to
P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{0}}
.
The lifted pre-gauge map yields a graded algebra homomorphism

\textstyle{Cl(TM,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{Cl(TM,g_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{\mathrm{conj}}Cl_{d,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\tau}_{g,g_{0}}\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{0}}\times_{\mathrm{conj}}Cl_{d,0}}

and a
Cl_{d,0}
-right linear map

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g}\times_{l}Cl_{d,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\tau}_{g,g_{0}}\times\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{P_{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}},g_{0}}\times_{l}Cl_{d,0}.}

Of course, these identifications are isometric, grading preserving maps.
Furthermore, the identifications maps between the spinor bundles are
Cl(TM,g)
-
Cl(TM,g_{0})
left-module maps.
That means the diagram

\textstyle{Cl(TM,g)\times\mathfrak{S}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{c}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\text{--},g_{0}}\times}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{Cl(TM,g_{0})\times\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{c}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}}

commutes. Consequently, using these identifications, we get (set-theoretical) bijections

\textstyle{Cl(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigsqcup_{g\in\mathrm{Riem}(M)}Cl(TM,g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\text{--},g_{0}}}
\textstyle{Cl(TM,g_{0})\times\mathrm{Riem}(M)}

(4.2)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigsqcup_{g\in\mathrm{Riem}(M)}\mathfrak{S}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{\text{--},g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\times\mathrm{Riem}(M).}

(4.3)

We call
p\mathcal{G}
the
pre-gauge
map.
If
g_{0}
is fixed, then we usually write
\Phi_{g}
and
p\mathcal{G}_{g}
for
\Phi_{g,g_{0}}
and
p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}
, respectively.

While the maps
\Phi
and
p\mathcal{G}
are fibre-wise isometries, the pre-gauge map does not induce isometries between the associated Hilbert spaces of square integrable spinors.
The reason is that the volume form in the definition of the inner product on the sections are also different.
In order to fix that we have to make a conformal change.

Definition 4.1.24
.

For
g,g_{0}\in\mathrm{Riem}(M)
let
\alpha^{2}_{g,g_{0}}\colon M\rightarrow M
be the density of
\mathrm{vol}_{g}
with respect to
\mathrm{vol}_{g_{0}}
.
We call
\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\alpha_{g,g_{0}}\cdot p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}
the
Gauge map
.

We simply write
\mathcal{G}_{g}
if
g_{0}
is fixed.

The following lemma is easily verified.

Lemma 4.1.25
.

The induced map of
\mathcal{G}_{g}
yields an isometry of Hilbert spaces

\mathcal{G}_{g}\colon L^{2}(M,g;\mathfrak{S}_{g})\rightarrow L^{2}(M,g_{0};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}).

Remark 4.1.26
.

Note that if
M
is not compact, then
p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\colon\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
may not extend to a bounded linear map between
L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
.
It extends to a bounded operator if and only if the map
\alpha_{g,g_{0}}
is bounded.

Definition 4.1.27
.

Let

Cl(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)\times M\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)\times M

be the bundles whose topologies are the unique ones such that
\Phi_{\text{--},g_{0}}
and
p\mathcal{G}_{\text{--},g_{0}}
in equation (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) become homeomorphisms if the right hand side is equipped with the product topology.

Informally, we think of these as bundles of bundles over
\mathrm{Riem}(M)
.
From now on, we assume that
M
has no boundary.

Definition 4.1.28
.

Let
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
be the bundle that is trivialised by

\mathcal{G}\circ-\circ\mathcal{G}^{-1}\colon\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)\times\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}});

(4.4)

here,
\mathrm{Riem}(M)
carries the smooth Fréchet topology (the smooth weak topology) and
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
carries the amplitude topology introduced in Appendix
B
.
The union
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{S})
carries the topology of a (trivial) Fréchet space bundle, even a
Fréchet algebra bundle if
M
is compact.
The same construction applies to the Atiyah-Singer closure
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(\mathfrak{S})
defined in Appendix
B
.

Remark 4.1.29
.

If
M
is compact, we can trivialise
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{S})
also with the conjugation of
p\mathcal{G}
.

We are specifically interested in a certain subspace of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Definition 4.1.30
.

An element
P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
is called a
pseudo Dirac operator
if it is a
symmetric, odd,
Cl_{d,0}
-linear operator, whose restriction to each fibre
P_{g}
has the same principal symbol as the Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
that is
\mathrm{symb}_{1}(P_{g})=i\mathbf{c}(g_{\sharp}(\cdot))
,
where
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{c}_{g}
denotes the left-module action of
Cl(TM,g)
on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
.
The set of all pseudo Dirac operators is denoted by
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)
.

Note that conjugation with
\mathcal{G}
does not map
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)
to
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\times\mathrm{Riem}(M)
.
Indeed, since
\mathcal{G}_{g}
is a bundle endomorphism, symbol calculus yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{symb}_{1}(\mathcal{G}_{g}\circ P\circ\mathcal{G}_{g}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}(g)\circ\mathrm{symb}_{1}(P)\circ\mathcal{G}_{g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{g}\circ i\mathbf{c}_{g}({g}_{\sharp}(\text{--}))\circ\mathcal{G}_{g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=p\mathcal{G}_{g}\circ i\mathbf{c}_{g}({g}_{\sharp}(\text{--}))\circ p\mathcal{G}_{g}^{-1}

\displaystyle=i\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}(\Phi_{g}\circ g_{\sharp}(\text{--})),

which shows that
\mathcal{G}_{g}\circ P\circ\mathcal{G}_{g}^{-1}
has not the correct principal symbol, which would be
i\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}({g_{0}}_{\sharp}(\text{--}))
.
Nevertheless,
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)
is a fibre bundle over
\mathrm{Riem}(M)
with fibre
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)_{g}=\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
the affine space of all pseudo Dirac operators on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
.

It is an affine bundle over
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})
because the principal symbol is specified.
It is even a trivial bundle because it has a globally non-vanishing smooth section, which sends a Riemannian metric to its Dirac operator.

Definition 4.1.31
.

A map
P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})
is smooth, if

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(P)\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)\times\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}),

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{g_{P(t)},g_{0}}\circ P(t)\circ\mathcal{G}^{-1}_{g_{P}(t),g_{0}}

is a smooth map into a Fréchet space.
Here
g_{P(t)}
denotes the underlying metric of
P(t)
.

Note that this definition does not depend on the choice of
g_{0}
, because different choices result in conjugation with a fixed, smooth vector bundle isomorphism.

Theorem 4.1.32
.

The Dirac operator defines a smooth section

{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}\colon\mathrm{Riem}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)

in the sense that it maps smooth maps
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
to smooth maps
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Proof.

We need to show that
g\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}\circ\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}=:{\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is a smooth map

\mathrm{Riem}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}).

The topologies of
\mathrm{Riem}(M)
and
\Psi\mathrm{DO}(\mathfrak{S})
are generated by a family of semi-norms defined in in terms of local coordinates.
Thus, it suffices to show in local coordinates that smooth Riemannian metrics are send to smooth amplitudes.
The calculation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{g,g_{0}}p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}\circ\alpha_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}p\mathcal{G}^{-1}_{g,g_{0}}

\displaystyle={p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}+p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\circ\mathbf{c}_{g}(g_{\sharp}(-\alpha_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{d}\alpha_{g,g_{0}})\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}

\displaystyle={p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}-p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\circ\mathbf{c}_{g}(\alpha_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{grad}_{g}(\alpha_{g,g_{0}})\otimes\mathrm{id})\circ p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}

\displaystyle={p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}-\alpha_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}(\Phi_{g,g_{0}}(\mathrm{grad}_{g}(\alpha_{g,g_{0}}))\otimes\mathrm{id})

allows us to conjugate with
p\mathcal{G}
instead because adding a smooth endomorphism does not affect the smoothness of the map.

Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(T^{\vee}M\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(TM\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{c}_{g}}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(T^{\vee}M\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(TM\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}\circ(\Phi_{g,g_{0}}\otimes\mathrm{id})}
\textstyle{\Gamma(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}).}

It is clear that
g\mapsto g_{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}
and
g\mapsto\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}\circ(\Phi_{g,g_{0}}\otimes\mathrm{id})
are induced by smooth maps of vector bundles (because
\tau_{\text{--},g_{0}}
depends smoothly on the first entry).
Since the Fréchet space of smooth bundle endomorphism embeds into the Fréchet space of pseudo differential operators of order zero (equipped with the amplitude topology), see Example
B.0.7
together with Theorem
B.0.17
,
the assignments
g\mapsto g_{\sharp}\otimes\mathrm{id}
and
g\mapsto\mathbf{c}_{g_{0}}\circ(\Phi_{g,g_{0}}\otimes\mathrm{id})
yield smooth maps

\mathrm{Riem}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(T^{\vee}M\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}},TM\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\bigr)

and

\mathrm{Riem}(M)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(TM\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}).

The difference of two connections is an endomorphism-valued
1
-form.
Thus it suffices to show that the map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Riem}(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{1}(M;\mathrm{End}\,\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}),

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}-\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}}=p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}\circ\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}\circ p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}^{-1}-\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}}

is smooth.

First note that the analogous map for the Levi-Cevita connections

\displaystyle\mathrm{Riem}(M)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{1}(M;\mathrm{End}\,TM)

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto{p\mathcal{G}_{g,g_{0}}}_{\ast}\nabla^{L.C.,g}-\nabla^{L.C.,g_{0}}
