\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}
and
\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}
, respectively, then they are equivalent by the following constants:

\displaystyle||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+d\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\partial_{t_{k}}h||_{\mathrm{op}}\right)||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+d\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\partial_{t_{k}}h||_{\mathrm{op}}\right)^{2}||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}}.

Proof.

Using the generalised triangle inequality and Lemma
4.5.9
, we estimate

\displaystyle||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left(||\text{--}||_{0}^{2}+||\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}(\text{--})^{2}||_{0}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(||\text{--}||_{0}^{2}+||\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}(\text{--})+(\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}-\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id})(\text{--})^{2}||_{0}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(||\text{--}||_{0}^{2}+||\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}(\text{--})^{2}||_{0}\right)^{1/2}+||(\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}-\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id})(\text{--})||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}}+||\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}-\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}||_{0,0}\cdot||\text{--}||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+d\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\partial_{t_{k}}h||_{\mathrm{op}}\right)||\text{--}||_{1,\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}}.

The proof of the second estimate is similar.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5.6
.

Let
P\in{B_{n}}
with underlying block map
h\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}(M)
.
Set
g=\mathrm{susp}(h)
.
We will prove the theorem by showing that
\mathrm{susp}(P)^{2}
is invertible.
Recall from Lemma
4.5.5
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{susp}(P)^{2}

\displaystyle=(P^{\mathrm{ex}})^{2}+\{P^{\mathrm{ex}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}\}+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(P^{2})^{\mathrm{ex}}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}\cdot[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{j}^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}]-\Delta+\sum_{1\leq j<k}\mathfrak{R}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k}).

Note that

-\Delta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}_{\partial_{j}})^{2}

is a non-negative operator. The proof is analogous to the one for the Dirac operator presented in
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Proposition II.5.3.
Since
P
is invertible,
P^{2}
is strictly positive.
We have, for each compactly supported smooth section, the following estimate

\displaystyle\langle(P^{\mathrm{ex}})^{2}u,u\rangle

\displaystyle=||P^{\mathrm{ex}}(u)||_{0}^{2}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}||P_{t}u||^{2}_{0,\mathfrak{S}_{g}|_{M_{t}}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u||^{2}_{1,\mathfrak{S}_{g}|_{M_{t}},\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id}}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{4}\int\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u||^{2}_{1,\mathfrak{S}_{g}|_{M_{t}},\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}}\mathrm{d}t.

Here, we used in the first estimate that
H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g}|_{M_{t}},\nabla\otimes\mathrm{id})
is isometric isomorphic to
H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{h(t)},\nabla)\otimes Cl_{n,0}
, so that we can apply
||P_{t}u||_{0}\geq\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u||_{1}
component-wise.
The second estimate follows from the equivalence of norms (Corollary
4.5.10
) and condition (
4.5
) of
{B_{\bullet}}
.

In the following estimate we denote by
||\text{--}||_{1;t}
the Sobolev norm on
\mathfrak{S}_{g}|_{M_{t}}
with respect to
\nabla^{\mathrm{susp}}
:

\displaystyle\left|\langle\sum_{j=1}^{n}[P^{\mathrm{ex}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}]u,u\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle\partial_{j}\cdot[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}_{\partial_{j}}]u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\right|_{0;t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}||\sum_{j}\partial_{j}\cdot[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}_{\partial_{j}}]_{t}u_{t}||_{0;t}||u_{t}||_{0;t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}||\sum_{j}\partial_{j}\cdot[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}_{\partial_{j}}]_{t}||_{1,0;t}||u_{t}||_{1;t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\underbrace{||\partial_{j}\cdot||_{0,0;t}}_{=1}||[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}_{\partial_{j}}]||_{1,0;t}||u_{t}||^{2}_{1;t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle<\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}1/32\cdot\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u_{t}||^{2}_{1;t}\mathrm{d}t.

We estimate the fourth summand with the help of Corollary
4.5.8
in a similar manner and notation as before

\displaystyle|\langle\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\mathfrak{R}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k})(u),u\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|\langle\sum_{j<k}\mathfrak{R}_{t}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k})(u_{t}),u_{t}\rangle|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}||\sum_{j<k}\mathfrak{R}_{t}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k})||_{0,0;t}||u_{t}||_{0;t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}||\sum_{j<k}\mathfrak{R}_{t}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k})||_{\mathrm{op},t}||u_{t}||_{1;t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}d^{2}/2\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}||(\partial_{j}h)^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},t}\right)^{2}||u||_{1;t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle<\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}1/64\cdot 4^{-(d+1)}\cdot d^{2}/2\cdot\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u_{t}||^{2}_{1;t}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle<\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}1/32\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u_{t}||^{2}_{1;t}\mathrm{d}t.

Plugging everything together, we get:

\displaystyle\quad\ |\langle\mathrm{susp}(P)^{2}u,u\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq\langle(P^{\mathrm{ex}})^{2}u,u\rangle-\langle\Delta u,u\rangle-|\langle\{P^{\mathrm{ex}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}\}u,u\rangle|-|\langle\sum_{1\leq j<k\leq n}\mathfrak{R}(\partial_{j},\partial_{k})u,u\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(1/4-2/32)\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}||u_{t}||_{1;t}^{2}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq 3/16\min\{\mathrm{low}(t)^{2}\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\}||u||_{0}^{2},

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle||\mathrm{susp}(P)u||_{0}\geq\sqrt{3}/4\min\{\mathrm{low}(t)\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\}||u||_{0}.

Since
\mathrm{susp}(P)
is a self-adjoint operator, this inequality implies that
\mathrm{susp}(P)
is invertible.
∎

Finally, we wish to show that
{B_{\bullet}}
is a Kan set and that the inclusion is a weak homotopy equivalence.
To this end, we need to know how the anti-commutator behaves under rescaling.

Lemma 4.5.11
.

Let
D\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an open subset of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and let
\varphi\colon D\rightarrow D
and
P\colon D\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}(M)
be a smooth maps.
The map
P
comes with an underlying smooth map
h\colon D\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
.
Then we have

\displaystyle[(P\circ\varphi)^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{t_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi)}}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{t_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h)}}]\circ\varphi\cdot\partial_{t_{i}}\varphi_{k},

\displaystyle\partial_{t_{i}}(h\circ\varphi)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\partial_{t_{k}}h)\circ\varphi\cdot\partial_{t_{i}}\varphi_{k}.

Proof.

The second equation is simply the chain rule for differentials, so it remains to prove the first equation.
Let
g_{00}\colon D\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
be the constant map with value
g_{00}
.
Set further
g_{0}=\mathrm{susp}(g_{00})
.
Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.5.2
the identity

p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h),g_{0})_{\ast}(P^{\mathrm{ex}})=(p\mathcal{G}(h,g_{00})_{\ast}P)^{\mathrm{ex}}.

In the following, we will abbreviate
p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h),g_{0})
to
p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h))
and
p\mathcal{G}(h,g_{00})
to
p\mathcal{G}(h)
.
Using Lemma
4.5.1
, we calculate

\displaystyle\qquad p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi))_{\ast}\left([(P\circ\varphi)^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{t_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi)}}]\right)

\displaystyle=[(p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi))_{\ast}(P\circ\varphi))^{\mathrm{ex}},(p\mathcal{G}(h\circ\varphi)_{\ast}\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi)}})_{\partial_{t_{i}}}]

\displaystyle=[(p\mathcal{G}(h\circ\varphi)_{\ast}(P\circ\varphi))^{\mathrm{ex}},\partial_{t_{i}}]=[\bigl((p\mathcal{G}(h)_{\ast}(P))\circ\varphi\bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}},\partial_{t_{i}}]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{rule}}{\overset{\mathrm{chain}}{=}}\Bigl(\partial_{t_{i}}\bigl((p\mathcal{G}(h)_{\ast}P)\circ\varphi\bigr)\Bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}((\partial_{t_{k}}p\mathcal{G}(h)_{\ast}P)\circ\varphi)^{\mathrm{ex}}\cdot\partial_{t_{i}}\varphi_{k}.

A similar calculation shows

\displaystyle p\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{susp}(h\circ\varphi))_{\ast}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}([P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{t_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}h}}])\circ\varphi\cdot\partial_{t_{i}}\varphi_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}((\partial_{t_{k}}p\mathcal{G}(h)_{\ast}P)\circ\varphi)^{\mathrm{ex}}\cdot\partial_{t_{i}}\varphi_{k},

so the lemma follows.
∎

Proposition 4.5.12
.

The cubical set
{B_{\bullet}}
is a Kan set.

The rough idea of the proof is the following one:
A given cubical
n
-horn
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
gives rise to a well defined map
P_{0}
on the complement of an infinte half-cylinder
Q_{K}
with base
K
, where
K
is a compact, convex set with smooth boundary.
By the block form of the operator-valued maps in the given horn, the operator-valued map
P_{0}
is the elongation of
P_{0}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}<S\}}
for some sufficiently large
S
.
We need to "extend"
P_{0}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}<S\}}
to
Q_{K}
and we do this by modifying
P_{0}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}<S\}}
on a compact set of
\{\varepsilon x_{i}\leq S\}
.
More precisely, we do this by pushing down the argument of
P_{0}
in
\varepsilon x_{i}
-direction as closer we get to
Q_{K}
so that we can extend the modification to
Q_{K}
by
P_{(i,-\varepsilon)}\circ p_{i}
.

Proof.

Given an arbitrary cubical
n
-horn

\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}=\{P_{(j,\omega)}\in{B_{n-1}}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}P_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}P_{(j,\omega)};\,j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\}.

Since that horn contains only finitely many block maps, there is a sufficiently large
\rho>0
such that all block maps
P_{(j,\omega)}
decompose outside of
\rho I^{n-1}
.
We restrict
\sigma_{j}P_{(j,\omega)}
to a map
\{\omega x_{j}>\rho\}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
.
By the compatibility requirement, these restrictions agree on the intersection of their domains and we can therefore glue them together to a map

P_{0}\colon\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\right)^{c}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M),

where
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,x_{j}\in[-\rho,\rho]\text{ if }j\neq i,\,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho\}
is the cuboid with diameter
\rho
.
Let
K\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
be a compact, convex subset that contains
\rho I^{n-1}
, that is point symmetric at the origin, and that has a smooth boundary.
Let
Q_{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,p_{i}(x)\in K,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho\}
be the cuboid with base
K
.
Restricting
P_{0}
yields

P_{0}\colon Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M),

for a fixed choice of
R_{1}>1
.

For the chosen
K
, there is a unique norm
||\text{--}||_{K}
whose unit ball is
K
.
Since
\partial K
is smooth, the norm is smooth on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}
.
For a sufficiently large
R_{2}=R_{2}(\rho,R_{1})
to be determined later, pick
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1.2}
, vanishes on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq R_{2}-1}
, and whose first and second derivative satisfy
|\chi^{\prime}|\leq 1.3/R_{2}
and
|\chi^{\prime\prime}|\leq 10/R_{2}^{2}
.
Furthermore, pick a monotonically increasing function
q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
that is the identity on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho}
, the constant map with value
\rho+R_{1}-1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\rho+R_{1}}
, and whose derivatives satisfy
q^{\prime}\leq 1
and
|q^{\prime\prime}|\leq 1.2/R_{1}
.
The map
q
defines a map

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\varepsilon q(\varepsilon x_{i}),x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}).

Finally, we define

\displaystyle H\colon\left(Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\}\right)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\},

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})te_{i}

and set

\displaystyle P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}P_{0}\circ H\bigl(q(x),\chi(||p_{i}(x)||_{K})\bigr),&\text{if }x\in Q_{K}^{c},\\
P_{(i,-\varepsilon)}(p_{i}(x)),&\text{if }x\in Q_{K}.\end{cases}

Note that
P
is well defined and smooth because
P_{0}
agrees with
P_{(i,-\varepsilon)}\circ p_{i}
on the set
\{-\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
and
\partial Q_{K}
lies in the preimage of
\{-\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
under the map
H\circ(q(\text{--}),\chi(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K}))
.

The map
P
is a block map.
Indeed,
\chi(||p_{i}(x)||_{K})=0
on
\{\omega x_{j}>R_{2}\}
for all
j\neq i
, which yields

P(x)=P_{0}(H(q(x),0))=P_{0}(q(x))=P_{0}(x)=P_{(j,\omega)}(p_{j}(x))

because
P_{(j,\omega)(p_{j}(\text{--}))}
is independent of
x_{i}
if
|x_{i}|>\rho
.
For all
x
with
-\varepsilon x_{i}>R_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle P(x)

\displaystyle=P_{0}(q(x)-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi(||p_{i}(x)||_{K})e_{i})

\displaystyle=P_{0}(x-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi(||p_{i}(x)||_{K})e_{i})

\displaystyle=P_{(i,-\varepsilon)}(p_{i}(x))

because, in this case,
-\varepsilon x_{i}+2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})t>R_{2}
for every
t\geq 0
.
Lastly, for all
x
with
\varepsilon x_{i}>R_{2}>\rho+R_{1}
we have

P(x)=P(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1}-1),x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n})

by the definition of
q
.

The proof also shows that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P=P_{(j,\omega)}
for all
(j,\omega)\neq(i,\varepsilon)
.
This means that
P
is a filler for
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
in
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{n}(M)
.

It remains to show that
P
lies in
B_{n}
.
It is clear that
P
satisfies the defining conditions of
B_{n}
in an open neighbourhood of
Q_{K}
, for example on
\chi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{<1.2})
.
We will show that
P
satisfies the defining conditions of
B_{n}
on
\chi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>1.1})
, too.
Clearly, we have

\mathrm{low}_{P}(t)=\mathrm{low}_{P_{0}}(H(q(t),\chi(||p_{i}(t)||_{K})).

Actually, this equation holds for every smooth self map
\varphi\colon Q_{K}^{c}\rightarrow Q_{K}^{c}
.
For every such self map
\varphi
and each smooth map
Q\colon Q_{K}^{c}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
the underlying map of Riemannian metrics
h_{Q}
satisfies
h_{Q\circ\varphi}=h_{Q}\circ\varphi
.
By Lemma
4.5.11
, we find the following upper bound for its derivatives

\displaystyle||(\partial_{j}h_{Q\circ\varphi})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{Q\circ\varphi}}

\displaystyle=||\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\partial_{k}h_{Q})^{\mathrm{op}}\circ\varphi\cdot\partial_{j}\varphi_{k}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{Q\circ\varphi}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum|\partial_{j}\varphi_{k}|\cdot||(\partial_{k}h_{Q})^{\mathrm{op}}\circ\varphi||_{\mathrm{op},h\circ\varphi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\partial_{j}\varphi_{k}|\cdot||(\partial_{k}h_{Q})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h}\circ\varphi.

We would like to apply this estimate to
Q=P_{0}
and
\varphi=H\circ(q,\chi(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K})
.
For
j\neq i
, the derivatives of
\varphi_{k}
are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{j}\varphi_{k}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }k\notin\{i,j\},\\
1,&\text{if }k=j,\\
-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\cdot(\partial_{j}||\text{--}||_{K})\circ p_{i},&\text{if }j<i,k=i,\\
-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\cdot(\partial_{j-1}||\text{--}||_{K})\circ p_{i},&\text{if }j>i,k=i.\end{cases}

For
j=i
, they are given by

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\varphi_{k}=\begin{cases}q^{\prime}(\varepsilon\cdot\text{--}),&\text{if }k=i,\\
0,&\text{if }k\neq i.\end{cases}

Plugging these results into the previous estimate yields

\displaystyle||(\partial_{j}h_{P})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P}}\leq\begin{cases}||(\partial_{j}h_{P_{0}})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},P_{0}}\circ\varphi+|\partial_{j}\varphi_{i}|\cdot||(\partial_{t_{i}}h)^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P_{0}}}\circ\varphi,&\text{if }j\neq i,\\
q^{\prime}(\varepsilon\cdot\text{--})\cdot||(\partial_{t_{i}}h_{P_{0}})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P_{0}}}\circ\varphi,&\text{if }j=i,\end{cases}

which gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{j=1}^{n}||(\partial_{j}h_{P})^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P}}\leq&\sum_{j=1}^{n}||\partial_{j}h_{P_{0}}^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P_{0}}}\circ\varphi\\
&+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{2(\rho+R_{1})\cdot 1.3}{R_{2}}\cdot|\partial_{j}||\text{--}||_{K}|\circ p_{i}\cdot||\partial_{i}h_{P_{0}}^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P_{0}}}\right)\circ\varphi.\end{split}

Applying Lemma
4.5.11
to
[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{j}}]
we obtain, by a similar calculation, the estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}||[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{j}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h_{P})}}]||_{1,0,h_{P}}\\
&\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}||[P_{0}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{j}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h_{P_{0}})}}]||_{1,0,h_{P_{0}}}\circ\varphi\\
&\quad\ +\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{2.6(\rho+R_{1})}{R_{2}}|\partial_{j}||\text{--}||_{K}|\circ p_{i}\cdot||[P_{0}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{i}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(h_{P_{0}})}}]||_{1,0,h_{P_{0}}}\right)\circ\varphi.\end{split}

Since
\partial_{i}h^{\mathrm{op}}_{P}
and
[P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{i}}]
have smooth extensions from
\chi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>1.1})
to
\chi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1.05})
their norms are uniformly bounded by
B_{1}
, say.
Also all
|\partial_{j}||\text{--}||_{K}|
are uniformly bounded by
B_{2}
, say.
Thus, if we choose
R_{2}>2.6(\rho+R_{1})B_{1}B_{2}\mathcal{I}^{-1}
, where
\mathcal{I}=\min\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}\}
is the minimum of

\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{inf}\left\{\frac{1}{32}\,\mathrm{low}_{P_{0}}(t)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}||[P_{0}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{j}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}h_{P_{0}}}}]||_{1,0,h_{P_{0}}}\,:\,t\in Q_{K}^{c}\right\}

and

\mathcal{I}_{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left\{\mathrm{min}\{d^{-1},2^{-(d+4)}\mathrm{low}(t)\}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\partial_{k}h^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},h_{P_{0}}(t)}\,:\,t\in Q_{K}^{c}\right\}

then
P
satisfies the defining equations of
{B_{\bullet}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{I}>0
because
P_{0}|_{Q_{K}^{c}}
is a union of restriction of degenerate elements of
B_{n}
and the defining conditions of
B_{n}
are local.

Thus,
P\in{B_{n}}
is a filler for the given cubical
n
-horn and
{B_{\bullet}}
is therefore a Kan set.
∎

Corollary 4.5.13
.

The inclusion
{B_{\bullet}}\hookrightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

We first prove that the inclusion induces a surjection on homotopy groups.
Let
[P]\in\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M),P_{0})
be represented by a smooth block map
P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)
that is constant with value
P_{0}
outside some cube
rI^{n}
.
For each
\rho\geq 1
the rescaled map
{}_{\rho}P(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P(\rho^{-1}x)
is still constant near infinity with value
P_{0}
and thus represents an element in
\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
, too.
By Lemma
4.5.11
there is a
\rho_{0}\geq 1
such that
{}_{\rho}P\in\pi_{n}({B_{\bullet}})
for all
\rho\geq\rho_{0}
because the left hand side of the defining inequalities for
{B_{\bullet}}
rescale with
1/\rho
, while the right hand side is scaling invariant.

We need to show
{}_{\rho}P
and
P={}_{1}P
are homotopic relative boundary.
A homotopy
H\in\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{n+1}(M)
relating
{}_{\rho}P
and
{}_{1}P
is given by

H(t,x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={}_{(1-\chi(t))\rho+\chi(t)}P(x),

where
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
is a smooth function that is identically zero on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}
and identically
1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
.
Indeed,
H
satisfies

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}_{1}H(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}H(-R,x)={}_{\rho}P(x),

\displaystyle\partial^{1}_{1}H(x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}H(R,x)={}_{1}P(x),

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}H(t,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}H(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\varepsilon R,x_{i},\dots,x_{n-1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}P\left(((1-\chi(t))\rho+\chi(t))^{-1}\cdot(x_{1},\dots,\varepsilon R,\dots,x_{n-1})\right)

\displaystyle=P_{0}=\sigma_{1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-1}P(t,x).

To prove injectivity, we follow the common strategy that uniqueness is just a relative form of existence.
For each
[P]\in\pi_{n}({B_{\bullet}},P_{0})
with
0=[P]\in\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M),P_{0})
there exists an element
H\in\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{n+1}(M)
that satisfies

\displaystyle\partial^{1}_{1}H

\displaystyle=P_{0},

\displaystyle\partial^{-1}_{1}H

\displaystyle=P,

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{j}H

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{j-1}P=P_{0}.

As before, there is a sufficiently large
\rho\geq 1
such that
{}_{\rho}H\in{B_{n+1}}
.
Note that
{}_{\rho}H
is a homotopy between
{}_{\rho}P
and
P_{0}
in
{B_{\bullet}}
, so it remains to find a homotopy between
P
and
{}_{\rho}P
in
{B_{\bullet}}
.
An example for such a homotopy is
\mathcal{H}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sigma_{1}P\circ{}_{R}\varphi
, where

\displaystyle{}_{R}\varphi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

\displaystyle(t,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(t,((1-\chi(R^{-1}t)\rho+\chi(R^{-1}t))^{-1}x\right),

provided
R
is sufficiently large.
Abbreviate
(1-\chi(t))\rho+\chi(t)
to
\mu(t)
and set
{}_{R}\mu(t)=\mu(R^{-1}t)
.
Furthermore, abbreviate
(t,x)
to
y
so that
t=y_{1}
and
x_{i}=y_{i+1}
.
Lemma
4.5.11
, applied to
y_{1}=t
yields

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{1}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{\mathcal{H}})}}\right](y)

\displaystyle=\left[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}}\circ{}_{R}\varphi,\nabla_{\partial_{y_{1}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P}\circ{}_{R}\varphi)}}\right](y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P})}}]\circ{}_{R}\varphi\cdot\partial_{y_{1}}{}_{R}\varphi_{k}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{R}\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P})}}]\circ{}_{R}\varphi\cdot{}_{R}\mu^{-2}\cdot((\rho-1)\cdot{}_{R}(\chi^{\prime}))y_{k}

where we used
[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P})}}_{\partial_{y_{1}}}]=[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\partial_{y_{1}}]=(\partial_{y_{1}}\sigma_{1}P)^{\mathrm{ex}}=0
.
Applied to
y_{i}=x_{i-1}
for
i>1
, Lemma
4.5.11
yields

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{\mathcal{H}})}}\right](y)=\dots

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P})}}]\circ{}_{R}\varphi\cdot{}_{R}\mu^{-1}\delta_{ik}

\displaystyle={}_{R}\mu^{-1}\cdot[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{i}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}g_{P})}}]\circ{}_{R}\varphi

\displaystyle={}_{R}\mu^{-1}\cdot\sigma_{1}\left([P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{x_{i-1}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P})}}]\right)\circ{}_{R}\varphi.

We assumed that
P=P_{0}
outside of
rI^{n}
, so
[\sigma_{1}P^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{k}}}]\circ{}_{R}\varphi
is supported within
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\times\rho rI^{n}
for
k>1
and therefore
it is uniformly bounded.
Thus, we can choose
R
sufficiently large such that
[\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{t}}]
is arbitrarily small.
Since
\mathrm{low}_{\mathcal{H}}=\mathrm{low}_{\sigma_{1}P}\circ{}_{R}\varphi=\mathrm{low}_{P}\circ p_{1}\circ{}_{R}\varphi=\sigma_{1}(\mathrm{low}_{P})\circ{}_{R}\varphi
and since
{}_{R}\mu^{-1}\leq 1
, we have

\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\left|\left|\left[\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{y_{k}}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{\mathcal{H}})}}\right](y)\right|\right|_{1,0,M_{y}}<1/32\mathrm{low}_{\mathcal{H}}(y)^{2},

so we can choose
R>1
so large that
\mathcal{H}
satisfies the second defining inequality (
4.6
) of
{B_{\bullet}}
.

The same strategy shows that the underlying map of metrics
g_{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies the first defining inequality (
4.5
) after enlarging
R
, if necessary.
∎

4.6
Kan Property of the Operator Concordance Set

The main theorem of this section is the analogous result of Theorem
3.3.1
for invertible block Dirac operators.

Theorem 4.6.1
.

The operator concordance set
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan set for all closed, spin manifolds
M
that admit at least one invertible pseudo Dirac operator.

The proof of this theorem relies on an operator theoretic analog of Lemma
3.3.2
.

Lemma 4.6.2

(Operator Modification Lemma)
.

Let
P
be a block Dirac operator on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
with underlying block metric
g
.
Assume that the core of
P
is contained in
M\times\rho/2\cdot I^{n}
for
\rho>20
(in particular,
g
decomposes on the complement).
Assume further that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P
is invertible if
(j,\omega)\neq(1,1)
.
Let
H
and
\Phi
as in Theorem
3.2.2
and denote
{}_{R}\Phi\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=R\Phi\circ(\mathrm{id}\times R^{-1})
.
Then

(o)

the operator
P
restricts to
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
for all
1\leq a<b\leq 2
and all
R\geq 1
.

For all
R\geq 1
, there is a Riemannian metric
G_{R}
on
M\times R\Phi(H\times[1,2])
, a compact subset
K=K(R)
of
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, a relative compact, open subset
L=L(R)\supseteq K
of
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and a pseudo Dirac operator
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Q(R)
on
\Gamma_{c}(R\Phi(H\times(1,2)),\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}})
that satisfy:

(i)

The operator
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
restricts to
M\times H\times(a,b)
for all
R\leq a<b\leq 2R
such that
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
restricts to
{}_{R}\Phi^{-1}(L)
and to the complement of
{}_{R}\Phi^{-1}(K)
.

(ii)

The restriction of
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
and
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}P
to
M\times H\times(1.8R,2R)
and to the complement of
K
agree.

(iii)

The metric
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}
is a product metric on
M\times H\times[R,1.2R]
.
Under the decomposition
\mathfrak{S}_{{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}}|_{M\times H\times[R,1.2R]}\cong\mathfrak{S}_{{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times H\times{1.1R}}\boxtimes Cl_{1,0}
, the operator
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
decomposes into

{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q={}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{{M\times H\times{1.1R}}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}.

(iv)

There is an
R_{0}\geq 1
and a constant
c>0
, such that
Q
is bounded from below with lower bound
\geq c
for all
R\geq R_{0}
.

(v)

If
P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}
is bounded, then the operator
Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}}
is bounded.

Proof.

We start with the proof of
(o)
.
The block form of
P
implies that it restricts to
V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})
, see Lemma
4.2.6
, and that
P|_{V_{R\rho({\bm{\alpha}})}}
can be identified with
P(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}
via a permutation of coordinates.
The same permutation of coordinates gives a diffeomorphism between
R\Phi(H\times(1,2))\cap V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})
and
(-R\rho,R\rho)^{|\mathrm{Null\,{\bm{\alpha}}}|}\times R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}(H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\times(1,2))
.
The first factor contains the core of
P(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})
.
On the second factor,
P
acts as the differential operator
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}
.
Thus,
P
restricts to
M\times V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\cap R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
for all
1\leq a<b\leq 2
.

As
\{V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\,:\,{\bm{\alpha}}\neq\mathbf{0},(1,0\dots,0)\}
covers
R\Phi(H\times(1,2))
, the operator
P
restricts to a symmetric operator on
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
.

The same strategy shows that
P
also restricts to a symmetric operator on the complement of
V_{R\rho}(\mathbf{0})
.
This proves
(o)
.

For all
R\geq 1
, let
G_{R}
be the metric from the Metric Modification Lemma
3.3.2
.
Define the operator
Q
on
\Gamma_{c}(M\times R\Phi(H\times(1,2)),\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}})
via

Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Q(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}(P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}})+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}}.

Since
Q
is obtained from
P
by pushing it forward with a vector bundle map and adding a differential operator to
P
, we conclude
Q\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}})
.
The operator
Q
also restricts to
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
for all
1\leq a<b\leq 2
, to the intersection of
L(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=V_{R(\rho+5)}(\mathbf{0})
and
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
, and to the complement of
K(R)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\overline{V_{R(\rho+4)}(\mathbf{0})}
in
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
, which proves
(i)
.

The gauge map
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})
is an isometry between spaces of square integrable spinors, so
Q
is symmetric.
We will calculate below that the principal symbol of
Q
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}}
agree, thus
Q
is a symmetric pseudo Dirac operator.

To prove
(ii)
, recall that
g_{P}
and
G_{R}
agree on
M\times R\Phi(H\times(1.8,2))
, so that
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})=\mathrm{id}
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}}
on this subset.
Thus,
P=Q
on this subset.
The same is true on the complement of
K(R)=\overline{V_{R(\rho+4)}(\mathbf{0})}
.

Next we prove
(iii)
.
Consider the restriction of
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
to
{}_{R}\Phi^{-1}(V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}))
.
Recall from the Metric Modification Lemma
3.3.2
that, on these subsets, the metrics decompose into

\displaystyle{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}g_{P}=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus({}_{R}\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}=R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g\oplus k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}.

Thus, the gauge map
\mathcal{G}({}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}g_{P},{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R})
decomposes into
\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\mathcal{G}(({}_{R}\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})
.
Since the gauge map is natural with respect to linear maps, we get

\displaystyle{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}({}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}g_{P},{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle)_{\ast}({}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}g_{P}})+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\mathcal{G}(({}_{R}\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}\bigl(P(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{({}_{R}\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}-\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{({}_{R}\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\bigr)\end{split}\\
&\quad+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{\ast}\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}\end{split}

\displaystyle=P(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}.

Since
k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}=k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{H\times 1.1R}\oplus\mathrm{d}t^{2}
on
H^{{\bm{\alpha}}}\times(R,1.2R)
, the Dirac operator
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}
decomposes accordingly on this set, hence
Q
does, too.
These decompositions are compatible on the intersections of different
V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})
, so we get a global decomposition

{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q={}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times H\times 1.1R}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}},

which proves
(iii)
.

We can read off the principal symbol of
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q
from the (local) decompositions.
On
{}_{R}\Phi^{-1}(V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}))
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{symb}_{1}({}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}Q)

\displaystyle=i\mathbf{c}_{\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}(\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g_{\sharp}(\text{--}))\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes i\mathbf{c}_{k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}({k_{R}^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}_{\sharp}(\text{--}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{symb}_{1}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}G_{R}}),

so
Q
also has the same principal symbol as
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}}
.

We continue with the proof of
(iv)
.
Since the gauge map
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})
induces an isometry between
H^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P}})
and
H^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}})
, the operator
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}(P)
is bounded from below with the same lower bound as
P
.
Being bounded from below is an open condition for elliptic operators in
\mathrm{Hom}(H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}}),H^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}}))
,
see Lemma
4.3.13
, so it suffices to show that
Q
and
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}P
are sufficient close.

The difference of these two operators is a differential operator that is supported within
M\times R\Phi(H\times[1,1.8])
.

The previous calculation shows that

\displaystyle(R\Phi)^{\ast}(Q-\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}(P))=\left(\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}-\mathcal{G}((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{(R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\right)

on
R\Phi^{-1}(V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}))
, which we abbreviate to
\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes T
.
Recall that
R\Phi^{-1}(V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}}))=M\times(-\rho,\rho)^{\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})}\times H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\times(1,2)
for
{\bm{\alpha}}\neq\mathbf{0},(1,0\dots,0)
.
Hence, for each
\sigma\in\Gamma_{c}(R\Phi^{-1}(V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})),\mathfrak{S}_{\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}\boxtimes\mathfrak{S}_{R^{2}k^{{\bm{\alpha}}}})
, using Fubini’s theorem we have

\displaystyle\qquad||\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes T(\sigma)||_{0}^{2}=\langle\mathrm{id}\boxtimes T^{2}(\sigma),\sigma\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{M\times(-R\rho,R\rho)^{|\mathrm{Null}({\bm{\alpha}})|}}\int_{H^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\times(1,2)}||\mathrm{id}\boxtimes T^{2}\sigma(t,x)||^{2}_{\mathfrak{S}_{\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}\boxtimes\mathfrak{S}_{R^{2}k^{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\int\int||T||_{1,0}^{2}\left(||\sigma(t,x)||^{2}+||\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}}\sigma(t,x)||^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}(x)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}(t)

\displaystyle\leq||T||_{1,0}^{2}||\sigma||_{1}^{2},

where the first inequality is the operator norm inequality applied to the section
\sigma(t,\text{--})
.
This gives
||\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes T||_{1,0}\leq||T||_{1,0}
.

It follows from
[
hijazi1986conformal
]
*Prop 4.3.1 and 4.2.1 that

\mathcal{G}(R^{2}g,g)_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{2}g}=R^{-1}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{G}(R^{2}g,g)_{\ast}\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{R^{2}g}}=\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g}}

for all Riemannian metrics
g
and all values
R>0
.
The latter equation implies that the Gauge map induces an isometry between all Sobolev spaces.
Furthermore, we have
\mathcal{G}\bigl(R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}},k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{G}\bigl((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\bigr)=\mathcal{G}\bigl((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}\bigr)
.
Putting these facts together yields

\displaystyle||T||_{1,0}

\displaystyle=||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}-\mathcal{G}((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{(R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}||_{1,0}

\displaystyle=||\mathcal{G}(R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}},k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}\left({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}-\mathcal{G}((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,R^{2}k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{(R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\right)||_{1,0}

\displaystyle=||R^{-1}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}-\mathcal{G}((R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{(R\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})||_{1,0}

\displaystyle=||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}-\mathcal{G}({\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,k^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}})_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{{\Phi^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})||_{1,0}/R\xrightarrow{R\to\infty}0.

It follows that
Q
and
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}(P)
are arbitrarily close if
R
is sufficient large.
Together with Lemma
4.3.13
, we find an
R_{0}\geq 1
such that for all
R\geq R_{0}
, the operator
Q
is bounded from below with lower bound
c>0
, that only depends on the lower bound of
P
and some chosen upper bound of the distance of
Q
to
\mathcal{G}(g_{P},G_{R})_{\ast}(P)
.
In fact, the proof of Lemma
4.3.13
shows, that the constant
c
can be made arbitrarily close to the lower bound of
P
at the cost of increasing
R_{0}
.

It remains to prove
(v)
.
On
V_{R\rho}({\bm{\alpha}})\cap M\times R\Phi(H\times(1,2))
, we have

\displaystyle{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}(Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}})=(P(\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}})-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\partial^{\hat{{\bm{\alpha}}}}g})\boxtimes\mathrm{id},

which is bounded by assumption of
P
.
Hence,
{}_{R}\Phi^{\ast}(Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{G_{R}})
is bounded by a partition of unity argument.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6.1
.

By assumption,
M
has at least one invertible pseudo Dirac operator so that
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{0}(M)
(and hence
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
) is non-empty.
A given cubical
n
-horn
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is represented by the following set of invertible block Dirac operators

\{P_{(j,\omega)}\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n-1}(M)\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}P_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}P_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\}.

The problem is symmetric in
(i,\varepsilon)
, so we assume
(i,\varepsilon)=(1,1)
.

We pick a sufficient large
\rho>20
such that the cores of all
P_{(j,\omega)}
are contained in
M\times\rho/2I^{n-1}
.
The operators
\sigma_{j}P_{(j,\omega)}|_{\{\omega x_{j}>\rho\}}
restrict to each
\{\eta x_{k}>\rho\}
if the intersection is not empty and the restrictions agree there, see Lemma
4.4.12
.
Thus, the union of these operators form an operator
P
on the complement of the half-infinite cuboid
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho}}=\bigcup_{((j,\omega)\neq(i,\varepsilon))}\{\omega x_{j}>\rho\}
that restricts to
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho^{\prime}}}
for all
\rho^{\prime}>\rho
.

Applying Corollary
4.3.22
inductively, we conclude that the restriction of
P
to
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho^{\prime}}}
is bounded from below for all sufficient large
\rho^{\prime}
.

To meet the assumptions of the Operator Modification Lemma
4.6.2
, we extend
P
to a block Dirac operator
\mathtt{P}
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
By increasing
\rho
if necessary, we may assume that the core of
\mathtt{P}
is contained in
M\times\rho/2I^{n}
.

Let
H
,
\Phi
, and
U_{left}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho}}
be as in the Embedding Theorem
3.2.2
.
By the Operator Modification Lemma
4.6.2
,
\mathtt{P}
restricts to
P
on
M\times R\Phi(H\times(a,b))
for all
1\leq a<b\leq 2
and acts like a differential operator on the cylinder variable; more precisely, it is a
{(R\Phi)^{-1}}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{\ast}\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(a,b)
-derivation.
Thus,
\mathtt{P}
also restricts to
P
on
M\times\bigl(U_{left}\cup R\Phi(H\times(1.8,2))\bigr)
.

By the Operator Modification Lemma
4.6.2
, there is a
Q=Q(R)
on
\Gamma_{c}(M\times R\Phi(H\times(1,2);\mathfrak{S}_{G_{R}})
for all
R\geq 1
that agrees with
P
on
M\times R\Phi(H\times(1.8,2))
and decomposes on
M\times R\Phi(H\times(1,1.2))
as follows

\Phi_{R}^{\ast}Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Phi_{R}^{\ast}Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times H\times 1.1R}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}.

Let
{}_{R}\Theta
and
{}_{R}\Psi_{n}
be as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.1
.
To ease the notation, we abbreviate
\mathrm{id}\times{}_{R}\Theta
and
\mathrm{id}\times{}_{R}\Psi_{n}
to
{}_{R}\Theta
and
{}_{R}\Psi_{n}
, respectively.

The operator
({}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n})_{\ast}(Q)
is again a symmetric pseudo Dirac operator.
We chose
{}_{R}\Theta
in the proof of Theorem
3.3.1
such that
{}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n}\circ{}_{R}\Phi(m,h,s)=(m,R\Phi(h,1)-(s-R)e_{1})
on
M\times H\times R[1,1+\epsilon))
for all
1/10>\epsilon>0
.
Since
{}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n}\circ{}_{R}\Phi(H\times R[1,1+\epsilon))=\{R(\rho+1-\epsilon)<x_{1}\leq R(\rho+1)\}
, we obtain the decomposition

({}_{R}\Theta{}_{R}\Psi_{n})_{\ast}(Q)=Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=R(\rho+1)\}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}

on
\{R(\rho+1-\epsilon)<x_{1}\leq R(\rho+1)\}
.

By
(iv)
of the Operator Modification Lemma
4.6.2
, the operator
Q
and hence
({}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n})_{\ast}(P)
is bounded from below with lower bound
c>0
that is independent of
R
, provided
R
is sufficiently large.
Lemma
4.4.9
implies that the restriction
Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=R(\rho+1)\}}
is bounded from below with lower bound bigger than
c/2
, for all sufficient large
R
.
Thus, the constant extension of
Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=R(\rho+1)\}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}
to
\{R(\rho+1-\epsilon)<x_{1}\}
is bounded from below with lower bound bigger than
c/2
.

It follow from Corollary
4.3.22
and Lemma
4.3.20
, that

\overline{Q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=({}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n})_{\ast}(Q)\cup Q\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=R(\rho+1)\}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}

is a pseudo Dirac operator on
M\times\bigl({}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n}\circ{}_{R}\Phi(H\times R(1,2))\cup\{R(\rho+1-\epsilon)<x_{1}\}\bigr)
that is symmetric and bounded from below, provided
R
is sufficiently large.

Of course,
\overline{Q}
restricts to
M\times R\Phi(H\times(1.8,2))
and agrees there with
P
.
Applying Corollary
4.3.22
and
4.3.20
a second time, the operator
P_{\mathrm{fill}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P\cup\overline{Q}
is a symmetric pseudo Dirac operator on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that is bounded from below, provided
R
is sufficiently large.

It remains to show that
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
is a block Dirac operator.
Let
\mathtt{R}_{1}=\mathtt{R}_{1}(R)>R(\rho+1)
be sufficiently large such that
M\times\mathtt{R}_{1}I^{n}
contains
L(R)
and
{}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n}(L(R))
and such that
{}_{R}\Theta
and
{}_{R}\Psi_{n}
restrict to the identity on the complement of
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\mathtt{R}_{1}}}
.
Then
M\times\mathtt{R}_{1}I^{n}
serves as a core for
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
.
Since
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
restricts to
P
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\mathtt{R}_{1}}}
and since, by construction,

P_{\{x_{1}>R(\rho+1)\}}=({}_{R}\Theta\circ{}_{R}\Psi_{n})_{\ast}(Q)\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{\{x_{1}=R(\rho+1)\}}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}},

we deduce that
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
has block form.
Finally,
P_{\mathrm{fill}}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P_{\mathrm{fill}}}}
is bounded, because it is true for all the three summands whose union forms
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
.

Thus,
P_{\mathrm{fill}}
a filler for the given
n
-horn, so
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan set.
∎

4.7
The Symbol Map is a Kan Fibration

Recall that every pseudo Dirac operator
P
comes with an underlying Riemannian metric
g_{P}
that determines the principal symbol of
P
(and also determines the spinor bundle on which
P
acts).
We demand that
g_{P}
is a block metric and that the decompositions of
P
and
g_{P}
are compatible.
In Section
4.4
we have established that the map that assigns to a pseudo Dirac operator its underlying metric assembles to a cubical map
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
which we refer to as the symbol map.

We use the Kan property of
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
from the previous subsection to establish the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.7.1
.

The symbol map
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan fibration.

This result is remarkable because it implies that all index theoretic properties are stored in a single fibre
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
because
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
is combinatorially contractible, see Proposition
2.1.33
.
The fibre
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
is a Kan set by formal reasons, see Lemma
2.1.23
.
When we are determining the homotopy groups of
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
, we will make explicit use of the Kan property of the fibre
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
, because we will compare the fibre to the space of Fredholm operators on a
single
Hilbert space determined by
g_{0}
.

Of course, Theorem
4.7.1
implies Theorem
4.6.1
by formal reasons.
However, the proof of Theorem
4.7.1
relies on
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
being a Kan set.
It goes roughly as follows:
For a given
i
-horn of invertible block Dirac operator and a given block metric, we find a filler by the Kan property of
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
.
So far, the underlying metric may disagree with the given one.
To correct this mismatch, we will modify the filler so that they do agree.

The modification procedure will rely on two tools.
The first tool is a fibration-kind result inspired from the result of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Section 4.2 although the methods are quite different.
It allows us to “rotate” the principal symbol of the filler to the required one.
The second tool is based on the gluing constructions from Section
4.3
.

The first tool is based on the following analytical result and its consequences.

Lemma 4.7.2
.

Let
P\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)
such that all faces
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P
are invertible.
Then there are constants
c,\texttt{C}>0
, each depending only on the faces
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P
, and a compact subset
K\subseteq M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, such that all sections
0\neq u\in L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P}})
with
||Pu||_{0}\leq\gamma||u||_{0}
for
\gamma<c
satisfy

\frac{||u||_{0,K}}{||u||_{0}}\geq\frac{10}{11}\left(\frac{9}{10}-\mathtt{C}\gamma\right)>0,

(4.7)

where
||u||_{0,K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=||1_{K}\cdot u||_{0}
.

Proof.

For this proof, it will be more convenient to use the following variant of the Sobolev
1
-norm

||u||_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=||u||_{0}+||\nabla u||_{0},

which is equivalent to the (usual) Sobolev 1-norm.
More generally, for each measurable
K\subset M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
we will denote, just for this proof,

||u||_{1,K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=||u||_{0,K}+||\nabla u||_{0,K}
.
For each compactly supported smooth function
\varphi
, we can estimate
||\varphi u||_{1}
against
||u||_{1,\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi}
as follows:

\displaystyle||\varphi u||_{1}

\displaystyle=||\varphi u||_{0}+||\nabla\varphi u||_{0}

\displaystyle=||\varphi u||_{0}+||\mathrm{d}\varphi\otimes u+\varphi\nabla u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||\varphi u||_{0}+||\mathrm{grad}(\varphi)||_{\infty}\cdot||u||_{0,\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi}+||\varphi\nabla u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||\varphi||_{\infty}||u||_{1,\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi}+||\mathrm{grad}(\varphi)||_{\infty}\cdot||u||_{0,\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi}.

Let
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth, even function that is identically
1
on
(-r,r)
and such that
\chi^{-1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0})=(-r^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for some
r^{\prime}>r>0
.
We assume that
r
and
r^{\prime}
are sufficiently large such that the interior of
M\times rI^{n}
contains the core of
P
.
Let
\{\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}\}
be the associated partition of unity from Definition
4.2.5
and set
K\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{supp}\,\chi^{\mathbf{0}}
.
Recall from the proof of Proposition
4.3.7
that invertibility of
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P
implies, for all sections
u
supported within
\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}
, the inequality

||Pu||_{0}\geq c(i,\varepsilon)||u||_{1},

where
c(i,\varepsilon)
is the product of the operator norm of
(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P)^{-1}\colon L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P}})\rightarrow H^{1}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P}})
and a constant that comes from the equivalence of the two variants of the Sobolev 1-norms on
H^{1}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P}})
.
The second constant only depends on
g_{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P}
, too.

Recall further that the block form implies
\mathrm{supp}\,P\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}u\subseteq\mathrm{supp}\,\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}
by Corollary
4.2.7
.
Indeed, if we use the notation from Corollary
4.2.7
, then
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi^{{\bm{\alpha}}}
is the closure of
V_{r<r^{\prime}}({\bm{\alpha}})
and the claim follows from applying Corollary
4.2.7
to
V_{r-\epsilon<r^{\prime}+\epsilon}
for all sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.

For
{\bm{\alpha}}\neq\mathbf{0}
, this implies the inequality

\displaystyle||\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}u||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{c(i,\varepsilon)^{-1}\}||P\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}u||_{0,\mathrm{supp}\,\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{c(i,\varepsilon)^{-1}\}\left(||\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}Pu||_{0}+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi)^{\bm{\alpha}}||_{\infty}||u||_{0,\mathrm{supp}\,\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{c(i,\varepsilon)^{-1}\}\left(||Pu||_{0}+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi)^{\bm{\alpha}}||_{\infty}||u||_{0}\right).

Putting these inequalities together yields

\displaystyle||u||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq||\chi^{\mathbf{0}}u||_{1}+\sum_{{\bm{\alpha}}\neq\mathbf{0}}||\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}u||_{1}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\leq||u||_{1,K}+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\mathbf{0}})||_{\infty}||u||_{0,K}\\
&\qquad\qquad\ +\underbrace{3^{n}\max\{c(i,\varepsilon)^{-1}\}}_{=:\mathtt{C}}\left(||Pu||_{0}+\max\{||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\bm{\alpha}})||_{\infty}\}||u||_{0}\right).\end{split}

We now assume that
||Pu||_{0}\leq\gamma||u||_{0}
.
The previous estimate then yields

\displaystyle||u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||u||_{0}+||\nabla u||_{0}-||\nabla u||_{0,K}=||u||_{1}-||\nabla u||_{0,K}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\leq||u||_{1,K}+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\mathbf{0}})||_{\infty}||u||_{0,K}-||\nabla u||_{0,K}\\
&\qquad\qquad\ +\mathtt{C}\left(\gamma||u||_{0}+\max\{||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\bm{\alpha}})||_{\infty}\}||u||_{0}\right)\end{split}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\mathbf{0}})||_{\infty}\right)||u||_{0,K}+\mathtt{C}\left(\gamma||u||_{0}+\max\{||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\bm{\alpha}})||_{\infty}\}||u||_{0}\right),

or equivalently,

\bigl(1-\mathtt{C}(\gamma+\max\{||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\bm{\alpha}})||_{\infty}\})\bigr)||u||_{0}\leq(1+||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{\mathbf{0}})||_{\infty})||u||_{0,K}.

By replacing
\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}
with
\chi^{\bm{\alpha}}(\rho^{-1}\,\cdot\,)
, where
\rho
is sufficiently large, we may assume
7
7

7

Keep in mind that rescaling the partition of unity replaces
K
by
\rho K
.

||\mathrm{grad}(\chi^{{\bm{\alpha}}})||_{\infty}<\min\{1/10,1/10\mathtt{C}^{-1}\}
.
For
u\neq 0
, we end up with the desired inequality:

\frac{||u||_{0,K}}{||u||_{0}}\geq\frac{10}{11}\left(\frac{9}{10}-\mathtt{C}\gamma\right).

This lower bound is positive if and only if

\gamma<3^{2-n}\frac{\min\{c(i,\varepsilon)\}}{10}=:c.\qed

This inequality has important implications concerning the spectrum of
P
.
Recall that a sequence of elements
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
in a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
is a sequence of
approximate eigenvectors for

P
if
||v_{n}||=1
and there is a (necessarily unique)
\lambda\in\mathord{\mathbb{C}}
such that the sequence
(P-\lambda\mathrm{id})v_{n}
converges to zero.
In that case,
\lambda
is called an
approximate eigenvalue
(associated to
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
).
Note that the spectrum of a self-adjoint operator consists of approximate eigenvalues only.

Corollary 4.7.3
.

Let
P
be as in the previous lemma.
Then each sequence of approximate eigenvectors of
P
with approximate eigenvalue
\lambda\in(-c,c)
has a convergent subsequence. In particular,
\lambda
is an eigenvalue.

Proof.

For a given spectral value
\lambda\in(-c,c)
of
P
take a sequence of approximate eigenvalues
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
associated to it. Since
||Pv_{n}||_{0}<c||v_{n}||_{0}=c
for almost all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
, the fundamental elliptic estimate implies
v_{n}\in H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P}})
and that
(||v_{n}||_{1})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded.

Pick a smooth compactly supported function
\varphi
that is identically
1
on
K
, the compact set from Lemma
4.7.2
.
Then
||v_{n}||_{1,K}\leq||\varphi v_{n}||_{1}\leq||\varphi||_{1,1}||v_{n}||_{1}
and the classical Rellich lemma implies that
(\varphi v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
has a convergent subsequence with respect to
||\text{--}||_{0}
.
Thus
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
has a convergent subsequence with respect to
||\text{--}||_{0,K}
.
But inequality
4.7
implies that the convergent subsequence also converges with respect to
||\text{--}||_{0}
.
This limit is an eigenvector and
\lambda
its associated eigenvalue.
∎

As an application, we prove the for us very important

Proposition 4.7.4

(Sausage Lemma)
.

Let
P
and
c
as in Lemma
4.7.2
.
Then
\mathrm{spec}(P)\cap(-c,c)
consists of eigenvalues only and the linear hull
\bigoplus_{|\lambda|<c_{0}}\mathrm{Eig}(P,\lambda)
of eigenspaces forms a finite dimensional subvector space for all
c_{0}<c
.

Proof.

The first claim was part of the previous corollary.

To prove the second claim, we first show that every eigenspace is finite dimensional.
Obviously,
\mathrm{Eig}(P,\lambda)
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P}})
, hence a Hilbert space.
For a given sequence of normalised eigenvectors
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
, the previous corollary implies the existence of a convergent subsequence.
Thus, the unit ball of
\mathrm{Eig}(P,\lambda)
is compact, so
\mathrm{Eig}(P,\lambda)
is finite dimensional.

We now prove that
P
has only finitely many eigenvalues in
[-c_{0},c_{0}]
for all
c_{0}<c
.
Assume the contrary.
Then there is a sequence
(\lambda_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
of pairwise different eigenvalues lying in
[-c_{0},c_{0}]
.
For each
\lambda_{n}
, pick a normalised eigenvector
v_{n}
.
For
\lambda\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\limsup_{n}\lambda_{n}\in[-c_{0},c_{0}]
, we can pick a subsequence of
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
that serves as a sequence of approximate eigenvectors for
\lambda
.
But Corollary
4.7.3
implies that this subsequence has a convergent subsequence
(v_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
.
The limit
w
is an eigenvector corresponding to
\lambda
and eigenvectors corresponding to different eigenvalues are perpendicular, so we end up with

0=\langle v_{n_{k}},w\rangle\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}\langle w,w\rangle=1,

a contradiction.
∎

The Sausage Lemma offers the possibility to use spectral-flow-style arguments.

Lemma 4.7.5
.

Let
P\colon X\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)
be a map, such that
{\mathcal{G}_{g_{P},g_{0}}}_{\ast}(P)\colon X\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}),H^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))
is continuous and such that
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P
is a constant map of invertible operators.
Assume that there is a constant
c>0
such that

\mathrm{spec}(P_{y})\cap(-c,c)=:\{\lambda_{0}(y)\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{N_{y}}(y)\}

consists of finitely many eigenvalues for all
y\in X
.
Then, for each
x\in X
, there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
such that
N_{(\text{--})}
is constant on
U
and all
\lambda_{j}\colon U\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
are continuous functions.

Proof.

For every
c_{0}
that lies strictly between
\max\{|\lambda_{0}(x)|,|\lambda_{N}(x)|\}
and
c
, choose a smooth, monotonically increasing function
b\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[-2c,2c]
that is the identity on
[-c_{0},c_{0}]
.
The bounded transform
\hat{P}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=b(\mathcal{G}_{g_{P},g}\circ P\circ\mathcal{G}_{g_{0},g_{P}})
is a continuous map of bounded, self-adjoint operators
X\rightarrow B(L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))

[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Proposition 3.7 whose values have the same eigenvalues between
(-c_{0},c_{0})
as the values of
P
.
The statement now follows from the fact that eigenvalues of bounded operators depend continuously on the operator with respect to the operator norm
[
booss1993elliptic
]
*Lemma 17.1.
∎

Remark 4.7.6
.

The functions of eigenvalues
\lambda_{j}
are only defined locally near
x
because they may leave the interval
(-c,c)
.
Also, the number of eigenvalues may not be locally constant.

Recall from Theorem
B.0.30
that a smooth path
P\colon(0,1)\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
with underlying path
g_{P}
of Riemannian metrics has an extension
P^{\mathrm{ex}}\in Op^{1}(L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P})}))
whose values are not necessarily in the subspace
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P})})
.
However, this is a nice property to have, so we will give it a name.

Definition 4.7.7
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be open and let
P\colon X\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
be a smooth map with underlying map
g_{P}
of Riemannian metrics. Then
P
called
suspensionable
if the extension
P^{\mathrm{ex}}
given by

(P^{\mathrm{ex}}\sigma)(n,x)=P_{x}(\sigma(\text{--},x))

is an element of
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P})})
.

The first tool is the following fibration-like result.

Proposition 4.7.8

(Path Lifting Property)
.

Consider
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
as a topological space equipped with the smooth Fréchet topology and
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
as a topological space equipped with the subspace topology of
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
.
Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
be a smooth curve that is constant near the end points and satisfies

\gamma_{t}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}}=\sigma_{i}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\gamma_{0}|_{\{\varepsilon x_{i}>R\}}

for some
R>0
, all
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, and all
t\in[0,1]
.

Then each commutative outer square

\textstyle{\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q_{0}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{Q}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)}

can be filled with a smooth map that is constant near the boundary and such that the upper triangle commutes and the lower right triangle commutes up to homotopy relative to the boundary.
In fact
g_{Q_{(\,\cdot\,)}}
and
\gamma_{(\,\cdot\,)}
differs by a (non-diffeomorphical) reparametrisation.
Furthermore, we may assume that the lift is suspensionable and satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q_{t}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q_{0}
for all
(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

In contrast to the proof in
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Section 4.2 our argument is quite geometrical.
The idea is the following one: For an ordered pair of two Riemannian metric
(g_{1},g_{2})
and a non-zero covector
\xi\in T^{\vee}M
, there is a unique
A\in\mathord{\mathrm{GL}}^{+}(d)
that stretches and rotates
{g_{1}}_{\sharp}(\xi)
to
{g_{2}}_{\sharp}(\xi)
in the plane spanned by the two vectors and that acts identically on the orthogonal complement.
We find a lift
b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi)
in
Cl_{d,0}
such that its induced linear map
b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi)(\text{--})b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi)^{\ast}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
agrees with
A
.
We quantise the symbol to get a pseudo differential operator
B_{g_{1},g_{2}}
.
If
B_{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t}}PB_{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t}}^{\ast}
were a curve of invertible operators, then it would be the desired lift.
Unfortunately, it is not clear that this curve is invertible, so we need to stop the curve from time to time and “push the spectral values away from zero”.

Let us now introduce the required concepts.

Definition 4.7.9
.

For
h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
and
R>0
, denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}
the space of all block metrics that agree with
h
outside of
M\times RI^{n}
equipped with the smooth compact open topology.

Lemma 4.7.10
.

For all
h\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
and all
R>0
, there is a smooth map
B\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})
that satisfies the following three conditions:

(i)

The following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{target}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{source}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}.}

(ii)

For all
g_{1},g_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}
, the operator
B_{g_{1},g_{2}}-p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
is compactly supported.

(iii)

For all
g_{1},g_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}
, we have

\mathrm{symb}_{0}(B_{g_{1},g_{2}})\circ g_{1\,\sharp}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot_{g_{1}}\circ\,\mathrm{symb}_{0}(B_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\ast})=g_{2\,\sharp}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot_{g_{2}},

where
\cdot_{g_{j}}
is the Clifford left action on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{j}}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi\colon\mathrm{Pos}\rightarrow M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be the bundle whose fibres consist of positive definite, symmetric bilinear forms and
\mathfrak{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Pos}
be the fibre bundle whose fibre of
g_{x}
is
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{x}}
.
Similarly, we define
Cl(T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\text{--})\rightarrow\mathrm{Pos}
to be the bundle whose fibre of
g_{x}
is the Clifford algebra
Cl(T_{x}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),g_{x})
.
We denote with
||\text{--}||\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=||\text{--}||_{g_{x}}
the norm on
Cl(T_{x}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),g_{x})
induced by
g_{x}
and we denote with
\cdot_{g_{x}}
the Clifford multiplication and left action of
Cl(M,g_{x})
on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{x}}
.
Recall furthermore that

\Phi_{g_{1,x},g_{2,x}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Cl(\tau_{g_{1,x},g_{2,x}})\colon Cl(T_{x}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),g_{1,x})\rightarrow Cl(T_{x}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),g_{2,x})

denotes the algebra isomorphism induced by the pre-gauge map on the tangent spaces.
Using this notation, we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi):=&\frac{||\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)||^{1/2}}{||g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)||^{1/2}}\cdot\\
&\quad\frac{\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)/||\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)||+g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)/||g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)||}{||\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)/||\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)||+g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)/||g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)||\,||}\cdot_{g_{1}}\frac{g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)}{||g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)||}\cdot_{g_{1}}.\end{split}

The map
b
takes values in even and
Cl_{n,0}
-right linear symbols, more precisely,

b_{g_{1},g_{2}}\in\{\sigma\in\mathrm{Symb}_{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}})\,:\,\sigma\text{ is even and }Cl_{d,0}\text{-right linear}\},

see Definition
B.0.12
for a reminder of
\mathrm{Symb}_{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}})
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Pos}^{2}=\mathrm{Pos}\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}
the fibre bundle of pairs of positive definite, symmetric, bilinear forms on
T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
.
We consider it as fibre bundle over
\mathrm{Pos}
, whose bundle projection is the projection to the first component.
Furthermore, let

F\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{(v,w)\in T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\setminus\{0\}\oplus T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\setminus\{0\}\,:\,v\neq-\lambda w\text{ for all }\lambda>0\}

be the bundle of pairs of tangent vectors whose cones are not antipodal half lines.

The map
b\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Symb}_{0}^{\times}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})
is continuous because it is induced by the following sequence of bundle maps

\textstyle{\pi^{\ast}T^{\vee}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})\setminus\{0\}\times_{M}\mathrm{Pos}^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{\ast}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Cl_{,}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\text{--})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Hom}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S}).}

The first map is given by
(g_{1},g_{2})\mapsto(g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi),\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}(g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)))
.
Notice that this map indeed takes values in
\pi^{\ast}F
because
g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)=-\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}(\lambda g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi))
for some
\lambda>0
implies that
g_{1}
is not positive definite:

\displaystyle g_{1}\bigl(g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi),g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\lambda g_{1}\bigl(g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi),\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}(g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi))\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\lambda g_{1}\bigl({g_{1}}_{\sharp}(\xi),\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}({g_{2}}_{\sharp}\circ g_{1}^{\flat}\,{g_{1}}_{\sharp}(\xi)\bigr)

\displaystyle=-\lambda g_{1}\bigl({g_{1}}_{\sharp}(\xi),\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}^{3}({g_{1}}_{\sharp}(\xi))\bigr)<0

because
\Phi_{g_{2},g_{1}}|_{T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})}=\tau_{g_{1},g_{2}}
is the pre-gauge map, which is positive definite and self-adjoint with respect to
g_{1}
.
The second map is given by

(v,w)\mapsto\frac{||w||^{1/2}_{g_{1}}}{||v||^{1/2}_{g_{1}}}\frac{w/||w||_{g_{1}}+v/||v||_{g_{1}}}{||w/||w||_{g_{1}}+v/||v||_{g_{1}}||_{g_{1}}}\cdot_{g_{1}}\frac{v}{||v||_{g_{1}}}\cdot_{g_{1}},

which is clearly continuous.
The third map is just the action of the Clifford algebra bundle on the spinor bundle, which is even isometric.
Note, in particular, that
b
takes values in invertible symbols.

Away from
M\times RI^{n}
, the map
b
is the constant map with value
\mathrm{id}_{\mathfrak{S}_{h_{x}}}
.
Recall from Lemma
B.0.23
the continuous (with respect to the amplitude topology) section
\rho\colon\mathrm{Symb}_{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}})\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}})
.
We set
\tilde{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\rho(b_{g_{1},g_{2}})-\rho(b_{g_{1},g_{1}})+\mathrm{id}
.
So far,
\tilde{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}-\mathrm{id}
might not have compact support.
To achieve this, we pick smooth compactly supported functions
\varphi,\psi\colon M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
on
M\times RI^{n}
and
\psi\equiv 1
on
\mathrm{supp}\,\varphi
.
The operator
\hat{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\psi\tilde{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\varphi+\mathrm{id}\cdot(1-\varphi)
has the same principal symbol as
\tilde{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
and differs from the identity by a compactly supported operator.
Recall the function
\alpha_{g_{2},g_{1}}^{2}=\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}(g_{2})/\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}(g_{1})
.
Finally, we define the desired map

\displaystyle B\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S}),

\displaystyle(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\circ\alpha_{g_{1},g_{2}}\hat{B}_{g_{1},g_{2}}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}}).

By the definition of the topology of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfrak{S})
, the map
B
is smooth if and only if
\hat{B}
is smooth, which follows from the construction.
It is clear, that
B
satisfies
(i).

The composition of compactly supported pseudo differential operators with vector bundle morphisms are still compactly supported.
Furthermore,
g_{1}=g_{2}
on the complement of
M\times RI^{n}
so that
\alpha_{g_{1},g_{2}}=1
on this set.
Hence,
B_{g_{1},g_{2}}-p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
is compactly supported, which is
(ii)
.

We now prove
(iii)
.
Using the relation
(v+w)\cdot w=v\cdot(v+w)
for unit-length vectors
v,w
in the Clifford algebra, we derive

b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi)\cdot_{g_{1}}g_{1\,\sharp}(\xi)\cdot_{g_{1}}b_{g_{1},g_{2}}(\xi)^{\ast}\cdot_{g_{1}}=g_{2\,\sharp}(\xi)\cdot_{g_{1}}.

Recall that the pre-Gauge map is an isometric vector bundle map and
Cl(M,g_{1})
-
Cl(M,g_{2})
-equivariant.
It induces a linear map
Op(p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}})\colon L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{2}})
.
The adjoints of the two maps are related by the formula

Op(p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}})^{\ast}=Op(\alpha_{g_{2},g_{1}}^{2}p\mathcal{G}_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\ast})=Op(\alpha_{g_{2},g_{1}}^{2}p\mathcal{G}_{g_{2},g_{1}})

because the underlying volume induced by the metrics is different.
Symbol calculus now implies the third claim

\mathrm{symb}_{0}(B_{g_{1},g_{2}})\circ g_{1\,\sharp}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot_{g_{1}}\circ\,\mathrm{symb}_{0}(B_{g_{1},g_{2}}^{\ast})=g_{2\,\sharp}(\,\cdot\,)\cdot_{g_{2}}.\qed

Since compactly supported pseudo differential operators have compactly supported adjoint, the previous proposition has the following important consequence.

Corollary 4.7.11
.

For every
g\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}
and every block Dirac operator
P
with underlying metric
g_{P}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{h,R}
the operator
B_{g_{P},g}\circ P\circ B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
is a block Dirac operator with underlying metric
g
.

Proof.

The composition
B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
lies in
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
because
B_{g_{P},g}
is an actual pseudo differential operator of order zero,
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})
is local, and compositions on actual pseudo differential operators are actual pseudo differential operators.
The composition is clearly a symmetric operator.

Next we show that the composition is of block form.
Let
r_{2}>r_{1}>R
be a real numbers such that
M\times r_{1}I^{n}
contains the core of
P
, the support of
B_{g_{P},g}-p\mathcal{G}_{g_{P},g}
and
B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-p\mathcal{G}_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
and such that
M\times rI^{n}
and such that
P
decomposes away from
M\times r_{1}I^{n}
.
Lemma
4.2.6
applied to
P
now implies that
M\times r_{2}I^{n}
serves as a core for
B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
.

Since
g_{P}=g=h
away from
M\times r_{1}I^{n}
, we deduce that
B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
decomposes there as
P
.

The operator
B_{g_{P},g}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is a pseudo differential operator of order zero with compact support, hence a bounded operator.
The calculation

\displaystyle B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}

\displaystyle=B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-B_{g_{P},g}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}+B_{g_{P},g}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}

\displaystyle=B_{g_{P},g}\left(P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{P}\right)B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}+\left(B_{g_{P},g}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}B_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}\right)

implies that
B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}
is the sum of two bounded operators, hence bounded.

In conclusion,
B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
is a block Dirac operator with underlying metric
g
.
∎

Unfortunately, the information that the eigenvalues of a continuous family of block Dirac operators are continuous functions is not good enough for the arguments to come.
More refined information for the case
g\mapsto B_{g_{P},g}PB_{g_{P},g}^{\ast}
is provided by the next lemma.
To formulate it, we recall some notations taken from
[
kato1966perturbation
]
*Section IV.2.

Definition 4.7.12
.

Let
S,T\colon X\rightarrow Y
be two closed, unbounded operators between the Banach spaces
X
and
Y
.
We define
\delta(S,T)
to be the smallest number
\delta
that satisfies for all
u\in\mathrm{dom}(S)
the inequality

\mathrm{dist}\bigl((u,Su);\mathrm{Graph}(T)\bigr)\leq\delta(||u||_{X}+||Su||_{Y})\overset{\text{def}}{=}\delta\cdot||(u,Su)||_{X\oplus Y}

and
\hat{\delta}(S,T)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\max\{\delta(S,T);\delta(T,S)\}
.

It is shown in
\mathrm{loc}.

\mathrm{cit}.
that
\hat{\delta}
is a (incomplete) metric on the set
C(X,Y)
of all closed, unbounded operators from
X
to
Y
.
The distance
\hat{\delta}
behaves nicely with compositions of invertible bounded operators.

Lemma 4.7.13
.

Let
T\in C(X,Y)
and let
A\colon X\rightarrow X
,
B\colon Y\rightarrow Y
be bounded invertible operators such that
A
and
A^{-1}
preserve the domain of
T
.

Then

\displaystyle\delta(T,BT)\leq||\mathrm{id}-B||,

\displaystyle\delta(BT,T)\leq||\mathrm{id}-B||,

\displaystyle\delta(T,TA)\leq||\mathrm{id}-A^{-1}||,

\displaystyle\delta(TA,T)\leq||\mathrm{id}-A||.

Proof.

Let
u\in{\mathrm{dom}\,}T
be non-zero. For
v=u\in{\mathrm{dom}\,}BT
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}\bigl((u,Tu);\mathrm{Graph}(BT)\bigr)\leq||(u,Tu)-(v,BTv)||

\displaystyle=||(0,(\mathrm{id}-B)Tu)||

\displaystyle\leq||\mathrm{id}-B||\cdot||u||.

If we choose
v=A^{-1}u\in{\mathrm{dom}\,}T
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}\bigl((u,Tu);\mathrm{Graph}(TA)\bigr)\leq||(u,Tu)-(v,TAv)||

\displaystyle=||((\mathrm{id}-A^{-1})u,0)||

\displaystyle\leq||\mathrm{id}-A^{-1}||\cdot||u||.

The other two inequalities are derived in a similar manner.
∎

Important for us is the fact that spectral gaps of operators are, in a more general sense, lower semi-continuous (they cannot suddenly decrease but they may suddenly increase) with respect to the topology generated by
\hat{\delta}
.

Theorem 4.7.14
.

[
kato1966perturbation
]
*Theorem IV.3.1
Let
T\in C(X,Y)
be a closed operator and let
R\colon\zeta\mapsto(T-\zeta)^{-1}\in B(Y,X)
be its resolvent function.
For every compact subset
\Gamma
of the resolvent set
\rho(T)
of
T
, there is a
\delta>0
such that if
\hat{\delta}(S,T)<\delta
, then
\Gamma
is a subset of
\rho(S)
.
An explicit choice for
\delta
is

\delta=\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}(1+|\zeta|^{2})^{-1}(1+||R(\zeta)||^{2})^{-1/2}.

Proof of the Path Lifting Property.

Let the following commutative square be given

\textstyle{\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Q_{0}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M).}

By assumption,
\gamma
factors through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)_{\gamma_{0},R}
for some
R>0
.
Let
0<c<1
be a constant smaller than the corresponding constant from the Sausage Lemma for the faces
\{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}Q_{0}\}
.
Choose a positive number
\mu_{0}<c/8
.
Since
\gamma
is uniformly continuous there is an
N\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
such that all
s,t\in[0,1]
with
|s-t|<1/N
satisfy

||B(\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t})-\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}}||_{0,0}=||B(\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t})\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{s},\gamma_{t}}^{\ast}-\mathrm{id}||_{0,0}<\mu_{0}/8.

(4.8)

We will prove the following slightly stronger statement.
For every
P\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
that contains
(-2\mu_{0},2\mu_{0})
within its resolvent set, we construct a smooth path
Q\colon[0,1/N]\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
that is constant near the boundary, lifts the restriction of
\gamma|_{[0,1/N]}
with initial value
Q_{0}=P
up to reparametrisation, satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q_{t}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q_{0}
, and whose endpoint contains
(-2\mu_{0},2\mu_{0})
in its resolvent set.

We will apply this construction iteratively over the partition
[k/N,(k+1)/N]
starting with the given operator
Q_{0}
, which satisfies our construction assumptions.
The union of all these curves will then be the desired homotopy lift.

Let us now describe the construction in more detail.
Fix, once and for all, a smooth, odd, injective function
\varphi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\setminus[-3\mu_{0},3\mu_{0}]
that is the identity on the complement of
(-c/2,c/2)
.
The construction is divided into two steps.
The first step is to extend the inital value
Q_{0}
to a path, the second step is adjusting the eigenvalues of the endpoint
Q_{1/N}
.

Let us start with the first step:
Abbreviate
B_{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{s}}
to
B_{s}
and consider on
[0,1/N]
the map
t\mapsto B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}
.
By Lemma
4.7.10
and Corollary
4.7.11
this map is a smooth map with values in
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)
.

To see that
B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}
is invertible, we argue as follows.
The distance between the spectrum of
Q_{0}
and
\Gamma\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[-\mu_{0},\mu_{0}]
is bigger than
\mu_{0}
.
Thus we can estimate the constant
\delta
in Theorem
4.7.14
by

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=\min_{\zeta\in\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}(1+|\zeta|^{2})^{-1}(1+||R(\zeta)||^{2})^{-1/2}
