\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(1+\mu_{0}^{2})^{-1}(1+\mu_{0}^{-2})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\mu_{0}(1+\mu_{0}^{2})^{-3/2}>\frac{1}{3}\mu_{0}.

The last inequality holds because
\mu_{0}<1/8
.

On the other hand Lemma
4.7.13
and inequality (
4.8
) together with the estimate from the geometric series yield for all
t\in[0,1/N]
the estimate

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\ast}B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}\mathcal{G}_{t},P)

\displaystyle\leq\hat{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\ast}B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}\mathcal{G}_{t},\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\ast}B_{t}P)+\hat{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\ast}B_{t}P,P)

\displaystyle\leq\max\{||B^{\ast}_{t}-\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{t},\gamma_{0}}||_{0,0},||(B^{\ast}_{t})^{-1}-\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t}}||_{0,0}\}+||B_{t}-\mathcal{G}_{\gamma_{0},\gamma_{t}}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{0}/8\cdot(1-\mu_{0}/8)^{-1}+\mu_{0}/8<\mu_{0}/3<\delta,

so all
B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}
have
[-\mu_{0},\mu_{0}]
in their resolvent set.

Compose
t\mapsto B_{t}PB_{t}^{\ast}
with a smooth, monotone increasing, surjective self map on
[0,1/N]
such that the composition is a map
[0,1/N]\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
that is constant near the boundary.
By abuse of notation, we will denote the composition again with
BPB^{\ast}
.

Now comes the second step.
Let
\lambda_{0}^{+}\leq\dots\leq\lambda_{m^{+}}^{+}
be all positive eigenvalues and let
\lambda_{0}^{-}\geq\dots\geq\lambda^{-}_{m^{-}}
be all negative eigenvalues of
BQ_{0}B^{\ast}
inside
(-c,c)
.
Pick eigenvectors
v_{j}^{\pm}
corresponding to
\lambda_{j}^{\pm}
such that they form an orthonormal system and pick smooth compactly supported sections
w_{j}^{\pm}
that satisfy
||v_{j}^{\pm}-w_{j}^{\pm}||_{0}<\mu_{0}/C\cdot 10^{-j}
, where
C\geq 1
is a constant to be determined later.
Let
\Phi\colon[1/N,1+1/N]\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{0\}
be the homotopy between
\mathrm{id}
and
\varphi
given by convex combination and compose the first entry with a smooth, monotonically increasing, surjective self-map on
[1/N,1+1/N]
such that
\Phi(\,\cdot\,,x)=x
near
1/N
and
\Phi(\,\cdot\,,x)=\varphi(x)
near
1+1/N
.
Denote the result again with
\Phi
.
Finally, define

\displaystyle\Theta\colon[1/N,1+1/N]\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}}}),

\displaystyle t\mapsto B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}+\sum_{j=0}^{m^{\pm}}(\Phi(t,\lambda_{j}^{\pm})-\lambda_{j}^{\pm})\langle\cdot,w_{j}^{\pm}\rangle w_{j}^{\pm}

and the auxiliary map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Theta}\colon[1/N,1+1/N]\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}}}),

\displaystyle t\mapsto B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}+\sum_{j=0}^{m^{\pm}}(\Phi(t,\lambda_{j}^{\pm})-\lambda_{j}^{\pm})\langle\cdot,v_{j}^{\pm}\rangle v_{j}^{\pm}.

For all
t\in[1/N,1+1/N]
, the two operators
\Theta_{t}
and
\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}
differ from
B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}
by a self-adjoint infinitely smoothing operator of finite rank.
They are therefore self-adjoint pseudo differential operators of order
1
with the same principal symbol as the Dirac operator of the underlying metric.
They also differ from this Dirac operator by a bounded operator, because
B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}
does it.
Since all
w_{j}^{\pm}
are compactly supported,
\Theta_{t}
is of block form and has the same faces as
B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}
; hence,
\Theta_{t}
is a block Dirac operator.

We prove invertibility of
\Theta
by showing that
\widetilde{\Theta}
is invertible and that
\Theta
is a sufficiently small perturbation of
\widetilde{\Theta}
.
On the positive subspace of
B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}
, i.e., the one on which
B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}
is positive definite,
we have, by construction,

\widetilde{\Theta_{t}}\geq\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{+})\mathrm{id}.

A similar result holds for the negative subspace, so the interval
(\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{-}),\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{+}))
does not intersect the spectrum of
\widetilde{\Theta_{t}}
.
In particular, each
\widetilde{\Theta_{t}}
is invertible.

It remains to show that
\Theta
is a sufficiently small perturbation of
\widetilde{\Theta}
.
First observe

\displaystyle\quad\ ||\langle\,\cdot\,,w_{j}^{\pm}\rangle w_{j}^{\pm}-\langle\,\cdot\,,v_{j}^{\pm}\rangle v_{j}^{\pm}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq||\langle\,\cdot\,,w_{j}^{\pm}-v_{j}^{\pm}\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}}||w_{j}^{\pm}||_{0}+||\langle\,\cdot\,,v_{j}^{\pm}\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}}||v_{j}^{\pm}-w_{j}^{\pm}||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\left(2+||v_{j}-w_{j}||_{0}\right)||v_{j}+w_{j}||_{0}<2.5\mu_{0}/C10^{-j}.

This implies

\displaystyle||\Theta_{t}-\widetilde{\Theta_{t}}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{M,N}\underbrace{|\Phi(t,\lambda_{j}^{\pm})-\lambda_{j}^{\pm}|}_{\leq c}2.5\mu_{0}/C10^{-j}

\displaystyle\leq c\cdot 2.5\cdot 2\cdot 10/9\cdot\mu_{0}/C

\displaystyle\leq 6\mu_{0}/C.

Thus, the spectrum of
\Theta_{t}
does not intersect
(\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{-})+6\mu_{0}/C,\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{+})-6\mu_{0}/C)
.
If we choose
C
sufficiently large, for example
C=12
and estimate
\tilde{\Theta}_{t}
against its lowest positive eigenvalue
\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{+})
on its positive subspace, we derive the following chain of inequalities

\displaystyle\Theta_{t}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{t}+\tilde{\Theta}_{t}-\tilde{\Theta}_{t}\geq\left(\Phi(t,\lambda_{0}^{+})-||\tilde{\Theta}_{t}-\Theta_{t}||_{0,0}\right)\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\lambda_{0}^{+}-||\tilde{\Theta}_{t}-\Theta_{t}||_{0,0}\right)\mathrm{id}\geq\left(2\mu_{0}-||\tilde{\Theta}_{t}-\Theta_{t}||_{0,0}\right)\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle\geq(2\mu_{0}-6\mu_{0}/C)\mathrm{id}\geq\mu_{0}\mathrm{id}.

A similar result holds on the negative subspace, so
\Theta_{t}
is invertible for all
t\in[1/N,1+1/N]
.
Using
\Phi(1+1/N,\lambda_{0}^{+})=\varphi(\lambda_{0}^{+})\geq 3\mu_{0}
and

\displaystyle\Theta_{1+1/N}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{1+1/N}-\tilde{\Theta}_{1+1/N}+\tilde{\Theta}_{1+1/N}

\displaystyle\geq\left(3\mu_{0}-||\Theta_{1+1/N}-\tilde{\Theta}_{1+1/N}||_{0,0}\right)\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle\geq(3\mu_{0}-\mu_{0}/2)\mathrm{id}>2\mu_{0}\mathrm{id},

we see that the spectrum of
\Theta_{1/N}
does not intersect
(-2\mu_{0},2\mu_{0})
.

The desired homotopy lift is now given by

\textstyle{Q\colon[0,1/N]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\mapsto(N+1)t}
\textstyle{[0,1+1/N]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B_{(\cdot)}Q_{0}B_{(\cdot)}\cup\Theta}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M).}

Since
Q_{1/N}
satisfies the assumption of the described construction, we can construct a lift on
[1/N,2/N]
starting with
Q_{1/N}
instead of
Q_{0}
(but with the same
\mu_{0}
).
If we repeat this construction
(N-2)
-times further and glue the results together, we end up with the desired (homotopy) lift
Q\colon[0,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
.
Since the homotopy between
g_{Q}
and
\gamma
is given by reparametrisation relative endpoints, the map
Q
is indeed a homotopy lift.

It remains to prove that our construction is a suspensionable path.
More precisely, if
Q
also denotes the constant extension of the constructed lift, we need to show that
Q^{\mathrm{ex}}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}};\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\gamma)})
.
Since
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
is local, see Proposition
B.0.27
, it suffices to prove the statement only for each piece of the construction.

To verify
\bigl(B_{(\cdot)}Q_{0}B_{(\cdot)}^{\ast}\bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S})
, take a sequence of actual pseudo differential operators
P_{n}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\gamma_{0}})
that approximates
Q_{0}
with respect to the Fréchet structure on
Op^{1}
(the one that induces the Atiyah-Singer topology on
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\gamma_{0}})
).
Since
B-p\mathcal{G}
is compactly supported, Proposition
B.0.15
and a partition of unity argument shows that
P\mapsto BPB^{\ast}
is continuous with respect to the amplitude topology.
But
(\text{--})^{\mathrm{ex}}
, which is denoted with
\text{--}\tilde{\boxtimes}\mathrm{id}
in the appendix, is a continuous linear map, see Theorem
B.0.30
, so we conclude

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\bigl(BP_{n}B^{\ast}\bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}}

\displaystyle=B^{\mathrm{ex}}\lim_{n\to\infty}P_{n}^{\mathrm{ex}}(B^{\ast})^{\mathrm{ex}}

\displaystyle=\bigl(BQ_{0}B^{\ast}\bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\mathrm{susp}(BQ_{0}B^{\ast})}}).

For
\tilde{\Theta}
it is even easier to verify because it is the sum of a block Dirac operator (interpreted as constant curve) and a smooth curve
W
of infinitely smoothing operators.
We conclude

\tilde{\Theta}^{\mathrm{ex}}=\bigl(B_{1/N}Q_{0}B_{1/N}^{\ast}\bigr)^{\mathrm{ex}}+W^{\mathrm{ex}}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}};\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{1/N})})

from the Theorem
B.0.28
and Theorem
B.0.30
.
∎

The next tool allows to glue two matching invertible pseudo Dirac operators together.
To introduce this tool, we need to discuss shift operators on pseudo differential operators.

Definition 4.7.15
.

Let
E\rightarrow M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a vector bundle.
For
v\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, define the
shift operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{v}\colon\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{v}(\sigma)(m,x)

\displaystyle=\sigma(m,x-v).

The shift operators induce linear maps on the spaces of pseudo differential operators via push-forward

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{v\,\ast}\colon\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E),

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Sh}_{v}\circ P\circ\mathrm{Sh}_{-v}.

In case that
E
is the tangent or the spinor bundle, there is a less ad-hoc way to define the shift operator.
The translation
\mathrm{trans}_{v}\colon(m,x)\mapsto(m,x+v)
is a diffeomorphism on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Its differential gives an isometry

T\mathrm{trans}_{v}\colon(T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),g)\rightarrow(T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\mathrm{trans}_{-v}^{\ast}g).

Clearly,
\mathrm{trans}_{-v}^{\ast}g
and (the ad-hoc version of)
\mathrm{Sh}_{v}g
agree.
Since the spinor bundle construction is functorial with respect to Pin structure preserving isometries, we obtain an isomorphism of Clifford-module bundles
\mathfrak{S}_{g}\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{v}g}
over
\mathrm{trans}_{v}
.
The fibres
(\mathfrak{S}_{g})_{(m,x-v)}
and
(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{v}(g)})_{(m,x)}
are the same, so the push forward with this Clifford bundle isomorphism on the level of sections is again given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{v}\colon\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathfrak{S}_{g})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{v}g}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sh}_{v}(\sigma)(m,x)

\displaystyle=\sigma(m,x-v).

Since the Sobolev norms, as defined in Appendix
A
, depend only on the local geometry of the bundles in considerations, all shift operators induce isometries between the corresponding Sobolev spaces

\mathrm{Sh}_{v}\colon H^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{g})\rightarrow H^{k}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{v\,\ast}g}).

Together with the observation made above that conjugations with
\mathrm{Sh}_{v}
preserves the set of (actual) pseudo differential operators, we conclude that these conjugations induce linear maps on the set of pseudo differential operators that are continuous with respect to the Atiyah-Singer topology.
Continuity with respect to the Atiyah-Singer topology allows us to extend
\mathrm{Sh}_{v\,\ast}
continuously to the Atiyah-Singer closure.

We will write
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}
instead of
\mathrm{Sh}_{Re_{j}}
.

Definition 4.7.16
.

Let
P,Q\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)
be two block Dirac operators whose cores are contained in
M\times\rho I^{n}
for some
\rho>0
and that satisfy
\partial^{1}_{i}P=\partial^{-1}_{i}Q
for some
1\leq i\leq n
.
Then we define, for all
R>\rho
, the block metric

g_{P}+_{i,R}g_{Q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={\mathrm{Sh}_{i,-2R}}_{\ast}(g_{P})\cup{\mathrm{Sh}_{i,2R}}_{\ast}(g_{Q})

and the block operator

P+_{i,R}Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={\mathrm{Sh}_{i,-2R}}_{\ast}(P)\cup{\mathrm{Sh}_{i,2R}}_{\ast}(Q)

on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{p}+_{i,R}g_{Q}}
, where
\cup
was defined in Definition
4.3.19
.

Proposition 4.7.17
.

With the notation from the previous definition, the operator
P+_{i,R}Q
is a block Dirac operator, whose faces are given by

\partial^{\omega}_{j}(P+_{i,R}Q)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\begin{cases}\partial^{-1}_{i}P,&\text{if }j=i,\omega=-1,\\
\partial^{1}_{i}Q,&\text{if }j=i,\omega=1,\\
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P+_{i-1,R}\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q,&\text{if }j<i,\\
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P+_{i,R}\partial^{\omega}_{j}Q,&\text{if }j>i.\end{cases}

If
P
and
Q
are invertible, then there is an
R_{0}
, depending on
P
and
Q
, such that
P+_{i,R}Q
is also invertible for all
R>R_{0}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
P+_{i,R}Q\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P+_{i,R}Q}})
because the space is local, see
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that it is a block operator with the claimed faces.
Symbol calculus implies
\mathrm{symb}_{1}(P+_{i,R}Q)=\mathbf{c}(g_{P+_{i,R}Q\,\sharp}(\text{--}))
.
Corollary
4.3.20
implies that
P+_{i,R}Q
is symmetric.

Since the shift operator is induced by the pushforward of a diffeomorphism, we have
{\mathrm{Sh}_{i,R}}_{\ast}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{i,R}(g)}
so that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}+_{i,R}g_{Q}}={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}+_{i,R}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{Q}}
.
It follows that

P+_{i,R}Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P+_{i,R}Q}}=P+_{i,R}Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}}+_{i,R}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{Q}}=(P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P}})+_{i,R}(Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{Q}})

is a convex combination of two bounded operators and hence itself bounded.

Assume now that
P
and
Q
are invertible, then there are positive constants
c_{P}
and
c_{Q}
respectively, such that
||P\sigma||_{0}\geq c_{P}||\sigma||_{0}
and
||Q\sigma||_{0}\geq c_{Q}||\sigma||_{0}
.
By Corollary
4.3.22
there is an
R_{0}
, such that
P+_{i,R}Q
is bounded from below for all
R\geq R_{0}
.
Since
P+_{i,R}Q
is symmetric and differs from
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{P+_{i,R}Q}}
by a bounded operator,
P+_{i,R}Q
is also self-adjoint and therefore invertible.
∎

Finally, we are able to prove the main theorem of this section.

Proof of the Fibration Theorem
4.7.1
.

We need to show that every commutative outer square

\textstyle{\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Box[n]_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M).}

has a dotted lift. In other words, for a given set of elements

\{P_{(j,\omega)}\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n-1}(M)\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}P_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k}P_{(j,\omega)}\text{ for }j<k,\,(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\}

and a block metric
g\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)
such that
g_{P_{(j,\omega)}}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}g
, we need to find a
P\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
such that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P=P_{(j,\omega)}
and
g_{P}=g
.
The problem is completely symmetric in
(i,\varepsilon)
, so we will assume that
(i,\varepsilon)=(n,1)
.
Since
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan set, there is a
\hat{P}\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n}(M)
such that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}\hat{P}=P_{(j,\omega)}
.
However, in general
g_{\hat{P}}\neq g
and
g_{\partial^{1}_{n}\hat{P}}=\partial^{1}_{n}g_{\hat{P}}\neq\partial^{1}_{n}g
.

We will first modify
\hat{P}
such that the resulting invertible block Dirac operator
P^{\mathrm{aux}}
additionally satisfies
g_{\partial^{1}_{n}P^{\mathrm{aux}}}=\partial^{1}_{n}g
.
To this end, pick a smooth function
\chi\colon[-1,1]\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
0
near
-1
and identically
1
near
1
.
The map

\gamma\colon t\mapsto(1-\chi(t))g_{\partial^{1}_{n}\hat{P}}+\chi(t)\partial^{1}_{n}g

is a smooth curve
\gamma\colon[-1,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n-1}}(M)_{\partial^{1}_{n}\hat{P},R}
for some sufficiently large
R>0
because of the assumed compatibility conditions
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\hat{P}}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}g
for all
(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

The Path Lifting Proposition
4.7.8
applied to
Q_{-1}:=\partial^{1}_{n}\hat{P}
and
\gamma
yield a smooth, suspensionable curve of invertible block Dirac operators
Q\colon[-1,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n-1}(M)
, constant near the boundary, such that
g_{Q_{1}}=\partial^{1}_{n}g
.
Extend it constantly to a smooth map
Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{n-1}(M)
.
By re-parametrising
Q
, if necessary, we may assume that

\tilde{Q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{susp}(Q)=Q^{\mathrm{ex}}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes\partial_{n}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}

is an invertible operator on
L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\gamma)})
.
By Proposition
4.7.17
, there is a sufficiently large
R>0
such that the block Dirac operator

P^{\mathrm{aux}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={\mathrm{Sh}_{n,2R}}_{\ast}(\hat{P}+_{n,R}\tilde{Q})

is invertible.
Since the faces of
Q
are independent of the curve parameter, we have
\partial^{\omega}_{j}\tilde{Q}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}\sigma_{n}\partial^{1}_{n}\hat{P}
for all
(j,\omega)\neq(n,1)
.
We conclude that
\partial^{\omega}_{j}\left(P^{\mathrm{aux}}\right)=\partial^{\omega}_{j}P=P_{(j,\omega)}
.
By construction,
\partial^{1}_{n}\left(P^{\mathrm{aux}}\right)=\partial^{1}_{n}\tilde{Q}=Q_{1}
.
Thus,
P^{\mathrm{aux}}
is an invertible block Dirac operator that satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{P^{\mathrm{aux}}}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}g
for all
(j,\omega)\in\{1,\dots,n\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

Proceeding as before, the smooth curve

\Gamma\colon[-1,1]\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{n}}(M)_{g_{P^{\mathrm{aux}}}},\qquad t\mapsto(1-\chi(t))g_{P^{\mathrm{aux}}}+\chi(t)g

satisfies
\partial^{\omega}_{j}\Gamma_{t}=\partial^{\omega}_{j}\Gamma_{-1}
for all
t\in[-1,1]
and all face maps.
Apply the path-lifting lemma to
P^{\mathrm{aux}}
and
\Gamma
to find a homotopy lift
\mathcal{P}
of
\Gamma
.
Since the homotopy between
\Gamma
and
g_{\mathcal{P}}
is relative to the boundary, the operator
P_{\mathrm{fill}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathcal{P}_{1}
is the desired filler.
∎

4.8
Operator Theoretic Addition

This section is the operator-theoretic analog to Section
3.4
.
We will prove a version of the “angle rotation” for operators.
Once this result is established, we deduce the operator-theoretic analogs of the results of Section
3.4
from the pictures therein.
The construction also produces an underlying block metric
g^{\angle}_{R}
that can be thought, up to a shift, as the elongation of the resulting metric of the construction described in Proposition
3.4.2
.
For the construction, recall the shift operator in the context of spinor bundles from Section
4.7
.

Proposition 4.8.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
and let
P
be a block Dirac operator with underlying block metric
g
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
whose core is contained in
M\times\rho I^{n}
.
Then, for all
R\geq 1
, there is a block Dirac operator
P_{R}^{\angle}

on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with underlying block metric
g^{\angle}_{R}
satisfying the following properties:

(i)

The faces of
P^{\angle}_{R}
are given by

\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}(P_{R}^{\angle})=\begin{cases}\sigma_{n+1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P,&\text{if }i<n,\\
\sigma_{n+1}\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}P,&\text{if }i\geq n,\varepsilon=1,\\
P,&\text{if }i\geq n,\varepsilon=-1.\end{cases}

(ii)

If
P
is invertible, so is
P^{\angle}_{R}
for all sufficiently large
R
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
3.4.2
, let
K
be a compact, convex, point-symmetric body inside
I^{2}
with smooth boundary
\partial K
that further agrees with
I^{2}
inside
\{|x_{1}|\leq 1/2\}
and
\{|x_{2}|\leq 1/2\}
.
Let
\gamma\colon[0,l]\rightarrow\partial K\cap\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}
be the smooth curve parametrised by arclength that satisfies
\gamma(0)=e_{1}
, let
v\colon[0,l]\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be the normalised vector field that is perpendicular to
\gamma^{\prime}
and satisfies
\det(v,\gamma^{\prime})>0
, and let
\kappa
be the curvature of
\gamma
.
Recall that this sign convention implies
v(0)=e_{1}
and that
v^{\prime}=\kappa\gamma^{\prime}
.
Denote by
\gamma_{R}
,
v_{R}
, and
\kappa_{R}
the corresponding objects obtained by replacing
K
by
R\cdot K
.

Since
K\cap\{|x_{j}|\leq 1/2\}
is a rectangle, we can form the elongation

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=K\cap\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}^{2}\cup\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}^{2}\,:x_{1}\in K\text{ or }x_{2}\in K\}\cup\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}^{2}

and extend
\gamma
,
v
,
\kappa
in a constant manner to smooth maps
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
The same holds true for
R\cdot K
and the rescaled analogs
\gamma_{R}
,
v_{R}
, and
\kappa_{R}
.

The extended objects introduce coordinates on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus R\mathbf{K}
via

\displaystyle\Xi_{R}\colon(0,\infty)\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus R\mathbf{K}

\displaystyle(r,\varphi)

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{R}(\varphi)+r\cdot v_{R}(\varphi),

such that

\left(\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\right)_{(r,\varphi)}=\mathrm{d}r^{2}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}.

Now that we have recalled the required notation, we can start with the construction.
In order to avoid case distinctions, we assume that the operator
P
is invertible to begin with.
This is no restriction because each step in the construction can be carried out without the invertibility assumption (of course, the resulting operator then has no chance to be invertible).

We fix a lower bound
c_{P}
for
P
that we assume to be smaller than
1
.
We abbreviate
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
to
N
so that
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}=N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
(the last coordinate is the distinguished one).
For a fixed
\rho^{\prime}>\max\{32/c_{P},\rho\}
, the operator
\mathrm{Sh}_{n,2\rho}(P)
restricts to
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
and we denote the restriction with
Q
.

Define on
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
(in
\Xi_{R}
-coordinates) the metric

(g_{R})_{(n,r,\varphi)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left(\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\right)_{(n,r)}+(1+r\kappa_{R}(\varphi))^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}

and on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}}\rightarrow N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
the operator

\mathtt{P}_{R}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathcal{G}(\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2},g_{R})_{\ast}(Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id})+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}}.

Note that
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(g_{R})=\left(\mathrm{Sh}_{n,2\rho^{\prime}+R}\right)(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{n+1}^{2}
on
N\times\Xi_{R}((0,\rho^{\prime})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
, so that the metrics
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(g_{R})
and
\partial^{-1}_{n}g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
agree there.
Since
\rho^{\prime}>\rho
, the operators
Q
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{n,2\rho^{\prime}\ast}g}
decompose on
N\times(0,\rho^{\prime})
into

\displaystyle Q=\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{(0,\rho^{\prime})}\quad\text{ and }\quad{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{n,2\rho^{\prime}\ast}g}={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\partial^{-1}_{n}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{(0,\rho^{\prime})},

where
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{(0,\rho^{\prime})}
denotes the Dirac operator of the Euclidean metric on
(0,\rho^{\prime})
.
Thus, the operators
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
and
\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
restrict to
N\times\Xi_{R}((0,\rho^{\prime})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
and agree there.
They also act as derivations in
r
-direction, so we can glue these operators together
8
8

8

That means that we are in setup of Definition
4.3.19
although we refrain from spelling out the constants
a
and
b
explicitly. For the
R
in Definition
4.3.19
, we choose
R=4/c_{P}
so that
2R<\rho^{\prime}/2
. For completeness, the remaining data is the following: The ambient manifold
M
of Definition
4.3.19
is here
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, the separating hypersurface is
N\times\Xi_{R}({\rho^{\prime}/2}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
, the operator
P_{1}
is
\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
on
N\times\bigl(RK\cup\Xi_{R}((0,\rho^{\prime})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\bigr)
, and
P_{2}
is
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
.
We define

P_{R}^{\angle}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{-(R+2\rho^{\prime})(e_{n}+e_{n+1})\,\ast}\bigl(\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\cup\Xi_{R,\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})\bigr)

with underlying metric
g_{R}^{\angle}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{-(R+2\rho^{\prime})(e_{n}+e_{n+1})\,\ast}(g_{R}\cup\partial^{-1}_{n}g\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}})
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.

Note that
P_{R}^{\angle}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}^{\angle}})
because the space
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}^{\angle}})
is local and
P_{R}^{\angle}
is obtained from
P
by adding other pseudo differential operators (in the Atiyah-Singer sense), tensoring it with the identity, and pushing operators forward with vector bundle maps and diffeomorphisms.

The operator
P_{R}^{\angle}
is also a block operator of Dirac type.
Indeed, its core is contained inside
M\times(R+5\rho^{\prime})I^{n}
as one can check for sections that are either supported within the union of
N\times R\cdot\mathbf{K}
and the gluing-strip
N\times\Xi_{R}((0,\rho^{\prime})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
, or supported outside of
N\times R\cdot\mathbf{K}
separately.

The operator
P_{R}^{\angle}
is symmetric because
\mathtt{P}_{R}
acts on
N\times(0,\rho^{\prime})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
as a differential operator in
r
-direction, so we can apply Corollary
4.3.20
.

The operator
(P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}
induces a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}})
, which also implies that its principal symbol vanishes by Lemma
B.0.24
.
Hence,
\mathtt{P}_{R}
differs from the Dirac operator of its underlying metric
g_{R}
by a bounded operator and has the same principal symbol as the Dirac operator of its underlying metric.
The same is true for
\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
, so its also true for the union
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}\mathtt{P}_{R}\cup(\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}})
.

Visual reasons, see figure
4.1

and the fact that we modify the operator
P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}
only in the last two coordinates imply that
P^{\angle}_{R}
has the faces as claimed.
This proves (i).

The construction of

We will prove in Corollary

below that
||\mathtt{P}_{R}\sigma||_{0}\geq c_{P}/3||\sigma||_{0}
if

is sufficiently large.
It follows that
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
is bounded from below on
\mathfrak{S}_{(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(g_{R})}\rightarrow N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\setminus R\cdot\mathbf{K}
with lower bound
c_{P}/3
.
Since
\rho^{\prime}>32/c_{P}
, Corollary
4.3.22
(with gluing strip length
4/c_{P}
) applied to
(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
and
\partial^{-1}_{n}P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}
implies that
P^{\angle}_{R}
is bounded from below by

\displaystyle c_{P_{R}^{\angle}}^{2}

\displaystyle=1/4\cdot\min\{c^{2}_{(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})}-c^{2}_{P}/16,c_{\partial^{-1}_{1}P}-c^{2}_{P}/16\}

\displaystyle=1/4\cdot(c^{2}_{(\Xi_{R})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})}-c_{P}^{2}/16)

\displaystyle\geq 1/4(1/9-1/16)c_{P}^{2}>c_{P}^{2}/100.

As
P_{R}^{\angle}
is a block Dirac operator, in particular self-adjoint, it is invertible, which proves (ii).
∎

Lemma 4.8.2
.

Using the notation from the proof of the previous proposition, the operator
D\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}
is given by

D=(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-2}r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})\partial_{\varphi}\cdot.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})
to
\mathcal{G}
.
We need to express
D
in the coordinates induced by
\Xi_{R}
.
To this end, we fix a local
(\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})
-orthonormal frame
e_{1},\dots,e_{n},e_{n+1}
such that
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
are independent of
\varphi
and
e_{n+1}=\partial_{\varphi}
.

Recall the pre-gauge map
\tau_{g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}}
from Definition
4.1.20
and its induced map
\Phi=\Phi_{g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}}
between the corresponding Clifford algebras.
Define the
g_{R}
-orthonormal frame

f_{j}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\tau_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2},g_{R}}(e_{j})=\begin{cases}e_{j},&\text{if }j<n+1,\\
(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{\varphi},&\text{if }j=n+1.\end{cases}

Recall from the proof of Lemma
4.1.32
the formula

\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})=p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})-\alpha^{-1}\Phi(\mathrm{grad}_{g_{R}}\alpha)\cdot\ ,

where

\alpha^{2}=\alpha^{2}_{g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g_{R}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}}}=(1+r\kappa_{R}).

Differentiating
\alpha
yields

\mathrm{d}\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1/2}\left(\kappa_{R}\mathrm{d}r+r\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R}\mathrm{d}\varphi\right).

hence, the gradient is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{grad}_{g_{R}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{grad}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)+(1+r\kappa_{R})^{2}\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1/2}\left(\kappa_{R}\mathrm{grad}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}(r)+r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})\cdot(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-2}\partial_{\varphi}\right)

so that the first summand is given by

\displaystyle\alpha^{-1}\Phi(\mathrm{grad}_{g_{R}}\alpha)=\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\left(\kappa_{R}\mathrm{grad}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}(r)+r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})\cdot(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}\right).

The determination of the second summand requires calculations of Christoffel symbols.
From
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem II.4.14 follows

\displaystyle p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})

\displaystyle=p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}f_{i}\cdot\nabla_{f_{i}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}e_{i}\cdot p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}\left(\nabla^{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}}}\right)_{f_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}e_{i}\cdot\left(\partial_{f_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j<k}{}_{f}^{g_{R}}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}e_{j}e_{k}\right),

where
{}_{f}^{g_{R}}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}
are the Christoffel symbols for the Levi-Cevita connection of
g_{R}
expressed in the local orthonormal frame
f_{1},\dots,f_{n+1}
, and
\partial_{f_{j}}
is the usual partial differential in
f_{j}
-direction under the identification

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}})

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}\boxtimes Cl_{n,0})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)})\otimes Cl_{n,0}).

We also denote
f_{n+1}
by
f_{\varphi}
to emphasise the distinguished coordinate.
The vector fields
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are independent of
\varphi
, so the commutator
[f_{j},f_{\varphi}]
is given by

\displaystyle[f_{j},f_{\varphi}]=[f_{j},(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}]

\displaystyle=-(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{f_{j}}(r)\cdot\kappa_{R}\partial_{\varphi}

\displaystyle=-(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{f_{j}}(r)\kappa_{R}f_{\varphi}

The Koszul formula for the Christoffel symbols implies

{}^{g_{R}}{{}_{f}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}}={}^{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}{{}_{e}\Gamma_{ij}^{k}},\quad{}^{g_{R}}{{}_{f}\Gamma_{ij}^{\varphi}}={}^{g_{R}}{{}_{f}\Gamma_{\varphi j}^{k}}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad{}^{g_{R}}{{}_{f}\Gamma_{\varphi j}^{\varphi}}=(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\kappa_{R}\partial_{f_{j}}(r).

Thus, the second summand is given by

\displaystyle p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}

\displaystyle=p\mathcal{G}_{\ast}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}f_{j}\cdot\nabla_{f_{j}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{R}}}\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{n}e_{j}\cdot\nabla_{e_{j}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\left(\partial_{f_{\varphi}}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\kappa_{R}\partial_{f_{j}}(r)e_{j}\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\right)

\displaystyle=(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\left(\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{R}\mathrm{grad}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}(r)\cdot\right).

If we put everything together, we end up with

\displaystyle D=\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}=(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-2}r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})\partial_{\varphi}\cdot

as claimed.
∎

Corollary 4.8.3
.

Abbreviate
\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi)
to
\mathcal{G}
.
For
\bar{\rho}>5\rho^{\prime}
, define
N_{\bar{\rho}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=N\times(0,\bar{\rho})\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then there is a constant
C
only depending on
\bar{\rho}
such that, on
N_{\bar{\rho}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\left|\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})-Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}-\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\right|\right|_{1,0}\leq C/R.

In particular,
||\mathtt{P}_{R}\sigma||_{0}\geq c_{P}/12||\sigma||_{0}
for all sufficiently large
R
and all sections supported within
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

The previous lemma implies that

\displaystyle\quad\ \mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})-Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}-\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}=D-\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}

\displaystyle=-(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-1}r\kappa_{R}\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}-\frac{1}{2}(1+r\kappa_{R})^{-2}r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})\partial_{\varphi}\cdot.

Since
\kappa_{R}=1/R\cdot\kappa(\text{--}/R)
, we estimate the operator norm of
D-\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\partial/\partial\varphi
on
N_{\bar{\rho}}
as follows

\displaystyle\left|\left|D-\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}\right|\right|_{1,0}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\left|\frac{r\kappa_{R}}{1+r\kappa_{R}}\right|\right|_{0,0}\cdot\underbrace{\left|\left|\partial_{\varphi}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}\right|\right|_{1,0}}_{=1}+\frac{1}{2}\left|\left|\frac{r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa_{R})}{(1+r\kappa_{R})^{2}}\right|\right|_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{R}\left|\left|\frac{r(\kappa\circ(1/R))}{(1+r\kappa_{R})}\right|\right|_{\infty,N_{\bar{\rho}}}+\frac{1}{R^{2}}\left|\left|\frac{r(\partial_{\varphi}\kappa)\circ(1/R)}{(1+r\kappa_{R})^{2}}\right|\right|_{\infty,N_{\bar{\rho}}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\bar{\rho})/R.

Since
Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}
is a block Dirac operator, it satisfies the fundamental elliptic estimate.
This operator is further bounded from below by
c_{P}
.
By Lemma
4.3.13
, we find a constant
R_{0}
, only depending on
Q
, such that for all
R>R_{0}
the operators
\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
and
Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}
are sufficiently close on
N_{\bar{\rho}}
such that
||\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P_{R}})\sigma||_{0}>c_{P}/3||\sigma||_{0}
for all compactly supported sections inside of
N_{\bar{\rho}}
.
The gauge map
\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})
is a bundle map over the identity that induces an isometry between the corresponding Hilbert spaces of square integrable spinors, so the same holds for
\mathtt{P}_{R}
.

On
N_{>5\rho^{\prime}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>5\rho^{\prime}}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, the metrics
g_{R}
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}
decompose into

g_{R}|_{N_{>5\rho^{\prime}}}=\partial^{1}_{n}g\oplus\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)|_{N_{>5\rho^{\prime}}}=\partial^{1}_{n}g\oplus\mathrm{d}r^{2}\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2}

so that
\mathcal{G}(g_{R},\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})=\mathrm{id}_{N}\boxtimes\mathcal{G}(\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,\mathrm{d}r^{2}\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})
.
The operator
Q
decomposes there into

Q|_{N_{>5\rho^{\prime}}}=\partial^{1}_{n}Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>5\rho^{\prime}}}.

Tracing through the definitions now yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})

\displaystyle=(Q-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{r,2\rho^{\prime}}(g)})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}+\mathcal{G}_{\ast}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{R}})

\displaystyle=\partial^{1}_{n}Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>5\rho^{\prime}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes\mathcal{G}(\Xi_{R}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle,\mathrm{d}r^{2}\oplus\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2})_{\ast}\bigl(\Xi_{R}^{\ast}({\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{2}})\bigr),

from which we deduce that
\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P_{R}})^{2}\geq c_{\partial^{n}_{1}P}\mathrm{id}
is uniformly positive.
Hence
\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})
is bounded from below with lower bound
c_{\partial^{n}_{1}P}
.
By choosing the difference
\bar{\rho}-5\rho^{\prime}
sufficiently large, we can apply Corollary
4.3.22
to deduce that
||\mathcal{G}_{\ast}(\mathtt{P}_{R})\sigma||_{0}\geq c_{P}/12||\sigma||_{0}
for all compactly supported sections
\sigma
inside
N\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, which proves the addendum.
∎

Operator Theoretic Addition on the Operator Concordance Set

Since
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a Kan set, the (combinatorial) homotopy groups are well-defined and carry a combinatorical group structure.
This group structure is formally defined through the Kan condition and, as we do not keep track of the fillers, the group structure is only defined on homotopy classes.
We have constructed several geometric group structures on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
in subsection
3.4
that almost work on the level of representatives.
The analogous operations for invertible block Dirac operators have already been introduced in Definition
4.7.16
.
Recall that, for two invertible block operators
P
,
Q\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
with
\partial^{1}_{j}P=\partial^{-1}_{j}Q
whose cores are contained in
M\times\rho I^{n}
, the operator

P+_{j,R}Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(P)|_{\{x_{j}\leq 0\}}\cup\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(Q)|_{\{x_{j}\geq 0\}}

is a block Dirac operator over the block metric

\displaystyle g_{P+_{j,R}Q}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(g_{P})|_{\{x_{j}\leq 0\}}\cup\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(g_{Q})|_{\{x_{j}\geq 0\}}

\displaystyle=g_{P}+_{j,R}g_{Q}

for all
R>\rho
.
If
R
is sufficiently large, then the resulting block Dirac operator is also invertible.

The next theorem is the operator-theoretic analogue of Theorem
3.4.4
.

Theorem 4.8.4
.

There are
n
-many group structures on
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
that are defined as follows:
If
P,Q
are representatives of
[P],[Q]\in\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
and if
R
is sufficiently large, then we set

[P]+_{j}[Q]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[P+_{j,R}Q].

If
r_{j}
denotes the reflection at the hyperplane
\{x_{j}=0\}
, then the inverse element of
[P]
for
+_{j}
is given by
[r_{j}^{\ast}P]
.
The structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to each other.
Furthermore,
+_{1}
agrees with the group structure from cubical set theory.

Proof.

The entire proof is completely analogous to the proof of Theorem
3.4.4
except for a small technical nuisance in the proof that
+_{j,R}
is independent of
R
.

For two operators
P,Q
, let
R_{0}<R_{1}
be sufficiently large such that
P+_{j,R_{k}}Q
is defined.
We would like to construct a concordance between them.

If
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
is a smooth function with
\chi|_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\leq-1}=0
and
\chi|_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq S}}=1
with
|\chi^{\prime}|\leq/1/S
and
|\chi^{\prime\prime}|\leq 4/S^{2}
, then
P+_{j,R_{\chi}}Q
is a path over
g_{P}+_{j,R_{\chi}}g_{Q}
joining
P+_{j,R_{0}}Q
and
P+_{j,R_{1}}Q
.

If
\mathrm{susp}(P+_{j,R_{\chi}}Q)
were an element in
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P}+_{j,R}g_{Q})})
, then, provided
S
is sufficiently large, the operator
\mathrm{susp}(P+_{j,R_{\chi}}Q)
would be an invertible block Dirac operator and, hence, serve as a concordance.
We cannot apply Theorem
B.0.29
directly to the curve
P+_{j,R_{\chi}}Q
, so we have to employ the following “Swiss army knife trick”:
Consider the diffeomorphisms
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,\pm 2R_{\chi}}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
given by

\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R_{\chi}}\colon x\mapsto x+\Bigl((1-\chi(x_{n+1}))R_{0}+\chi(x_{n+1})R_{1}\Bigr)\cdot e_{j}.

Theorem
B.0.28
implies that
P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{P}\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{n+1}^{2}})
and similarly
Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{Q}\oplus\mathrm{d}x_{n+1}^{2}})
.
It follows that
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-R_{\chi}}(P\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R_{\chi}}(g)+\mathrm{d}x_{n+1}^{2}})
.
A similar result holds for
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R_{\chi}}(Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id})
.
Since
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}
is local, we conclude that

\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-R_{\chi}}(P\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\cup\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R_{\chi}}(Q\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P}+_{j,R_{\chi}}g_{Q})})

and hence
\mathrm{susp}(P+_{j,R_{\chi}}Q)\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{P}+_{j,R_{\chi}}g_{Q})})
because the difference between the two operators is a differential operator.
∎

Index Additivity for Block Dirac Operators

As an application of Proposition
4.7.17
, we prove an additivity formula for the Fredholm index of a block Dirac operator with invertible faces.
There are several theorems of this kind in the existing literature, see
[
booss1993elliptic
]
,
[
bunke1995Callias
]
,
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
.
The new features of our version is that it works for pseudo differential operators and, more importantly, for separating hypersurfaces that are non-compact.

Although Bunke
[
bunke1995Callias
]
was the first who proved such an additivity theorem, we follow in notation and strategy
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
.

For
\alpha\in\{0,\dots,3\}
, let
P_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)
be block Dirac operators with underlying block metrics
g_{\alpha}
.
For a fixed but arbitrary
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, assume that
\partial^{1}_{i}P_{\alpha}=\partial^{-1}_{i}P_{\beta}
for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\{(0,1),(2,3),(0,3),(2,1)\}
(this implies that the corresponding faces of the underlying metrics agree) and that all other faces are invertible.
Let
R_{0}
be a positive number such that the cores of all
P_{j}
lie in the interior of
M\times R_{0}I^{n}
.
For all
R>R_{0}
define
P_{\alpha\beta,R}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P_{\alpha}+_{R,i}P_{\beta}
.
These are block Dirac operators over
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\alpha\beta,R}}
, where
g_{\alpha\beta,R}=g_{\alpha}+_{R,i}g_{\beta}
.

Theorem 4.8.5

(Index Additivity)
.

There is an
R_{1}>R_{0}
such that, for all
R>R_{1}
, the block Dirac operators
P_{\alpha\beta,R}
have invertible faces and hence are unbounded Fredholm operators.
Their indices are independent of
R
, and, moreover, they satisfy the following additivity formula:

\mathrm{index}(P_{01})+\mathrm{index}(P_{23})=\mathrm{index}(P_{03})+\mathrm{index}(P_{21})\in KO^{-(d+n)}(\mathrm{pt}).

(4.9)

Proof.

Since the face operators commute with the gluing construction, we find, by Corollary
4.3.22
, a sufficiently large
R_{1}
such that
P_{\alpha\beta,R}
has invertible faces for all
R>R_{1}
.
Proposition
4.3.7
implies that
P_{\alpha\beta,R}
is a Fredholm operator.

The assignment

s\mapsto\mathcal{G}(g_{\alpha\beta,(1-s)R_{1}+sR_{2}},g_{\alpha\beta,R_{1}})_{\ast}\circ P_{\alpha\beta,(1-s)R_{1}+sR_{2}}\circ\mathcal{G}(g_{\alpha\beta,R_{1}},g_{\alpha\beta,(1-s)R_{1}+sR_{2}})_{\ast}

is a path of unbounded Fredholm operators between
P_{\alpha\beta,R_{1}}
and an operator that is unitarily equivalent to
P_{\alpha\beta,R_{2}}
.
Since the index is invariant under homotopy and unitary equivalence, we have
\mathrm{index}(P_{\alpha\beta,R_{1}})=\mathrm{index}(P_{\alpha\beta,R_{2}})
.

In the following, we will drop the suffix
R
from the Hilbert spaces and operators if it does not lead to confusion.
Set
H_{\alpha\beta}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{\alpha\beta}})
.
Let
H_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{op}}
be the
opposite
Clifford module with Clifford action
-\mathbf{r}
, the negative of the canonical right action, and equipped with the opposite grading
-\mathrm{e/o}
.
The operator
P_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the “same” as
P_{\alpha\beta}
, but acting on
H_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

By
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Lemma 2.20 the claimed formula is equivalent to

\mathrm{index}(P_{01})+\mathrm{index}(P_{23})+\mathrm{index}(P_{03}^{\mathrm{op}})+\mathrm{index}(P_{21}^{\mathrm{op}})=0.

(4.10)

The left hand side can be written as the index of a single operator
P
.
Indeed, let
H=H_{01}\oplus H_{23}\oplus H_{03}\oplus H_{21}
with Clifford action
r
and grading
\iota
be given by

r=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{r}_{01}&&&\\
&\mathbf{r}_{23}&&\\
&&-\mathbf{r}_{03}&\\
&&&-\mathbf{r}_{21}\end{bmatrix}_{,}\qquad\iota=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{e/o}_{01}&&&\\
&\mathrm{e/o}_{23}&&\\
&&-\mathrm{e/o}_{03}&\\
&&&-\mathrm{e/o}_{21}\end{bmatrix}_{.}

The operator
P
is then given by

P=\begin{bmatrix}P_{01}&&&\\
&P_{23}&&\\
&&P_{03}&\\
&&&P_{21}\end{bmatrix}_{.}

For later purpose, we merge the Sobolev spaces
H^{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=H^{1}_{01}\oplus H_{23}^{1}\oplus H^{1}_{03}\oplus H^{1}_{21}\subseteq H
.

Pick
\lambda_{0},\mu_{0}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mathrm{supp}\,\lambda_{0}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq-1}
,
\mathrm{supp}\,\mu_{0}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1}
and
\lambda_{0}^{2}+\mu_{0}^{2}=1
.
We may assume that
|\lambda_{0}^{\prime}|,|\mu_{0}^{\prime}|\leq 1
.
Define
\lambda_{R},\mu_{R}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
by
\lambda_{R}(x)=\lambda_{0}(R^{-1}x_{i})
and
\mu_{R}(x)=\mu_{0}(R^{-1}x_{i})
.
The operator

J_{0}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\begin{bmatrix}&&-\mu_{R}&-\lambda_{R}\\
&&-\lambda_{R}&\mu_{R}\\
\mu_{R}&\lambda_{R}&&\\
\lambda_{R}&-\mu_{R}&&\end{bmatrix}

is a well-defined operator on
H=H_{R}
because, for example, for the upper right corner, the restrictions agree

\mathfrak{S}_{g_{21}}|_{\mathrm{supp}\,\,\lambda_{R}}=\mathfrak{S}_{g_{1}}|_{\mathrm{supp}\,\lambda_{R}}=\mathfrak{S}_{g_{01}}|_{\mathrm{supp}\,\lambda_{R}}

and we can use these identities to transplant section from
H_{21}
to
H_{01}
.
Similar results hold for the other entries.

The operator
J\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=J_{0}\iota
is then a self-adjoint, Clifford-linear, odd (with respect to
\iota
), and bounded involution in
\mathrm{Fred}^{d+n,0}(H)
.
Note that, for algebraic reasons,
J
cannot be essentially positive or essentially negative.
To verify equation (
4.10
), it is enough to find a path in
\mathrm{Hom}(H^{1},H)
between
P
and
J
that take values in unbounded, self-adjoint, odd, Clifford-linear Fredholm operators.
We claim that such a path is given by

P_{s}\colon[0,\pi/2]\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(H^{1},H),\quad s\mapsto\cos(s)P+\sin(s)J.

Clearly, each
P_{s}
is self-adjoint, odd, Clifford-linear.
It remains to show that
P_{s}
is a Fredholm operator, provided
R
is sufficiently large.

The anti-commutator of
P
and
J
is the operator

\{P,J\}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=PJ+JP=\begin{bmatrix}&&-\mu_{R}^{\prime}&-\lambda_{R}^{\prime}\\
&&-\lambda_{R}^{\prime}&\mu_{R}^{\prime}\\
\mu_{R}^{\prime}&\lambda_{R}^{\prime}&&\\
\lambda_{R}^{\prime}&-\mu_{R}^{\prime}&&\end{bmatrix}\iota\partial_{x_{i}}

because
\lambda_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
are locally constant away from
\{-R<x_{i}<R\}
, and
P
acts as a differential operator in
x_{i}
-direction on the strip
\{-R<x_{i}<R\}
.
It is a bounded operator and, by increasing
R
, we may choose its operator norm to be arbitrarily small (but positive).
However, if
n>1
, the anti-commutator is
not
compact anymore, and this is the very reason why we now need to deviate from the proof presented in
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
.

The proof that
P_{s}
is Fredholm is similar to the proof of Proposition
4.3.7
.
However,
P_{s}
is not a block Dirac operator and mixes sections of different bundles, so we cannot formally deduce the Fredholm property from Proposition
4.3.7
.
However, due to the similarities, we will not repeat every detail.

Let us fix some notation: An element
u\in H
can be thought as a tuple with entries
u_{\alpha\beta}\in H_{\alpha\beta}
and we will not notionally distinguish a vector with a single non-zero entry from its entry.

We have, for all
u\in H^{1}
, the equation

||P_{s}u||^{2}_{0}=\cos(s)^{2}||Pu||_{0}^{2}+\sin(s)^{2}||u||_{0}^{2}+\cos(s)\sin(s)\langle\{P,J\}u,u\rangle

(4.11)

and analogously

||P_{s}u_{\alpha\beta}||^{2}_{0}=\cos(s)^{2}||P_{\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+\sin(s)^{2}||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+\cos(s)\sin(s)\langle\{P,J\}u_{\alpha\beta},u_{\alpha\beta}\rangle.

If
u_{\alpha\beta}
is supported within
\{\omega x_{j}>R\}
such that
P_{\alpha\beta}
decomposes there, then we have, by the invertibility of
\partial^{\omega}_{j}P_{\alpha\beta}
, the estimate

\displaystyle||P_{s}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq c(j,\omega)^{2}\cos(s)^{2}||u_{\alpha\beta}||^{2}_{1}+\sin(s)^{2}||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+\cos(s)\sin(s)\langle\{P,J\}u_{\alpha\beta},u_{\alpha\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\underbrace{\left(c(j,\omega)^{2}-||\{P,J\}||_{0,0}\right)}_{>0}||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{1}^{2}

because the constant
1>c(j,\omega)
can be chosen independently of
R
, see Corollary
4.3.22
.

Assume now that
u_{\alpha\beta}
is contained in a fixed compact neighbourhood
K
whose interior containes the core of
P_{\alpha\beta}
.
For
s\in[0,\pi/2)
, the classical elliptic estimate Lemma
4.3.2
and the relation between
||P_{s}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}
and
||P_{\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}
described by equation (
4.11
) yield

\displaystyle||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+||P_{\alpha\beta}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=C\bigl(||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+\cos(s)^{-2}||P_{s}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}-\tan(s)^{-2}||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}\\
&\qquad\ -\tan(s)\langle\{P,J\}u_{\alpha\beta},u_{\alpha\beta}\rangle\bigr)\end{split}

\displaystyle\leq\cos(s)^{-2}(1+||\{P,J\}||_{0,0})\cdot C(||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+||Pu_{\alpha\beta}||^{2}).

We now use the partition of unity
\{\phi_{l}\}_{l\in L}
from Lemma
4.3.3
to patch these inequalities together.
As in the proof of Proposition
4.3.7
, we have

\displaystyle||u||_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\beta}||u_{\alpha\beta}||_{1}^{2}\leq(2n+1)\sum_{\alpha\beta}\sum_{l\in L}||\phi_{l}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(2n+1)\sum_{\alpha\beta}\left(C_{\alpha\beta,0}(s)(||\phi_{0}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+||P_{s}\phi_{0}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2})+\sum_{L\setminus 0}C_{\alpha\beta,l}(s)||P_{s}\phi_{l}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(s)\sum_{\alpha\beta}\left(||\phi_{0}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+||P_{s}u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}+\sum_{L}||[P_{s},\phi_{l}\cdot]u_{\alpha\beta}||_{0}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=C(s)\left(||P_{s}u||_{0}^{2}+||\phi_{0}u||_{0}^{2}+\sum_{L}||[P_{s},\phi_{s}\cdot]u||_{0}^{2}\right).

Since
[P_{s},\phi_{l}\cdot]=\cos(s)[P,\phi_{l}\cdot]=\cos(s)\mathrm{grad}(\phi_{l})\cdot
we end up with

||u||_{1}^{2}\leq C(s)\left(||P_{s}u||_{0}^{2}+||\phi_{0}u||^{2}+\sum_{L}||\mathrm{grad}(\phi_{l})\cdot u||_{0}^{2}\right).

As before, the operators
\phi_{0}\cdot
and
\mathrm{grad}(\phi_{l})\cdot
are compact operators because block matrices of compact operators are compact operators.
Lemma
4.3.8
now yields that
P_{s}
has closed image and finite dimensional kernel.
Since
P_{s}
is self-adjoint, it must be Fredholm.

In conclusion,
s\mapsto P_{s}
is a continuous path between
P_{0}
and the invertible operator
J=P_{1}
.
Homotopy invariance of the index implies
\mathrm{index}(P)=\mathrm{index}(J)=0
and the theorem is proven.
∎

As in
[
gromov1983positive
]
, we deduce the following consequence.

Corollary 4.8.6
.

Let
(M^{d},g_{0})
be a closed spin manifold such that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
is invertible and let
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\chi(t)=0
for
t\ll 0
and
\chi(t)=1
for
t\gg 0
.
The index difference map
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))\rightarrow KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})
given by

P\mapsto\mathrm{index}\bigl(\chi\cdot\mathrm{susp}(\mathcal{G}(g_{P},g_{\chi})_{\ast}P)+(1-\chi)\cdot{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g_{\chi})}\bigr),

where
g_{\chi}=\chi\cdot g_{P}+(1-\chi)(g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}})
is the interpolating block map of Riemannian metrics,
is a well-defined map, in particular, independent of the interpolation function
\chi
.
Furthermore, the index map is a well-defined group homomorphism.

4.9
Comparison to real
K
-Theory

After the effort we have made to construct
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
and to prove several properties of it, we would like to know whether it is a classifying space for real
K
-theory.
Fortunately, this is the case, so we can give a precise formulation of Theorem
B
:

Theorem 4.9.1
.

Let
(M^{d},g_{0})
be a spin manifold such that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
is an invertible operator. Then the suspension

\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)}
\textstyle{B_{\bullet}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)}

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Since the canonical map
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}_{\bullet}}(M)
is a Kan fibration and the base space is combinatorially contractible, it suffices to show that the restriction to the fibre

\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}}
\textstyle{B_{\bullet,g_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}}

is a weak homotopy equivalence.

The proof will be carried out in two steps.
The first step is to compare the two different cubical sets of invertible pseudo Dirac operators to
KK
-theory.
More precisely, we will construct a diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(\text{--})\sharp\alpha_{n+1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})}

(4.12)

We will show in Theorem
4.9.9
below that the diagram commutes, from which we deduce that
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
is injective.

The second step is to show that
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
factors as follows:

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}).}

If
n\geq 1
, then these maps are group homomorphisms, so, as all
KO
-theory groups are cyclic,
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
must also be a surjective map.
If
n=0
, we show surjectivity of
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
“by hand”.

The idea to compare
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
against
KK
-theory is inspired from the PhD thesis of Lukas Buggisch
[
buggisch2019spectral
]
, where he shows that the index difference of Hitchin and Gromov-Lawson agree in the context of higher index theory.
More precisely, he establishes, for all smooth, closed manifolds
X
, a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[X,\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{inddif}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{inddif}_{GL}}
\textstyle{KK\bigl(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\times X,C^{\ast}\pi_{1}(M))\bigr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{Cl_{0,1}}(\text{--})\sharp\alpha_{1}}
\textstyle{KK\bigl(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathcal{C}(X,C^{\ast}\pi_{1}(M))\bigr).}

Unfortunately, his results and proofs do not allow an inductive treatment.
Furthermore, some of the proofs there rely on properties of the Dirac operator that do not carry over to general pseudo Dirac operators, like the Lichnerowicz formula.
This is the reason why we present a complete proof.

Kasparov KK-groups

We recall the basic facts of
KK
-theory that are needed for our proof.
We refrain from giving all details as they can be found, for example, in
[
kasparov1980operator
]
and
[
blackadar1998k
]
.

For separable, graded
C^{\ast}
-algebras
A,B
(real, Real, or complex) the abelian group
KK(A,B)
is defined as the set of homotopy classes of Kasparov (
A
-
B
)-modules
(E,\rho,F)
.
Recall that a Kasparov module consists of a graded, separable Hilbert
B
-right-module
E
, a graded
\ast
-homomorphism
\rho\colon A\rightarrow\mathbf{Lin}_{B}(E)
into the space of adjoinable, continuous
B
-right linear endomorphism of
E
, and an odd operator
F\in\mathbf{Lin}_{B}(E)
that satisfies the following identities modulo compact operators:

\rho(a)(F-F^{\ast})\equiv 0,\quad\rho(a)(F^{2}-\mathrm{id})\equiv 0,\quad[\rho(a),F]\equiv 0\qquad\mod\mathcal{K}_{B}.

Here,
\mathcal{K}_{B}
is the set of
B
-compact operators, which is defined as the closed linear hull of all
\theta_{e_{1},e_{2}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=e_{1}\cdot(\text{--},e_{2})
for all
e_{j}\in E
.
Recall further that a homotopy between two Kasparov (
A
-
B
)-modules
(E_{0},\rho_{0},F_{0})
and
(E_{1},\rho_{1},F_{1})
is a Kasparov (
A
-
\mathcal{C}([0,1],B)
)-module that, up to unitary equivalence, restricts to the given ones.

These groups generalise real
K
-homology and real
K
-theory of
C^{\ast}
-algebras in the sense that

KO^{d}(A)\cong KK(A\otimes Cl_{0,d},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\quad\text{ and }\quad KO_{d}(A)\cong KK(\mathord{\mathbb{R}},A\otimes Cl_{0,d}).

In fact, Kasparov proved that the groups
KK(A\otimes Cl_{p_{1},q_{2}},B\otimes Cl_{p_{2},q_{2}})
only depend on
d=(p_{1}-q_{2})-(q_{1}-p_{2})
, see
[
kasparov1980operator
]
*Theorem 5.4, and that the canonical homomorphism

\displaystyle\tau_{Cl_{p,q}}\colon KK(A,B)

\displaystyle\rightarrow KK(A\otimes Cl_{p,q},B\otimes Cl_{p,q}),

\displaystyle[E,\rho,F]

\displaystyle\mapsto[E\otimes Cl_{p,q},\rho\otimes\mathrm{id},F\otimes\mathrm{id}]

is an isomorphism
[
kasparov1980operator
]
*Proof of Theorem 5.2.

The connection to our set up is provided by the following isomorphism

\displaystyle[\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\cup\{\infty\},\{\infty\};\,\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}_{0}(H),GL_{0}(H)]

\displaystyle\rightarrow KK(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\mapsto[\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},H),\check{\rho},F],

see
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*App. A.
Here,
H
is a graded, ample Hilbert
Cl_{d,0}
-right-module with right action
\rho
and grading
\iota
.
The
Cl_{0,d}
left-action
\check{\rho}
is the unique one that is defined on the level of vectors by
\check{\rho}(v)=\rho(v)\circ\iota
.
The space
\mathrm{Fred}_{0}^{d,0}(H)
is the weakly equivalent subspace consisting of all operators with
F^{2}-\mathrm{id}
compact and
GL_{0}(H)
is the subspace of involutions
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*App. A.

For later purposes, it will be useful to describe an explicit isomorphism between
KK(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
and
KO^{-p}(\mathrm{pt})
.
Let
\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{r,s}
be the abelian monoid of isomorphism classes of finitely generated, graded
Cl_{r,s}
-modules.
Recall that the monoids
\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{r,s}
and
\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{s,r}
are canonically isomorphic.
Indeed, if
\rho\colon Cl_{r,s}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(W,\iota)
is a graded algebra homomorphism, where
\iota
is the grading, then the map
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{r,s}\ni v\mapsto\rho(v)\circ\iota
extends to a graded algebra homomorphism
\check{\rho}\colon Cl_{s,r}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(W,\iota)
.
The assignment
\rho\mapsto\check{\rho}
provides an example of this isomorphism.

Recall that
KO^{-p}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{p,0}/\mathrm{incl}^{\ast}\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{p+1,0}\cong\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{0,p}/\mathrm{incl}^{\ast}\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{0,p+1}
.
The first isomorphism can be found in
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Section 1.9 or
[
eschenburg2021bott
]
, the second one is induced by the isomorphism just constructed.

Lemma 4.9.2
.

The map

KK(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow KO^{-p}(\mathrm{pt})\quad\text{ given by }\quad[E,\rho,F]\mapsto[\ker(F)]

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We may assume that
F
is self-adjoint and graded-commutes with
Cl_{0,p}
, see
[
blackadar1998k
]
*Section 17.4.
Since
Cl_{0,p}
is a unital algebra, the identity

\rho(1)\cdot(F^{2}-\mathrm{id})=(F^{2}-\mathrm{id})\equiv 0\quad\mod\mathcal{K}_{Cl_{0,p}}

implies that
F
is a Fredholm operator.
The kernel is therefore a finite-dimensional
Cl_{0,p}
-module.

To see that the map is well-defined, we work with
KK_{oh}(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
instead of
KK(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
.
The former group is the group of equivalence classes of Kasparov modules of the equivalence relation generated by operator homotopy and by adding degenerate
KK
-modules.
Recall that an operator homotopy is a
Cl_{0,p}
-
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
-module
(E,\rho,F_{t})
, where the Hilbert space
E
and the representation
\rho
are independent of
t\in I
.
The canonical map
KK_{oh}(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow KK(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
is an isomorphism, see
[
blackadar1998k
]
*p.148.

The operators of degenerate modules have trivial kernel, and two operator homotopic operators
F_{0}
,
F_{1}
represent the same element in
\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{0,p}/\mathrm{incl}^{\ast}\hat{\mathfrak{M}}_{0,p+1}
, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem III.10.8.
In conclusion, the map is well-defined.

The map clearly surjective: Each element
[M,\rho]
is the image of
[M,\rho,0]+x_{\mathrm{deg}}
(the degenerate module
x_{\mathrm{deg}}
is needed to assure that our Hilbert space is ample).

To see that the map is also injective, assume that the
Cl_{0,p}
-module structure on
\ker(F)
extends to a
Cl_{0,d+1}
-module structure.
If
\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{\ker(F)}\colon E\rightarrow\ker(F)
denotes the orthogonal projection onto the kernel, then it is easy to check that
[E,\rho,F+\rho(e_{n+1})\circ\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{\ker(F)}]
is a degenerate Kasparov module.
Furthermore,
[E,\rho,F_{t}]
with
F_{t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=F+t\rho(e_{n+1})\circ\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{\ker(F)}
is an operator homotopy between these two Kasparov modules.
Thus,
[E,\rho,F]
already represents the zero element in
KK(Cl_{0,p},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
, so the map is injective.
∎

The advantage of Kasparov’s
KK
-groups is the existence of a Kasparov product, which is a bilinear pairing

\sharp_{D}\colon KK(A_{1},B_{1}\otimes D)\otimes KK(A_{2}\otimes D,B_{2})\rightarrow KK(A_{1}\otimes A_{2},B_{1}\otimes B_{2}).

There are elements

\alpha_{n}=[L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{n,0}),\check{\mathbf{r}},{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(1+{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}^{2})^{-1/2}]\in KK(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{0,n}),\mathord{\mathbb{R}})=KO_{n}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})

and

\beta_{n}=[\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{0,n}),\lambda,\frac{\lambda(x)}{(1+||x||^{2})^{1/2}}]\in KK(\mathord{\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{0,n}))=KO^{-n}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),

where
\lambda
denotes left multiplication, that satisfy

\alpha_{n}\sharp\beta_{n}=1\in KK\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{0,n}),\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{0,n})\bigr)\text{ and }\beta_{n}\sharp\alpha_{n}=1\in KK(\mathord{\mathbb{R}},\mathord{\mathbb{R}}).

A detailed proof for the complex case with
n=1
can be found in
[
cuntz2017ktheory
]
*p.99-105.
The real case for each
n
can be reduced to the cited one because the Kasparov product commutes with complexification
[
kasparov1980operator
]
*Theorem 4.4.2 and the complexified elements
\alpha_{n}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}=\alpha_{1}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\sharp_{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\dots\sharp_{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\alpha_{1}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}
as well as
\beta_{n}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}=\beta_{1}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\sharp_{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\dots\sharp_{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}\beta_{1}^{\mathord{\mathbb{C}}}
, see
[
kasparov1980operator
]
*p.546-547.
Kasparov proved that

(\text{--})\sharp\alpha_{n}\colon KK(A,B\otimes\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};Cl_{0,n}))\rightarrow KK(A,B)

is an isomorphism
9
9

9

Here, we applied the Kasparov product with
D=\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};Cl_{0,n})
, and
A_{2}=B_{2}=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.
,
[
kasparov1980operator
]
*Theorem 5.7.

In general, it is hard to compute Kasparov products.
To reduce this difficulty, we will use unbounded Kasparov modules introduced by Baaj and Julg
[
baaj1983theorie
]
, which we refer to as
spectral triples
following the convention of
[
cuntz2007topological
]
.

Definition 4.9.3
.

Let
A
and
B
be separable, graded, real
C^{\ast}
-algebras.
An
A
-
B

spectral triple

(E,\rho,D)
consists of a graded Hilbert
B
-right module
E
, together with a graded
\ast
-homomorphism
\rho\colon A\rightarrow\mathbf{Lin}_{B}(E)
into the set of continuous, adjoinable,
B
-right linear operators, and an odd, self-adjoint and regular
B
-right linear map
D
that satisfies:

(1)

The graded commutator
[\rho(a),D]
extends to a bounded operator for all elements
a
in a dense subset of
A
.

(2)

The operator
\rho(a)(1+D^{2})^{-1}
is compact
10
10

10

Compact in the Hilbert
C^{\ast}
-module sense,
\mathrm{i.e.}
,
\rho(a)(1+D^{2})^{-1}\in\mathcal{K}_{B}\subset\mathbf{Lin}_{B}(E)
for all
a\in A
.

A
homotopy
between two
A
-
B
spectral triples
(E_{j},\rho_{j},D_{j})
with
j=0,1
is an
A
-
\mathcal{C}([0,1];B)
spectral triple
(E,\rho,D)
that restricts to given ones.
We will simply use the term
spectral triple
if the
C^{\ast}
-algebras
A
and
B
are clear from the context and denote the set of homotopy classes of all spectral triples by
\Psi(A,B)
.

Baaj and Julg proved that the bounded transform

b\colon\Psi(A,B)\rightarrow KK(A,B)\quad\text{given by}\quad(E,\rho,D)\mapsto\bigl(E,\rho,D/(1+D^{2})^{1/2}\bigr)

is surjective
[
baaj1983theorie
]
.
The following result of Kucerovski gives a sufficient criterium for a spectral triple to represent a Kasparov product.

Theorem 4.9.4

(
[
kucerovsky1997kk
]
*Lemma 10, Theorem 13)
.

Let
(E_{1},\rho_{1},D_{1})\in\Psi(A,C)
and
(E_{2},\rho_{2},D_{2})\in\Psi(C,B)
be two spectral triples.
Assume
E=(E_{1}\otimes_{\rho_{2}}E_{2},\rho_{1}\otimes\mathrm{id},D)\in\Psi(A,B)
satisfies the following properties:

(i)

For all
x
in a dense subset of
\rho_{1}(A)E_{1}
of pure degree, the operators

DT_{x}-(-1)^{\mathrm{deg}(x)}T_{x}D_{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad D_{2}T_{x}^{\ast}-(-1)^{\mathrm{deg}(x)}T_{x}^{\ast}D

extend to bounded operators, where
T_{x}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=x\otimes(\text{--})
.

(ii)

\mathrm{dom}\,D\subseteq\mathrm{dom}\,D_{1}
.

(iii)

There is a
c\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\langle Dx,(D_{1}\otimes_{\rho_{2}}\mathrm{id})x\rangle_{B}+\langle(D_{1}\otimes_{\rho_{2}}\mathrm{id})x,Dx\rangle_{B}\geq c\langle x,x\rangle_{A}

for all all
x\in\mathrm{dom}D
.

Then the Kasparov module
(E_{1}\otimes_{\rho_{2}}E_{2},\rho_{1}\otimes\mathrm{id},D/(1+D^{2})^{1/2})
represents the Kasparov product of
(E_{1},\rho_{1},D_{1}/(1+D_{1}^{2})^{1/2})
and
(E_{2},\rho_{2},D_{2}/(1+D_{2}^{2})^{1/2})
.

Construction of the comparison maps

We will obtain the horizontal maps in diagram (
4.12
) by constructing spectral triples.
First, recall that a dice function adapted to
\rho>0
is a function

\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

that
\partial_{j}\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}(x)\neq 0
only if
|x_{j}|>\rho
.
We may choose (and do so) the function to be proper, to vanish near the origin, and to have a bounded total differential, see Section
3.2
for a construction.

Construction of

\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\Psi(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))
:

Pick a smooth function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\chi(t)=1
for
t\gg 0
and
\chi(t)=0
for
t\ll 0
.
For a given element
P\in\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
, which is a smooth block map
P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)_{g_{0}}
that restricts to
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
outside of
\rho I^{n}
for some
\rho>0
, define the block map

Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)_{g_{0}}\quad\text{ via }\quad(x,t)\mapsto\chi(t)P+(1-\chi(t)){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}.

For the chosen
\rho
, define

h=h_{\rho}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\quad\text{ via }\quad x\mapsto(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}(x)^{2})^{1/4}.

Lemma 4.9.5
.

Let
\mathbf{r}
be the canonical
Cl_{d,0}
-right action on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}
and let
\check{\mathbf{r}}
be the induced
Cl_{0,d}
-left action.
The triple
(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},L^{2}(M,\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})),\check{\mathbf{r}},hQh)
is a
Cl_{0,d}
-
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
spectral triple.

Proof.

The canonical isomorphism

L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\otimes_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\cong\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1};L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))\quad\text{ induced by }\quad f\otimes\sigma\mapsto f\cdot\sigma,

where
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
is trivially graded, identifies the right hand side as a Hilbert
Cl_{0,d}
-
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
-bimodule.

Considered as an operator on
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))
, the block map
hQh
is self-adjoint as a
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
-right linear map because it is point-wise self-adjoint.

The operator-valued map
Q
is
Cl_{d,0}
-right linear, so it graded commutes with the associated
Cl_{0,d}
-left action.
Indeed, it suffices to prove it for vectors
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\subseteq Cl_{0,d}
, which is easily calculated

\displaystyle Q\circ\check{\mathbf{r}}(v)

\displaystyle=Q\circ\mathbf{r}(v)\circ\mathrm{e/o}=\mathbf{r}(v)\circ Q\circ\mathrm{e/o}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{r}(v)\circ\mathrm{e/o}\circ Q=-\check{\mathbf{r}}(v)\circ Q.

Thus, the first condition of a spectral triple is satisfied.

Since
Q
is a block map that takes values in invertible operators outside a compact subset of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, there is a
c_{Q}>0
such that
||Q_{x}\sigma||_{0}^{2}\geq c_{Q}^{2}||\sigma||_{0}^{2}
for all
\sigma\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
and all
x
in the complement of this compact subset.
Together with
hQ=Qh
, this implies

\displaystyle(hQh)^{2}

\displaystyle=h^{4}Q^{2}=(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}(x)^{2})Q^{2}\geq c_{Q}^{2}(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}(x)^{2})

for all
x
outside the given compact subset.
Since the manifold
M
is closed and since
hQh\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
takes values in elliptic operators,
(1+hQh)^{-1}
is a (norm-)continuous map of compact operators that vanishes near
\infty
by the previous inequiality.
Thus,
(1+hQh)^{-1}
is a
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
-compact operator, so the second condition of a spectral triple is also satisfied.
∎

Different choices yield homotopic spectral triples
11
11

11

to spell it out: Different choices of
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}
, the representative
P
of
[P]
, and the interpolating path between
P
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
can be joined by paths and these paths produce operator homotopies of spectral triples.
,
so we get a well-defined map

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\Psi(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))\quad\text{ given by }\quad[P]\mapsto[hQh].

Lemma 4.9.6
.

The composition with the bounded transform

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Psi(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))}

is an isomorphism if
n\geq 1
and a bijection if
n=0
.

Proof.

Abbreviate
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\cup\{\infty\}
to
S^{n+1}
and
\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0}(L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))
to
\mathrm{Fred}^{d}
.
Consider the following diagram

\textstyle{[S^{n+1},\{\infty\};\,\mathrm{Fred}_{0}^{d},GL_{0}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{P\mapsto b(Q)}
\scriptstyle{P\mapsto b(hQh)}
\textstyle{[S^{n+1},\{\infty\};\,\mathrm{Fred}^{d},GL].}

The inclusion induces a bijection on homotopy classes by a spectral deformation argument, see
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Appendix A or Atiyah-Singer
[
atiyah1969indexskew
]
*p.12.
The lower horizontal map represents the index difference, see Theorem
4.1.41
,
so it is also an isomorphism, respectively, a bijection.
Lastly, the upper horizontal map is an isomorphism that is nicely described in
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Appendix A.
If
H=(H,\rho,\iota)
is a Hilbert space with an ample
Cl_{d,0}
-right action
\rho
and grading
\iota
, then the map is given by

\bigl(F\colon(S^{n+1},\infty)\rightarrow(\mathrm{Fred}^{d,0},GL_{0})\bigr)\quad\mapsto\quad\bigl(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},H),\check{\rho},F\bigr)\in\Psi(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})).

From this description, it is easy to see that the composition agrees with the composition from the statement.

It remains to prove that the diagram commutes.
Since
h
and
Q
commute and
h
is a positive function, the operators
Q
and
hQh
have the same kernel and the same positive and negative subspace.
Functional calculus now implies that

s\mapsto sb(Q)+(1-s)b(hQh)

is a homotopy between
\mathrm{incl}\circ b(Q)
and
b(hQh)
with values in Fredholm operators because the kernel is independent of
s
and finite dimensional.
∎

Construction of
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\Psi(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
:

Pick a smooth function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
that satisfies
\chi(t)=1
for
t\gg 0
and
\chi(t)=0
for
t\ll 0
.
For each element
P\in\Omega_{g_{0}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
, we define a block map of block Dirac operators

Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{n}(M)_{g_{0}}\quad\text{ by }\quad t\mapsto\chi(t)P+(1-\chi(t)){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}.

Its suspension
