\mathrm{susp}(Q)=\chi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{n+1}\cdot P^{\mathrm{ex}}+(1-\chi\circ\mathrm{pr}_{n+1})\cdot{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}}+\partial_{x_{n+1}}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n+1}}

is a block Dirac operator on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}}\rightarrow M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
For
\rho>0
such that the core of
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
is contained in
\rho I^{n+1}
, define the function

h\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=h_{\rho}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}^{2})^{1/4}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}.

The action
\check{\mathbf{r}}
turns
L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
into a Hilbert
Cl_{0,d+n+1}
-
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
-bimodule on which
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
acts as an odd, symmetric, unbounded operator whose domain is the dense subspace of all compactly supported smooth sections.

Lemma 4.9.7
.

The triple
(L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}),\check{\mathbf{r}},h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)
is a
Cl_{0,d+n+1}
-
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
spectral triple.

Proof.

We need to show that

(o)

the closure of operator
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
is self-adjoint,

(i)

that the graded commutator
[\check{\mathbf{r}}(v),h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h]
extends to a bounded operator for all
v\in Cl_{0,n+d+1}
, and

(ii)

that
\check{\mathbf{r}}(v)(1+(hQh)^{2})^{-1}
is compact for all
v\in Cl_{0,n+d+1}
.

The prove of (o) is similar to the proof of
[
higson2000analytic
]
*Proposition 10.2.10:
Since
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
decomposes outside of
\rho I^{n+1}
, it acts as differential operator on
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}
and
h=h_{\rho}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})]

\displaystyle=[h^{2}\mathrm{susp}(Q),\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})]+[\mathrm{grad}(h)\cdot,\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})]

\displaystyle=h^{2}[\mathrm{susp}(Q),\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})]

\displaystyle=h^{2}\mathrm{grad}(\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}))\cdot

\displaystyle=(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}^{2})^{1/2}(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})^{-1}\mathrm{grad}(\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})\cdot.

We chose
\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}
to be proper and to have bounded derivative, so the commutator
[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})]
extends to a bounded operator.
With the help of
\log(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})
we find smooth functions
\varphi_{n}\colon M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that
\varphi_{n}\rightarrow 1
uniformly on a compact set and such that
[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\varphi_{n}]\rightarrow 0
in norm.

Assume now that
u\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}})
lies in the maximal domain.
Since
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
is a block operator, it maps sections supported in the interior
M\times RI^{n+1}
to sections that are also supported there, hence

\displaystyle h\bigl(\mathrm{susp}(Q)\bigr)hfu=f\cdot h\bigl(\mathrm{susp}(Q)\bigr)hu+[h\bigl(\mathrm{susp}(Q)\bigr)h,f]u

is also bounded for all compactly supported functions
f
.
The operator
h(\mathrm{susp}(Q)-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}})h
is bounded on compact sets, so the proof of
[
higson2000analytic
]
*Lemma 10.2.5 implies that
fu
is also in the minimal domain of
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
, because we can choose Friedrichs’ mollifiers in a manner so that they map sections supported in
\mathrm{supp}\,(f)
to sections supported in a fixed compact neighbourhood
L
of
\mathrm{supp}\,(f)
.
In particular,
\varphi_{n}u
belongs to the minimal domain of
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
.
Since
u
is square integrable, we have
\varphi_{n}u\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}u
in norm, while

h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h\varphi_{n}u=\varphi_{n}h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu+[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\varphi_{n}]u\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}u,

so
u
belongs to the minimal domain of
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
.

Condition (i) is satisfied because
[\check{\mathbf{r}}(v),h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h]=0
.

The proof of condition (ii) is borrowed from
[
ebert2016elliptic
]
*Theorem 2.40.
The difference to the proof presented there is that we avoid to use coercive functions.

Note that the restriction of
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
to
\{\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
is an operator that is bounded from below in the sense of Definition
4.3.11
for all
(i,\varepsilon)\in\{1,\dots,n+1\}\times\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
Corollary
4.3.22
applied inductively to all restrictions of
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
implies
||\mathrm{susp}(Q)u||_{0}\geq c||u||_{0}
for some
c>0
and all sections
u\in\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
that are supported in the complement of
\rho^{\prime}I^{n+1}
for all sufficiently large
\rho^{\prime}\geq\rho
.
To keep the notation readable, we assume without loss of generality
\rho^{\prime}=\rho
.
Thus, if
\chi
is a smooth function with compact support in the complement of
\rho I^{n+1}
, then we have

\displaystyle||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h\chi u||_{0}\cdot||\chi u||_{0}\geq c||h\chi u||_{0}\cdot||\chi u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq c\inf\{h(t)\,:\,t\in\mathrm{supp}\,\chi\}||\chi u||_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{low}\cdot||\chi u||_{0}^{2}.

Since
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
is a block Dirac operator over
g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}
, the operator
\mathrm{susp}(Q)-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}}
is bounded, which implies that the commutator
[\mathrm{susp}(Q),\chi]
is a bounded operator.
By Lemma
4.2.6
,
\mathrm{susp}(Q)u
is supported in
M\times RI^{n+1}
if
u
is supported there for all sufficient large
R>0
.
Thus, if
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi
is contained in
RI^{n+1}
, then the operator
[\mathrm{susp}(Q),\chi]
is supported in
M\times RI^{n+1}
, too.
It follows that
[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]=h[\mathrm{susp}(Q),\chi]h
also extends to a bounded operator whose operator norm is bounded by

||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0}\leq\sup\{h(t)^{2}\,:\,t\in\mathrm{supp}\,([\mathrm{susp}(Q),\chi])\}\cdot||[\mathrm{susp}(Q),\chi]||_{0,0}.

Combining the two inequalities yields

\displaystyle||\chi u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h\chi u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}(||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]u||_{0}+||\chi h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu||_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\left(||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0}\cdot||u||_{0}+||\chi||_{\infty}||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu||_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}\cdot\left(||u||_{0}+||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h||_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}\left(||u||_{0}^{2}+||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

If we interpret
Q
as a complex linear operator on the complexification
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{C}}
, we can extend the previous chain of inequalities

\displaystyle||\chi u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}\left(||u||_{0}^{2}+||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}||(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h+i)u||_{0}

for all real sections
u\in\Gamma_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{C}})
.
Since
h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h
is self-adjoint, its spectrum is real so that
(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h\pm i)^{-1}
exists and its operator norm is bounded by
1
.
Thus, by substituting
u=(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h+i)^{-1}v
, the previous inequality becomes

||\chi(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h+i)^{-1}v||_{0}\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}||v||_{0},

which implies

\displaystyle||\chi\cdot(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{-2})||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq||\chi(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h+i)^{-1}||_{0,0}\cdot||(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h+i)^{-1}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\chi]||_{0,0},||\chi||_{\infty}\}.

Pick
\tilde{\eta}_{0}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
that is compactly supported and identically
1
on the interval
[0,a]
, where
a\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\max\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}((\rho+1)I^{n+1})
.
If
||\mathrm{d}\,\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}||_{\infty}\leq B
, then the mean value theorem gives
|\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}(x)|\leq B||x||
for all
x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
For all
m\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
, pick a smooth function
\tilde{\eta}_{m}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow[0,1]
that satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}_{m}|_{[0,a+(m-1)m/2]}=1,\ \tilde{\eta}_{m}|_{[a+m(m+1)/2,\infty)}=0,\ \text{and }\left|\tilde{\eta}_{m}^{\prime}|_{a+[(m-1)m/2,m(m+1)/2]}\right|\leq 2/m,

where the interval
[(m-1)m/2,m(m+1)/2]
has length
m
.
Set
\eta_{m}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\tilde{\eta}_{m}\circ\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}
.
Since
\eta_{m}
is constant on
\rho I^{n+1}
, we have
[\mathrm{susp}(Q),h]=\mathrm{grad}(h)
.
Recall that
h_{\rho}=(1+\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}^{2})^{1/4}
.
We estimate

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl|[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h,\eta_{m}]\bigr|\bigr|_{0,0}

\displaystyle=\bigl|\bigl|[h^{2}\mathrm{susp}(Q),\eta_{m}]+[h\mathrm{grad}(h)\cdot,\eta_{m}]\bigr|\bigr|_{0,0}

\displaystyle=||h^{2}\mathrm{grad}(\eta_{m})\cdot||_{0,0}=||h^{2}\tilde{\eta}_{m}^{\prime}\circ\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}\cdot\mathrm{grad}(\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq||h^{2}||_{\infty,\mathrm{supp}\,\eta_{m}}\cdot||\eta^{\prime}_{m}\circ\mathrm{dice}_{\rho}\cdot\mathrm{grad}(\mathrm{dice}_{\rho})||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\left(\frac{a+m(m+1)}{2}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/4}\frac{2B}{m}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{C}_{a}\cdot B.

The operator
\eta_{0}(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}
is compact because it factors through
H^{1}_{\mathrm{supp}\,\eta_{0}}
, the space of elements in
H^{1}
supported within
\mathrm{supp}\,\eta_{0}
,
so it remains to check that
(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}
is compact.
Clearly, the sequence converges pointwise:

\eta_{m}(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}.

The limit is also the norm limit of
\eta_{m}(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}
by the following estimate:

\displaystyle\quad\ ||(\eta_{l}-\eta_{m})(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle=||(\eta_{l}-\eta_{m})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\max\{||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h||_{0,0},||\eta_{l}-\eta_{m}||_{\infty}\}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{low}^{-1}\sqrt{2}\mathrm{C}_{a}\max\{B,1\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup\{h(t)^{-1}\,:t\in\mathrm{supp}\,(\eta_{l}-\eta_{m})\}/c\cdot\sqrt{2}\mathrm{C}_{a}\max\{B,1\}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0,

because
h
is proper and
\mathrm{supp}\,(\eta_{l}-\eta_{m})
lies in the complement of
(a+m(m+1)/2)I^{n+1}
.

In conclusion,
(1-\eta_{0})(1+(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)^{2})^{-1}
is compact as it is the norm limit of a sequence of compact operators.
∎

Again, different admissible choices yield homotopic spectral triples, so we get a well-defined map

\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})\rightarrow\Psi(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\quad\text{via}\quad[P]\mapsto[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h].

Lemma 4.9.8
.

The map just defined composed with the bounded transform
b
is a group homomorphism provided
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[P]\mapsto[hQh]}
\scriptstyle{[P]\mapsto\ker(Q)}
\textstyle{\Psi(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\ker(\text{--})}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt}).}

The vertical map is the one from Lemma
4.9.2
and therefore an isomorphism.
The diagonal map is a group homomorphism by Corollary
4.8.6
.
Thus, the horizontal composition must be a group homomorphism, too.
∎

Unbounded representatives of

\alpha_{n+1}
:

The spectral triple
(L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0}),\check{\mathbf{r}},{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}})\in\Psi(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{0,n+1}),\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
is well known.
Its bounded transform represents the fundamental class
\alpha_{n+1}\in KO_{n+1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
because its complexification represents the fundamental class in
K_{n+1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
, see
[
higson2000analytic
]
*p.314ff, and complexification induces an isomorphism
KO_{n+1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\rightarrow K_{n+1}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
.

When we calculate the Kasparov product, we will use
h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h
instead of
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}
.
This is unproblematic because the bounded transform of these two operators over
(L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0}),\check{\mathbf{r}})
represent the same class in
KK(\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{0,n+1}),\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
.
Indeed, pick a small open ball
rD^{n+1}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
such that
h=1
on this set.
Extension by zero
j\colon\mathcal{C}_{0}(rD^{n+1})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})
is a homotopy equivalence (in the sense of
C^{\ast}
-algebras) because
rD^{n+1}
and
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
are properly homotopy equivalent.
By
[
higson2000analytic
]
*Proposition 10.8.8 and that
h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h
and
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}
agree on
rD^{n+1}
, we have

j^{\ast}\bigl(b[{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}]\bigr)=[\![{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}|_{rD^{n+1}}]\!]=j^{\ast}\bigl(b[h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h]\bigr)\in KK(\mathcal{C}_{0}(rD^{n+1},Cl_{0,n+1}),\mathord{\mathbb{R}}).

Here,
[\![{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}|_{rD^{n+1}}]\!]
denotes the K-homology class that is defined in
[
higson2000analytic
]
*Definition 10.8.3.
The claim follows now from homotopy invariance of
KK
-theory, see
[
blackadar1998k
]
*Proposition 17.9.1.

From now on, we will denote a spectral triple only by its operator if the underlying Hilbert
C^{\ast}
-module and the representation are clear from the context.

Calculation of the Kasparov Product

We have constructed the horizontal homomorphisms in diagram (
4.12
), so it remains to show that it commutes.
If we choose the same interpolation function
\chi
in the construction of the upper and lower horizontal arrow, we have

\mathrm{susp}\left(\chi\mathrm{susp}(P)+(1-\chi){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\right)=\mathrm{susp}(\chi P+(1-\chi){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}).

It thus remains to show
\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(b([hQh]))\sharp\alpha_{n+1}=b([h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h])
.

The first step is to observe that the underlying Hilbert
C^{\ast}
-modules are canonically isomorphic

\displaystyle\quad\ \mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))\otimes Cl_{0,n+1}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\otimes Cl_{0,n+1},\mathrm{\mathrm{taut}}\otimes\check{\mathbf{r}}}L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}),\mathrm{taut}}L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})

\displaystyle\cong L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\boxtimes Cl_{n+1,0})\cong L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}),

where
\mathrm{taut}
is the left action of
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
on
L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})
via left multiplication.
The first isomorphism is the universal one, the second isomorphism is induced by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{c}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}))\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})}\Gamma_{c}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\boxtimes Cl_{n+1,0}),

\displaystyle\sigma\otimes\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\left((m,x)\mapsto\sigma(x)(m)\otimes\varphi(x)\right).

The next step is to show that
[h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h]
represents the Kasparov product of
\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(b([hQh]))
and
\alpha_{n+1}=b([h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h])
by showing that it satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.9.4
.

Theorem 4.9.9
.

For each
[P]\in\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
, let
Q\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\chi P+(1-\chi){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
for some
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\chi(t)=1
for
t\gg 0
and
\chi(t)=0
for
t\ll 0
.
Then we have

\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(b([hQh]))\sharp\alpha_{n+1}=b(\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}([hQh]))\sharp\alpha_{n+1}=b([h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h]).

Proof.

To verify the first condition of Theorem
4.9.4
, it suffices to show that

h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hT_{\sigma}+(-1)^{\mathrm{deg}(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h

extends to a bounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
of pure degree because, in our case, the second operator in condition
(i)
of Theorem
4.9.4
is the adjoint of the first operator.
Recall that
T_{\sigma}
was defined as

T_{\sigma}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sigma\otimes(\text{--})\colon L^{2}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1},Cl_{n+1,0})\rightarrow L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}).

We abuse notation by abbreviating
\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}
to
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}
.
The Leibniz rule implies that the graded commutator
[{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}},T_{\sigma}]
is given by

\displaystyle[{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}},T_{\sigma}]

\displaystyle={\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}T_{\sigma}-(-1)^{\mathrm{deg}(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\mathrm{deg}(\sigma)}\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{x_{j}}(\sigma)\otimes\partial_{j}\cdot

\displaystyle=:{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(\sigma)\cdot.

We use this identity to compute

\displaystyle\quad\ \,h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hT_{\sigma}-(-1)^{\deg(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h

\displaystyle=h(Q^{\mathrm{ex}}+{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}})hT_{\sigma}-(-1)^{\deg(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h

\displaystyle=hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hT_{\sigma}+h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(\sigma)\cdot h+(-1)^{\deg(\sigma)}hT_{\sigma}{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h-(-1)^{\deg(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h

\displaystyle=hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hT_{\sigma}+h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(\sigma)\cdot h.

Since
\sigma
is compactly supported, Fubini’s theorem and the triangle inequality yield

\displaystyle||h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hT_{\sigma}-(-1)^{\deg(\sigma)}T_{\sigma}h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h||_{0,0}\leq\left(||Q^{\mathrm{ex}}\sigma||_{0}+||{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}\sigma||_{0}\right)\cdot||h^{2}||_{\infty,\mathrm{supp}\,\,\sigma},

which proves the first condition of Theorem
4.9.4
.

The second condition
\mathrm{dom}(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h)\subseteq\mathrm{dom}(hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}h)
is clearly satisfied because, by definition,
{\mathrm{dom}\,}(\mathrm{susp}(h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h))={\mathrm{dom}\,}(hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}h)\cap{\mathrm{dom}\,}(h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h)
.

To prove the third condition, we need to show that there is a
c\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\langle hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu\rangle+\langle h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu,hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu\rangle\geq c||u||_{0}^{2}

(4.13)

for all
u\in\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1};\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
.
Recall the identity

\displaystyle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}Q^{\mathrm{ex}}+Q^{\mathrm{ex}}{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}\cdot(\partial_{j}Q)^{\mathrm{ex}}=:{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}}).

from the proof of Lemma
4.5.5
and that
h
and
Q^{\mathrm{ex}}
commute.
The calculation

\displaystyle\langle h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}hu,hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Q^{\mathrm{ex}}{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}hu,h^{3}u\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}Q^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,h^{3}u\rangle+\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})hu,h^{3}u\rangle

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=-\langle Q^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})hu\rangle-\langle hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,h{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}hu\rangle\\
&\qquad\qquad+\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})h^{2}u,h^{2}u\rangle\end{split}

implies

\displaystyle\quad\ \,\langle hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu\rangle+\langle h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu,hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu\rangle

\displaystyle=2||hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu||_{0}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot Q^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,hu\rangle+\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})h^{2}u,h^{2}u\rangle.

Fubini’s theorem gives

\displaystyle\quad\ \,||hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu||_{0}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot Q^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,hu\rangle+\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})h^{2}u,h^{2}u\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}h(t)^{2}\left(2||Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle+\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}t,

where
u_{t}=u(\text{--},t)\in\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\otimes Cl_{n+1,0})
is the restriction to the slice
M=M\times\{t\}
.

We will show the required inequality (
4.13
) on each slice.
The difference
B-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
is a block map of pseudo differential operators of order zero because
Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)_{g_{0}}
is a block map of pseudo Dirac operators over a fixed metric
g_{0}
.
Hence,
{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})={\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(B^{\mathrm{ex}})

restricts to a bounded operator on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\otimes Cl_{n+1,0}\rightarrow M\times\{t\}

for all
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
Their operator norms are uniformly bounded from above.
We conclude

\langle{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\geq c_{1}||u_{t}||_{0}^{2}

for all
c_{1}\geq-\mathrm{sup}\{||{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}(B^{\mathrm{ex}})\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times\{t\}}||_{0,0}\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\}>-\infty
.

On the other hand,
Q^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Q^{\mathrm{ex}}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{M\times\{t\}}=Q_{t}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{Cl_{n+1,0}}
is an elliptic operator of order
1
over the closed manifold
M
, so the Hilbert space
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\otimes Cl_{n+1,0})
decomposes into eigenspaces
\mathrm{Eig}(Q_{t}^{\mathrm{ex}},\lambda_{i}(t))
, where
(\lambda_{i}(t))_{i\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}
is the sequence of eigenvalues with
\lambda_{i}\leq\lambda_{i+1}
.
This sequence of eigenvalues is unbounded in both directions, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*Theorem II.5.8.

Denote the orthogonal projection onto
\mathrm{Eig}(Q_{t}^{\mathrm{ex}},\lambda_{i}(t))
by
\Pi_{\lambda_{i}(t)}
and the spectrum of
Q_{t}^{\mathrm{ex}}
by
\Sigma_{t}
.
The operators
Q^{\mathrm{ex}}
and
\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot
anti-commute.
Thus, if
\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})(t)\neq 0
, it restricts to an isomorphism

\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot\colon\mathrm{Eig}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t},\lambda_{i}(t))\rightarrow\mathrm{Eig}(Q^{\mathrm{ex}}_{t},-\lambda_{i}(t)).

This implies

\displaystyle\quad\ \,2h^{2}||Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in\Sigma_{t}}2h^{2}\langle(Q^{\mathrm{ex}})^{2}\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{\mu}u_{t}\rangle+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot Q^{\mathrm{ex}}\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{\mu}u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda,\mu\in\Sigma_{t}}2h^{2}\lambda^{2}(t)\langle\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{\mu}u_{t}\rangle+\lambda\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{\mu}u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{t}}2h^{2}\lambda^{2}(t)\langle\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}\rangle+\lambda\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t},\Pi_{-\lambda}u_{t}\rangle.

The spectrum of
Q_{t}^{\mathrm{ex}}
is symmetric and
h\geq 1
, so we can estimate

\displaystyle\quad\ \,2h^{2}||Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})\cdot Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{t}}\lambda^{2}||\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}-||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||_{\infty}\cdot|\lambda|\cdot||\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}||\Pi_{-\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{t}\cap\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\lambda^{2}\left(||\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}+||\Pi_{-\lambda}u_{t}||^{2}_{0}\right)-2||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||_{\infty}\cdot|\lambda|\cdot||\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}||\Pi_{-\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{\lambda\in\Sigma_{t}\cap\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}}\left(\lambda^{2}-||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||_{\infty}\cdot|\lambda|\right)\left(||\Pi_{\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}+||\Pi_{-\lambda}u_{t}||_{0}^{2}\right).

Since
||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||_{\infty}\leq B
for some
B\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, since
Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}(M)_{g_{0}}
is a block map, and since eigenvalues of self-adjoint elliptic operators depend continuously on the elliptic operator
[
booss1993elliptic
]
*Section 17A, the set

\{i\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}\,:\,\text{there is a }t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}\text{ such that }\lambda_{i}^{2}(t)<||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||\cdot|\lambda_{i}(t)|\}

is finite.
Thus, there is a
c_{2}\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\lambda^{2}(t)-||\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})||_{\infty}\cdot|\lambda(t)|\geq c_{2}

for all
\lambda(t)\in\Sigma_{t}
and all
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.
We conclude

h^{2}||Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t}||^{2}_{0}+\langle\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})Q^{\mathrm{ex}}u_{t},u_{t}\rangle\geq c_{2}||u_{t}||_{0}^{2}.

Note that this inequality is trivially satisfied if
\mathrm{grad}(h^{2})(t)=0
.

Integrating these inequalities over
t
gives

\langle hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu,h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu\rangle+\langle h\mathrm{susp}(Q)hu,hQ^{\mathrm{ex}}hu\rangle\geq\mathrm{min}\{c_{1},c_{2}\}||u||_{0}^{2},

so the third and final condition of Theorem
4.9.4
is satisfied.
We apply it to deduce that

\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(b([hQh]))\sharp\alpha_{n+1}=b([h\mathrm{susp}(Q)h])

and the theorem is proven.
∎

Corollary 4.9.10
.

The operator suspension
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
induces an injective map on all homotopy groups.

Proof.

Recall the split diagram

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d},\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{Cl_{0,n+1}}(\text{--})\sharp\alpha_{n+1}}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}}).}

The upper horizontal map is a bijection by Lemma
4.9.6
and the right vertical map is an isomorphism by Kasparov theory.
The diagram commutes by the previous theorem, so the composition of the left vertical arrow and the lower horizontal arrow is a bijection.
In particular,
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
is split injective on the set of path components and on all homotopy groups.
∎

The Determination of
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))

Recall diagram (
4.12
) from Corollary
4.9.10
.
We know that three out of four groups are isomorphic to
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})
, so we are left with the following situation

\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt}).}

The group
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})
is cyclic, so if we could show that there is a surjective map
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
, then
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
would be cyclic, too, and an easy diagram chase would imply that
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
is also surjective.

In conclusion, we are left to prove the next theorem.

Theorem 4.9.11
.

If
M
is a closed,
d
-dimensional, spin manifold with Riemannian metric
g_{0}
such that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}
is invertible, then there is a surjective map

KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}})

for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.

The proof idea is the same as the one of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Prop 4.3.
We will compare the set
\Omega_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
, suitably topologised, to
(B+\mathbf{Kom})^{\times}
, the space of invertible, compact pertubations of an invertible operator
B
and show that these two spaces are weakly homotopy equivalent.
The latter space is homotopy equivalent to
\Omega\mathrm{Fred}^{d+n}
, see
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Proof of Prop 4.3 and Lemma 4.9, which is a classifying space for
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})
.

Note that the cited arguments work only for a
fixed
(but arbitrary) Hilbert space with an ample
Cl_{d+n+1,0}
-right action,
\mathrm{i.e.}
, a right action that contains infinitely many copies of all irreducible
Cl_{d+n+1,0}
-representations.
The requirement to fix the Hilbert space is the reason why we work with the fibre
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
instead of the total space
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
.

Let us begin with fixing notation.
Let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of all compactly supported, bounded operators on
L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
and let
\mathbf{B}
be subset of
\mathcal{B}
consisting of all self-adjoint, odd, and Clifford linear operators.
We endow
\mathbf{B}
with the operator norm topology so that it becomes a locally convex vector space.
Furthermore, we define
\mathbf{B}_{act}=\mathbf{B}\cap\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}_{c}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
as the set of all compactly supported pseudo differential operators of order zero that are self-adjoint, odd, and Clifford-linear.

Let further
\mathcal{K}
be the set of all compact operators on
L^{2}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
and let

\mathbf{Kom}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{A\in\mathcal{K}\,:\,A\text{ is s.a., odd, Clifford-linear}\}.

Finally, we denote the spinor connection on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}
with
\nabla
and define the operator
T\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{-1/4}
.

Recall that we have defined the Sobolev spaces
H^{s}
as the closure of all compactly supported sections
\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
under the norm

||u||_{s}^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\langle(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}u,u\rangle_{\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}(g_{0})

so that
T\colon H^{s}\rightarrow H^{s+1/2}
is an isometric isomorphism.

Lemma 4.9.12
.

The assignment
B\mapsto TBT
gives rise to well-defined continuous maps

T(\text{--})T\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}\quad\text{and}\quad T(\text{--})T\colon\mathbf{B}_{(act)}\rightarrow\mathbf{Kom}

whose images are dense.

Proof.

The map
T(\text{--})T
is continuous because
T
is a bounded operator.

The spinor connection
\nabla
is even and Clifford-right linear, so the operator
TBT
is self-adjoint, odd, and Clifford-linear if
B
is self-adjoint, odd, and Clifford-right linear.
Thus, it remains to show that
TBT
is compact.

Since
B
is supported within
M\times RI^{n}
for some
R>0
, every compactly supported function with
\chi\equiv 1
on
M\times RI^{n}
satisfies
B=B\chi\cdot
.
Thus, the operator
TBT\colon H^{0}\rightarrow H^{0}
factors as follows

\textstyle{H^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{H^{1/2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\chi\cdot}
\textstyle{H^{1/2}_{\mathrm{supp}\,(\chi)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}_{\mathrm{supp}\,(\chi)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B}
\textstyle{H^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{H^{1/2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}.}

The inclusion
H^{1/2}_{\mathrm{supp}\,(\chi)}\hookrightarrow H^{0}_{\mathrm{supp}\,(\chi)}
is compact by Rellich’s lemma, so
TBT
is compact.

We first prove that
T(\text{--})T\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
has a dense image.
Since
T\colon H^{s}\rightarrow H^{s+1/2}
is an isometry for all
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, it induces an homeomorphism
T\colon H^{\infty}\rightarrow H^{\infty}
, where
H^{\infty}=\bigcap_{s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}H^{s}
is endowed with the inverse limit topology.
Thus,
T
maps dense subsets
W\subseteq H^{\infty}
to dense subsets.

For each dense subset
W\subseteq H^{\infty}
, the linear hull of the set of rank 1 projections

\{\langle\text{--},w_{1}\rangle w_{2}\,:\,w_{j}\in W\}
arising from
W
is a dense subset of
\mathcal{K}
.
The identity

T\circ\langle\text{--},w_{1}\rangle w_{2}\circ T=\langle T(\text{--}),w_{1}\rangle T(w_{2})=\langle\text{--},T(w_{1})\rangle T(w_{2})

implies that
T(\text{--})T
maps bijectively rank 1 projections arising from
W
to rank
1
projections arising from
T(W)
.
The tensor product
\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\otimes\Gamma_{c}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{n,0})
is a dense subspace of
\Gamma_{c}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
, which is itself a dense subset of
H^{\infty}
.
Clearly,

\{\langle\text{--},\sigma_{1}\otimes\varphi_{1}\rangle\sigma_{2}\otimes\varphi_{2}\,:\,\sigma_{j}\in\Gamma(M,\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}),\varphi\in\Gamma_{c}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Cl_{n,0})\}\subseteq\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{c}^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\subseteq\mathcal{B}

because the sections are compactly supported.
It follows that
T\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{c}^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})T
and
T\mathcal{B}T
are dense in
\mathcal{K}
.

In order to show that the restriction
T(\text{--})T\colon\mathbf{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{Kom}
has dense image, we make use of the projection (4.7) of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
.
If
\Gamma
denotes the multiplicative group generated by the standard basis
e_{j}\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d+n}\subseteq Cl_{d+n,0}
, then the projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{K}}\colon\mathcal{K}\rightarrow\mathbf{Kom}
is defined by

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{4|\Gamma|}\left[\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma K\gamma^{-1}-\mathrm{e/o}\gamma K\gamma^{-1}\mathrm{e/o}\right)-\left(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\gamma K\gamma^{-1}-\mathrm{e/o}\gamma K\gamma^{-1}\mathrm{e/o}\right)^{\ast}\right].

If we can show that this assignment gives rise to a projection
\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
, then the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{c}^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\mathcal{K}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}_{act}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Kom}}

implies that the restriction of
T(\text{--})T
to
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}_{act}
has dense image, too.

Note that if
A
and
B
are supported within
M\times RI^{n}
and
M\times SI^{n}
, respectively, then
A+B
is supported within
M\times(R+S)I^{n}
.
If
f
is a vector bundle map over the identity, then
fA
and
Af
are still bounded operators and supported with in
M\times RI^{n}
.
It is easily veryfied that if
A
is supported within
M\times RI^{n}
, then also
A^{\ast}
is supported within
M\times RI^{n}
.

In conclusion,
\prod_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}
is well-defined, which finishes the proof.
∎

Since we are working over non-compact manifolds the next result does not follow from general theory.

Lemma 4.9.13
.

The operator
T{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T\colon H^{0}\rightarrow H^{0}
is bounded.

The proof relies on the following abstract operator theoretic result.

Lemma 4.9.14
.

Let
P
be a possibly unbounded, self-adjoint, non-negative operator and
B
be a bounded self-adjoint operator with
B\geq 1
.
Then we have

(B+P)^{-1/4}\leq(1+P)^{-1/4}.

Proof.

We clearly have
B+P\geq 1+P
, so both operators are invertible.
Since the inverse of an unbounded operator is bounded, we can apply the proof of
[
murphy2014c
]
*Theorem 2.2.5 (4), to deduce
(1+P)^{-1}\geq(B+P)^{-1}
. Applying
[
murphy2014c
]
*Theorem 2.2.6 twice, we deduce
(B+P)^{-1/4}\leq(1+P)^{-1/4}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.9.13
.

For
c\geq 1
, define
T_{c}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(c+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{-1/4}
.
Because multiplication with
c
and
\nabla^{\ast}\nabla
commute, unbounded functional calculus implies that
T_{c}\circ T^{-1}
is bounded with operator norm
||T_{c}\circ T^{-1}||_{0,0}\leq c
.
Thus,
T{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T
is bounded if and only if
T_{c}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T_{c}
is bounded for some
c\geq 1
.

Since
M
is closed, there is a
c>0
such that
c-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g_{0})/4>1
.
Using the Lichnerowicz formula and Lemma
4.9.14
we deduce

\displaystyle T_{c}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T_{c}

\displaystyle=(c-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g_{0})/4+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}^{2})^{-1/4}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}(c-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g_{0})/4+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}^{2})^{-1/4}

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}^{2})^{-1/4}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}(1+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}^{2})^{-1/4}

\displaystyle={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}(1+{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}^{2})^{-1/2},

so
T_{c}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T_{c}
is bounded.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.9.11
.

Set
B\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=T{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}T
.
Recall from Corollary
B.0.25
that
{\mathrm{symb}}^{1}_{K}\colon\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}_{K}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Symb}^{0}_{K}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
is continuous for all compact sets
K\subseteq M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and that its kernel is contained in
\mathcal{K}(H^{1},H^{0})
by Lemma
B.0.24
.
Thus

\mathfrak{B}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{P-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}\,:\,P\in\Omega_{g_{0}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}\}

injects into
\mathcal{K}(H^{1},H^{0})
.
In fact, it injects even into the smaller space
\mathbf{K}_{c}(H^{1},H^{0})
of all compact operators from
H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
to
H^{0}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})=L^{2}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
that are odd, self-adjoint (considered as unbounded operators) and Clifford linear.
Thus, we equip
\mathfrak{B}
with the subspace topology of the operator norm topology of
\mathcal{K}(H^{1},H^{0})
.
We equip the spaces in the lower row with the topologies such that the vertical arrows in the following diagram become homeomorphisms:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}+}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{B}_{act}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Kom}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B+}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\mathbf{B}_{act}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\textstyle{B+\mathbf{Kom}.}

The diagram restricts to the subspaces of invertible operators:

\textstyle{U_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}+}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces U_{1/2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\textstyle{U_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{B+}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\mathbf{B}_{act})^{\times}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\text{--})T}
\textstyle{(B+\mathbf{Kom})^{\times}.}

Note that
U_{1/2}
is the preimage of
U_{1}
under
T(\text{--})T
in
\mathbf{B}_{act}
.
Furthermore
\mathbf{B}_{act}
is a locally convex topological vector space and its inclusions into
\mathfrak{B}
is continuous and has dense image (because infinite smoothing operators are dense in the compact operators as explained in the proof of Lemma
4.9.12
).
By Palais’ theorem
[
palais1966homotopy
]
*Corollary of Theorem 12, the two maps,
T(\text{--})T\colon U_{1/2}\rightarrow U_{1}
and the inclusion
U_{1/2}\hookrightarrow U_{0}
, are weak homotopy equivalences.
This implies, in particular, that we have a zig-zag of bijections

\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\xleftarrow{\cong}\pi_{0}(({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\mathbf{B}_{act})^{\times})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{0}((B+\mathbf{Kom})^{\times}).

We will prove in Lemma
4.9.15
below that the surjection
\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
is a group homomorphism so that we have the following zig-zag

\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\twoheadleftarrow\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{0}((B+\mathbf{Kom})^{\times}).

Combined with Ebert’s result that
(B+\mathbf{Kom})^{\times}
is homotopy equivalent to
\Omega\mathrm{Fred}^{d+n}
, see
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
*Lemma 4.9 or Proof of Proposition 4.3, the zig-zag produces, by inverting the isomorphism, the following surjection:

KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}).\qed

Lemma 4.9.15
.

The map
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})\colon\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
factors through
\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
.

Proof.

Clearly, the suspension also induces a map

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\pi_{n}(B_{\bullet,g_{0}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(\Omega_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})}

because a combinatorial homotopy in
B_{n+1,g_{0}}
between
P_{-1}
and
P_{1}
suspends to a path between
\mathrm{susp}(P_{-1})
and
\mathrm{susp}(P_{1})
by suspending along the last
n
coordinates.

It remains to show that the map
\pi_{0}(\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))
that sends a representative of a path component to its concordance is well-defined.
Denote by
\mathbf{B}_{act}
the locally convex vector space of all compactly supported pseudo differential operators of order zero on
\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}}\rightarrow M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that are self-adjoint, odd and Clifford linear equipped with the operator norm topology.
The canonical map
\iota\colon\mathbf{B}_{act}\rightarrow\mathfrak{B}
is a continuous, injective, linear map with dense image, hence, by Palais’ result
[
palais1966homotopy
]
*Corollary of Theorem 12, it restricts to a weak equivalence
\iota^{-1}(U_{0})\rightarrow U_{0}
.

If
P_{-1},P_{1}\in\Omega^{n}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
represent the same path component, then slight perturbations
\tilde{P}_{j}
of
P_{j}
such that
\tilde{P}_{j}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}\in\iota^{-1}(U_{0})
still represent the same path component.
We find therefore a sufficiently slowly parametrised path
Q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\iota^{-1}(U_{0})
that connects
\tilde{P}_{-1}
with
\tilde{P}_{1}
and such that
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
is invertible.

However,
\mathrm{susp}(Q)
might not yet be a block operator because the amplitude topology does not control the diameter of a core.
In order to fix this problem, consider the smooth block map

t\mapsto B_{t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Q_{t}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}

of compactly supported pseudo differential operators of order zero (hence bounded operators).

For the sake of readability, we will drop
\iota
from the notation.
For each given
\varepsilon>0
, we find a sufficiently large
N\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
, such that if
t\in[k/N,(k+1)/N]
, then
||B_{t}-B_{k/N}||_{0,0}<\varepsilon/3
for all
k\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Assume that
B_{k/N}
is supported within
M\times R_{k}I^{n}
.
As a block map,
B
is locally constant near infinity, so we may (and do) assume the sequence
(R_{k})_{k\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}
to be bounded.
Let
R
be the supremum of this sequence.
Fix a function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
1
on
RI^{n}
and identically zero on the complement of
2RI^{n}
.
The path
t\mapsto\chi B\chi
then satisfies

\displaystyle||B_{t}-\chi B_{t}\chi||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq||B_{t}-B_{k/N}||_{0,0}+||B_{k/N}-\chi B_{t}\chi||_{0,0}

\displaystyle=||B_{t}-B_{k/N}||_{0,0}+||\chi(B_{k/N}-B_{t})\chi||_{0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon/3+\varepsilon/3<\varepsilon.

Since invertibility is an open condition, we may choose
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small such that
t\mapsto{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\chi B_{t}\chi
is a block map of invertible operators that connect

\tilde{P}_{-1}
and
\tilde{P}_{1}
.

However, we cannot form the suspension yet, because we do not know whether the path
t\mapsto\chi B_{t}\chi
is continuous with respect to the
amplitude topology
.
Luckily, the path only takes values in operators that are supported within
M\times 2RI^{n}
, in other words, we have a continuous path
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}_{M\times 2RI^{n}}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
(the target is equipped with the operator norm topology and has is locally constant with values
\tilde{P}_{\pm 1}
).
Since
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}_{M\times 2RI^{n}}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\hookrightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}_{M\times 2RI^{n}}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
is continuous and has dense image,
we can now use Palais theorem (and smoothing theory) to replace
t\mapsto\chi B_{t}\chi
by a smooth path
\hat{B}_{t}\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{M\times 2RI^{n}}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
of operators such that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}+\hat{B_{t}}
is invertible, that connects
\tilde{P}_{-1}
and
\tilde{P}_{1}
, and whose suspension is an invertible block Dirac operator.

Different interpolations of
P_{-1}
and
P_{1}
can be connected by convex-combination, which is a suspensionable path.
Hence, sending an element of a path component to its concordance class gives indeed a well-defined map.

To see that the composition

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(B_{\bullet},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(\Omega_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}^{n}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})}

agrees with
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})\colon\pi_{n}(B_{\bullet,g_{0}},{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
, just observe that the suspension of the convex combination between
\mathrm{susp}(P)
and its slight perturbation
\widetilde{\mathrm{susp}(P)}
give rise to a concordance connecting these two elements in
\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})
.
∎

An immediate consequence is the proof of Theorem
4.9.1
that the operator suspension map is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof of Theorem
4.9.1
.

As discussed at the beginning of this chapter, it suffices to prove this statement for the restriction to the fibre
B_{\bullet,g_{0}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
.
By Corollary
4.9.10
we already know that the suspension induces an injective map on all homotopy groups.
If
n\geq 1
, then Theorem
4.9.11
and Diagram (
4.12
) yield a sequence of surjective maps

\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KK(Cl_{0,d+n+1},\mathord{\mathbb{R}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt}).}

Since surjective endomorphisms between cyclic groups are bijective, three out of four maps in Diagram (
4.12
) are isomorphisms, so the remaining homomorphism
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
must be an isomorphism, too.

If
n=0
, we use that
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})\hookrightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{g_{0}})
is dense to deduce that
\Omega^{0}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{0}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)_{g_{0}}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
Surjectivity of
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet})
follows now from Lemma
4.9.15
.
∎

Chapter 5
The Factorisation of the Index Difference

We have put much effort into the construction of
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
and into the proof that it is a classifying space for real
K
-theory.
It remains to prove Theorem
C
, namely that the index difference factors through the concordance space.
We will give a precise formulation of this Theorem together with its proof in Section
5.1
.
Applications regarding the non-triviality of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
for special
M
using the existing literature, in particular Theorem
D
, will be given in Section
5.2
.

5.1
The Factorisation Theorem

Theorem 5.1.1

(The Factorisation Theorem)
.

Let
{B_{\bullet}}
be the cubical subset from Definition
4.5.3
.
There is a weakly homotopy equivalent cubical subset
{A_{\bullet}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
such that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{A_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}}
\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{B_{\bullet}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M).}

The two maps
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp}
and
\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}
do not agree.
We will establish this theorem by finding suitable conditions on the block maps of psc metrics so that the operator norm of the
error term

\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp}
becomes sufficiently small.
Since invertibility is an open condition, convex interpolation serves then as a homotopy between the two maps.

Definition 5.1.2
.

Let
{A_{\bullet}}
be the following cubical subset of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
:
A block map
g\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}(M)
is contained in
{A_{n}}
if and only if it satisfies the following conditions:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{j}g)|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)|^{2}<\frac{1}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t)),

(5.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}||\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},g(t)}<\min\{d^{-1},2^{-(d+4)}\mathrm{low}(t)\},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}||[{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g(t)}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{\partial_{j}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}g}}]||_{1,0;M\times t}<1/32\cdot\mathrm{low}(t)^{2},

(5.3)

where
\mathrm{low}(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\inf\{||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g(t)}v||_{0,g(t)}\,:\,||v||_{1,g(t)}=1\}=\mathrm{low}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}}(t)
.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

The sequence of sets form indeed a cubical subset of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof that
{B_{\bullet}}
is a cubical subset.
Note that the three defining conditions are local and stable, so this lemma follows from Lemma
2.2.7
.
∎

Next, we wish to show that the inclusion is a weak homotopy equivalence. We employ the same strategy as before: We first show that
{A_{\bullet}}
is a Kan set and then use the usual rescaling trick on elements of the combinatorial homotopy groups.

Proposition 5.1.4
.

The cubical subset
{A_{\bullet}}
is a Kan set.

Proof of Theorem
5.1.1
.

The proof is similar to the proof that
{B_{\bullet}}
is Kan.
In fact, we have written that proof in a manner such that it can be easily complemented to prove the this proposition.
We are going to employ the same construction, but, in contrast to the proof of Proposition
4.5.12
, we need to choose the constant
R_{1}>1
more carefully.

We first recall the construction.
For a given cubical
n
-horn

\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}=\{g_{(j,\omega)}\in{A_{n-1}}\,:\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}g_{(k,\eta)}=\partial^{\eta}_{k-1}g_{(j,\omega)};\,j<k;(j,\omega),(k,\eta)\neq(i,\varepsilon)\},

there is a sufficient large
\rho>0
such that all block maps
g_{(j,\omega)}
decompose appropriately outside of
\rho I^{n-1}
.
We restrict
\sigma_{j}g_{(j,\omega)}
to a map
\{\omega x_{j}>\rho\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
By the compatibility requirement, these restriction agree on the intersection of there domains and we can therefore glue them together to a map

g_{0}\colon\left({\mathrm{Cub}_{n,1,1}^{\rho}}\right)^{c}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M),

where
{\mathrm{Cub}_{n,i,\varepsilon}^{\rho}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,x_{j}\in[-\rho,\rho]\text{ if }j\neq i,\,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho\}
is the cuboid with diameter
\rho
.
Let
K\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}
be a compact, convex subset that contains
\rho I^{n-1}
, is point symmetric at the origin, and has a smooth boundary.
Let
Q_{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{x\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\,:\,p_{i}(x)\in K,\varepsilon x_{i}\geq-\rho\}
be the cuboid with base
K
.
Restricting
g_{0}
yields

g_{0}\colon Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M),

for a fixed choice of
R_{1}>1
.

For the chosen
K
, there is a unique norm
||\text{--}||_{K}
whose unit ball is
K
.
Since
\partial K
is smooth, the norm is smooth on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\setminus\{0\}
.
For a sufficient large
R_{2}=R_{2}(\rho,R_{1})
to be determined later, pick
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 1.2}
, that vanishes on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq R_{2}-1}
, and whose first and second derivative satisfy
\chi^{\prime}\leq 1.3/R_{2}
and
|\chi^{\prime\prime}|\leq 10/R_{2}^{2}
.
Furthermore, pick a monotonically increasing function
q\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
that is the identity on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq\rho}
, the constant map with value
\rho+R_{1}-1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\rho+R_{1}}
, and whose derivatives satisfy
q^{\prime}\leq 1
and
|q^{\prime\prime}|\leq 1.2/R_{1}
.
The map
q
defines a map

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},q(x_{i}),x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}).

Finally, we define

\displaystyle H\colon\left(Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\}\right)\times[0,1]

\displaystyle\rightarrow Q_{K}^{c}\cap\{\varepsilon x_{i}<\rho+R_{1}\},

\displaystyle(x,t)

\displaystyle\mapsto x-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})te_{i}

and set

\displaystyle g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}g_{0}\circ H\bigl(q(x),\chi(||p_{i}(x)||_{K})\bigr),&\text{if }x\in Q_{K}^{c},\\
g_{(i,-\varepsilon)}(p_{i}(x)),&\text{if }x\in Q_{K}.\end{cases}

Note that
g
is well defined and smooth because
g_{0}
agrees with
g_{(i,-\varepsilon)}\circ p_{i}
on the set
\{-\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
and
\partial Q_{K}
lies in the preimage of
\{-\varepsilon x_{i}>\rho\}
under the map
H\circ(q(\text{--}),\chi(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K}))
.

The same argument as in the proof for Proposition
4.5.12
shows that
g
is a smooth block map and a filler of
\sqcap^{n}_{(i,\varepsilon)}
in
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}(M)
, from which we already know that there must exists one.
It remains to show that
g
lies in
{A_{n}}
, provided
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
were chosen large enough.

We need to find a sufficient large lower bound for
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
such that
g
satisfies the first condition.
This is slightly more tricky, because it involves second derivatives.
We abbreviate
H(q(\text{--}),\chi(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K}))
to
\varphi
.
Recall the chain rule for the (total) second derivative:

D^{2}(g_{0}\circ\varphi)=D^{2}_{\varphi(\text{--})}g_{0}(D\varphi\cdot,D\varphi\cdot)+D_{\varphi(\text{--})}g_{0}\cdot D^{2}\varphi(\cdot,\cdot).

In matrix form, the first and second derivative of
\varphi
are given by

\displaystyle D\varphi

\displaystyle=DH\cdot(Dq,D_{||\text{--}||_{K}\circ p_{i}}\chi\cdot D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i}(\text{--}))

\displaystyle=Dq-\left(2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\cdot D_{||\text{--}||_{K}\circ p_{i}}\chi\cdot D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i}(\text{--})\right)e_{i}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}D^{2}\varphi&=D^{2}q-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\cdot\bigl[(\chi^{\prime}(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K}))^{\prime}\cdot D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad+\chi^{\prime}(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K})D(D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i})\bigr]e_{i}\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=D^{2}q-2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\bigl[\chi^{\prime\prime}(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K})\cdot(D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i}\otimes D_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}\cdot p_{i})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\chi^{\prime}(||p_{i}(\text{--})||_{K})\cdot D^{2}_{p_{i}}||\text{--}||_{K}(p_{i}(\text{--}),p_{i}(\text{--}))\bigr]e_{i}.\end{split}

This yields, for the first and second derivatives of the block map
g
on
Q_{K}^{c}
, the following expressions:
For
j<i
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{j}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}g_{0}\circ\varphi-\partial_{i}g_{0}\circ\varphi\cdot 2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j}||\text{-}||_{K},

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{j}^{2}g&=D^{2}g(e_{j},e_{j})=(\partial_{j}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi+(\partial_{i}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot\bigl(2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j}||\text{-}||_{K}\bigr)^{2}\\
\hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-4(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j}||\text{--}||_{K}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\partial_{i}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot 2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\bigl(\chi^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{j}||\text{-}||_{K})^{2}+\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j}^{2}||\text{-}||_{K}\bigr),\end{split}\end{split}

for
j>i
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{j}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}g_{0}\circ\varphi-\partial_{i}g_{0}\circ\varphi\cdot 2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j}||\text{-}||_{K},

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{j}^{2}g&=D^{2}g(e_{j},e_{j})=(\partial_{j}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi+(\partial_{i}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot 2\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j-1}||\text{-}||_{K}\\
\hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-4(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}g_{0}\circ\varphi)\cdot\varepsilon(\rho+R_{1})\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j-1}||\text{--}||_{K}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\partial_{i}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot 2\varepsilon(R+R_{1})[\chi^{\prime\prime}(\partial_{j-1}||\text{-}||_{K})^{2}+\chi^{\prime}\partial_{j-1}^{2}||\text{-}||_{K}],\end{split}\end{split}

and, for
j=i
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{i}g

\displaystyle=(\partial_{i}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot q^{\prime},

\displaystyle\partial_{i}^{2}g

\displaystyle=(\partial_{i}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot(q^{\prime})^{2}+(\partial_{i}g_{0})\circ\varphi q^{\prime\prime}.

By definition,
|q^{\prime\prime}|\leq 1.2/R_{1}
, so we can choose
R_{1}
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle\quad\sup\biggl\{\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{i}^{2}g)-\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{i}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi\cdot(q^{\prime})^{2}\,:\,t\in Q_{K}^{c}\biggr\}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{3}\min\biggl\{\frac{1}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g_{0})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g_{0}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}}_{0})|+\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}}_{0})^{2}\biggr\}.

Recall that
\partial_{j}||\text{-}||_{K}
and
\partial_{j}^{2}||\text{-}||_{K}
are bounded on every closed subset of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
that does not contain the origin.
Also recall that
g_{0}
has bounded first and second derivatives on
Q_{K}^{c}
.
Since
|\chi^{\prime}|
or
\chi^{\prime\prime}
appear in all partial derivatives
\partial_{j}g
and
\partial_{j}^{2}g
except for
j=i
and since
|\chi^{\prime}|\leq 1.3/R_{2}
and
|\chi^{\prime\prime}|\leq 10/R_{2}^{2}
, we can choose
R_{2}
to be sufficiently large (in dependence of
R
and
R_{1}
) such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\sup_{t\in\overline{Q_{K}^{c}}}\biggl\{\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}(((\partial_{j}g)^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g_{0}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})\circ\varphi|\\
&\quad\qquad+\sum_{j\neq i}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)-\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g_{0})\circ\varphi|\\
&\quad\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)|^{2}-|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g_{0})|^{2}\circ\varphi\biggr\}\\
&\quad<\frac{1}{3}\min\biggl\{\frac{1}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g_{0})-\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}}_{0})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{j}g_{0})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g_{0})|^{2}\biggr\}.\end{split}

Since
g_{0}
satisfies the Condition
5.1
, also
g
satisfies it by a simple triangle inequality argument.

Thus, if we increase
R_{2}
such that it additionally satisfies
R_{2}>2(\rho+R_{1})B_{1}B_{2}\mathcal{I}^{-1}
, where
\mathcal{I}=\min\{\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}\}
is the minimum of

\mathcal{I}_{1}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{inf}\left\{\frac{1}{32}\,\mathrm{low}_{{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}}(t)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}||[{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g_{0}}^{\mathrm{ex}},\nabla_{j}^{\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}g_{0}}}]||\,:\,t\in Q_{K}^{c}\right\}

and

\mathcal{I}_{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{inf}\left\{\mathrm{min}\{d^{-1},2^{-(d+4)}\mathrm{low}(t)\}-\sum_{k=1}^{n}||\partial_{k}g_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}||_{\mathrm{op},g_{0}(t)}\,:\,t\in Q_{K}^{c}\right\},

then
g
also satisfies the remaining defining conditions of
{A_{\bullet}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{I}>0
because
g_{0}|_{Q_{K}^{c}}
is a union of restrictions of degenerate elements of
{A_{n}}
and the defining conditions of
{A_{n}}
are local.

Thus,
g\in{A_{n}}
is a filler for the given cubical
n
-horn and
{A_{\bullet}}
is therefore a Kan set.
∎

Corollary 5.1.5
.

The inclusion
{A_{\bullet}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Corollary
4.5.13
that
{B_{\bullet}}\hookrightarrow\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
∎

Lemma 5.1.6
.

The suspension restricts to a cubical map
\mathrm{susp}\colon{A_{\bullet}}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.

Proof.

By an iterative application of Lemma
C.1.1
we have

\displaystyle|\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(g))-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{3}{4}(\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g)^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle<\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g)^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}|

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g).

Thus,
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(g))(m,t)>\frac{7}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t))(m)>0
, which proves the claim.
∎

The Lichnerowicz formula implies that
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}
restricts to a cubical map
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
.
By the second and third defining condition of
{A_{\bullet}}
the Dirac Operator restricts to a cubical map
{A_{\bullet}}\hookrightarrow{B_{\bullet}}
. Thus, we have two cubical maps

{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp},\,\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\colon{A_{\bullet}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M).

Surprisingly, these two maps are not the same.
We will show that they are homotopic by controlling their difference.

Definition 5.1.7
.

Define the
error term
to be

\mathfrak{Err}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)}-\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g}).

Lemma 5.1.8
.

For each block map
g\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(M)
, the error term
\mathfrak{Err}(g)
is a differential operator of order zero that has block form. It can be expressed by the formula

\mathfrak{Err}(g)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{4}\left(\mathrm{tr}{\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}}}\right)\partial_{j}\cdot.

Proof.

As a difference of two differential operators of order 1 with the same principal symbol,
\mathfrak{Err}(g)
is a differential operator of order zero.
The formula follows from the calculation of the Christoffel symbols in Lemma
4.5.1
.
Since
g
is a block metric,
\mathfrak{Err}(g)
decomposes accordingly, so it is a block operator of order zero.
∎

Lemma 5.1.9
.

For each
g\in A_{n}
and all
u\in H^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)})
the following estimates hold true

\displaystyle||\mathfrak{Err}||^{2}_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{64}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t)),

\displaystyle||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)}u||_{0}-||\mathfrak{Err}(g)u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\{1,1/8\inf\{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t))^{1/2}\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\}\}\cdot||u||_{1}.

Proof.

The first estimate follows from Lemma
5.1.8
and the fact that the (fibre-wise) operator norm satisfies the
C^{\ast}
-identity

\displaystyle||\mathfrak{Err}||^{2}_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle=||\mathfrak{Err}^{\ast}\mathfrak{Err}||_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\frac{1}{16}\sum_{k,l}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{k}g)\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{l}g)\partial_{k}^{\ast}\partial_{l}\right|\right|_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\frac{1}{16}\sum_{k\leq l}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{k}g)\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{l}g)(\partial_{k}\partial_{l}+\partial_{l}\partial_{k})\right|\right|_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle=\left|\left|\frac{2}{16}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{k}g)^{2}\mathrm{id}\right|\right|_{\mathrm{op},t}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{16}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{k}g(t))^{2}<\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{1}{8}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t)).

This inequality together with the Lichnerowicz formula implies the second inequality by the following chain of estimations.

\displaystyle\quad\ \,||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)}u||_{0}-||\mathfrak{Err}(g)u||_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(||\nabla u||_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-||\mathfrak{Err}(g)u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\left(||\nabla u||_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{64}}||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\left(||\nabla u||_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}-1/8||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(||\nabla u||_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{8}||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||_{0}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\Bigl\{1,1/8\inf\{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t))^{1/2}\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\}\Bigr\}\cdot||u||_{1}.\qed

We can use the previous Lemma to re-prove that
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})
is invertible, something we already know to be true by Condition
5.2
and
5.3
.

Corollary 5.1.10
.

For each
g\in{A_{n}}
the block Dirac operator
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})
is invertible.

Proof.

The Lichnerowicz formula implies
||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)}u||^{2}_{0}\geq 1/4||\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)^{1/2}u||^{2}_{0}
, in particular, this operator is invertible.
From Lemma
5.1.9
and

\displaystyle||\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})u||_{0}\geq\left|||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}g}u||_{0}-||\mathfrak{Err}(g)u||_{0}\right|

we conclude that
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})
has trivial kernel and closed image.
Since
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})
is self-adjoint, it is also surjective and hence invertible.
∎

Definition 5.1.11
.

Let
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
be a smooth map that is identically zero on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}
and identically
1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
and let

\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\colon(\Box[1]\otimes{A})_{\bullet}\rightarrow\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)

be the following cubical map:
If
\varphi\in\Box[1]_{k}
and
g\in{A_{n-k}}
then

\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)=\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)\cdot\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}+(1-\chi)\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)\cdot{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)},

where
\bm{\sigma}\varphi=\sigma_{n}\dots\sigma_{k+1}\varphi
.
More precisely,
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{1}(\mathfrak{S}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)})
is the unique operator defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}

\displaystyle(\varphi,g)(u)(m,t)=

\displaystyle\chi(\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)(t))\cdot\left(\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}u\right)(m,t)+(1-\chi(\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)(t))\cdot\left({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}u\right)(m,t).

Theorem 5.1.12
.

The sequence
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
consist of well defined maps and assembles to a cubical map. It is a homotopy between
\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp}
.

Proof.

For well-definedness, we need to check that
\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\sigma_{k+1}\varphi,g)=\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\varphi,\sigma_{1}g)
for all
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and that
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is an invertible block Dirac operator for all
\varphi\in\Box[1]_{k}
and all
g\in{A_{n-k}}
.

We first show that
\mathcal{H}_{n}
preserves the equivalence relation of
(\Box[1]\otimes{A})_{\bullet}
.
Let
\varphi\in\Box[1]_{k}
and
g\in{A_{n-k}}
be given.
Since
\sigma_{k+1}\varphi\in\Box[1]_{k+1}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\,\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\sigma_{k+1}(\varphi),g)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\chi\circ\sigma_{n}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{k+2}(\sigma_{k+1}\varphi)\mathrm{susp}\,{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k+1}g}\\
&\qquad+(1-\chi\circ\sigma_{n}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{k+2}(\sigma_{k+1}\varphi)){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k+1}g)}\end{split}

\displaystyle=\chi\circ\sigma_{n}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{k+1}\varphi\,\mathrm{susp}\,{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k+1}g}+(1-\chi\circ\sigma_{n}\circ\dots\circ\sigma_{k+1}\varphi){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k+1}g)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\varphi,\sigma_{1}g)

During the entire proof, we slightly abuse notation by not indicating the length of composition
\bm{\sigma}
, i.e., we write
\bm{\sigma}\varphi
instead of
\bm{\sigma}\sigma_{k+1}\varphi
.

The operator
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
has block form.
Indeed, if
\varphi
is constant then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)=\begin{cases}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)},&\text{if }\varphi\equiv-1,\\
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}),&\text{if }\varphi\equiv 1.\end{cases}

If
\varphi
is not constant then, by Lemma
2.1.3
, there is a unique coordinate
t_{s}
with
1\leq s\leq k
such that
\varphi(t)=t_{s}
.
It is easy to see that
M\times RI^{n}
, which is the core of
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}
and
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g})
, is also the core of
\mathcal{H}_{n}(g,\varphi)
.
Since
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a differential operator it decreases the support, so that it restricts in particular to each
U_{R}({\bm{\varepsilon}})
.
The
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
-linearity of
(\Phi_{\bm{\varepsilon}})_{\ast}
implies

\displaystyle(\Phi_{\bm{\varepsilon}})_{\ast}\Bigl(\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)|_{U_{R}({\bm{\varepsilon}})}\Bigr)=

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Biggl(\chi(t_{c({\bm{\varepsilon}})(s)})\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}({\bm{\varepsilon}}|_{\{1,\dots,k\}})&+(1-\chi({c({\bm{\varepsilon}})(s)})){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}\sigma g}({\bm{\varepsilon}}_{\{1,\dots,k\}})\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\\
&+\left.\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes\sum_{j\in\mathrm{dom}{\bm{\varepsilon}}|_{\{k+1,\dots,n\}}}\partial_{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\right).\end{split}

Hence, for all sufficient large
R>0
such that
g|_{U_{R}({\bm{\varepsilon}})}
does not depend on the variables parametrised by
\mathrm{supp}\,\,{\bm{\varepsilon}}
, the operator
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
decomposes as required on
U_{R}({\bm{\varepsilon}})
.

The operator
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a convex combination of
\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g})
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}
using positive real valued function instead of real numbers.
This implies that
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a differential operator with the same symbol as
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}{\sigma}(g))}
and the block form implies that it differs from
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}
by a bounded operator.
As a convex combination of symmetric differential operators,
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a symmetric differential operator whose principal symbol has finite propagation speed, hence
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is self-adjoint by
[
higson2000analytic
]
*Prop 10.2.11

So far, we have shown that
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a block Dirac operator.
Next, we will show that it is invertible by showing that it is bounded from below using Lemma
5.1.9
:

\displaystyle||\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\left|||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}u||_{0}-||(\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)-{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)})u||_{0}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|||\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}u||_{0}-||(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi))\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}-(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g})||_{0}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}\sigma_{1}^{k}g}u||_{0}-||(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi))\mathfrak{Err}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)u||_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\geq||{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}u||_{0}-||\mathfrak{Err}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)u||_{0}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\Bigl\{1,1/8\inf\{\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)(t)^{1/2}\,:\,t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\}\Bigr\}||u||_{1}.

We verify now that
