\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
is a cubical map.
From the proof that
\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)
is a block map and that
\mathrm{susp}
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}
are cubical maps we deduce

\displaystyle\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\to\infty}{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}H_{n}(\varphi,g)(i,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\lim_{R\to\infty}{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}\left(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)|_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(i,\varepsilon)}\cdot\mathrm{susp}\,{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}(i,\varepsilon)\right.\\
&\qquad\left.\left(1-\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)|_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(i,\varepsilon)}\right)\cdot{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}(i,\varepsilon)\right)\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\lim_{R\to\infty}{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)|_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(i,\varepsilon)})\cdot\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}\sigma_{1}^{k}g}\\
&\qquad 1-{\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}(\chi\circ\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)|_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}(i,\varepsilon)}){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}({\delta^{R\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ast}\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}\end{split}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}_{n-1}(\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}\varphi,g),&\text{if }i\leq k,\\
\mathcal{H}_{n-1}(\varphi,\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i-k}g),&\text{if }i>k.\end{cases}

For the degeneracies, we use the simple observation
(f\cdot P)\boxtimes\mathrm{id}=\sigma_{n+1}(f)\cdot(P\boxtimes\mathrm{id})
in the following calculation:

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)=\mathrm{Cycl}(i,n+1)_{\ast}\left(\mathcal{H}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\mathrm{Cycl}(i,n+1)_{\ast}\left(\chi\circ\sigma_{n+1}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)(\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id})\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,+(1-\chi)\circ\sigma_{n+1}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}\,\sigma_{1}^{k}g}\boxtimes\mathrm{id})+\mathrm{e/o}\boxtimes{\not{\mathit{D}}}_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\right)\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\mathrm{Cycl}(i,n+1)_{\ast}\left(\chi\circ\sigma_{n+1}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{n+1}\sigma_{1}^{k}g}\right.\\
&\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,+(1-\chi\circ\sigma_{n+1}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}\sigma_{n+1}\sigma_{1}^{k}g}\right)\end{split}

\displaystyle=\chi\circ\sigma_{i}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi)\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{1}^{k}g})+(1-\chi)\circ\sigma_{i}\bm{\sigma}(\varphi){\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{1}^{k}g}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\sigma_{i}\varphi,g),&\text{if }i\leq k,\\
\mathcal{H}_{n+1}(\varphi,\sigma_{i-k}g),&\text{if }i>k.\end{cases}

Lastly, we need to show that
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
is a homotopy between
\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp}
.
If
\varphi=(-1,\dots,-1)\in\Box[1]_{k}
then

\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)=\mathrm{susp}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\sigma_{1}^{k}g})=\sigma_{1}^{k}(\mathrm{susp}{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{g})

and if
\varphi=(1,\dots,1)\in\Box[1]_{k}
then

\mathcal{H}_{n}(\varphi,g)={\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(\sigma_{1}^{k}g)}=\sigma_{1}^{k}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\mathrm{susp}(g)}),

so
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is indeed a homotopy between
\mathrm{susp}\circ{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}
and
{\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet}\circ\mathrm{susp}
.
∎

5.2
Applications

We sketch how our work relates to the existing literature and give some application of our main theorem.
The presentation here is less detailed as in the rest of this thesis as the missing details can be found in the existing literature.
We also give a counterexample to the stronger concordance-implies-isotopy conjecture, namely that the suspension
\mathrm{susp}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, defined on a weakly equivalent subset, is a weak equivalence.

Relations to the work of Botvinnik, Ebert, Randal-Williams:

The strongest detection result for non-trivial elements in the homotopy groups of
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
that does not take the fundamental group of the manifold into account was achieved by Botvinnik, Ebert and Randal-Williams in
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
.
Using sophisticated tools from homotopy theory, they proved that the index difference is rationally surjective.

Theorem 5.2.1

(
[
botvinnik2017infinite
]
*Theorem A)
.

Let
M^{d}
be a closed spin manifold of dimension
d\geq 6
.
Then

\pi_{n}(\mathrm{inddif})\otimes\mathbb{Q}\colon\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M))\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}

is surjective.
If
d+n+1\equiv 1,2\mod 8
so that
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, then
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{inddif})
is surjective.

By Theorem
5.1.1
, index difference factors over
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, so we deduce:

Corollary 5.2.2
.

Let
M^{d}
be a closed spin manifold of dimension
d\geq 6
.
Then

\pi_{n}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet})\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}\colon\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M))\otimes\mathord{\mathbb{Q}}=KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\otimes\mathbb{Q}

is surjective.
If
d+n+1\equiv 1,2\mod 8
so that
KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
, then
\pi_{n}({\not{\mathfrak{D}}}_{\bullet})
is surjective.

Relations to the work of Hitchin:

Hitchin was the first to discover that
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
can be non-contractible if
M^{d}
is any closed spin manifold of dimension
8k
that carries at least one psc metric.
Despite being a consequence of the previously discussed by results of Botvinnik, Ebert and Randal-Williams, it is worthwhile to recall Hitchin’s results in detail as we will use them below.
Hitchin’s argument in
[
hitchin1974harmonic
]
is roughly the following one:
The diffeomorphism group
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
acts on
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
via pull back.
For each choice of a base point
g_{0}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
, the pull back produces elements
[\varphi^{\ast}g_{0}]\in\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M))
for each element
[\varphi]\in\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Diff}(M))
.
The key observation is now that the composition of the (Hitchin) index difference and the pull back action

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Diff}(M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
pull back
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{inddif}}
\textstyle{KO^{-(d+n+1)}(\mathrm{pt})}

agrees with the topological index of the total space of the clutching construction
M\times D^{n+1}\cup_{\varphi}M\times D^{n+1}\rightarrow S^{n+1}
, provided the total space is a spin manifold.
More precisely, under the aforementioned conditions, we have

\mathrm{inddif}(\varphi^{\ast}g_{0},g_{0})=\alpha(M\times D^{n+1}\cup_{\varphi}M\times D^{n+1}).

In the case
n=0,1
, there are diffeomorphisms
\varphi\in\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Diff}(M))
such that

M\times D^{n+1}\cup_{\varphi}M\times D^{n+1}\cong(M\times S^{n+1})\#\Sigma^{d+n+1},

where
\Sigma^{d+n+1}
is an exotic sphere with
\alpha(\Sigma^{d+n+1})\neq 0
.
Since the topological index
\alpha\colon\Omega_{\ast}^{\mathord{\mathrm{Spin}}}(\mathrm{pt})\rightarrow KO^{-\ast}(\mathrm{pt})
is a ring-homomorphism, we deduce

\displaystyle\alpha((M\times S^{n+1})\#\Sigma^{d+n+1})=\alpha(M)\underbrace{\alpha(S^{n+1})}_{=0}+\alpha(\Sigma^{d+n+1})\neq 0.

This implies that
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M))
and
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M))
are non-trivial.

For later purposes we remark that the special diffeomorphisms are in the image of
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{Diff}(D^{d},S^{d-1}))
, the group of diffeomorphisms on an embedded disc
D^{d}\subseteq M
that are the identity near
S^{d-1}
.

The connection to our work is given by Theorem
5.1.1
.
The left vertical map is the a cubical map that models the space level description of the (Hitchin) index difference.
Applying homotopy groups to the diagram in Theorem
5.1.1
together with Hichtin’s result implies the following corollary.

Corollary 5.2.3
.

Let
M^{d}
be a closed, spin, psc manifold of dimension
8k
.
Then
\pi_{0}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
and
\pi_{1}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
are non-trivial.

Relations to the work of Ruberman:

We present an example where
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is not a weak homotopy equivalence.
The following proposition is just a reformulation of the results in
[
ruberman2002positive
]
.

Proposition 5.2.4
.

[
ruberman2002positive
]
*Corollary 5.2, Theorem 5.5
For
n\geq 2
and
k>10n
, define
M\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\#_{2n}\mathbb{CP}^{2}\#_{k}\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
.
Then
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is not injective on the level of connected components.
In fact, every concordance class contains infinitely many different isotopy classes.

Non Surjectivity Results:

We can also apply Hitchin’s methods directly to
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
to produce non-trivial elements in its homotopy groups.
Fix a base point
g_{0}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
The corresponding base point in
\pi_{k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
is then
g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}
.
Fix further an embedded disc
D^{d+k}\subseteq M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
.

Let
\varphi
be the diffeomorphism used by Hitchin in the subsection above (for
n=0
).
Since this diffeomorphism is the identity near the boundary of the embedded disc and since the disc lies in the interior of some
M\times RI^{k}
, the pull back
g\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\varphi^{\ast}(g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k}})
is a block metric.
We claim that
[g]\neq 0\in\pi_{k}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M))
provided
d+k\equiv 0\mod 8
.

Assume this were not the case, then there would be a block metric
G
on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k+1}
that satisfies
\partial^{-1}_{1}G=g
and
\partial^{\varepsilon}_{i}G=g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}
if
(i,\varepsilon)\neq(1,-1)
.
Pick a sufficiently large
R^{\prime}>R
such that
G
decomposes outside of
M\times R^{\prime}I^{k+1}
.
Then
G
induces a psc metric
\bar{G}
on

M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{T}^{k}=M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\times(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{k}/2R^{\prime}\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}^{k}),

which serves as a concordance between the psc metric
\bar{g}\in\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M\times\mathbb{T}^{k})
and
g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}
.
Due to the locality of this construction,
\bar{g}
is the psc metric we would obtain if we would apply Hitchin’s construction to
g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}
on
M\times\mathbb{T}^{k}
.
Thus, the Gromov-Lawson index difference of these two metrics vanishes.
On the other hand Theorem
5.1.1
, or alternatively Theorem A of
[
ebert2017indexdiff
]
, together with Hitchin’s result now imply that

\displaystyle\mathrm{inddif}_{GL}\bigl(\bar{g},g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigr)=\mathrm{inddif}_{H}\bigl(\bar{g},g_{0}\oplus\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{T}^{k}}\bigr)=\alpha(\Sigma^{d+k+1})\neq 0,

which is a contradiction.

If we now assume that
\dim M=2
or
3
, then
M
is spin if and only if it is orientable, see
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*p.86.
The previous argument now implies that
\pi_{6}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(S^{2}))
and
\pi_{5}(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M^{3}))
are non-trivial.
However, we know from
[
rosenberg149metrics
]
*Theorem 3.4 and
[
bamler2019ricci
]
*Theorem 1.1 that
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is contractible for all orientable psc manifolds of dimension 2 or 3.
This argument proves Theorem
D
of the introduction:

Theorem 5.2.5
.

The suspension
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is not a weak equivalence if
\dim(M)=2
or
3
.

Chapter 6
The PSC Hatcher Spectral Sequence

A consequence of the Factorisation Theorem
5.1.1
is that the index difference cannot distinguish different isotopy classes in the same conocrdance class.
This question has to be addressed with tools and techniques beyond index theory.

As a first attempt, we will make full use of the “spacification” of the isotopy versus concordance problem we have carried out in Chapter
3
and Chapter
5
:
The comparison map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, defined on a weakly equivalent cubical subset
A_{\bullet}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
, allows us to the define the homotopy fibre
\mathrm{hofib}\left(A_{\bullet}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)\right)
, which we abbreviate to
\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.
Since
A_{\bullet}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
are cubical Kan sets,
\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
is also a cubical Kan set by formal reasons.
The combinatorial description of its homotopy groups allows us to put a filtration on them, the so called
isotopy filtration
.
This filtration, roughly speaking, measures how far away from an isotopy a general concordance is.
The
psc Hatcher spectral sequence
, the main result of this section stated in Theorem
6.3.3
, approximates the difference between a filtration subgroup and its successor.

This chapter is structures as follows.
First, we will develop the geometric foundation to construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence in Section
6.1
.
This includes the discussion of the isotopy filtration in Definition
6.1.1
, the defect fibrations in Definition
6.1.9
, and the connection between those two, which is explained by Lemma
6.1.10
.

Secondly, we present in Section
6.2
an abstract construction of a spectral sequence that generalises the construction of a spectral sequence out of an exact couple of abelian groups.
In our setup, we allow some abelian groups to be replaced by monoids or even mere pointed sets.

Finally, in Section
6.3
, we apply this abstract spectral sequence construction of Theorem
6.2.8
to the geometric foundation established before to derive the psc Hatcher spectral sequence.

6.1
Geometric Foundations

Recall that we have defined in Chapter
5
a weakly equivalent cubical subset
A_{\bullet}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
, which is Kan and on which the suspension map takes values in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
In this section, it will be more convenient to work with a slightly bigger model given by

A_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left\{g\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{n}(M)\,:\,(1+d)\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)|^{2}+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|<\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\right\}.

Observe that it shares the just mentioned properties of its analog in Chapter
5
.

During the entire section, we fix a base point
g_{0}\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{0}(M)=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{0}}(M)
.
We remind the reader that we do not notionally distinguish
g_{0}
from its image under degeneracy maps.
We furthermore abbreviate
\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon A_{\bullet}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M);g_{0})
to
\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
and the dimension of
M
with
d
.

Let us start with the definition of the isotopy filtration.
For that recall the set of loops of a cubical set from Definition
2.1.26
.

Definition 6.1.1
.

For
0\leq p\leq n
, we set the
p
-th term of the
isotopy filtration
to be

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Bigl\{&g\in\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}\,:\,g_{(m,x)}=g^{(p+1)}_{(m,x)}+\sum_{j=p+2}^{n+1}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}\text{ and}\\
&(1+d)\sum_{j=p+2}^{n+1}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|<\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\Bigr\}.\end{split}

Here,
g^{(p+1)}_{(m,x)}
denotes the restriction of
g
to the subspace
T_{m}M\oplus\mathrm{span}(e_{1},\dots,e_{p+1})
in
T_{m}M\oplus T_{x}\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
.

The filtration of
\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
induces a filtration of
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
, the set of concordance classes of
\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.
Informally speaking, the set
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
consists of block metrics that are isotopies in the last
(n-p)
coordinates.
This observation inspires Definition
6.1.2
and Lemma
6.1.3
.

Definition 6.1.2
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{n})
be the topology space of all Riemannian metrics with positive scalar curvature that have product structure near each face equipped with the smooth Whitney topology.
For
p\geq 0
, let
F_{p}
be the topological space whose underlying set is

F_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\left\{g\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{p+1}}(M)\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}g\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{p}(M),\,\partial^{\omega}_{j}g=g_{0}\text{ for }(j,\omega)\neq(1,1)\right\}

and whose topology is the (subspace topology of the) colimit topology coming from the embeddings
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{p+1})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times SI^{p+1})
given by elongations.

We remind the reader that
F_{p}
carries the colimit topology induced by embeddings between Hausdorff spaces.
By Lemma
2.2.12
and Lemma
2.2.13
, every continuous map from a compact space into
F_{p}
factors through some
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{n+1})
.
This observation will be important in the forthcoming proofs.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

The suspension induces a map
\pi_{n-p}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
whose image is the set of concordance classes of
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.
Furthermore, the suspensions decompose into a sequence of maps

\textstyle{\pi_{n}(F_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0,n}}
\textstyle{\pi_{n-1}(F_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1,n-1}}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{n-1,1}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(F_{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{n,0}}
\textstyle{\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}),}

where all
i_{p,n-p}
are induced by “suspending along the first parameter variable” and
\iota_{n,0}
sends an isotopy class to its concordance class.

Proof.

For later purposes, we define the morphisms
\iota_{p,q}
as induced morphisms from continuous maps.
Set

F_{p}^{slow}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{g\in F_{p}\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}g\in A_{p}(M)\}

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{&g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\rightarrow F_{p}^{(slow)}\,:\,\text{smooth block map},\ g=g_{0}\text{ near }\infty,\\
&\ (1+d)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g)^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|<\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)\}.\end{split}

The latter space includes into the loop space modelled by continuous block maps, which is, of course, weakly homotopy equivalent to the classical loop space.
Approximating continuous maps by smooth maps and a re-scaling argument show that the inclusions
\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{slow}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}F_{p}^{slow}
and
F_{p}^{slow}\hookrightarrow F_{p}
are weak homotopy equivalences, see Lemma
6.1.4
below.

Define

\mathrm{susp}\colon\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\rightarrow\Omega^{q-1}F_{p+1}^{(slow)}

via

\mathrm{susp}(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+2})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=g(x_{p+2},t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})}+\mathrm{d}x_{p+2}^{2}.

These maps are continuous by the following argument:
Recall the adjunction between functions and sections

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(N;\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M\times I^{p}))\cong\Gamma^{\infty}(M\times I^{n}\times N;\mathrm{Pos}(T(M\times I^{p}))\times N),

where
N
is a smooth compact manifold with faces and
\mathrm{Pos}(T(M\times I^{p}))
is the bundle of all positive definite, symmetric bilinear forms on
T(M\times I^{p})
over
M\times I^{p}
.
Under this adjunction, the suspension is induced by the following smooth map of fibre bundles

\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(TM\times I^{p})\times I\times I^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}^{\ast}(\text{--})+\mathord{\mathrm{pr}}_{2}^{\ast}\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Pos}(T(M\times I^{p}\times I))\times I^{q-1},}

(6.1)

via post-composition.
Thus, it induces a continuous map
\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{q-1}F_{p+1}
.
In fact, this map factors through the space of smooth loops.

The map
\iota_{p,q}
is now defined as

\textstyle{\iota_{p,q}\colon\pi_{q}(F_{p})=\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}(\mathrm{susp})}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q-1}F_{p+1}^{(slow)})=\pi_{q-1}(F_{p+1}),}

and
\iota_{n,0}\colon\pi_{0}(F_{n}^{slow})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
is the surjective map that sends the isotopy class of a block metric to its concordance class.

Clearly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\iota_{p+k,q-k}\circ\dots\circ\iota_{p,q}&(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-k-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+k+2})}\\
&=g(x_{p+2},\dots,x_{p+k+2},t_{1},\dots,t_{q-k-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})}+\sum_{j=p+2}^{p+k+2}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2}\end{split}

for
k<q
and all metrics for which the composition is defined.
Thus, the composition
\pi_{q}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{p+q}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
sends a block map to the concordance class of its suspension.

In remains to prove that the image of the suspension
\pi_{n-p}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
is the set of concordance classes of
F_{p}\Omega_{g_{0}}^{n}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.
Consider the map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{pr}\colon F_{p}\Omega_{g_{0}}^{n}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Omega^{n-p,\infty}F_{p}^{slow},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(y\mapsto g_{(\cdot,\cdot,y)}\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1})}\right).

If we turn the set
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
into a topological space by endowing it with the colimit topology, then
\mathfrak{pr}
is continuous and the inverse of
\mathrm{susp}\colon\Omega^{n-p,\infty}F_{p}^{slow}\rightarrow F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.

In particular, the suspension maps the group
\pi_{n-p}(F_{p})
onto the isotopy classes of
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
and thus onto the concordance classes of
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.4
.

Let
\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}
and
F_{p}^{slow}
be as in the proof of Lemma
6.1.3
. Then the inclusions

\displaystyle F_{p}^{slow}\hookrightarrow F_{p}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}F_{p}^{(slow)}

are weak homotopy equivalences.

Proof.

To prove the first statement, we will show the equivalent statement that every map
(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(F_{p},F_{p}^{slow})
can be homotopied through such maps to a map
D^{n}\rightarrow F_{p}^{slow}
.

Given a such map
g\colon(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(F_{p},F^{slow}_{p})
.
As
F_{p}
is a sequential colimit of embeddings between Hausdorff spaces, we can find a sufficiently large
\rho
such that each
g(t)
decomposes on
M\times\rho I^{p+1}
.

By assumption, each
g(t)
decomposes

g(t)|_{\{x_{1}>\rho\}}=g(t)^{TM}+\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}\mathrm{d}x_{j}^{2},

where
g^{TM}
is the induced metric on
TM\subseteq T(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1})
.
The block form of
g(t)
implies its independence of the coordinate
x_{1}
.
Thus, there is a unique map
\gamma\colon D^{n}\times\rho I^{p}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
such that

g(t)=\mathrm{susp}(\gamma(t,\text{--}))
on
\{x_{1}>\rho\}
.
The map
\gamma
is continuous in the
D^{n}
-variable and smooth in the
\rho I^{p}
-variable.

Recall that the defining conditions of
A_{p}
are algebraic expressions of the
2
-jet of
g
.
By compactness, we find therefore a sufficiently large
R>1
such that the map
x\mapsto\gamma(t,x/R)
is an element of
A_{p}
for all
t\in D^{n}
.

Pick a sufficiently large
L=L(R)>0
and a smooth function
\Phi\colon[\rho,\rho+L]\rightarrow[1,R]
that is monotonically increasing and satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi\equiv 1\text{ near }\rho,

\displaystyle\Phi\equiv R\text{ near }\rho+L,

\displaystyle 0\leq\Phi^{\prime}\ll 1,

\displaystyle|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|\ll 1.

For each
s\in[0,1]
, set
\Phi_{s}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1-s)\cdot 1+s\Phi
.
Since
\gamma|_{\partial\rho I^{p}}=\mathrm{const}_{g_{0}}
, we can extend it via
g_{0}
to a block map
\gamma\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
.
The map

\displaystyle\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}\colon D^{n}\times[\rho,\rho+L]\times\rho I^{p}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M),

\displaystyle(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma\bigl(t,1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1})\bigr)

can be extended to a family of block maps

\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}\colon D^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M).

Consider the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}\colon D^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow F_{p}\quad\text{given by}\quad(t,s)\mapsto g(t)|_{\{x_{1}\leq\rho\}}\cup\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})|_{\{x_{1}\geq\rho\}}.

This map is well-defined because
g(t)=\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})
near
\{x_{1}=\rho\}
and
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})>0
provided
|\Phi^{\prime}|
and
|\Phi^{\prime\prime}|
are sufficiently small, see Lemma
6.1.5
below.
It satisfies
\mathcal{H}(\text{--},0)=\gamma
and
\mathcal{H}(\text{--},1)
takes values in
F^{slow}_{p}
.
Since
|(1/\Phi)^{\prime}|\leq 1
and
|(1/\Phi)^{\prime\prime}|\leq 1
, the map
\mathcal{H}
restricts to a map
S^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow F_{p}^{slow}
.

Thus,
\mathcal{H}
is a homotopy of pairs between
g
and a map with values in
F_{p}^{slow}
, so the first statement is proven.

In order to show that
\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}F_{p}^{(slow)}
is a weak homotopy equivalence we consider

\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow F_{p}^{(slow)}\,:\,g\text{ smooth block map},g=g_{0}\text{ near }\infty\},

the space of all smooth block maps into
F_{p}^{(slow)}
without “speed constrains”.
Approximation theory, see Section
2.2.2
for details, yields that the inclusion
\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\hookrightarrow\Omega^{q}F_{p}^{(slow)}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

We will show that the inclusion
\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\hookrightarrow\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}
is a homotopy equivalence.

Consider the map
R\colon\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
given by

R(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{\sup}_{M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}}\left\{1+(1+d)\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g)^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|\right\}.

The values of
R
are always finite because we only plug in block maps that take values in block metrics.
The map
R
is also continuous because it is an algebraic expression of the
2
-jet of
g
considered now as section over the space
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
.

The same holds for the map
\mathtt{m}\colon\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\rightarrow(0,1]
given by

g\mapsto\frac{1}{2d}\inf\left(\bigl\{\left(\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g(t))(m,x)\right)^{1/2}\,:\,(m,x,t)\in M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\bigr\}\right).

The inclusion
\varphi\colon\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}\hookrightarrow\bar{\Omega}^{q,\infty}F_{p}^{(slow)}
is a homotopy equivalence.
Its homotopy inverse is given by
\psi\colon g\mapsto g\circ\left(\mathtt{m}/(1+\mathtt{m}^{2})^{1/2}\cdot R^{-1}\right)
.
The homotopy between the identity and
\varphi\circ\psi
, respectively
\psi\circ\varphi
, is given by

(g,s)\mapsto g\circ\left(s\cdot\mathtt{m}/(1+\mathtt{m}^{2})^{1/2}\cdot R^{-1}+(1-s)\cdot 1\right).

because if
g\in A_{n}
, then also all re-parametrisation of
g
that are “slower” than
g
are also elements of
A_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 6.1.5
.

The suspension of
\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}
defined in the proof of Lemma
6.1.4
has positive scalar curvature.

Proof.

We know from

\displaystyle 0<\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(\gamma))

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma)+\sum_{j=2}^{p+1}\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}\gamma^{op})^{2})-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}\gamma)^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{j}\gamma)

\displaystyle=:\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)+\mathfrak{err}(g)

that
\mathfrak{err}(\gamma)>-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma)
.
The block form of the map
g
actually implies
\mathfrak{err}(\gamma)>-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma)+\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
The relevant differentials of
\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}
are given by

\partial_{j}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\Phi_{s}(x_{1})}\cdot(\partial_{j}\gamma)(1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1}),&\text{ if }j>1,\\
\frac{-\Phi_{s}^{\prime}(x_{1})}{\Phi_{s}(x_{1})}\cdot\sum_{k=2}^{p+1}(\partial_{k}\gamma)(1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1}))x_{k},&\text{ if }j=1,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\partial_{j}^{2}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}=\frac{1}{\Phi_{s}(x_{1})^{2}}\cdot(\partial_{j}^{2}\gamma)(1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1})),\qquad\text{ if }j>1.

The last partial differential is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{1}^{2}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}=&\frac{(\Phi_{s}^{\prime})^{2}}{\Phi_{s}^{4}}\cdot\sum_{k,l=2}^{p+1}(\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\gamma)(1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1}))x_{k}x_{l}\\
&\quad+\frac{-\Phi_{s}^{\prime\prime}\Phi_{s}+2(\Phi_{s}^{\prime})^{2}}{\Phi_{s}^{4}}\cdot\sum_{k=2}^{p+1}(\partial_{k}\gamma)(1/\Phi_{s}(x_{1})\cdot(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1}))x_{k}.\end{split}

Since
\gamma(t,\text{--})
is constant outside of
\rho I^{p}
for all
t\in D^{n}
, the map
\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}
is constant outside of
\rho R\cdot I^{p}
.
Thus, by increasing
L(R)
, we can make
|\partial_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}|
and
|\partial_{1}^{2}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}|
arbitrary small (by choosing
\Phi
with sufficiently small derivatives).

This, in particular, implies we can choose
L
and
\Phi
such that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{err}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{s}(x_{1})^{-2}\cdot\mathfrak{err}(\gamma)\circ 1/\Phi_{s}+\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s,op})^{2})-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})^{2}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&>-\Phi_{s}(x_{1})^{-2}\cdot\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma)\circ 1/\Phi_{s}+\varepsilon\\
&\quad+\,\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s,op})^{2})-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\partial^{2}_{1}\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})^{2}\end{split}

\displaystyle>-\Phi_{s}(x_{1})^{-2}\cdot\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s})\circ 1/\Phi_{s}=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}),

and hence so that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{\mathrm{aux}}^{s}))
is positive.
∎

The images of
F_{p}\Omega^{n}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
in
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
form a filtration of subsets.
We will see later that this filtration is a filtration of subgroups and that the quotients of the filtration groups and its pre-successors agree with the infinity page of a spectral sequence.
To set up the spectral sequence, we first need to realise the maps
\iota_{p,q}
as the boundary operator of a long exact sequence associated to a fibration.
The next definition introduces the total space of this fibration.

Definition 6.1.6
.

For
p\geq 0
, let
G_{p}
be the topological space whose underlying set is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{p}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\Bigl\{g\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1})\,:\,&\partial^{1}_{1}g\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{p}(M),\\
&\partial^{\omega}_{j}g=g_{0}\text{ for }(j,\omega)\neq(1,1),\,(p+1,1)\Bigr\}\end{split}

and whose topology is the (subspace topology of the) colimit topology coming from the inclusions
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{p+1})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times SI^{p+1})
.
We further define
G_{p}^{slow}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{g\in G_{p}\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}g\in A_{p}\}
.

Note that the proof of Lemma
6.1.4
also implies that the inclusion
G_{p}^{slow}\hookrightarrow G_{p}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

There is a canonical map
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}\colon G_{p+1}^{(slow)}\rightarrow F_{p}^{(slow)}
given by
g\mapsto\partial^{1}_{p+1}g
.
Identifying this map as a Serre fibration is probably difficult as we would have to deal with arbitrary continuous maps.
Luckily, the following weakening is easier to verify in our setup and is still good enough for our purpose.

Definition 6.1.7
.

A smooth map between two smooth manifolds or between two open subsets of affine Fréchet spaces
p\colon E\rightarrow B
is a
fibration in the smooth category
, if for all given solid outer squares

\textstyle{D^{n}\times\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{D^{n}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}
\textstyle{B}

with
f
and
h
smooth and
h
stationary near the boundary of
[0,1]
, there exists a smooth map
\mathcal{H}
that is stationary near the boundary of
[0,1]
and makes the diagram commutative.

The next lemma is no surprise as continuous maps can be approximated by smooth ones relatively to closed subsets on which they are already smooth.
With the help of such approximations, the next lemma will be proved as the analogous statement for Serre fibrations.

Lemma 6.1.8
.

Let
p\colon E\rightarrow B
be a fibration in the smooth category.
Then
p
is a homotopy fibration, that is, the comparison map from each fibre into the homotopy fibre is a weak homotopy equivalence for all path components of
B
.

Proof.

Pick a point
b_{0}\in B
and denote its fibre by
F\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=p^{-1}(\{b_{0}\})
.
The fibre comparison map

\displaystyle j\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathrm{hofib}(f;b_{0})\qquad\text{ given by }\qquad f\mapsto(f,\mathrm{const}_{b_{0}})

is a weak homotopy equivalence if we find, for each
\varphi\colon(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(\mathrm{hofib}(f;b_{0}),F)
, a homotopy of pairs
H\colon[0,1]\times(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(\mathrm{hofib}(f;b_{0}),F)
that deforms
\varphi
into a map whose target is
F
.

Each map
\varphi\colon(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(\mathrm{hofib}(p;b_{0}),F)
is given by a commutative diagram

\textstyle{D^{n}\times\{1\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(1)}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{D^{n}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(2)}}
\textstyle{B}

so that
\varphi^{(2)}
restricts on
S^{n-1}\times[0,1]\cup D^{n}\times\{0\}
to the constant map with value
b_{0}
.
By approximation theory, there is a homotopy
\varphi^{(1)}_{t}
such that
\varphi^{(1)}_{0}=\varphi^{(1)}
and
\varphi^{(1)}_{1}
is a smooth map.
The homotopy can be chosen to be stationary near the boundary.
We modify this homotopy in three steps to a homotopy of diagrams that is stationary near
D^{n}\times\{0,1\}
.

First, we re-parameterise
\varphi^{(2)}=\varphi_{-1}^{(2)}
such that the result
\varphi_{0}^{(2)}
is stationary near the boundary.
We can choose the interpolating homotopy
\varphi^{(2)}_{t}
with
t\in[-1,0]
to be relative to the boundary.
Next, we define, for all
t\in[0,1]
, the homotopy

\displaystyle\varphi^{(2)}_{t}(x,s)=\begin{cases}\varphi^{(2)}(x,(1+t)s),&s\in[0,(1+t)^{-1}],\\
p(\varphi^{(1)}_{(1+t)s-1}(x)),&s\in[(1+t)^{-1},1].\end{cases}

The map is
\varphi^{(2)}_{1}
is not smooth yet, but it smooth near
D^{n}\times\{0,1\}
and constant on the boundary
D^{n}\times\{0\}\cup S^{n-1}\times[0,1]
.
Thus, as the last step, we can homotopy it relative to the boundary to a smooth map
\varphi^{(2)}_{2}
that is stationary near
D^{n}\times\{0,1\}
using approximation theory.

This diagram homotopy restricts to the diagram homotopy

\textstyle{S^{n-1}\times\{1\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(1)}_{t}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{S^{n-1}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(2)}_{t}}
\textstyle{b_{0}}

In conclusion, each map
\varphi\colon(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(\mathrm{hofib}(p;b_{0}),F)
can be homotopied through map of pairs to a smooth map
\psi
, so that
\psi^{(2)}
are stationary near
D^{n}\times\partial[0,1]
.

To ease the notation, assume that
\varphi
has this property in the first place.
As
p\colon E\rightarrow B
is a fibration in the smooth category, we find a lift
\Phi
with restriction

\textstyle{D^{n}\times\{1\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(1)}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{S^{n-1}\times\{1\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(1)}|_{S^{n-1}}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D^{n}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(2)}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{B,}
\textstyle{S^{n-1}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(2)}|_{S^{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi|_{S^{n-1}}}
\textstyle{b_{0}.}

We use
\Phi
to define
\mathcal{H}\colon[0,1]\times D^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow E
as
\mathcal{H}\colon(t,x,s)\mapsto\Phi(x,ts).

The desired homotopy
H\colon[0,1]\times(D^{n},S^{n-1})\rightarrow(\mathrm{hofib}(f,b_{0}),F)
is the continuous map that corresponds to

\textstyle{[0,1]\times D^{n}\times\{1\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{H}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{[0,1]\times D^{n}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p\circ\mathcal{H}}
\textstyle{B,}

so the lemma is proven.
∎

Proposition 6.1.9
.

For all
p\geq 0
, the restriction to the last front face
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}\colon G_{p+1}\rightarrow F_{p}
is well-defined and continuous.
Furthermore, the sequence

\textstyle{F_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{G_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\upharpoonleft}}
\textstyle{F_{p}}

is a fibration in the smooth category.

Proof.

The cubical identities imply that
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}=\partial^{1}_{p+2}
maps
G_{p+1}
to
F_{p}
.

The restriction to the last front face is a continuous map
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{p+2})\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{p+1})
as it is induced by a pull back of a vector bundle map.
Since these restriction maps are compatible with the inclusions
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{q})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times SI^{q})
, they induce a map
G_{p+1}\rightarrow F_{p}
, which agrees with
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}
.

Given a commutative diagram of smooth maps

\textstyle{D^{n}\times\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{G_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\upharpoonleft}}
\textstyle{D^{n}\times[0,1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{F_{p}}

such that
h
is stationary near
D^{n}\times\partial[0,1]
.
By Lemma
2.2.12
, the maps
f
and
h
factor through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times\rho I^{p+2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times\rho I^{p+1})
, respectively, as
G_{p+1}
and
F_{p}
are colimits of relative
T_{1}
inclusions.
Let
\Phi\colon D^{n}\times\partial[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow F_{p}
be given by

\displaystyle h(\text{--},0),\text{ on }D^{n}\times 0\times[0,1],

\displaystyle h,\text{ on }D^{n}\times 1\times[0,1],

\displaystyle h(\text{--},0),\text{ on }D^{n}\times[0,1]\times 0,

\displaystyle h,\text{ on }D^{n}\times[0,1]\times 1.

Since
h
is stationary near
\partial[0,1]
, we can extend
\Phi
to a smooth map on
D^{n}\times I^{2}
that is independent of the normal variable of each face of
I^{2}
near
\partial I^{2}
.
Let
\Psi(x,t)
be the block map extending
[\rho,\rho+1]\ni s\mapsto\Phi(x,s-\rho,t)
by elongation and let
\mathcal{H}\colon D^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow G_{p+1}
be given by

\mathcal{H}(x,t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=f\cup\mathrm{susp}\,\Phi\left(x,\frac{(\text{--})-\rho}{R},t\right)=:f|_{\{x_{p+2}\leq\rho\}}\cup\mathrm{susp}\left({}_{R}\Psi(x,t)\right)|_{\{x_{p+2}\geq\rho\}}.

If
R
is so large that

|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{s}{}_{R}\Psi)^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{s}{}_{R}\Psi)^{2}|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{s}^{2}{}_{R}\Psi)|<\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}({}_{R}\Psi(x,t))(s),

then
\mathrm{susp}({}_{R}\Psi)(x,t)
is a psc metric on
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, which also agrees with
f(x)
near
M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\times\{\rho\}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{H}\colon D^{n}\times[0,1]\rightarrow G_{p+1}
is well-defined.
The map
\mathcal{H}
is smooth because
\Phi
smooth and
\mathrm{susp}
is a smooth map between smooth Fréchet spaces as
\mathrm{susp}
is induced by vector bundle maps, see Equation
6.1
.
From
{}_{R}\Psi(x,t)(\rho+R)=h(x,t)
follows that
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}(\mathcal{H}(x,t))=h(x,t)
, so
\mathcal{H}
lifts the homotopy
h
.
The lift
\mathcal{H}
is also stationary near
\partial[0,1]
because
\Phi
is stationary near the corner
(1,1)\in[0,1]^{2}
.
Thus, the restriction to the last front face
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}
is a fibration in the smooth category.
∎

Lemma
6.1.8
and Proposition
6.1.9
yield a long exact sequence of homotopy groups.
We would like to relate the connecting homomorphisms
\partial_{q}\colon\pi_{q}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{q-1}(F_{p+1})
to
\iota_{p,q}
.
Before we formulate the next lemma, let us recall that every fibration
F\hookrightarrow E\rightarrow B
induces a long exact sequence on homotopy groups via the Puppe sequence, see for example
[
davis2001lecture
]
*Theorem 6.42.
The boundary map
\partial_{1}\colon\pi_{1}(B)\rightarrow\pi_{0}(F)
, in this picture, is given by evaluating the fibre transport at the base point of the fibre.
With this in mind, we denote by
(-1)\partial_{1}
the map that evaluates the inverse fibre transport at the base point.

Lemma 6.1.10
.

For all
p\geq 0
and
q\geq 1
, we have

\iota_{p,q}=(-1)^{q}\partial_{q}\colon\pi_{q}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{q-1}(F_{p+1}).

Proof.

We consider the case
q=1
first.
Recall that
\partial_{1}\colon\pi_{1}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{0}(F_{p+1})
comes from the Puppe sequence.
Each map
d
, possibly only defined on a weakly equivalent subspace, that makes the diagram

\textstyle{\Omega F_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq_{w}}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{F_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq_{w}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})}

homotopy commutative gives
\partial_{1}=\pi_{0}(d)
.
Here,
\mathrm{hofib}
is the homotopy fibre of the canonical map
\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})\rightarrow G_{p+1}
.
The homotopy fibres are defined using block maps, so for example,

\begin{split}\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})=\{(g,\gamma)\,|\,&g\in G_{p+1},\ \gamma\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow F_{p}\text{ block map},\\
&\lim_{t\to-\infty}\gamma(t)=g_{0},\lim_{t\to\infty}\gamma(t)=\mathord{\upharpoonleft}(g)\}.\end{split}

It is straighfoward to check that the composition

\textstyle{\delta\colon\Omega^{1,\infty}F_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\Omega F_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})}

is given by

(t\mapsto g(t))\mapsto(g_{0},t\mapsto g(t))

and that the composition

\textstyle{\mathrm{susp}\colon\Omega^{1,\infty}F_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{susp}}
\textstyle{F_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})}

is given by

(t\mapsto g(t))\mapsto(\mathrm{susp}(g),\mathrm{const}_{g_{0}}).

We will construct a path between
\delta(\bar{g})
and
\mathrm{susp}(g)
for all
g\in\Omega^{1,\infty}F_{p}
, where
\bar{\cdot}
is the standard involution on the loop space.
This will then imply the claim for
q=1
.

Choose a
\rho>0
such that the smooth block map
g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow F_{p}
is constant (with value
g_{0}
) outside of
M\times[-\rho,\rho]
.
Let
\Phi\colon[-\rho,\rho]\times[-1,1]\rightarrow[-\rho,\rho]
be a smooth map that satisfies

\displaystyle\Phi(\text{--},-1)=-\rho\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi(\text{--},s)=\begin{cases}-\rho,&\text{ near }\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-\rho},\\
s\cdot\rho,&\text{ near }\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq\rho}.\end{cases}

Fix
R\geq 1
such that
R^{-1}\left(|\partial_{t}\Phi(t,s)|+|\partial_{t}^{2}\Phi(t,s)|\right)<1
for all
(t,s)\in[-\rho,\rho]\times[-1,1]
and define the path

\displaystyle H_{g}\colon[-1,1]\rightarrow\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft}),

\displaystyle s\mapsto[\mathrm{susp}(t\mapsto g(\Phi(t/R,s))),\,t\mapsto g(-\Phi(t,-s))].

We need to show that this path is a well-defined map.

First, we show that
the scalar curvature of
\mathrm{susp}(t\mapsto g(\Phi(t/R,s)))
is positive for all
s\in[-\rho,\rho]
.
We abbreviate
\Phi(\text{--}/R,s)
to
{}_{R}\Phi
as
s
is not important in the calculation.
The chain rule gives

\displaystyle\quad\,\left|\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(g\circ{}_{R}\Phi))-\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g\circ{}_{R}\Phi)\right|

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\biggl|\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{t}g^{op})^{2})\cdot R^{-2}{}_{R}(\partial_{t}\Phi)^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{t}g)^{2}\cdot R^{-2}{}_{R}(\partial_{t}\Phi)^{2}\\
&\qquad-\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{t}^{2}g)\cdot R^{-2}{}_{R}(\partial_{t}\Phi)^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{t}g)\cdot R^{-2}{}_{R}(\partial_{t}^{2}\Phi)\biggr|\end{split}

\displaystyle<(1+d)\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{q}|\mathrm{tr}((\partial_{j}g)^{2})|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}g)^{2}|+|\mathrm{tr}(\partial_{j}^{2}g)|,

provided
R
is sufficiently large.
This implies
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(\mathrm{susp}(g\circ{}_{R}\Phi))>0
.

From
\mathrm{susp}(t\mapsto g(\Phi(t/R,s)))=g(s\rho)+\mathrm{d}t^{2}
on
\{t\geq R\}
we conclude
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}\mathrm{susp}(t\mapsto g(\Phi(t/R,s)))=g(s\rho)
, which agrees with

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}g(-\Phi(t/R,-s))=g(-\Phi(\rho R/R,-s))=g(s\rho).

Thus,
H_{g}
takes values in
\mathrm{hofib}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})
. It is also smooth, as
\Phi
is a smooth map.

The elements
H_{g}(1)
and
\mathrm{susp}(g)
are connected through the two paths

[0,1]\ni\lambda\mapsto[\mathrm{susp}(g(\lambda\cdot(\text{--}/R)+(1-\lambda)\Phi(\text{--}/R,1))),\mathrm{const}_{g_{0}}],

and

[1,R]\ni r\mapsto[\mathrm{susp}(g(\text{--}/r)),\mathrm{const}_{g_{0}}],

while
\delta(\overline{g})
and
H_{g}(-1)
are connected through the path

\lambda\mapsto[g_{0},g(-\lambda\mathrm{id}-(1-\lambda)\Phi(\text{--}/R,1))].

Thus, the elements
\mathrm{susp}(g)
and
\delta(\bar{g})
lie in the same path-component, which implies the claim for the case
q=1
.

The general case
q\geq 1
can be deduced from the special one as follows.
By definition,
\partial_{q}=\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q-1}\delta)
, so it is enough to compare the two maps

\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}_{1},\,\mathrm{susp}_{q}\colon\Omega^{q,\infty}F_{p}\rightarrow\Omega^{q-1}F_{p+1}.

The first map is defined as

\bigl((t_{1},\dots,t_{q})\mapsto g(t_{1},\dots,t_{q})\bigr)\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})\mapsto\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1}),

where

\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+2})}=g(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1},x_{p+2})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})}+\mathrm{d}x_{p+2}^{2}

while the second map
\mathrm{susp}_{q}
was defined as

\mathrm{susp}_{q}(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+2})}=g(x_{p+2},t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})_{(m,x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})}+\mathrm{d}x_{p+2}^{2}.

Thus,

\mathrm{susp}_{q}(g)=\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}_{1}\circ(1\ 2\ \dots\ q),

where
(1\ 2\ \dots\ q)(g)(t_{1},\dots,t_{q})=g(t_{q},t_{1},\dots,t_{q-1})
.

This identity now implies the claim via

\displaystyle\iota_{p,q}

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}(\mathrm{susp}_{q})=\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}_{1}\circ(1\ 2\ \dots\ q))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{q-1}\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q-1}\mathrm{susp}_{1})=(-1)^{q-1}\pi_{0}(\delta\circ\bar{\cdot})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{q}\pi_{0}(\Omega^{q-1}\delta)=(-1)^{q}\partial_{q}.

∎

If
p\geq 1
, then every element
g\in G_{p+1}
or
g\in F_{p}
restricts to the base point
g_{0}
on at least one pair of opposite faces.
This allows us to glue two block metrics together along their common faces.
Sadly, the gluing construction does not give an
H
-space structure because of the following reason:
The cubes outside of those the metrics decompose are of unknown length, and the function that assigns to a block metric the diameter of its smallest cube outside it decomposes is not continuous.
However, different choices yield elements that can be canonically deformed into each other, so we can still introduce “
H
-space like group structures” on the homotopy groups.

Recall that
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}\colon x\mapsto x+Re_{j}
is a diffeomorphism and the abbreviation
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,R\ast}(g)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-R}^{\ast}(g)
.

Lemma 6.1.11
.

For
p\geq 1
and
q\geq 0
, there are
p
-many monoid structures on
\pi_{q}(G_{p+1})
that are defined as follows:

Let
g,h\colon D^{n}\rightarrow G_{p+1}
represent
[g]
,
[h]\in\pi_{q}(G_{p+1})
and assume that they factor through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}}(M\times RI^{p+2})
.
Then, for all
2\leq j\leq p+1
, we define

(g+_{j,R}h)(t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-2R}(g(t))|_{\{x_{j}\leq 0\}}\cup\mathrm{Sh}_{j,2R}(h(t))|_{\{x_{j}\geq 0\}}

and

[g]+_{j}[h]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[g+_{j,R}h]\in\pi_{q}(G_{p+1}).

If
p\geq 2
, then all of these monoid structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to each other.
If
q\geq 1
, then these monoids structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to the group structure of the homotopy group.

The same statement is true for
\pi_{q}(F_{p})
.

Proof.

Since
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,-2R}(g(t))
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{j,2R}(h(t))
agree with the base point near
\{x_{j}=0\}
, the metric
(g+_{j,R}h)
is well-defined and has positive scalar curvature.
The shift operators also preserves the defining conditions of
G_{p+1}
, so
g+_{j,R}h\in G_{p+1}
.

For
R_{0}\leq R_{1}
and all
s\in[0,1]
, we set
R_{s}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(1-s)R_{0}+sR_{1}
.
A homotopy relative to
\partial D^{n}
between
g+_{j,R_{0}}h
and
g+_{j,R_{1}}h
is given by
s\mapsto g+_{j,R_{s}}h
.

Let
g,h\colon(D^{n}\times I^{n},\partial D^{n}\times I)\rightarrow(G_{p+1},g_{0})
homotopies relative to the boundary.
For a sufficiently large
R
, the map
g+_{j,R}h
is a homotopy relative to the boundary between
g(\text{--},0)+_{j,R}h(\text{--},0)
and
g(\text{--},1)+_{j,R}h(\text{--},1)
.

It is straightforward to check that these structures are associative and that the neutral element is given by the base point
g_{0}
.

The proof that these structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to each other if
p\geq 2
is formally identical with the proof that higher homotopy groups are abelian.

The proof that these monoid structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to the group structure from the homotopy groups is formally the same as the proof for
H
-spaces.
∎

Corollary 6.1.12
.

For
q\geq 1
, the groups
\pi_{q}(G_{p+1})
and
\pi_{q}(F_{p})
are abelian if
p+q\geq 2
.

The reader certainly has notice the similarities in notation and formulation to Theorem
3.4.4
.
This is no coincidence as it carries over to the homotopy fibre.

Proposition 6.1.13
.

There are
n
-many group structures on
\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet},g_{0})
, for all
n\geq 1
, defined as follows:
If
g,h
represent
[g],[h]\in\pi_{n}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
and decompose outside
M\times RI^{n+1}
, then

[g]+_{j}[h]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=[g+_{j,R}h]

for all
2\leq j\leq n+1
.

If
r_{j}
denotes the reflection at the hyperplane
\{x_{j}=0\}
, then the inverse element of
[g]
for
+_{j}
is given by
[r_{j}^{\ast}g]
.
The structures are Eckmann-Hilton related to each other.
Furthermore,
+_{2}
agrees with the group structure provided by cubical set theory.

Proof.

As the construction
g\mapsto g^{\angle}
preserves suspensions, see Proposition
3.4.2
, all arguments in the proof of Theorem
3.4.4
carry over immediately to the homotopy fibre.
∎

Corollary 6.1.14
.

The map
\iota_{p,q}\colon\pi_{q}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{q-1}(F_{p+1})
is a monoid homomorphism for all
p\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and all
q\geq 1
.
Furthermore,
\iota_{p,0}\colon\pi_{0}(F_{p})\rightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathrm{hofib}_{p})
is a monoid homomorphism for
p\geq 1
.

Proof.

For
q>1
, the claim follows from Lemma
6.1.10
, so it remains to check the case
q=1
.

If we realise elements of
\pi_{1}(F_{p})
as homotopy classes of block maps
\gamma\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow F_{p}
, then the group structure is given
[\gamma_{1}]\cdot[\gamma_{2}]=[\gamma_{1}(\cdot-2\rho)\cup\gamma_{2}(\cdot+2\rho)]
for all sufficiently large
\rho>0
such that
\gamma_{j}
is constant (with value
g_{0}
) outside of
[-\rho,\rho]
.

The claim now follows from

\displaystyle\iota_{p,1}([\gamma_{1}]\cdot[\gamma_{2}])

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{1}(\cdot+2\rho)\cup\gamma_{2}(\cdot-2\rho))]

\displaystyle=[{\mathrm{Sh}_{-2\rho}}(\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{1}))|_{\{x_{p+2}\leq 0\}}\cup{\mathrm{Sh}_{2\rho}}(\mathrm{susp}(\gamma_{2}))|_{\{x_{p+2}\geq 0\}}]

\displaystyle=\iota_{p,1}([\gamma_{1}])+_{p+2}\iota_{p,1}([\gamma_{2}]).

To see that
\iota_{p+1,0}
is a monoid homomorphism, one uses the geometric addition on
\pi_{p+1}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
, which agrees with the usual one by Proposition
6.1.13
.
∎

Definition 6.1.15
.

Define the space of
psc pseudo isotopies
to be the following subspace

{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{g\in G_{p}\,:\,\partial^{1}_{1}g=g_{0}\}

of
G_{p}
.

Lemma 6.1.16
.

The inclusion
{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)\hookrightarrow G_{p}^{slow}
is a weak homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

We will actually show the following slightly stronger statement:
Every block map
g\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow G_{p}^{slow}
can be homotopied through block maps into a block map
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
such that if
g(t)\in{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
, then the homotopy at
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
stays in
{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
.

We find a sufficiently large
R>0
such that
g(t)
decomposes away from
M\times RI^{p+1}
for all
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Since each block map is uniquely determined by its restriction to a sufficiently large cube, we can find a sufficiently large
\rho>R
such that
(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}
is a psc metric for all
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, where
(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}
refers to the construction of Proposition
3.4.2
in the
x_{1}
-
x_{p+1}
plane in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}
around the point
(\rho,0,\dots,0,\rho)
, see Figure
6.1
for a visualisation.

Figure 6.1:
The construction for
p=1
and
p=2
.

So far,
(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}
is only a metric on
\{x_{1}\geq\rho,x_{p+1}\leq\rho\}
.
We use its product form near
\{x_{p+1}=\rho\}
to extend it to
\{x_{1}\geq\rho\}
.
Since
g(t)
and
(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}
agree near
\{x_{1}=\rho\}
, the map

t\mapsto g(t)|_{\{x_{1}\leq\rho\}}\cup(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}|_{\{x_{1}\geq\rho\}}

is a smooth block map
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow G_{p}
, which we denote by
g\cup(\partial^{1}_{1}g)^{\angle}_{\rho}
.
If
g(t)
lies in
{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
, then
g(t)\cup(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))_{\rho}^{\angle}
also lies in there.

We claim that this map is homotopic to
g
via a homotopy with the desired properties.
Since
\partial^{1}_{1}g(t)=\mathrm{susp}(h(t,\text{--}))
, where
h(t,\text{--})\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is a block map that lies in the subset
A_{p}
, the extension of
(\partial^{1}_{1}g)^{\angle}_{\rho}
to the half plane satisfies

(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))^{\angle}_{\rho}=\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\text{--})),

where
\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\text{--})\colon\{x_{1}\geq\rho\}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
is independent of
x_{1}
near
\{x_{1}=\rho\}
and
\{x_{1}\geq 2\rho\}
, see Proposition
3.4.2
.
We may assume, after rescaling
h(t,\text{--})
in the second component (with a constant independent of
t
)
1
1

1

Note that this does not change the homotopy type of the block map
h
, that
h(t,\text{--})
is sufficiently slowly parameterised such that
\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\text{--})
satisfies the same speed-constrains as an element of
A_{p+1}
.

If
\varphi\colon[\rho,2\rho]\rightarrow[\rho,2\rho]
is a smooth, monotonically increasing, surjective function that is constant near boundary, and such that
\mathrm{supp}\,(\varphi^{\prime}-1)
is contained in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
\{x_{1}=\rho\}
and
\{x_{1}=2\rho\}
on which
\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t;\text{--})
is still independent of
x_{1}
for all
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t;\varphi(\text{--}),\text{--}))
is still a psc metric and convex combinations yield the following chain of homotopies

\displaystyle g(t)\cup(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))_{\rho}^{\angle}

\displaystyle=g(t)\cup\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\text{--}))

\displaystyle\simeq g(t)\cup\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\varphi(\text{--}),\text{--})),

\displaystyle\text{via }s\mapsto(1-s)\mathrm{id}+s\varphi,

\displaystyle\simeq g(t)\cup\mathrm{susp}(\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\rho,\text{--})),

\displaystyle\text{via }s\mapsto(1-s)\varphi+s\rho.\,

Note that the last homotopy is a homotopy of positive scalar curvature metrics because
\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,(1-s)\rho+s\rho,\text{--})
satisfies the speed-constrains of
A_{p+1}
since
\mathfrak{h}_{\rho}(t,\varphi(\text{--}))
does it.

If
g\in{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
so that
\partial^{1}_{1}g(t)=g_{0}
, then
(\partial^{1}_{1}g(t))_{\rho}^{\angle}=g_{0}
and the homotopies stay in
{C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M)
.
∎

Corollary 6.1.17
.

The set
\pi_{q}(G_{p+1})
has
(p+1)
-many Eckmann-Hilton related monoid structures that extends the ones from Lemma
6.1.11
.

Proof.

The defined additions in Lemma
6.1.11
restrict to
\pi_{q}({C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M))
.
The additional monoid structure is given by
+_{1}
.
∎

6.2
Abstract Construction of the Spectral Sequence

We describe the algebraic machinery to construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence.
This forces us to leave the realm of homological algebra as not all sets we are considering are abelian groups.

The starting point is the following exact couple of
\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
-bigraded, pointed sets

\textstyle{D_{p-1,q+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{p-1,q+1}}
\scriptstyle{(1,-1)}
\textstyle{D_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(0,0)}
\scriptstyle{j_{p,q}}
\textstyle{E_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{(-1,0)}
\scriptstyle{k_{p,q}}

(6.2)

Here, the tuples in the diagram refer to the bigrading of the corresponding map.
We further require that the sets
D_{p,q}
satisfy the following properties:

•

D_{p,q}=0
if
p<0
.

•

D_{p,0}
is a monoid if
p\geq 1
, it is abelian if
p\geq 2
.

•

D_{p,q}
is a group if
q\geq 1
, it is abelian if
p+q\geq 2
.

In particular,
D_{p,q}
is not a monoid only if
p=q=0
, and it is not an abelian monoid only if
p+q\leq 1
.
We require the sets
E_{p,q}
to satisfy

•

E_{p,q}=0
if
p<0
or
q<0
.

•

E_{p,0}
is a monoid if
p\geq 2
, it is abelian if
p\geq 3
.

•

E_{p,q}
is a group if
q\geq 1
, it is abelian if additionally
p+q\geq 2
.

In particular,
E_{p,q}
is not a monoid only if
q=0
and
p\leq 1
, it is not an abelian monoid only if
p+q\leq 1
or
(p,q)=(2,0)
.
We require further that

•

all maps in diagram (
6.2
) are homomorphisms of monoids if domain and target are monoids.

This implies, in particular, that the kernel of a map,
\mathrm{i.e.}
, the pre-image of the base point is a submonoid or subgroup whenever the domain of the map is a monoid or a group.
Exact sequences of (abelian) monoids are in general badly behaved; monoid homomorphisms are not necessarily injective if the kernel is trivial, and the homomorphism-theorem for monoids fails to hold
2
2

2

A counterexample is
(\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0},+)\rightarrow(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\times)
,
n\mapsto[2^{n}]
. This monoid homomorphisms is not injective but has zero kernel and the homomorphism theorem fails to hold because
\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}/\{0\}=\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\neq(\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}_{2},\times)
.

If the target is an abelian group, then the homomorphism theorem still holds true.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

Given an exact sequence of abelian monoids

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

If
A
is an abelian group, then
M/f(L)=\mathrm{coker}(f)
is also an abelian group and
g
induces an isomorphism
\bar{g}\colon M/f(L)\xrightarrow{\cong}A
.

Proof.

Recall that
M/f(L)=M/\sim
is the set of equivalence classes with respect to the equivalence relation

m_{1}\sim m_{2}:\Longleftrightarrow\exists l_{1},l_{2}\in L\text{ s.t. }m_{1}+f(l_{1})=m_{2}+f(l_{2}).

It inherits the abelian monoid structure from
M
.
By exactness, the canonical map
\bar{g}
is well-defined and surjective.

To prove that
M/f(L)
is an abelian group we argue as follows:
For all
x\in M
, there is, since
g
is surjective, a
y\in M
such that
g(y)=-g(x)
.
Hence, by exactness,
[y]\in M/f(L)
is an additive inverse of
[x]\in M/f(L)
, so
M/f(L)
is an abelian group.

We already know that
\bar{g}
is surjective.
Since
M/f(L)
is an abelian group, it remains to show that
\ker(\bar{g})
is trivial.
But this follows immediately from the fact that
g^{-1}(0)=f(L)
and
M\rightarrow M/f(L)
that is surjective.
∎

Since
E_{p,q}=0
if
q<0
, the previous lemma implies the following consequence.

Corollary 6.2.2
.

The maps
\iota_{p,q}\colon D_{p,q}\rightarrow D_{p+1,q-1}
are isomorphisms of monoids if
p\geq 1
and
q<0
.

Although we cannot apply the machinery of homological algebra to turn this exact couple into a spectral sequence, we can still construct the spectral sequence in an ad-hoc manner by mimicking the construction of a spectral sequence out of an exact couple.
For
r\geq 1
, we define

\displaystyle Z_{p,q}^{r}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=k_{p,q}^{-1}\left(\mathrm{im}[i^{r-1}\colon D_{p-r,q+r-1}\rightarrow D_{p-1,q}]\right),

\displaystyle B_{p,q}^{r}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=j_{p,q}\left(\ker[i^{r-1}\colon D_{p,q}\rightarrow D_{p+r-1,q-r+1}]\right).

These sets fit into a chain of inclusions

0=B_{p,q}^{1}\subseteq B^{2}_{p,q}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Z^{2}_{p,q}\subseteq Z^{1}_{p,q}=E_{p,q}

and we use this chain to define

Z^{\infty}_{p,q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigcap_{r\geq 1}Z^{r}_{p,q}=Z^{p+1}_{p,q}\quad\text{and}\quad B^{\infty}_{p,q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigcup_{r\geq 1}B^{r}_{p,q}=B^{p+2}_{p,q},

where we used Corollary
6.2.2
to deduce
B^{\infty}_{p,q}=B^{q+2}_{p,q}
.

For
q\geq 1
, we define

E^{r}_{p,q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=Z^{r}_{p,q}/B^{r}_{p,q}.

Note that the groups
Z^{r}_{0,1}
and
B^{r}_{0,1}
do not need to be abelian, so
E^{r}_{0,1}
might be just a pointed set of left cosets.

We now define the differentials.
For
p,q\geq 0
, we set

d^{r}_{p,q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=j_{p-r,q+r-1}\circ(i^{r-1})^{-1}\circ k_{p,q}\colon Z^{r}_{p,q}\rightarrow E^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1}.

For all other sets, we define the differentials to be zero,
\mathrm{i.e.}
, the constant map whose value is the base point.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

(i)

The differentials are well-defined.

(ii)

\mathrm{im}\,d^{r}_{p,q}=B^{r+1}_{p-r,q+r-1}/B^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1}
for
r\geq 2
and
\mathrm{im}\,d^{1}_{p,q}=B^{2}_{p-1,q}
.

(iii)

\ker d^{r}_{p,q}=Z^{r+1}_{p,q}
for
r\geq 1
.

The proof of this lemma is inspired by the discussion of
[
boardman1999conditionally
]
.
However, we cannot rely on isomorphism-theorems to identify various quotients, so we have to calculate everything “by hand”.

Proof.

We start with the proof of
(i)
.
There is nothing to prove for
d^{1}_{p,q}
, so we assume
r\geq 2
.

By definition,
k_{p,q}\colon Z^{r}_{p,q}\rightarrow\mathrm{im}[i^{r-1}\colon D_{p-r,q+r-1}\rightarrow D_{p-1,q}]
.
As
r\geq 2
, the domain of
i^{r-1}
is a group.
We may also exclude the case
(p,q)=(1,0)
as the differential
d^{r}_{1,0}
has zero as target, so that the target of
i^{r-1}
is a monoid, too.

Thus, each pair of elements
x,y\in D_{p-r,q+r-1}
with
i^{r-1}(x)=i^{r-1}(y)=k_{p,q}(a)
are automatically in
\ker[i^{r}\colon D_{p-r,q+r-1}\rightarrow D_{p,q-1}]
.
By exactness, there is an element
e\in\ker\iota^{r-1}
such that
x\cdot e=y
.
Clearly,

j_{p-r,q+r-1}(e)\in j_{p-r,q+r-1}(\ker i^{r-1})=B^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1},

so the map
d^{r}_{p,q}\colon a\mapsto j_{p-r}(x)\in B^{r+1}_{p-r,q+r-1}/B^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1}\subseteq E^{r}_{p,q}
is well-defined.

For
(ii)
, we consider the case
r=1
first.
By exactness,
k_{p,q}\colon Z^{1}_{p,q}\rightarrow\mathrm{im}\,k_{p,q}
, so that

\displaystyle\mathrm{im}\,d_{p,q}^{1}

\displaystyle=j_{p-1,q}(\mathrm{im}\,k_{p,q})=j_{p-1,q}(\ker i_{p-1,q})=B^{2}_{p-1,q}.

For
r\geq 2
, the argument is similar. By exactness,
k_{p,q}\colon Z^{r}_{p,q}\rightarrow\mathrm{im}\,i^{r-1}\cap\ker i_{p-1,q}
.
Since
(i^{r-1})^{-1}(\im i^{r-1}\cap\ker i_{p-1,q})=\ker i^{r}
, we find for all
x\in\ker i^{r}
an element
a\in Z^{r}_{p,q}
with
i^{r-1}(x)=k_{p,q}(a)
.
Thus,
d^{r}_{p,q}
is surjective onto
B^{r+1}_{p-r,q+r-1}/B^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1}
.

We now prove
(iii)
: If
x\in Z^{r}_{p,q}
lies in the kernel of
d^{r}_{p,q}
, then there is a
\xi\in D_{p-r,q+r-1}
such that
k_{p,q}(x)=i^{r-1}(\xi)
and

\displaystyle j_{p-r,q+r-1}(\xi)

\displaystyle\in j_{p-r,q+r-1}\left(\ker i^{r-1}\colon D_{p-r,q+r-1}\rightarrow D_{p-1,q}\right)=B^{r}_{p-r,q+r-1}.

Hence, there is an
\tilde{\eta}\in\ker j_{p-r,q+r-1}\subseteq D_{p-r-1,q+r}
(since this is a group if
q\geq 0
) such that
\xi\cdot i_{p-r,q+r}(\tilde{\eta})\in\ker i^{r-1}
.
By exactness, there is an
\eta\in D_{p-r-1,q+r}
such that
\tilde{\eta}=\iota_{p-r,q+r}(\eta)
so that
\xi\cdot\iota_{p-r,q+r}(\eta)\in\ker\iota^{r-1}
.
If
(p,q)\neq(1,0)
, then domain and image of
i^{r-1}
are monoids and
i^{r-1}
is a map of monoids, so

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=i^{r-1}(\xi\cdot i_{p-r,q+r}(\eta))=i^{r-1}(\xi)i^{r-1}(i_{p-r,q+r}(\eta))

\displaystyle=k_{p,q}(x)\cdot i^{r}(\eta),

or equivalently,
k_{p,q}(x)=i^{r}(\eta^{-1})
.
Thus,
x\in k_{p,q}^{-1}(\mathrm{im}\,i^{r})=Z^{r}_{p,q}
.

If
(p,q)=(1,0)
, then
d^{r}_{1,0}=0
for
r\geq 2
as the target is zero.
The claim for
d^{1}_{1,0}
follows immediately from exactness of pointed sets.
∎

Corollary 6.2.4
.

E_{p,q}^{r+1}=H^{r}(E^{r},d^{r})_{p,q}
for all
q\geq 1
.

Remark 6.2.5
.

The reader may wonder how we define
E^{r}_{p,0}
for
r\geq 2
as
E^{1}_{p,0}
is only a monoid.
The answer is that we don’t!
Since
B^{2}_{p,0}=B^{\infty}_{p,0}
there is no gain in forming the quotient
E^{2}_{p,0}=Z^{2}_{p,0}/B^{2}_{p,0}
, even if these monoids were abelian groups.

If all pointed sets are abelian groups, and all maps are homomorphisms, then the here constructed spectral sequence agrees with the usual one derived from exact couple as described in
[
boardman1999conditionally
]
or
[
weibel1996homological
]
.

Convergence of the spectral sequence

Let us now discuss covergence of this spectral sequence.
We will show, in analogy to the spectral sequence derived from an exact couple of abelian groups, that the constructed spectral sequence converges to

\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{colim}}}{}[D_{p-1,q+1}\rightarrow D_{p,q}]

under the assumption that a certain comparison map exists.
We use Corollary
6.2.2
to identify this colimit with
D_{p+q+1,-1}
and define

F_{p,q}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{im}\,\mathrm{[}D_{p,q}\xrightarrow{i^{q+1}}D_{p+q+1,-1}].

Note that
F_{p,q}
is an abelian group if
p+q\geq 2
because
D_{p+q,0}
is abelian in this case and that
\iota_{p+q,0}\colon D_{p+q,0}\twoheadrightarrow D_{p+q+1,-1}
is surjective.

Lemma 6.2.6
.

Let
p,q\geq 0
and
p+q\geq 2
.
If the map

\textstyle{\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}\colon Z_{p,q}^{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{p,q}^{-1}}
\textstyle{D_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{q+1}}
\textstyle{F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}}

is well-defined, then it is surjective and has
B^{\infty}_{p,q}
as kernel.
In particular, it induces an isomorphism

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 25.2279pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.2279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z^{\infty}_{p,q}/B^{\infty}_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.6029pt\raise 6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.2279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.2279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 79.2279pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}.

Remark 6.2.7
.

A sufficient condition for
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}
to be well-defined is the following one:
For all
x_{1},x_{2}\in D_{p,q}
with
j_{p,q}(x_{1})=j_{p,q}(x_{2})
, there are
y_{1},y_{2}\in\ker j_{p,q}
such that
x_{1}+y_{1}=x_{2}+y_{2}
.
This condition is satisfied, for example, if
D_{p,q}
is a (not necessarily abelian) group.

Proof.

By exactness,
Z^{\infty}_{p,q}=\ker k_{p,q}=j_{p,q}(D_{p,q})
, so if
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}
exists, then it is clearly surjective.

To see that
\ker\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}=B^{\infty}_{p,q}
, we argue as follows:
Let
x\in D_{p,q}
such that
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}(x)=0
, or equivalently, that
i^{q+1}(x)\in F_{p-1,q+1}\subseteq F_{p,q}
.
By definition, there is a
y\in D_{p-1,q+1}
such that
i^{q+2}(y)=\iota^{q+1}(x)
.
Then
x+\iota_{p-1,q+1}(-y)\in\ker i^{q+1}\subseteq D_{p,q}
.
Exactness implies

j_{p,q}(\ker i^{q+1})\ni j_{p,q}(x-i_{p-1,q+1}(y))=j_{p,q}(x).

Thus, the pre-image of the base point under
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}\colon Z^{\infty}_{p,q}\rightarrow F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}
agrees with
j_{p,q}(\ker i^{q+1})=B^{\infty}_{p,q}
.

We apply Lemma
6.2.1
to the short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B^{\infty}_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z^{\infty}_{p,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}}
\textstyle{F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

to deduce that
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}\colon Z^{\infty}_{p,q}/B^{\infty}_{p,q}\rightarrow F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}
induces an isomorphism.
∎

We summarise the achivements of this subsection in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.2.8
.

For each exact couple of pointed sets satisfying the conditions below equation (
6.2
), there is a first quadrant spectral sequence
(E^{r}_{p,q},d^{r}_{p,q})
that starts with
E^{1}_{p,q}=E_{p,q}
, and whose differentials on the first page are given by
d^{1}_{p,q}=j_{p-1,q}\circ k_{p,q}
.
If the comparision maps
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}\colon Z_{p,q}^{\infty}\rightarrow F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}
are well-defined, then the spectral sequence converges to
D_{p+q+1,-1}
for
p+q\geq 2
.

6.3
Application to the Defect Fibration

We are now ready to construct the psc Hatcher spectral sequence.
Recall from Proposition
6.1.9
the fibrations in the smooth category

\textstyle{F_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{incl}}
\textstyle{G_{p+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\upharpoonleft}}
\textstyle{F_{p}}

for
p\geq 1
.
Since
F_{0}=G_{0}
, we can extend the fibrations to
p\leq-1
by setting
F_{p}=G_{p}=\{\ast\}
in this case.
By Lemma
6.1.10
, the fibrations induce the following long exact sequences on homotopy groups

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}\\
q\geq-1\end{subarray}}\pi_{q+1}(F_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{p,q}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}\\
q\geq 0\end{subarray}}\pi_{q}(F_{p+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q}(\mathrm{incl})}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}\\
q\geq 0\end{subarray}}\pi_{q}(G_{p+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})}

We will abbreviate
\pi_{q}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft})
to
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{p+1,q}
and
\pi_{q}(\mathrm{incl})
to
\mathrm{incl}_{p+1,q}
.
We are not yet in the setup of the previous section because the sequences end at
\pi_{0}(F_{p})
.
The next lemma allows us to extend the couple to the negative half-plane
q<0
.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

The sequence of pointed sets

\textstyle{\pi_{0}(G_{p+1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{p+1,0}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(F_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{p,0}}
\textstyle{\pi_{p}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is exact.

Proof.

For
p<0
, there is nothing to prove.
For all
p\geq 0
, we have the short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ker\iota_{p,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(F_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{p,0}}
\textstyle{\pi_{p}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

An element
\sigma\in\Omega^{p}_{g_{0}}\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}
represents the zero element if and only if there is an
h\in\mathrm{hofib}_{p+1}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{p+2}}(M)
that satisfies

\displaystyle{}^{\mathrm{hofib}}\partial^{1}_{1}h=\partial^{1}_{2}h=\sigma,

\displaystyle{}^{\mathrm{hofib}}\partial^{-1}_{1}h=\partial^{-1}_{2}h=g_{0},

\displaystyle{}^{\mathrm{hofib}}\partial^{\omega}_{j}h=\partial^{\omega}_{j+1}h=g_{0}\text{ for }j\geq 2.

If
\mathrm{cycl}(2:p+2)
denotes the cyclic permutation
(2\ 3\ \dots\ p+2)
, then one calculates

\displaystyle\mathrm{cycl}(2:p+2)\circ{\delta^{\varepsilon}_{2}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p+1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cycl}(2:p+2)(x_{1},\varepsilon,x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1})

\displaystyle=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1},\varepsilon)={\delta^{\varepsilon}_{p+2}}.

This implies that
h
is a homotopy in
\mathrm{hofib}_{p+1}
between
g_{0}
and
\sigma
if and only if
\mathrm{cycl}(2,p+2)_{\ast}h\in G_{p}
with
\mathord{\upharpoonleft}(h)=\partial^{1}_{p+2}(h)=\sigma
.

In particular,
\ker\iota_{p,0}=\mathrm{im}\,\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{p+1,0}
.
∎

If we define

\displaystyle D_{p,q}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\pi_{q}(F_{p})\text{ if }q\geq 0\text{ and }D_{p,q}=\pi_{p+q}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})\text{ if }q<0,

\displaystyle E_{p,q}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\pi_{q}(G_{p})\text{ if }q\geq 0\text{ and }E_{p,q}=0\text{ if }q<0,

then we are in the setup of the previous section.
Indeed, the required monoid and group structures follow from Lemma
6.1.11
.
The maps
j_{p,q}
and
k_{p,q}
are group homomorphisms if
q\geq 1
because they are induced by continuous maps.
For
q=0
and appropriate
p
, this follows because the geometric addition is compatible with inclusions and restriction to faces.
The maps
\iota_{p,q}
, for
p+q\geq 1
, are monoid homomorphisms by Corollary
6.1.14
.

By Theorem
6.2.8
this leads to a first quadrant spectral sequence starting with
