E_{p,q}=\pi_{q}(G_{p})
and whose differentials on the first page are given by
d^{1}_{p,q}=\mathrm{incl}_{p-1,q}\circ\mathord{\upharpoonleft}_{p,q}
.
By Lemma
6.1.16
, we can replace it by the homotopy groups of psc pseudo isotopies
E_{p,q}^{1}=\pi_{q}(C^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M))
.
Convergence of the spectral sequence follows from the following lemma that will be proved geometrically.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

The map

\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}\colon Z^{\infty}_{p,q}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{incl}_{p,q}^{-1}}\pi_{q}(F_{p})\xrightarrow{\iota^{q+1}}F_{p,q}/F_{p-1,q+1}.

is well-defined.

Proof.

It suffices to show that each pair of elements
[g_{-1}],[g_{1}]\in\pi_{q}(F_{p})
related by
\mathrm{incl}_{p,q}([g_{-1}])=\mathrm{incl}_{p,q}([g_{1}])
satisfy
\iota^{q+1}([g_{-1}])-\iota^{q+1}([g_{1}])\in F_{p-1,q+1}
.
Let
h\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{q}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow G_{p}
be a block map that serves as a homotopy between
g_{-1}
and
g_{1}
.
We may assume
h
to be smooth, that
g_{j}
are elements of
\Omega^{q+1,\infty}F_{p}
, and, after a possible reparametrisation, that
\mathrm{susp}(h)
has positive scalar curvature.

Since
\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}\mathrm{susp}(h)=\mathrm{susp}(\partial^{1}_{q}h)
, it follows that
[\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}\mathrm{susp}(h)]\in F_{p-1,q+1}
.
Assume that
\mathrm{susp}(h)
decomposes outside of
M\times\rho I^{p+q+2}
for a sufficiently large
\rho
.
Applying Proposition
3.4.2
to
\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}\mathrm{susp}(h)
in the
x_{p+q+1}
-
x_{p+q+2}
direction and glue it to
\mathrm{susp}(h)|_{\{x_{p+q+1}\leq\rho\}}
, we obtain a block metric
\mathrm{susp}(h)\cup(\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}\mathrm{susp}(h))^{\angle}
that serves at concordance between
\iota^{q+1}(g_{1})+_{p+q+1}\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}(\mathrm{susp}(h))
. Using that the geometric addition agrees with the addition in
\pi_{p+q+1}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
from cubical set theory, we conclude
[\iota^{q+1}(g_{1})]+[\partial^{1}_{p+q+1}\mathrm{susp}(h)]=[\iota^{q+1}(g_{-1})]
, so that we have
[\iota^{q+1}(g_{1})]-[\iota^{q+1}(g_{-1})]\in F_{p-1,q+1}
.
We conclude that
\mathfrak{I}_{p,q}
is well-defined.
∎

Thus, we have proved the main result of this chapter.

Theorem 6.3.3

(psc Hatcher Spectral Sequence)
.

There is a first-quadrant spectral sequence that starts with
E^{1}_{p,q}=\pi_{q}({C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M))
, whose differentials on the first page are given by
d^{1}_{p,q}=\pi_{q}(\mathord{\upharpoonleft}\circ\mathrm{incl})\colon\pi_{q}({C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p}(M))\rightarrow\pi_{q}({C}^{\mathrm{psc}}_{p-1}(M))
, and that converges to
\pi_{p+q}(\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet})
for
p+q\geq 2
.

Chapter 7
The Remains of the Day

Our overall goal was to develop the foundations to carry out a program for psc metrics similar to the study of
\mathrm{Diff}_{\bullet}(M)
via
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
This goal was achieved.
We constructed the main player
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
, the cubical set of all psc block metrics on
M
(Section
3.1
), showed that it is a Kan set (Theorem
3.3.1
), related it to the cubical model
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)
of the space of psc metric
\mathrm{Riem}^{+}(M)
via the suspension map
\mathrm{susp}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}(M)\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
(Lemma
5.1.6
), and established the first tool to study the difference between those spaces beyond index theory, namely the psc version of the Hatcher spectral sequence (Theorem
6.3.3
).

This is important because we showed that the index difference factors through
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
(Theorem
5.1.1
), so the index difference is as concordance invariant as possible - it cannot detect any higher isotopy informations.
To achieve the factorisation result, we constructed a new model for real
K
-theory based on the notion of invertible block Dirac operators.
The main feature of this new model is that the Gromov-Lawson index difference can be modeled by the cubical map that assigns a psc block metric its Dirac operator.

We now describe how one can proceed from here.
To get information on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}_{\bullet}}(M)
, one uses the fibration

\textstyle{{\mathrm{hAut}_{\bullet}(M)}/{\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}_{\bullet}}(M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}_{\bullet}}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\mathrm{hAut}_{\bullet}(M)}

because the fibre can be approached using surgery theory and the base can be approached using obstruction theory.
There is no immediate counterpart for homotopy automorphism in the world of psc metrics, but guided by the principle that generalisations may lead to easier computable objects, we could consider the bordism version of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
.
This is the cubical set
\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)
whose
0
-cubes are pairs of a closed, spin manifold and a positive scalar curvature on it, whose
1
-cubes are bordism of those, whose
2
-cubes are bordisms of bordisms and so on.
In general, an
n
-cube of
\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)
is a
(d+n)
-dimensional compact spin manifold with
2n
-many (possibly empty) faces with a positive scalar curvature metric on it that has product structure near each face
1
1

1

Actually, it should be the elongation of those so that we end up with a “block manifold”; but for the sake of simplicity we ignore this here.
.
The corresponding cubical set
\mathbf{Bord}_{\bullet}(d)
without the data of a psc metric should be a cubical model for
\Omega^{\infty+d}MSpin
because it is the cubical analog of Quinn’s bordism space.

Both of these sets are Kan, in fact, the proof that
\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)
is Kan is even easier than the proof that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M)
is Kan.

There is an obvious map from
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M^{d})
into the homotopy fibre of the forgetful map

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M^{d})\rightarrow\mathrm{hofib}_{\bullet}\bigl(\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)\rightarrow\mathbf{Bord}_{\bullet}(d),M^{d}\bigr).

The results of Stolz
[
stolz1998concordance
]
and straightforward generalisations thereof should imply that this map is a weak homotopy equivalence if
M
is simply connected.
In fact, the homotopy fibration

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\bullet}}(M^{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Bord}_{\bullet}(d)}

should be a space-level description of the exact sequence in
[
stolz1998concordance
]
(in the case of simply connected spin manifolds).
If
M
is not simply connected, then one has to enrich the model of
\mathbf{Bord}^{+}_{\bullet}(d)
and
\mathbf{Bord}_{\bullet}(d)
with reference maps to
B\pi_{1}(M)
.

In our index theoretic consideration, we have completely ignored the fundamental group.
In future work, one could construct a version of
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
that is suitable for higher index theory.
This approach would require to extend the theory developed in this thesis to pseudo differential operators on Hilbert
C^{\ast}
-modules.
Furthermore, we would need a new proof of Theorem
4.9.1
as the
K
-theory groups of a general
C^{\ast}
-algebra may not be cyclic (this assumption was used in the proof of Theorem
4.9.1
to deduce bijectivity from surjectivity) and the theorem of Kucerovsky
[
kucerovsky1997kk
]
is not directly applicable (because the positivity condition is not preserved by small perturbations).
However, all these obstacles are likely to be overcome.

The advantage of the models
\Psi\mathrm{Dir}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
and
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
for the classifying space of real
K
-theory is that they emphasise the role of the Dirac operator.
The analysis developed in this thesis allows us to define the cubical set
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{inv}}_{\bullet}(M)
of all block metrics whose Dirac operator is invertible.
The proof that
\widetilde{\Psi\mathrm{Dir}}^{\times}_{\bullet}(M)
is Kan also shows that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{inv}}_{\bullet}(M)
is Kan because a careful analysis of this proof shows that all applied constructions preserve the Dirac operator; in other words, if we carry out such a construction on a Dirac operator, we produce a Dirac operator.

We have constructed a psc version for the Hatcher spectral sequence, but, so far, this spectral sequence cannot be used for explicit calculations.
The differential
d^{1}_{p,q}
in the Hatcher spectral sequence for diffeomophism groups can be computed in the range
q\ll p
because
Igusa’s stabilisation map

\Sigma\colon C(M\times I^{p})\rightarrow C(M\times I^{p+1})

induces an isomorphism on all homotopy groups of degree
\leq q
.
With the methods we have developed here, it is easy to define an analogous stabilisation map for the psc pseudo isotopies.
A psc analog of Igusa’s stabilisation result would be of great and also independent interest.

Appendix A
Sobolev Spaces

We will recall the theory of Sobolev spaces for the convenience of the reader.
This also serves as the opportunity to fix notations and conventions.
As a disclaimer: Nothing presented in this section is original work from the author but a collection from the following sources:
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
,
[
bleecker2013index
]
,
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
and
[
palais1965seminar
]
.

Throughput this section
E\rightarrow M
denotes a complex vector bundle
1
1

1

This discussion also works for real and Real bundles, of course.
One simply has to replace the Hermitian metric by a Riemannian metric.
Then the Sobolev spaces are real and Real vector spaces.
over a not necessarily compact manifold
M^{d}
(without boundary),
h
is a Hermitian metric
E
.
A Riemannian metric
g
on
M
defines a canonical Borel regular measure
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}
on
M
.
If
M
is oriented the measure is given by its volume form, if
M
is not orientable, it is given by half of the volume form on its oriented cover.
In this section we call the tupel
(E,h,\nabla)\rightarrow(M,g)
a
geometric bundle
.

Definition A.0.1
.

Let
(E,h)\rightarrow(M,g)
be a Hermitian vector bundle over a Riemannian manifold.
Its
L^{2}
-
inner product
on
\Gamma_{c}(E)
is given by

\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{M}h_{x}(u_{1}(x),u_{2}(x))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x).

The Hilbert space of
square integrable sections

L^{2}(M;E)
(or simply
L^{2}(E)
)

is the completion of
\Gamma_{c}(E)
with respect to the norm induced by the
L^{2}
-inner product.

A connection
\nabla\colon\Gamma(E)\rightarrow\Gamma(T^{\vee}M\otimes E)
is a linear map that satisfies the Leibniz rule:
\nabla(fu)=\mathrm{d}f\otimes u+f\nabla u
.
One can show without using Sobolev theory that every connection has a formal adjoint, see
[
bleecker2013index
]
*Section 6.6 that is an operator
\nabla^{\ast}
that is uniquely satisfied by

\langle\nabla^{\ast}u,v\rangle=\langle u,\nabla v\rangle.

Unbounded operator theory
[
grubb2008distributions
]
*Theorem 12.11 implies that
\nabla^{\ast}\nabla
is an (unbounded) self adjoint operator, so we can apply unbounded functional calculus to it.

Definition A.0.2
.

Let
(E,h,\nabla)\rightarrow(M,g)
be a Hermitian vector bundle with connection over a Riemannian manifold.
For each
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
define the
Sobolev

s
-norm
on
\Gamma_{c}(E)
via

||u||_{s}^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{M}h_{x}((\mathrm{id}+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}u(x),u(x))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x).

The
Sobolev

s
-space

H^{s}(E)

is defined as the completion of
\Gamma_{c}(E)
with respect to the Sobolev
s
-norm.

In fact, Sobolev spaces are Hilbert spaces and
H^{0}(E)=L^{2}(E)
. We will use both notations interchangeably throughout this text.

Warning:
The definition of the Sobolev norms and the Sobolev spaces depends dramatically on the choices of Hermitian metric
h
, the connection
\nabla
, and the measure
\mathrm{vol}_{g}
. Different choices may yield to non-isomorphic
topological
vector spaces. In other words, for different choices, the identity on
\Gamma_{c}(E)
may not extend to a continuous linear map.

Let
K
be a compact set that is the closure of its interior.
There are two inequivalent ways to define Sobolev sections on
K
.
The first way is the completion of
\Gamma_{c}(K^{\circ},E|_{K^{\circ}})
with respect to the restricted Sobolev norms.
We denote the result with
H^{s}(K,E)
,
H^{s}(E|_{K})
or
H_{K}^{s}(E)

depending on the interpretation, if we want to consider the space in its own right or as a subspace
2
2

2

usually, the space
H^{s}_{K}(E)
denotes the set of all sections
u
supported within
K
, which means that
\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}}=0
for all
v\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
.
The differences between these two space are minimal if
K
is the closure of its interior: With this interpretation,
H_{K}^{s}(E)
equals the intersection of
H^{s}(E|_{L^{\circ}})
of all relative compact open subsets
L^{\circ}
of
K
.
of
H^{s}(M;E)

The second way only works if
s\geq 0
.
We define
H^{s}(K\subseteq M,E)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{u|_{K}\,:\,u\in H^{s}(E)\}
.
There are two norms
H^{s}(K\subseteq M,E)
.
The first one is just the usual Sobolev norm where the integration domain is just changed:

||u||_{s,K}^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{K}h_{x}((\mathrm{id}+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}u(x),u(x))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x).

The second norm is given by the infimum of the Sobolev norm of all extensions:

||u||_{s,K\subseteq M}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{||\tilde{u}||_{s}\,:\,\tilde{u}|_{K}=u\}.

In general
||u||_{s,K}\leq||u||_{s,K\subseteq M}
but they agree in general only if
s=0
.

Also the two variants of the Sobolev spaces are not the same.
In general
H(E|_{K})\subseteq H(K\subseteq M,E)
but, for example, every section
u\in H^{1}(E|_{K})
vanishes at the boundary,
[
grubb2008distributions
]
*Remark 4.26.

When we discuss pseudo differential operators on non-compact manifolds, we use other versions of Sobolev spaces which are not normed spaces.
The next definition is slightly modified from the one in
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
.

Definition A.0.3
.

For every
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
and each pair
(L,K)
of relative compact open subsets with
\overline{K}\subseteq L
, extension by zero yields an isometric embedding
H^{s}(K,E|_{K})\hookrightarrow H^{s}(L,E|_{L})
.
Define
H^{s}_{cpt}(E)
as the colimit over all these inclusions.

Definition A.0.4
.

For each
s\geq 0
, define
H^{s}_{loc}(E)

be the sub-vector space of all locally square integrable function
L^{2}_{loc}(E)
such that
x\mapsto h_{x}((1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}u(x),u(x))
is locally square integrable.
For negative
s
we define
H^{s}_{loc}(E)
as the dual space of
H^{-s}_{cpt}(E^{\vee})
.

Note that
\Gamma(E)\subseteq H^{s}_{loc}(E)
for all
s\geq 0
.
It is easy to see if
s
is an integers; the general case follows from
||(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{t}u||_{0}\leq||(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}u||_{0}
if
0\leq t\leq s
(where we allow the norms to be infinity).

The vector space
H^{s}_{loc}(E)
carries a natural Fréchet topology induced by the semi-norms
||\text{--}||_{s,K\subseteq M}
if
s\geq 0
.
Although
H_{cpt}^{s}(E)
includes into
H^{s}_{loc}(E)
, the colimit topology on
H^{s}_{cpt}(E)
is not the subspace topology of
H^{s}_{loc}(E)
.
Also note that both topologies are independent of the choices of the geometric data
h
,
\nabla
, and
\mathrm{vol}_{g}
.

Example A.0.5
.

If
\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the trivial bundle endowed with the standard metrics and the trivial connection.
Then
\nabla^{\ast}\nabla=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}^{2}
is the standard Laplacian.
Fourier transformation allows us to give a different description of the (standard) Sobolev
s
-norm

||u||_{s}^{2}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}|\hat{u}(\xi)|^{2}\mathrm{d}\xi.

There are positive constants
c_{s}\leq C_{s}
, depending only on
n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}
such that

c_{s}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}\leq\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}|\xi|^{2\alpha}\leq C_{s}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s},

so, for the integral
s
, the (standard) Sobolev
s
-norm is equivalent to

|||u|||_{s}^{2}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sum_{k=0}^{s}||\nabla^{k}u||_{0}^{2}.

For
s=1
, the obvious generalisation of the previous example
|||\text{--}|||_{1}
to a geometric bundle agrees with
||\text{--}||_{1}
.
This provides us the opportunity to do inductions proofs, when we are discussing block pseudo operators.

The standard Sobolev norms on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
gives another possibility to construct Sobolev norms on vector bundles by choosing local trivialisations of
E
and underlying charts of
M
, defining the Sobolev norms locally in the charts and trivialisations, and then gluing them together with a partition of unity.
The Sobolev norms then drastically depend on the choices of the involved charts, trivialisations and the partition of unity. In practise, it requires tedious work to verify that two different choices yield the same topological space
H^{s}
, not to mention equivalent Sobolev norms.
However, one can show, for example see
[
bleecker2013index
]
*p.197 ff, that
u\in H^{s}_{loc}
if and only if every restriction to some relative compact trivialisations domain
U
lies in
H^{s}(U\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N})
.
This observation turns out to be quite useful then we discuss pseudo differential operators for their defining properties are of local nature.

The proof sketch presented in
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*p.176 carries over to non-compact manifolds in the following manner.

Theorem A.0.6

(Sobolev Regularity)
.

For all compact neighbourhoods
K\subseteq M
and each
s>k+n/2
there is a constant
C(K,s)
such that all smooth sections
u\in\Gamma(E)
supported within
K
satisfy

||u||_{C^{k}}\leq C(K,s)||u||_{s}.

In particular,
H^{s}(E)\subseteq\Gamma^{k}(M;E)
.

Theorem A.0.7

(Rellich Lemma)
.

For each relative compact open subset
K\subseteq M
and every pair of real numbers
s<t
, the inclusion
H^{t}(K;E|_{K})\hookrightarrow H^{s}(E)
is a compact operator.

The next result is often stated for compact manifolds only although the proof for
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
can be easily adapted to the geometric set up.

Proposition A.0.8

(perfect pairing)
.

For each
(E,h,\nabla)\rightarrow(M,g)
and all section
u,v\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
the inner product

\langle u,v\rangle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{M}h_{x}(u(x),v(x))\mathrm{d}\mathrm{vol}_{g}(x)

extends to a unique non-degenerate bilinear form

\langle\text{--},\text{--}\rangle\colon H^{s}(E)\times\overline{H^{-s}(E)}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{C}}.

for all
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

We need to show that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\flat}\colon\overline{H^{-s}(E)}\rightarrow H^{s}(E)^{\vee}
is an isomorphism; in fact, it will be an isometric isomorphism.
The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
\langle u,v\rangle\leq||u||_{s}||v||_{-s}
implies that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
extends to a continuous bilinear form.
Furthermore, it shows
||\langle\text{--},v\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}}\leq||v||_{-s}
.

On the other hand, for all
v\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
, we have

\displaystyle||v||_{-s}^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{-s}v,v\rangle\leq||(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{-s}v||_{s}\cdot||\langle\text{--},v\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}}

\displaystyle=||v||_{-s}\cdot||\langle\text{--},v\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}},

which implies
||v||_{-s}\leq||\langle\text{--},v\rangle||_{\mathrm{dual}}
and hence equality.
Thus,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\flat}
is isometric, in particular, injective with closed image.

If
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\flat}
were not surjective, then we would find by the Hahn-Banach theorem a non-zero functional
\theta\in H^{s}(E)^{\vee\vee}
that vanishes identically on the image of
\overline{H^{-s}(E)}
.
Since
H^{s}(E)
is a Hilbert space, it is reflexive, so there is a unique
u_{\theta}\in H^{s}(E)
with
\mathrm{ev}_{u_{\theta}}=\theta
.
This implies

0=\theta(\langle\text{--},v\rangle)=\mathrm{ev}_{u_{\theta}}(\langle\text{--},v\rangle)=\langle u_{\theta},v\rangle,

for all
v\in H^{-s}(E)
.
Since
\Gamma_{c}(E)
lies dense in
H^{s}(E)
and
H^{-s}(E)
, this implies
u_{\theta}=0
, contradicting our initial assumption
\theta\neq 0
.
∎

If
H^{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\mathrm{invlim}_{k}H^{k}(E)=\bigcap_{k}H^{k}(E)
is topologised with the inverse limit topology, then
H^{\infty}(E)
is a subspace of
\Gamma(M;E)
with respect to the smooth (weak) topology.
Similarly,
H^{\infty}_{cpt}=\Gamma_{c}(E)
and
H^{\infty}_{loc}=\Gamma(E)
, where the smooth sections are equipped with the smooth (weak) topology
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
.
If we define
H^{-\infty}=\mathrm{colim}_{k}H^{k}=\bigcup H^{k}
endowed with the colimit topology, then the perfect pairing yields
H^{-\infty}=(H^{\infty})^{\vee}
.

Important remark:
While the Hilbert space structure
L^{2}(E)=H^{0}(E)
is geometrically motivated, the inner products on the other Sobolev spaces are not.
Their purpose is to provide regularity for their elements.
Thus, the precise Sobolev
s
-norm is of minor importance (except for
s=0
).
Only the underlying topological vector space is of interest.
This leads to the notion of an
hilbertisable
vector space and chains of hilbertisable vector spaces
[
palais1965seminar
]
.
In fact, Proposition
A.0.8
, identifies
\{H^{k}(E)\}_{k\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}
as a discrete chain of hilbertian spaces in the sense of
[
palais1965seminar
]
*Definition VIII.1.1.

Definition A.0.9
.

A
discrete chain of hilbertian
spaces is a sequence of hilbertian spaces
\{H^{k}\}_{k\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
such that

(i)

if
k\leq l
, then
H^{l}
is a linear subspace of
H^{k}
and the inclusion is continuous,

(ii)

the intersection
H^{\infty}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\bigcap_{k}H^{k}
is dense in
H^{0}
(and hence in all
H^{k}
), and

(iii)

H^{0}
is a Hilbert space.

For negative
k
, we set
H^{k}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=(H^{-k})^{\vee}
.
One can show that that there is a inclusion
H^{l}\hookrightarrow H^{k}
for all
k\leq l\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
, see
[
palais1965seminar
]
*p.126.

We would like to know how the construction of Sobolev spaces behaves under exterior tensor products.

Lemma A.0.10

(
[
palais1965seminar
]
*Theorem XIV.2)
.

For each pair of geometric bundles
(E_{j},h_{j},\nabla_{j})\rightarrow(M_{j},g_{j})
and for all
s\geq 0
, the Sobolev spaces of the exterior tensor product
(E_{1}\boxtimes E_{2},h_{1}\boxtimes h_{2},\nabla_{1}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}+\mathrm{id}\boxtimes\nabla_{2}\rightarrow M_{1}\times M_{2},g_{1}\times g_{2})
is given by

\displaystyle H^{s}_{cpt}(E_{1}\boxtimes E_{2})=\bigcap_{0\leq s\leq t}H^{t}_{cpt}(E_{1})\otimes H^{s-t}_{cpt}(E_{2})

and

\displaystyle H^{s}_{loc}(E_{1}\boxtimes E_{2})=\bigcap_{0\leq t\leq s}H^{t}_{loc}(E_{1})\otimes H^{s-t}_{loc}(E_{2})

In
[
palais1965seminar
]
the lemma is proven only for Hermitian vector bundles over closed manifolds.
However, the proof carries over, because we only consider (semi-)norms on compact subsets.
Alternatively, one could prove the lemma for the trivial bundles
\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
equipped with the standard Hermitian and Riemannian metric first and then use local patching arguments and the fact that two norms obtained in this manner are equivalent on compact subsets.

The next definition is a slight modification of
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
, which generalises the analogous definition of
[
palais1965seminar
]
to non-compact manifolds.

Definition A.0.11
.

Let
E,F\rightarrow X
be two Hermitian vector bundles over
(M,g)
and let
r\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Denote by
Op^{r}_{k}(E,F)
the set of all continuous linear maps
H^{s}_{cpt}(E)\rightarrow H^{s-r}_{loc}(F)
and by
Op^{r}(E,F)
the set of all linear maps
H^{\infty}_{cpt}(E)\rightarrow H^{\infty}_{loc}(F)
that extend to continuous linear map
H^{k}_{cpt}(E)\rightarrow H^{k-r}_{loc}(F)
for all
k\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Note that, in the definition of
Op^{r}
,

we do not need to demand continuity of the linear maps
H^{\infty}\rightarrow H^{\infty}
because
H^{\infty}
carries the inverse limit topology, as continuity follows from the continuity of the extensions.
The reader may wonder, why we restrict in the definition of
Op^{r}
to Sobolev spaces of integral order.
The reason is that it is good enough for our purpose and in many cases easier to check that an operator is in
Op^{r}
because we can avoid functional calculus arguments for
(1+\nabla^{\ast}\nabla)^{s}
if
s
is integral
3
3

3

if
s
is negative, we can use the perfect pairing to reduce it to non-negative
s
.

We can turn
Op^{r}_{s}
it into a Fréchet space using the topology of bounded convergence.
This means that a sequence of continuous linear maps
T_{\alpha}\colon H^{s}_{cpt}\rightarrow H^{s-r_{loc}}
converges to
T
if and only if
||T_{\alpha}-T||_{s,s-r;K}\rightarrow 0
for all compact neighbourhoods
K
, where

||S||_{s,s-r;K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sup\left\{||Sf||_{s-r;K}\,:\,f\in H^{s}(K,E|_{K})\text{ with }||f||_{s}=1\right\}.

The canonical inclusion

Op^{k}\hookrightarrow\prod_{m\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}}Op_{m}^{k}\quad\text{ given by }\quad A\mapsto(\dots,A,A,A,\dots)

has closed image, so that
Op^{k}
is also a Fréchet space.
The space
Op^{k}
is local in the sense that
A\in Op^{k}
if and only if
fAg\in Op^{k}
for all smooth and compactly supported real values functions
f
and
g
.

Appendix B
Foundation of Pseudo Differential Operators

We will recall the foundations of pseudo differential operators to set up notation and for the convenience of the reader because the general literature often develops the theory for compact manifolds only, while we mostly work with non-compact manifolds.
Nothing presented here is really new, and the author does not claim any originality for these passages.
In this thesis, we need to deal with several topologies on the space on pseudo differential operators.
Many existing proofs actually show continuity statement if one carefully keeps track of the constant, and this is why we wrote this appendix.
Those who are familiar with pseudo differential operators, should pay attention to the construction of the
Atiyah-Singer closure

\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
in Definition
B.0.20
originally defined in
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
, the generalised symbol exact sequence Lemma
B.0.24
, and, the reason why Atiyah and Singer considered the broader class, Theorem
B.0.28
and
B.0.29
.

We will define pseudo differential operators on vector bundles as operators that look locally like pseudo differential operators on trivial bundles over open subsets of the euclidean space.
Therefore, we will consider this model case first.

Since the definition of pseudo differential operators requires Fourier transformations, we cannot define them on real vector bundles but we have to define them on Real vector bundles.
Recall that a Real vector bundle is complex vector bundles
E
that is equipped with a
\mathord{\mathbb{C}}
-antilinear involution
\overline{\cdot}\colon E\rightarrow E
.
In this thesis, we will only deal with Real vector bundles that arise from real vector bundles by complexification, meaning, every Real vector bundle is of the form
E=E^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\mathord{\mathbb{C}}
and
the involution is just the complex conjugation on the second factor.
Moreover, every construction we will pursue in this thesis is a construction on the underlying real vector bundle.
For this reason, only in this section we will distinguish real and Real vector bundles.

Definition B.0.1
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be open and let
k
be a real number.
A smooth map
p\colon U\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
is an
amplitude of order k
, if on each compact subset
K\subseteq U
,
it has the following asymptotic behaviour

\forall\alpha,\beta\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{n}\,\exists C_{\alpha,\beta,K}:||\partial^{\beta}_{x}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}p(x,\xi)||\leq C_{\alpha,\beta,K}(1+||\xi||)^{k-|\alpha|}.

(B.1)

An amplitude is
Real
, if it additionally satisfies

\overline{p(x,\xi)}=p(x,-\xi).

(B.2)

We denote the set of all Real amplitudes by
A_{k}(U\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
.

The next theorem, which is taken from
[
bleecker2013index
]
, says that amplitudes induce linear operators on smooth functions. We follow the authors using the convention
\text{\dj}\xi=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\mathrm{d}\xi
.

Theorem B.0.2

(
[
bleecker2013index
]
*Theorem 8.3)
.

Every amplitude
p
on
U\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
defines a linear map

\mathrm{Op}(p)\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})

via the formula

\left(\mathrm{Op}(p)\right)(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\xi\rangle)p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)\text{\dj}\xi.

(B.3)

Even better, if
U=\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then this formula induces a map between the Schwartz spaces

\mathrm{Op}(p)\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}}).

We follow
[
bleecker2013index
]
by calling the assignment
p\mapsto\mathrm{Op}(p)
the
quantisation of

p
.
It is natural to ask whether an amplitude
p
is uniquely defined through it quantisation
\mathrm{Op}(p)
and under which condition
\mathrm{Op}(p)
restricts to a map between real valued function.
The answer is given in the next Proposition.

Proposition B.0.3
.

Let
p
be an amplitude on
U\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then

(i)

p=0
if and only if
\mathrm{Op}(p)|_{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}})}=0
.

(ii)

p
is Real if and only if
\mathrm{Op}(p)
descends to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{2}})
.

Proof.

For (i), we prove the non-trivial direction by contra-position.
Assume that there is a point
(x_{0},\xi_{0})
for which
p(x_{0},\xi_{0})\neq 0
.
Since the Fourier transformation is a bijection on the set of Schwartz functions
[
konigsberger2004analysisII
]
*p.331 the decay conditions for amplitudes imply that
\xi\mapsto p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)

is a Schwartz function.
Indeed, for each
\alpha\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{n}
and
l\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\leq\alpha}||\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}p(x,\xi)||\cdot||\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha-\beta}\hat{u}(\xi)||

\displaystyle\leq\sum C_{\beta,0,K}(1+||\xi||)^{k-|\beta|}\cdot||\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha-\beta}\hat{u}(\xi)||

\displaystyle\leq\sum C_{\beta,0,K}D_{\alpha-\beta,l+k}(1+||\xi||)^{k-|\beta|}(1+||\xi||)^{-(k-|\beta|+l)}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\left(\sum C_{\beta,0,K}D_{\alpha-\beta,l+k}\right)}_{=:D^{\prime}_{\alpha,l}}(1+||\xi||)^{-l}.

Thus, if we can find a real valued function
u
with compact support in
U
and
p(x_{0},\xi_{0})\hat{u}(\xi_{0})\neq 0
, we are done, because the inverse Fourier transformation is a unitary operator on all square-integrable functions
[
konigsberger2004analysisII
]
*p.333.

Note that
A|_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}}}=0
implies
A=0
for any
A\in\mathrm{End}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
, so we can pick a real vector
v_{0}
with
p(x_{0},\xi_{0})v_{0}\neq 0
.
By Lemma
B.0.4
below, we can find an arbitrary large
R\in(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0})^{n}
such that the Fourier transformation of the extension of
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp(-1/(1-R_{j}^{2}x_{j}^{2}))
to
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
by zero evaluated at
\xi_{0}
is not zero.
Thus,

\varphi(x)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-R^{2}(x_{j}-(x_{0})_{j})^{2}}\right)

has compact support within
U
(since
R
was chosen sufficiently large) and its Fourier transformation does not vanish at
\xi_{0}
.

For (ii), let
p
be a Real amplitude. If
u
is an eigenfunction of
\overline{\cdot}
, so is
\mathrm{Op}(p)(u)
as the following computation shows

\displaystyle\overline{\mathrm{Op}(p)(x)}

\displaystyle=\overline{\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\xi\rangle)p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)\text{\dj}\xi}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\overline{\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\xi\rangle)p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)}\text{\dj}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\xi\rangle)\overline{p(x,\xi)}\overline{\hat{u}(\xi)}\text{\dj}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,-\xi\rangle)p(x,-\xi)\hat{u}(-\xi)\text{\dj}\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}|\det(-\mathrm{id})|\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\xi\rangle)p(x,\xi)\hat{u}(\xi)\text{\dj}\xi=\mathrm{Op}(p)(x).

For the other direction, note that
p(x,\xi)-\overline{p(x,-\xi)}
is also an amplitude.
If
\mathrm{Op}(p)
restricts to a linear map
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{2}})
, then the previous calculation shows that

\mathrm{Op}(p-\overline{p}(\text{--},-\mathrm{id}(\text{--}))=0.

By part (
i
), this equality implies
p-\overline{p}(\text{--},-\mathrm{id}(\text{--}))=0
, so
p
is Real.
∎

Lemma B.0.4
.

For every
\xi_{0}\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
and there is an
R_{0}>0
such that for all
R>R_{0}

the Fourier transformation of
x\mapsto\exp(-1/(1-R^{2}x^{2}))
does not vanish at
\xi_{0}
.

Proof.

Substitution of variables show

\displaystyle\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\exp(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}x\xi_{0})\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-R^{2}x^{2}}\right)\text{\dj}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\exp\left(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}Rx\frac{\xi_{0}}{R}\right)\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-R^{2}x^{2}}\right)\text{\dj}x

\displaystyle=R^{-1}\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}}\exp\left(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}y\frac{\xi_{0}}{R}\right)\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-y^{2}}\right)\text{\dj}y

\displaystyle=R^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\left(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}y\frac{\xi_{0}}{R}\right)\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-y^{2}}\right)\text{\dj}y

\displaystyle=:h(R).

Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem yields

\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}R\cdot h(R)=\int_{-1}^{1}\exp(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}y\cdot 0)\exp\left(\frac{-1}{1-y^{2}}\right)\text{\dj}y>0.

Since
h
depends continuously on
R\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
, the claim follows.
∎

Definition B.0.5
.

A linear map
P\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
is a
canonical pseudo differential operator of order

k
if there is a (necessarily unique) amplitude
p
of order
k
such that
P=\mathrm{Op}(p)
.

In the real case, a linear map
P\colon\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{2}})

canonical pseudo differential operator of order

k
if its complex linear extension is a pseudo differential operator of order
k
.

Note that the amplitude of the operator in the real case is necessarily Real by Proposition
B.0.3
.
However, canonical pseudo differential operators are not good enough for our purposes.
We need to restrict to a smaller subclass of operators that allow the definition of a principal symbol.

Definition B.0.6
.

A canonical pseudo differential operator of order
k
is called
principal classical
if its
amplitude
p
satisfies the following assumptions:

(i)

For each
x\in U
and each
\xi\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
the limit

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(p)(x,\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda^{-k}\cdot p(x,\lambda\xi)

exists. It is called the
principal symbol
of
p
.

(ii)

For some cut-off function (and therefore every)
\chi
with

\displaystyle\chi(\xi)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }|\xi|\text{ is small,}\\
1,&\text{if }|\xi|\geq 1,\end{cases}

the function
p(x,\xi)-\chi(\xi)\cdot\mathrm{symb}_{k}(p)(x,\xi)
is a (canonical) amplitude of order
k-1
.

Example B.0.7
.

The following list of examples are taken form
[
bleecker2013index
]
.

1.

Every differential operator
P=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}a_{\alpha}(x)\partial^{\alpha}
of order
k
is a principal classical pseudo differential operator of order
k
.
Indeed, using the Fourier inversion formula, it is not hard to see that
P
has the amplitude

p(x,\xi)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}i^{|\alpha|}a_{\alpha}(x)\xi^{\alpha}
.

The principal symbol is given by
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(x,\xi)=\sum_{|\alpha|=k}i^{k}a_{\alpha}(x)\xi^{\alpha}
.
This observation was actually the motivation for the defining formula of
\mathrm{Op}(p)
.

2.

As a special case of
1
, the identity
\mathrm{id}=\mathrm{Op}(\mathrm{id})
is a principal classical pseudo differential operator of order zero.
More generally, the multiplication with a matrix valued function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U;\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}\times N_{2}})
is a pseudo differential operator of order zero.
Of course, this operator is Real if and only if
f
is real a valued function.
The principal symbol is given by
\mathrm{symb}_{0}(f\cdot)(x,\xi)=f(x)\cdot
.

3.

Every smooth function
K\colon U\times U\rightarrow\mathrm{End}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{2}})
such that

K(x,\cdot)
has compact support for all
x\in U
defines a principal classical
pseudo differential of order
-\infty
, which means that it is of order
k
for all
k\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
. The principal symbol is therefore
0
.

Proof of Example 3.

We will calculate the amplitude of
K
.
Since
K(x,\cdot)
has compact support and
\eta\mapsto\hat{u}(\eta)
is a Schwartz function,
the map
(y,\eta)\mapsto K(x,y)\hat{u}(\eta)
is integrable, so all operations in the following chain of equations are valid

\displaystyle(Ku)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}K(x,y)u(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}K(x,y)\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle y,\eta\rangle)\hat{u}(\eta)\text{\dj}\eta dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle y,\eta\rangle)K(x,y)\hat{u}(\eta)\text{\dj}\eta dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\eta\rangle)\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle y-x,\eta\rangle)K(x,y)\hat{u}(\eta)\text{\dj}\eta dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\eta\rangle)\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle z,\eta\rangle)(2\pi)^{n/2}K(x,z-x)\hat{u}(\eta)\text{\dj}z\text{\dj}\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle x,\eta\rangle)k(x,\eta)\text{\dj}\eta.

Here,
k(x,-\eta)
is the Fourier transformation of
z\mapsto(2\pi)^{n/2}K(x,z-x)
.
Since
z\mapsto(\partial^{\alpha}_{x}K)(x,z-x)
is compactly supported for all
\alpha\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{n}
, its Fourier transformation is a Schwartz function (in
\eta
-direction). Thus,
K=\mathrm{Op}(k)
is a pseudo differential operator of order
s
for all
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

Remark B.0.8
.

An pseudo differential of order
-\infty
is called
smoothing operator
.
The name comes from the fact that such operators applied to arbitrary square integrable functions yield smooth functions, c.f. Theorem
B.0.18
below.

It is a deep computational result, for example presented in
[
bleecker2013index
]
*Theorem 8.19, that canonical and principal classical pseudo differential operators are invariant under diffeomorphism in the following sense:

Suppose that
\kappa\colon U\rightarrow V
is a diffeomorphism between relatively compact open subsets of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and that
P=\mathrm{Op}(p)
is a canonical or principal classical pseudo differential operator of order
k
those amplitude has compact support in the first variable, which means that there is a compact subset such that
p(x,\text{--})=0
for all
x\in K^{c}
.
Then the pull-back
\kappa^{\ast}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P(\cdot\circ\kappa^{-1})\circ\kappa
is again a canonical or principal classical pseudo differential operator of order
k
whose amplitude has compact support in the first variable.
Furthermore, if
q
denotes the amplitude of
\kappa^{\ast}P
, then

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(q)(x,\xi)=\mathrm{symb}_{k}(p)(\kappa(x),{}^{T}(D_{x}\kappa)^{-1}(\xi)).

In other words,
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)
transforms like a section of
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\pi^{\ast}\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})\rightarrow T^{\vee}U
.

We are now in the position to define pseudo differential operators on arbitrary manifolds.

Definition B.0.9
.

Let
E,F\rightarrow M
be Real bundles over the manifold
M
.
A linear map
P\colon\Gamma_{c}(E)\rightarrow\Gamma(F)
is a
principal classical (canonically) pseudo differential operator of order k

if for all
x_{0}\in M
there exists a local chart
(U,\kappa)
around
x_{0}
,
local trivialisations

\Phi\colon E_{U}\rightarrow U\times\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}}
,
\Psi\colon F_{U}\rightarrow U\times\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}}
,
as well as a smooth function
f\colon M\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
1

in a neighbourhood of
x_{0}
and has compact support within
U
such that, under the obvious identification of sections with functions, the linear map

\kappa_{\ast}\left(\left(\Psi\circ f\cdot P\circ\Phi^{-1}\right)\right)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\kappa(U),\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\kappa(U),\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{N_{2}})

is a principal classical (canonical) pseudo differential operator of order
k
.
If
p_{f}
is the representing amplitude, we define

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(x_{0},\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\frac{p_{f}(x_{0},\lambda\xi)}{\lambda^{k}}.

We call such a choice
(U,\kappa,\Phi,\Psi,f)
an
amplitude datum
.
The set of all principal classical pseudo differential operators is denoted by
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
,

the set of all canonical pseudo differential operators is denoted by
C\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
.

Remark B.0.10
.

The
(principal) symbol
transforms accordingly under a change of coordinates and therefore gives rise a smooth homomorphism

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)\colon\pi^{\ast}E\rightarrow\pi^{\ast}F,

where
\pi\colon T^{\vee}M\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow M
. The symbol is positive homogeneous of degree
k
,
meaning
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(x,\lambda\xi)=\lambda^{k}\mathrm{symb}(P)(x,\xi)
for all
\lambda>0
.

For a proof see
[
bleecker2013index
]
*Theorem 8.19.

Note that
Pu
may not be compactly supported.
However, when applying analytical tools, it will be convenient to control the increase of the support. Following
[
LawsonMichelsonSpin
]
*p.183 we make the following definition.

Definition B.0.11
.

A pseudo differential operator
P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
has
support in K
if
\mathrm{supp}\,Pu\subseteq K
for all
u\in\Gamma_{c}(E)
and
Pu=0
if
\mathrm{supp}\,u\cap K=\emptyset
.

It follows from Proposition
B.0.3
that
p_{f}\equiv 0
for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M)
whose support does not intersect
K
.
In particular,
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(x,\text{--})=0
for all
x\in K^{c}
.

Definition B.0.12
.

For each
k\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
and each compact subset
K\subseteq M^{n}
, let

\mathrm{Symb}^{k}(E,F)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{\sigma\in\Gamma(T^{\vee}M\setminus\{0\},\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}E,\pi^{\ast}F))\,|\,\sigma(x,\lambda\xi)=\lambda^{k}\sigma(x,\xi)\}

the vector space of all (smooth) symbols of order
k

and

\mathrm{Symb}^{k}_{K}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\{\sigma\in\mathrm{Symb}^{k}(E,F)\,:\,\sigma(x,\text{--})=0\text{ if }x\in K^{c}\}

be its subspace of all symbols supported in
K
.
The map that sends a pseudo differential operator to its principal symbol is, of course, denoted by

\mathrm{symb}_{k}\colon\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)\rightarrow\mathrm{Symb}^{k}(E,F).

If we choose a Riemannian metric, then we can can identify the set of all symbols with

\Gamma(S(T^{\vee}M),\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}E,\pi^{\ast}F))
, where
S(T^{\vee}M)
denotes the unit-sphere bundle of the cotangent bundle
T^{\vee}M
defined by the chosen Riemannian metric.

The defining conditions of pseudo differential operators are of local nature.
If one wants to verify a property of pseudo differential operators, for example, that the composition of two pseudo differential operators is again a pseudo differential operator, then one might be tempted to verify this property in local coordinates and then use a partition of unity argument, to reduce the global statement to a local one.
However, an arbitrary partition
\{\chi_{j}\}
of unity is not good enough to deduce global properties of pseudo differential operators from local ones, even if each
\chi_{j}
is supported in a coordinate chart.
This comes from the fact that we need two different charts to detect
\chi_{j}P\chi_{i}u
and we have no model for this situation to compare it against.

The following lemma, which is a generalisation of
[
grubb2008distributions
]
*Lemma 8.4 to non-compact manifolds, allows us to overcome this difficulty.

Lemma B.0.13

(Covering Lemma)
.

Let
E,F\rightarrow M
be vector bundles over a smooth manifold
M
. Then there exists an open covering of charts
\{\kappa_{i}\colon U_{i}\rightarrow V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
over which the bundles trivialise and for which there is a subordinate partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that each quadruple
(\chi_{j},\chi_{k},\chi_{l},\chi_{m})
is supported in some
U_{i}
.

Proof.

By
[
greene1978complete
]
*Theorem 2’ there is a complete Riemannian metric
g
on
M
that has a positive injectivity radius.
Using this information, it is easy to construct a countable, locally finite collection of charts
\{\kappa_{j}\colon U_{j}\rightarrow V_{j}\}_{j\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
whose targets
V_{j}
are pair-wise disjoint and whose domains are contractible.

We do this as follows:
Let
1/3>\delta>0
be smaller than the injectivity radius of
g
and let
d=\mathrm{dist}(\text{--},x_{0})
be distance function centered at
x_{0}\in M
.
Since
g
is complete,
d
is proper.
Cover the compact sets
K_{n}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=d^{-1}([n-1,n])
by finitely many geodesic balls of radius
\delta
if they are non empty.
These geodesic balls together form a countable, locally finite open covering
\{U_{j}\}_{j\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
of
M
.
If
\log
denotes the inverse of the geodesic exponential map
\exp\colon B_{\delta}(0)\rightarrow U_{j}
then
\{j+\log\colon U_{j}\rightarrow B_{\delta}(j)\}_{j\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
is a locally finite, countable set of charts with disjoint targets.
In particular, the vector bundles trivialise over the chart-domains as every bundle over contractible paracompact spaces are trivial,
[
tomDieck2008algebraic
]
*Theorem 14.3.1.
A close inspection of the proof of Lemma 8.4 in
[
grubb2008distributions
]
shows that it is still applicable to the just constructed cover and that it yields a cover and a partition of unity with the stated properties.
∎

All examples presented in Example
B.0.7
carry over to vector bundles over manifolds.
These examples are, informally speaking, quite local in nature.
However, there are globally defined linear operators on sections and it might be hard to guess a local their local amplitude to identify them as pseudo differential operators.
The first application of Lemma
B.0.13
is the following useful characterisation of pseudo differential operators that puts the operator in the centre instead of the amplitude.
Before we state it, recall from Appendix
A
that
\Gamma_{c}(M,E)=H^{\infty}_{cpt}
and
\Gamma(M,E)=H^{\infty}_{loc}
, which allows us to topologise these spaces (with the inverse limit topology of the Sobolev spaces).

Theorem B.0.14

(
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*p.509)
.

Let
E,F\rightarrow M
be Real vector bundles over
M
. A linear map
P\colon\Gamma_{c}(M,E)\rightarrow\Gamma(M,F)
is a pseudo differential operator of order
k
if and only if
P
is continuous and for each point
x_{0}\in M
there is a chart
(U,\kappa)
around
x_{0}
, trivialisations
\Phi\colon E_{U}\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}}
,
\Psi\colon F_{U}\rightarrow U\times\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}}
, and a smooth function
f\colon M\rightarrow[0,1]
that is identically
1
near
x_{0}
and compactly supported within
U
such that, under the identification of sections and functions, the function

p_{f}(y,\xi)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\exp(\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle y,\xi\rangle)\Psi\circ P(f\cdot\Phi^{-1}(\cdot,\exp(-\mathord{\mathrm{i}}\langle\kappa(\cdot),\xi\rangle))

is a Real amplitude of order
k
.

Sketch of Proof.

Since the partition of unity from the Covering Lemma is locally finite,
P
is continuous if and only if each summand
\chi_{j}P\chi_{k}
is continuous.

Since
\chi_{j}
and
\chi_{k}
are supported in the contractible chart
U_{i}
, it is enough to verify the claimed equivalence for all summands
\chi_{j}P\chi_{k}
in local coordinates.
But this is done in
[
grubb2008distributions
]
*p.173ff.
∎

The Covering Lemma can also be used to reduce the following proposition on composition properties, which are stated in
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*p.510 for the complex case, to the local case.

Proposition B.0.15
.

Let
E,F,G
be Real vector bundles over
M
.

(i)

If
P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
,
Q\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{l}(F,G)
, and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(M)
, then
PfQ\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k+l}(E,G)
and
\mathrm{symb}(PfQ)=f\mathrm{symb}(P)\mathrm{symb}(Q)
.
In particular, if
P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
,
Q\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{L}^{l}(F,G)
, then
PQ\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{K\cup L}^{k+l}(E,G)
.

(ii)

P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{(K)}(E,F)
implies
P^{\vee}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{(K)}(F^{\vee},E^{\vee})
and
P^{\ast}=({h_{E}}_{\#}\circ P^{\vee}\circ h_{F}^{\flat})\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{(K)}(\bar{F},\bar{E})
, if
E
and
F
are equipped with Riemannian metrics
h_{E}
and
h_{F}
respectively.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{symb}(P^{\vee})=\mathrm{symb}(P)^{\vee}
and
\mathrm{symb}(P^{\ast})=\mathrm{symb}(P)^{\ast}
.

Sketch of Proof.

Use the Covering Lemma to reduce the statements to the case where
M
is an open neighbourhood of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and
E
and
F
are trivial bundles.
The statement now follows from the local version, as presented in
[
bleecker2013index
]
*Theorem 8.27.
∎

The proposition implies that
\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{K}^{0}(E,E)
is a
\ast
-algebra and that
\mathrm{symb}
is a
\ast
-homomorphism.
If
M
is closed, then
\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{M}^{0}(E,E)=\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{0}(E,E)
is unital.
Furthermore, in this case,
\Gamma_{c}(M,E)=\Gamma(M,E)
, so asking whether a pseudo differential operator is invertible actually makes sense.
Although the following result is a simple consequence of Lemma
B.0.23
below, we already state it here.

Proposition B.0.16
.

Let
E,F\rightarrow M
be a Real bundles over a closed manifold
M
. If
P\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
is invertible, then
P^{-1}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{-k}(F,E)
.

We use Proposition
B.0.3
to endow
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
with a structure of a
Fréchet space, even a Fréchet algebra, if we restrict our consideration to
\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{K}^{k}(E,E)
.

Theorem B.0.17
.

Let
E,F,G
be Real bundles over
M
.
There is a Fréchet structure on
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
induced by the following semi-norms:
For
A=(U,\kappa,\Phi,\Psi,f,\alpha,\beta)
consisting of a chart
\kappa\colon U\rightarrow V
, trivialisations
\Phi
,
\Psi
of
E_{U}
and
F_{U}
, respectively, a smooth function
f
with support within
U
, and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{n}
, we set

||P||_{A}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sup\left\{\frac{\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}p_{f}(x,\xi)}{(1+|\xi|)^{k}}\right\}.

(B.4)

Moreover, the compositions from Proposition
B.0.15
are (jointly) continuous.

Sketch of Proof.

The result about the Fréchet vector space structure is classical, mentioned, for example, in
[
atiyah1971indexIV
]
.
The proof that the composition of two pseudo differential operator is a pseudo differential operator also shows that the composition is continuous with respect to this Fréchet structure by carefully tracking the constants in the involved estimations.
∎

There are other topologies on
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
coming from operator theory.
They arise from the fact that pseudo differential operators extend to Sobolev spaces.

Theorem B.0.18

(
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*p.512)
.

Every pseudo differential operator of order
k

P\colon\Gamma_{c}(M,E)\rightarrow\Gamma(M,F)

extends, for each
s\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
, to a continuous linear operator

P_{s}\colon H_{cpt}^{s}(M,E)\rightarrow H_{loc}^{s-k}(M,F).

(B.5)

Moreover, these extensions induce a continuous map

\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)\rightarrow Op^{k}(E,F).

The theorem follows immediately from the Covering Lemma and the following local version that can be found in
[
wells2008differential
]
*Theorem 4.3.4 if one simply keeps track of the constants of the presented estimations.

Lemma B.0.19
.

Let
p\colon U\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
be an amplitude of order
k
such that the support of the first variable lies in the compact set
K
.

Then
\mathrm{Op}(p)\colon\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow\Gamma_{K}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
extends to a bounded map between Sobolev spaces
H^{s}(U,\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\rightarrow H^{s-k}_{K}(U;\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})
with operator norm

\displaystyle||\mathrm{Op}(p)||_{s,s-k}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathrm{vol}(K)\cdot\sum_{|\beta|\leq|s-k|+n+1}||p||_{\beta,0}.

Here,
||p||_{\beta,0}
denotes the infimum of all valid bounds
C_{\beta,0,K}
in the amplitude estimation
B.1
.

Using the previous theorem, we can make the following definition.

Definition B.0.20
.

Denote by
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
the closure of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)

in
Op^{k}
using the canonical inclusion.
We call this Fréchet space the
Atiyah-Singer closure
of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
.

We will also consider a third completion.

Definition B.0.21
.

For each
s\geq k
, we denote by
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}_{s}(E,F)
the closure of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
in
Op^{k}_{s}(E,F)
.
We will call this set the
Sobolev closure
of
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
.

We can give
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
three different topologies, Amp, the Fréchet structure described via all representing amplitudes, AS, the subspace topology from the Atiyah-Singer closure, and
\mathrm{S}_{s}
, the subspace topology coming from the Sobolev closure.
For these three topologies, the operations of Proposition
B.0.15
are continuous and extend to continuously to the closures.
They are related as follows:

Theorem B.0.22
.

For every two Real vector bundles
E,F
over the compact manifold
M
, the identity give rise to continuous maps

\textstyle{(\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F),\mathrm{Amp})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F),\mathrm{AS})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F),\mathrm{S}_{s})}

Proof.

The continuity of the first inclusion follows from Theorem
B.0.18
.
The second map is obviously continuous.
∎

Besides the smooth Fréchet topology, there is another reasonable topology on
\mathrm{Symb}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
that turns
\mathrm{Symb}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
into a Banach space.
The norm is given by the supremum of the operator norm taken over all points of the sphere bundle.
Its completion will be denoted by
\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
. It can be identified with the set
of continuous section from the sphere bundle
S(T^{\vee}M)
to
\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}E,\pi^{\ast}F)
that are supported within
K
.

Lemma B.0.23
.

If
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
carries the amplitude topology and
\mathrm{Symb}^{k}(E,F)
carries the smooth topology, then the symbol map is continuous and has a continuous right-inverse
\rho
.
The two maps restrict to the subspaces of operators and symbols with support in
K
, so we have the following two split exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k-1}_{(K)}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{(K)}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{symb}^{k}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Symb}_{(K)}^{k}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{0}

Proof.

We first construct the right-inverse
\rho
closely following
[
wells2008differential
]
.

We choose an open cover
\{U_{\mu}\}
with a subordinate partition of unity
\{\phi_{\mu}\}
as in the Covering Lemma.
For each
\phi_{\mu}
, we pick a smooth function supported in
U_{\mu}
that is identically
1
on
\mathrm{supp}\,\phi_{\mu}
.
Finally, we fix a cut-off function
\chi\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\chi\equiv 0
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}
and
\chi\equiv 1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
.

Let
\sigma\in\Gamma(T^{\vee}M\setminus\{0\},\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}E,\pi^{\ast}F))
be a given symbol.
Under the identifications
T^{\vee}U_{\mu}=U_{\mu}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
E_{U_{\mu}}=U_{\mu}\times\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}}
,
F_{U_{\mu}}=U_{\mu}\times\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}}
, the symbol
\sigma
corresponds to a smooth, matrix-valued map

s_{\mu}\colon U_{\mu}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}}).

Set
p_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\chi(||\xi||)\sigma_{\mu}(x,\xi)
and extending by zero to
U_{\mu}\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
It is an amplitude of order
k
as
s_{\mu}
is homogeneous of order
k
in the second component.
Define
P_{\mu}
is defined via

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(U_{\mu},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\mu}\mathrm{Op}(p_{\mu})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(U_{\mu},\mathord{\mathbb{C}}^{N_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}(U_{\mu},F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma_{c}(U_{\mu},E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{P_{\mu}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(M,F)}

and set

\rho(\sigma)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=P\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=\sum_{\mu}P_{\mu}.

Of course, the identifications are given by the charts and the local trivialisations.
It is clear that
P\in C\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F)
because it is locally the quantisation of an amplitude.

In the chosen trivialisations,
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P_{\mu})
is expressed by

\displaystyle\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P_{\mu})(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{symb}_{k}\left(\psi_{\mu}\cdot\xi(||\text{--}||)s_{\mu}(\text{--}\,,\,\text{--})\right)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\psi_{\mu}(x)\chi(||\lambda\xi||)s_{\mu}(x,\lambda\xi)}{\lambda^{k}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{\mu}(x)\chi(||\lambda\xi||)s_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\psi_{\mu}(x)s_{\mu}(x,\xi).

With abuse of notation we have

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)=\sum_{\mu}\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P_{\mu})\phi_{\mu}=\sum_{\mu}\psi_{\mu}s_{\mu}\phi_{\mu}=\sum_{\mu}\phi_{\mu}s_{\mu}=\sigma.

Clearly,
p_{\mu}(x,\xi)-\chi(||\xi||)\mathrm{symb}(p_{\mu})(x,\xi)=0
is an amplitude of order
\leq k-1
.
Thus, each
P\cdot\phi_{\mu}
is a principal classical pseudo differential operator of order
k
and so is
P
.
Consequently,
\rho
is indeed a right-inverse of
\mathrm{symb}^{k}
.
Note that
\rho
is
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,\mathord{\mathbb{R}})
-linear and that
\rho(\sigma)
is Real, if
\sigma
is Real.

Since
\rho
was constructed by patching local right-inverse together it restricts to a right-inverse
\rho\colon\mathrm{Symb}_{K}^{k}(E,F)\rightarrow\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
of the symbol map.

The two maps,
\mathrm{symb}_{k}
and
\rho
, are linear, so it suffices to check that they are continuous at
0
.

In the construction of
\rho
, we have chosen a partition of unity as in the Covering Lemma, so it is enough to verify its continuity in local coordinates.
We need to control the amplitude semi-norms of
\psi_{\mu}p_{\mu}
in terms of the semi norms of
s_{\mu}
.
To this end, we calculate

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\psi_{\mu}(x)\chi(\xi)s_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha\\
\beta^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime\prime}=\beta\end{subarray}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\psi_{\mu}(x)\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime}}\chi(\xi)\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}s_{\mu}(x,\xi).

Each summand
\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}s_{\mu}(x,\xi)
is homogeneous of order
k
, so the chain rule implies

||\partial_{x}^{\alpha^{\prime\prime}}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime\prime}}s_{\mu}(x,\xi/||\xi||)||\leq C_{\alpha^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}}\sum_{l\leq|\beta^{\prime\prime}|}||D^{\otimes l}|_{TS^{n-1}}s_{\mu}(x,\xi)||\cdot(1+||\xi||)^{k-|\beta^{\prime\prime}|}

on
\mathrm{supp}\,\chi
, where
D^{\otimes l}|_{TS^{n-1}}s_{\mu}(x,\xi/||\xi||)
denotes the
l
-th iterated (total) differential of
s_{\mu}(x,\text{--})
restricted to
(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}\xi^{\perp})^{\otimes l}
.
The function
\chi
vanishes near the origin and is constantly
1
outside some compact set.
Thus,

\partial_{\xi}^{\beta^{\prime}}\chi\leq C_{\beta^{\prime}}(1+||\xi||)^{k-|\beta^{\prime}|}

for all
\beta^{\prime}\neq 0
.
Putting these inequalities together, we conclude

||\psi_{\mu}\chi s_{m}||_{\alpha,\beta;U_{\mu}}\leq C\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\leq|\alpha|+|\beta|\end{subarray}}||D_{(x,\xi)}^{\otimes l}s_{\mu}||,

where
D_{(x,\xi)}^{\otimes l}s_{\mu}
denotes the
l
-iterated total differential of
s_{\mu}
.
This shows that
\rho
is continuous.

We deduce the continuity of the symbol map from the following inequality of amplitudes that are compactly supported within a chart domain

||\mathrm{symb}_{k}(p)||_{C^{\alpha};U_{\mu}}\leq\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha}||p||_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime};U_{\mu}},

where
||p||_{\alpha^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime\prime};U_{\mu}}
denotes the smallest constant in the amplitude defining inequality
B.1
for some compact set that contains the support of
p
.
∎

Lemma B.0.24
.

The symbol map is also continuous if
\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
carries the
s
-Sobolev topology
S_{s}
and
\mathrm{Symb}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
carries the
C^{0}
-topology.
The continuous extension to the completions yield a short exact sequences of Banach spaces

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}(H^{s},H^{s-k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}_{s,K}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\mathrm{symb}}^{k}}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}^{k}_{K}(E,F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Proof.

We will prove that the symbol map is continuous by showing

||\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)||_{\infty}\leq C_{s,k}||P||_{s,s-k}.

We start with the special case
s=k=0
.
The support of
\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)
is compact, so we find a
(x_{0},\xi_{0})\in S(T^{\vee}M)
with
||\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})||_{\mathrm{op}}=||\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)||_{\infty}
.
By a result attributed to Gohberg, see
[
seeley1965integro
]
*Theorem 2.2, or the original
[
gohberg1960Multidim
]
, applied in local coordinates, we find a sequence of smooth section
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathord{\mathbb{N}}}
such that

(i)

u_{n}(x)=0
if
|x-x_{0}|>1/n
,

(ii)

||u_{n}||_{0}=1
,

(iii)

||\chi Pu_{n}-\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})u_{n}||_{0}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
for some (and hence all) smooth function
\chi\leq 1
that vanish on
|x-x_{0}|>1
and are identically
1
on
|x-x_{0}|<1/2
.

We may further assume that

(iv)

all
u_{n}(x_{0},\xi_{0})
are colinear, non-zero, and satisfy

||\mathrm{symb}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})u_{n}(x_{0},\xi)||=||\mathrm{symb}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})||_{\mathrm{op}}||u_{n}(x_{0},\xi_{0})||,

otherwise, we may replace
u_{n}
by
A_{n}\cdot u_{n}
, where
A_{n}
is a local endomorphism on
E
that maps
u_{n}(x_{0},\xi_{0})
to a vector realises the operator norm of
\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})
.

The claim for the special case follows now from property (iii) and

\lim_{n\to\infty}||\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})u_{n}||_{0}=||\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})||_{\mathrm{op}}=||\mathrm{symb}_{0}(P)||_{\infty}.

We reduce the general case to the special one as follows:
For some compact neighbourhood
L
that contains
K
in its interior, we pick
\Lambda_{-s}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{-s}_{L}(E,E)
and
\Lambda_{s-k}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{s-k}_{L}(F,F)
that satisfy
\mathrm{symb}_{j}(\Lambda_{j})=\mathrm{id}
on
S(T^{\vee}M)|_{K}
.
Then,
\mathrm{symb}_{0}(\Lambda_{s-k}P\Lambda_{s})=\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)
on
S(T^{\vee}M)
and

||\Lambda_{s-k}P\Lambda_{-s}||_{0,0}\leq||\Lambda_{s-k}||_{s-k,0}||P||_{s,s-k}||\Lambda_{-s}||_{0,s},

which implies the general statement.

It remains to show that the continuous extension yields the claimed short exact sequence.
The compact operators lie in the kernel of
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{k}
because, with respect to the operator norm, every finite rank operator can be approximated by an infinitely smoothing operator and the latter lie in the kernel of
\mathrm{symb}_{k}
.

For the converse, assume we are given
P\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
that does not lie in the kernel.
First, we consider the special case
s=k=0
.
By a diagonal sequence argument, we can extend Gohberg’s result to the completion.
Assume that
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})\neq 0
and that
P
were compact.
If
u_{n}
is a sequence with property (i) - (iv), then
Pu_{n}
would have a convergent subsequence.
To ease the notation, we assume the whole sequence converges.
By property (iii), the sequence
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})u_{n}
must be convergent.
Condition (i) implies that the limit is zero.
But condition (ii) and (iv) imply

\lim_{n\to\infty}||\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})u_{n}||_{0}=||\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{0}(P)(x_{0},\xi_{0})||_{\mathrm{op}}\neq 0,

which is a contradiction.

Assume, for general
s
and
k
, there is a compact
P\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
such that
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{k}(P)\neq 0
.
Then
\Lambda_{s-k}P\Lambda_{-s}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}(E,F)
would be a compact operator with non-zero symbol contradicting the special case.

We first prove that
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{k}
is surjective for the special case
s=k=0
and
E=F
.
In this case,
\mathrm{symb}_{0}
is a
\ast
-homomorphism between
C^{\ast}
-algebras with dense image, so is must be surjective.

In the case
E\neq F
, we identify
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}_{K}(E,F)
with the closed subspace of odd operators in
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}_{K}(E\oplus F,E\oplus F)
and
\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}^{0}_{K}(E,F)
with the closed subspace of
\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}^{0}_{K}(E\oplus F,E\oplus F)
whose elements take values in the odd endomorphisms.
These inclusions are isometric and commute with the symbol maps, so the image of the symbol map must be closed.
Since we already know from Lemma
B.0.23
that the image of
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{0}
is dense, the symbol map must be surjective.

For general
s
and
k
, we argue as before: For every given symbol

\sigma\in\Gamma_{K}\bigl(S(T^{\vee}M),\mathrm{Hom}(\pi^{\ast}E,\pi^{\ast}F)\bigr)

pick
Q\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{0}(E,F)
with
\overline{\mathrm{symb}}_{0}(Q)=\sigma
and a pseudo differential
\Lambda_{k}\in\Psi\mathrm{DO}_{K}^{s}(F)
whose principal symbols is the identity.
Then
\Lambda_{k}Q\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
and its principal symbol satisfies

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)=\mathrm{symb}_{0}(Q)=\sigma

on
S(T^{\vee}M)
.
∎

Corollary B.0.25
.

The symbol map extends to the Atiyah-Singer closure

\mathrm{symb}_{k}\colon\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}_{K}^{k}(E,F)\rightarrow\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}^{k}_{K}(E,F).

It is worth pointing out that the right inverse
\rho

does not
extend in general to a continuous section
\overline{\rho}\colon\overline{\mathrm{Symb}}^{k}_{K}(E,F)\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}_{K}(E,F)
.
However, the right-inverse extends to continuous section if we control higher jets of the symbols.

Lemma B.0.26
.

The right inverse
\rho
is continuous if
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}_{s,K}(E,F)
carries the
s
-Sobolev topology
S_{s}
and
\mathrm{Symb}_{K}^{k}(E,F)
carries the
C^{|s-k|+\mathrm{dim}\,M+1}
-topology.
Moreover, if
E
and
F
are Riemannian vector bundles with connections and if
\nabla
denotes the induced connection on
\mathrm{Hom}(E,F)
, then

\displaystyle||\rho(\sigma)||_{s,s-k}\leq C\sum_{r\leq|s-k|+\mathrm{dim}\,M+1}||\nabla^{\otimes r}\sigma||_{\infty;K}.

Proof.

By construction,
\rho(\sigma)=\sum_{\mu}\mathrm{Op}(\psi_{\mu}p_{\mu})\cdot\phi_{\mu}
, where
\psi_{\mu}
and
\phi_{\mu}
are supported in a domain of an amplitude datum and
\psi_{\mu}\phi_{\mu}=\phi_{\mu}
.
This implies using Lemma
B.0.19

\displaystyle||P||_{s,s-k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mu}||\mathrm{Op}(\psi_{\mu}p_{\mu})\phi_{\mu}||_{s,s-k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mu}C(\phi_{\mu})||\mathrm{Op}(\psi_{\mu}p_{\mu})||_{s,s-k;U_{\mu}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mu}C(U_{\mu})\sum_{r\leq|s-k|+n+1}||\psi_{\mu}p_{\mu}||_{r,0;U_{\mu}}.

The calculations we made when we constructed
\rho
imply in this special case, where we do not differentiate in
\xi
-direction (the fibre direction), that

\displaystyle||P||_{s,s-k}\leq\sum_{\mu}\tilde{C}(U_{\mu})\sum_{|\alpha|\leq|s-k|+n+1}||\partial^{\alpha}_{x}s_{\mu}||_{\infty,U_{\mu}}.

Since
U_{\mu}
is a chart domain over which the chart map extends to a slightly bigger open subset and in which
U_{\mu}
is relative compact, all connections on
\mathrm{Hom}(E,F)
restricted to
U_{\mu}
yield equivalent
C^{|s-k|+\mathrm{dim}\,M+1}
-norms.
Thus, by increasing the constants, we deduce

||P||_{s,s-k}\leq\sum_{\mu}\tilde{C}(U_{\mu})\sum_{r\leq|s-k|+n+1}||\nabla^{\otimes r}s_{\mu}||_{\infty;U_{\mu}}\leq\tilde{C}\sum_{r\leq|s-k|+n+1}||\nabla^{\otimes r}\sigma||_{\infty;K}.\qed

The reason why we mostly to work with the Atiyah-Singer closure instead of the Sobolev closure is that operators in the Atiyah-Singer closure share many properties of (actual) pseudo differential operators.

Proposition B.0.27
.

1.

Each
P\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
induces a continuous map
\Gamma_{c}(E)\rightarrow\Gamma(F)
.

2.

The space
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
is a local space, which means that
P\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
if and only if
P|_{U}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E|_{U},F|_{U})
, where
P|_{U}
denotes the restriction of
P
to
\Gamma_{c}(U,E|_{U})\rightarrow\Gamma(U,F)
.

Proof.

For the first statement see
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*p.512, for the second statement, see
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*p.513.
∎

The most important property is, in contrast to actual pseudo differential operators, that
\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E,F)
is stable under exterior tensor products.

Theorem B.0.28

(
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*Theorem 5.3 and Theorem 5.4)
.

Let
E,F
be Real bundles over
M
and
G
a Real bundle over
N
. Then for all
k>0
there is a continuous map

-\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\colon\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(M;E,F)\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(M\times N;E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G)

defined by sending
P
to the continuous linear extension of

u\otimes v\mapsto P(u)\otimes v,

for all
u\in\Gamma_{c}(M,E)
,
v\in\Gamma_{c}(N,G)
.
Moreover,

\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P\boxtimes\mathrm{id})(m,n;\xi,\eta)=\mathrm{symb}_{k}(P)(m,\xi)\otimes\mathrm{id}

The proof of this theorem generalises to smooth families, provided we have the right notion of
smoothness.

Theorem B.0.29
.

Let
P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow(\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(E,F),\mathrm{Ampl})
be a smooth family and
k>0
.
Then there is a unique operator
P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\in\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G)
that satisfies

(P\boxtimes\mathrm{id})(u\otimes v)(m,t)=P_{t}(u)(m)\otimes v(t)

and this assignment yields a continuous map

\text{--}\boxtimes\mathrm{id}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\Psi\mathrm{DO}^{k}(M;E,F))\rightarrow\overline{\Psi\mathrm{DO}}^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G).

The proof of Theorem 5.4 in
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
carries over without essential change.
The reason why we have to take the amplitude Fréchet structure is that we have to guarantee the
amplitude estimates also for the differentials with respect to the parameters
t\in\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
If we would chosen the topology coming from the Atiyah-Singer closure, then we could only conclude
that
P\boxtimes\mathrm{id}
is in
Op^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G)
. With this choice of topology,
P
would be simply a special smooth
Op^{k}
-valued map.

Unfortunately, the proof of these theorems breaks down in the case
k=0
.
Fortunately, for this thesis, the following weaker statement, which is a family version of
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
*Theorem 5.3, is good enough.
Again, we will not prove this theorem as its proof essentially agrees with the one in
[
atiyah1968indexI
]
.

Theorem B.0.30
.

Let
P\colon\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow Op^{k}(M;E,F)
be a smooth map of operators and
k\geq 0
.
Let
G\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a bundle over
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
Then there is a unique operator

P\tilde{\boxtimes}\mathrm{id}\in Op^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G)

that satisfies

(P\tilde{\boxtimes}\mathrm{id})(u\otimes v)(m,t)=P_{t}(m)\otimes v(t),

and this assignment defines a continuous map

\text{--}\,\tilde{\boxtimes}\mathrm{id}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n},Op^{k}(M;E,F))\rightarrow Op^{k}(M\times\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n};E\boxtimes G,F\boxtimes G).

Appendix C
Formulas in Riemannian Geometry

We prove formulas from differential geometry and geometric analysis that are need in the main text but may interrupt the reading flow of the text.

C.1
The scalar curvature for a foliation of level sets

If
(M,g)
is a Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
and
\mathfrak{d}\colon M\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
is a function without critical values, then a standard result from differential topology, see
[
hirsch1997differential
]
*p.153ff, says that the (shifted) gradient flow provides an isomorphism

\textstyle{\Phi\colon\mathfrak{d}^{-1}(\{a\})\times[a,b]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{d}^{-1}([a,b])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathfrak{d}}
\textstyle{[a,b]}

Abbreviate
\mathfrak{d}^{-1}(\{a\})
to
H
.
Let
v=[\gamma]\in T_{m}H
be a tangent vector represented by a curve
\gamma\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\rightarrow H
.
The calculation

\displaystyle(\Phi^{\ast}g)_{(m,t_{0})}(\partial_{t},v)

\displaystyle=g\left(D_{(m,t_{0})}\Phi(\partial_{t}),D_{(m,t_{0})}\phi(v)\right)=g\left((\partial_{t}\Phi)_{(m,t_{0})},D_{(m,t_{0})}\phi(v)\right)

\displaystyle=g(||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d})||_{g}^{-1/2}\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d}),[\Phi_{t_{0}}\circ\gamma])

\displaystyle=||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d})||_{g}^{-1/2}\cdot\mathrm{d}\,\mathfrak{d}([\Phi_{t_{0}}\circ\gamma])

\displaystyle=||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d})||_{g}^{-1/2}\cdot[s\mapsto t_{0}]=0

implies the decomposition

\displaystyle(\Phi^{\ast}g)_{(m,t_{0})}

\displaystyle=\bigl((\Phi_{t_{0}})^{\ast}g\bigr)_{m}+||\mathrm{grad}(\mathfrak{d})||_{g_{m,t_{0}}}^{-2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}

\displaystyle=:h(t_{0})_{m}+f^{2}(m,t_{0})\mathrm{d}t^{2},

where
h\colon[a,b]\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(H)
is a curve of Riemannian metrics.

This example motivates the following setup.
Given a smooth curve of Riemannian metrics
h\colon(a,b)\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
and a smooth function
f\colon M\times(a,b)\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
This data defines a Riemannina metric on
M\times(a,b)
via

g_{m,t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=h(t)_{m}+f(m,t)^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}.

We are interested in the relation between the curvatures of
g
and
h
.
The following Proposition is a generalisation of
[
bar2005generalized
]
*Proposition 4.1.
The proof presented there straighfowardly generalises to our set up.
Only in the derivation of equation (
C.4
) below we need to be careful because the Riccati equation as stated in loc. cit. is not valid in our set up and needs to be replaced.
Nonetheless, we present all calculations as a service to the reader.
We also adopt the notation from their proof and write
\langle\text{--},\text{--}\rangle
instead of
g
and
also denote the first and second derivative of
h
with
\dot{h}
and
\ddot{h}
, respectively.
They satisfy the relation

\displaystyle\dot{h}(t_{0})(v,w)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{d}{dt}\Bigl(h(t)(v,w)\Bigr)\right|_{t=t_{0}},

\displaystyle\ddot{h}(t_{0})(v,w)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\Bigl(h(t)(v,w)\Bigr)\right|_{t=t_{0}}

for all tangent vectors
v,w\in TM_{t_{0}}=T(M\times\{t_{0}\})\subseteq T(M\times(a,b))
.

Instead of secretly identifying tangent vectors with local integrable vector fields, we will work with
time-independent
vector fields that are tangent to the level sets.
Because of the canonical split
T_{m,t}M\times(a,b)=T_{m}M\oplus T_{t}(a,b)
, each vector field
X
on
T(M\times(a,b))
can be written as
X(m,t)=[\bar{X}(m,t),\lambda(m,t)]
, where
\bar{X}(m,t)
takes values in
TM_{m}
and
\lambda(m,t)
in
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}=T_{t}(a,b)
.
A vector field
X
on
T(M\times(a,b))
is
time-independent
if
\bar{X}
and
\lambda
are independent of
t
.
A vector field is
tangent to the level sets
if
\lambda(m,t)=0
for all
m\in M
and
t\in(a,b)
.

Proposition C.1.1
.

Let
h\colon(a,b)\rightarrow\mathrm{Riem}(M)
be a smooth curve of Riemannian metrics and let
f\colon M\times(a,b)\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
be a smooth function.
If
g
is the Riemannian metric on
M\times(a,b)
given by
g_{(x,t)}=h(t)_{x}+f^{2}(x,t)\mathrm{d}t^{2}
, if
X,Y,U,V
are time independent vector fields that are tangent to the level sets, and
\nu=f^{-1}\partial_{t}
is the unit normal vector field, then we have the following identities:

\displaystyle\langle W(X),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}f^{-1}\dot{h}(X,Y),\qquad\text{where }W(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=-\nabla_{X}\nu

(C.1)

\displaystyle\langle R(U,V)X,Y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle R^{M_{t}}(U,V)X,Y\rangle+\frac{1}{4}f^{-2}\bigl[\dot{h}(U,X)\dot{h}(V,Y)-\dot{h}(U,Y)\dot{h}(V,X)\bigr]

(C.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle R(X,Y)U,\nu\rangle&=-\frac{1}{2}f^{-1}\bigl[\langle(\nabla_{X}^{M_{t}}\dot{h})(Y),U\rangle]-\langle(\nabla_{Y}^{M_{t}}\dot{h})(X),U\rangle\bigr]\\
&\quad\ +\frac{1}{2}f^{-2}\bigl[X(f)\dot{h}(Y,U)-Y(f)\dot{h}(X,U)\bigr]\end{split}

(C.3)
