\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle R(X,\nu)\nu,Y\rangle&=\frac{1}{2}f^{-2}\bigl[f^{-1}\dot{f}\dot{h}(X,Y)-\ddot{h}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{2}\dot{h}({\dot{h}}^{\mathrm{op}}(X),Y)\bigr]\\
&\quad\ +f^{-2}X(f)Y(f)-f^{-1}\mathrm{Hess}_{M_{t}}(f)(X,Y)\end{split}

(C.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)&=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h)+f^{-2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\dot{h}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\ddot{h})+f^{-1}\dot{f}\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})\right)\\
&\qquad-4f^{-2}|\mathrm{d}f|^{2}_{M_{t}}+2f^{-1}\Delta_{M_{t}}(f).\end{split}

(C.5)

Here,
b^{\mathrm{op}}=b^{op,h}
is the unique endomorphism that satisfies
b(v,w)=h(b^{\mathrm{op}}(v),w)
, where
b
is an arbitrary bilinear form,
|\mathrm{d}f|_{M_{t}}
is the norm of the differential of
f|_{M_{t}}
with respect to
h_{t}
, and
\Delta_{M_{t}}
is the Laplace-Beltrami operator
1
1

1

the trace of the Hessian
\mathrm{Hess}_{M_{t}}
with respect to
h_{t}
.
of
M_{t}
with respect to
h_{t}
.

Proof.

If
X
is tangent to the level sets, then
\langle X,\nu\rangle=0
.
If
X
is additionally time independent, then

[X,\nu]=[X,f^{-1}\partial_{t}]=X(f^{-1})\partial_{t}+f^{-1}[X,\partial_{t}]=-f^{-2}X(f)\partial_{t}=-f^{-1}X(f)\nu.

(C.6)

From

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\nu\langle X,\nu\rangle=\langle\nabla_{\nu}X,\nu\rangle+\langle X,\nabla_{\nu}\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle W(X),\nu\rangle-\langle[X,\nu],\nu\rangle+\langle X,\nabla_{\nu}\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle[X,\nu],\nu\rangle+\langle X,\nabla_{\nu}\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=-f^{-1}\cdot X(f)+\langle X,\nabla_{\nu}\nu\rangle

and
0=\nu 1=\nu\langle\nu,\nu\rangle=2\langle\nabla_{\nu}\nu,\nu\rangle
we conclude

\nabla_{\nu}\nu=-f^{-1}\mathrm{grad}(f_{t}),

(C.7)

where
f_{t}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=f(\text{--},t)
.
In particular, the vector field
\nabla_{\nu}\nu
is always tangent to the level set.

Since the Weingarten operator
W
is self-adjoint, Equation (
C.1
) follows from

\displaystyle\langle W(X),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-\nabla_{X}\nu,Y\rangle=-\langle\nabla_{\nu}X,Y\rangle

\displaystyle=-\nu\left(\langle X,Y\right)+\langle X,\nabla_{\nu}Y\rangle

\displaystyle=-f^{-1}\dot{h}(X,Y)-\langle X,W(Y)\rangle.

Equation (
C.2
) then follows by plugging equation (
C.1
) into the Gauß equation, see
[
petersen2006riemannian
]
*p.44

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle R(U,V)X,Y\rangle&=\langle R^{M_{t}}(U,V)X,Y\rangle+\langle W(U),X\rangle\langle W(V),Y\rangle\\
&\quad-\langle W(U),Y\rangle\langle W(V),X\rangle.\end{split}

We derive equation (
C.3
) by plugging equation (
C.1
) into the Codazzi-Mainardi equation
[
petersen2006riemannian
]
*p.44
2
2

2

We use the opposite sign convention for the Weingarten operator

\langle R(X,Y)U,\nu\rangle=\Bigl\langle(\nabla_{X}^{M_{t}}W)(Y),U\Bigr\rangle-\Bigl\langle(\nabla_{Y}^{M_{t}}W)(X),U\Bigr\rangle

and applying the product formula.

To prove equation (
C.4
) we need to generalise the Riccati equation to our set up.
The following calculation, as presented in
[
petersen2006riemannian
]
*p.44 with opposite sign convention, shows

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\nu}W)(X)-W^{2}(X)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\nu}(W(X))-W(\nabla_{\nu}X)-W(W(X))

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla_{X}\nu)+\nabla_{\nabla_{\nu}X}\nu-\nabla_{\nabla_{X}\nu}\nu

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla_{X}\nu)-\nabla_{[X,\nu]}\nu

\displaystyle=R(X,\nu)\nu-\nabla_{X}\nabla_{\nu}\nu.

Together with equation (
C.7
) we get the generalised Riccati equation

R(X,\nu)\nu=(\nabla_{\nu}W)(X)-W^{2}(X)-f^{-2}X(f)\cdot\mathrm{grad}(f_{t})+f^{-1}\nabla_{X}\mathrm{grad}(f_{t})

(C.8)

From the generalised Riccati equation, we derive equation (
C.4
) in two steps.
We first consider the summands involving the Weingarten maps.
Equation (
C.6
) and equation (
C.7
) yield

\langle W(X),[\nu,Y]\rangle=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\langle W([\nu,X]),Y\rangle=0

because the Weingarten operator restricts to an endomorphism on
TM_{t}
.
This implies

\displaystyle\quad\langle(\nabla_{\nu}W)(X),Y\rangle-\langle W^{2}(X),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=\nu\langle W(X),Y\rangle-\langle W(\nabla_{\nu}X),Y\rangle-\langle W(X),\nabla_{\nu}Y\rangle-\langle W^{2}(X),Y\rangle

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=\nu\langle W(X),Y\rangle-\langle W(X),\nabla_{\nu}Y\rangle-\langle W(\nabla_{X}\nu+[\nu,X]),Y\rangle\\
&\quad\ -\langle W^{2}(X),Y\rangle\end{split}

\displaystyle=\nu\langle W(X),Y\rangle+\langle W^{2}(X),Y\rangle

\displaystyle=f^{-1}\partial_{t}\bigl[\frac{-1}{2}f^{-1}\dot{h}(X,Y)\bigr]-\frac{1}{2}f^{-1}\dot{h}(W(X),Y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}f^{-2}\Bigl[f^{-1}\dot{f}\dot{h}(X,Y)-\ddot{h}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{2}\dot{h}({\dot{h}}^{\mathrm{op}}(X),Y)\Bigr]

For the other two summand of (
C.8
), we use that the Levi-Cevita connection of the induced metric on a level set agrees with the tangential part of the Levi-Cevita of the ambient manifold to we deduce
\mathrm{Hess}_{M_{t}}(X,Y)=\langle\nabla_{X}\mathrm{grad}(f_{t}),Y\rangle
. Therefore, the remaining summand of (
C.8
) can be written as

\displaystyle\langle f^{-2}X(f)\mathrm{grad}(f_{t})-f^{-1}\nabla_{X}\mathrm{grad}(f_{t}),Y\rangle=f^{-2}X(f)Y(f)-f^{-1}\mathrm{Hess}_{M_{t}}(f)(X,Y).

Splicing the results together, the generalised Riccati equation gives equation (
C.4
).

The equation for the scalar curvature now follows from plugging the previous results into the formula

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{Ric}(e_{i},e_{i})+\mathrm{Ric}(\nu,\nu)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\langle R(e_{j},e_{i})e_{i},e_{j}\rangle+2\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle R(e_{j},\nu)\nu,e_{j}\rangle.

Indeed, plugging equation (
C.2
) into the first summand gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\langle R(e_{j},e_{i})e_{i},e_{j}\rangle&=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\langle R^{M_{t}}(e_{j},e_{i})e_{i},e_{j}\rangle+\frac{1}{4}f^{-2}\Bigl(\dot{h}(e_{j},e_{i})\dot{h}(e_{i},e_{j})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\dot{h}(e_{j},e_{j})\dot{h}(e_{i},e_{i})\Bigr)\end{split}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h)+\frac{1}{4}f^{-2}(\mathrm{tr}((\dot{h}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})^{2}),

while plugging equation (
C.4
) into the second summand gives

\displaystyle\begin{split}2\sum_{j=1}^{d}\langle R(e_{j},\nu)\nu,e_{j}\rangle&=\sum_{j=1}^{d}f^{-2}\left(f^{-1}\dot{f}\dot{h}(e_{j},e_{j})-\ddot{h}(e_{j},e_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\dot{h}(\dot{h}^{\mathrm{op}}(e_{j}),e_{j})\right)\\
&\qquad+2f^{-2}e_{j}(f)e_{j}(f)-2f^{-1}\mathrm{Hess}_{M_{t}}(f_{t})(e_{j},e_{j})\end{split}

\displaystyle\begin{split}&=f^{-2}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}((\dot{h}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\mathrm{tr}(\ddot{h})+f^{-1}\dot{f}\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})\right)\\
&\qquad+2f^{-2}|\mathrm{d}f|^{2}_{M_{t}}-2f^{-1}\Delta_{M_{t}}(f).\end{split}

Their sum yields equation (
C.5
) so the proposition is proven.
∎

More importantly than the precise formula is how the scalar curvature behaves under rescaling.

Definition C.1.2
.

Let
U
be a subset of
\mathord{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
X
,
Y
be sets. For every map
f\colon X\times U\rightarrow Y
we denote with
{}_{R}f\colon X\times R\cdot U\rightarrow Y
the map

\displaystyle{}_{R}f(x,t)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{.}\vskip-3.44444pt\hbox{.}}}\joinrel\hskip 1.0pt=f(x,R^{-1}\cdot t).

Corollary C.1.3
.

Let
h
and
f
be as in the previous proposition but assume further that their
2
-jet is uniformly bounded.
Then

\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(R^{2}{}_{R}h+{}_{R}f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})\xrightarrow{R\to\infty}0.

If, additionally,
f
only depends on
t\in(a,b)
, then

\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}({}_{R}h+{}_{R}f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})-{}_{R}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h)\xrightarrow{R\to\infty}0.

In particular, two isotopic psc metrics are concordant.

Proof.

We first recall the rescaling behaviour for various objects of Riemannian geometry.
In the following, let
g
be an arbitrary Riemannian metric on
M
.

For each smooth function
f\colon M\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{R}}
the gradient satisfies

\mathrm{grad}_{R^{2}g}(f)=R^{-2}\mathrm{grad}_{g}(f),

which implies

\displaystyle|\mathrm{d}f|_{R^{2}g}^{2}=R^{2}g(\mathrm{grad}_{R^{2}g}(f),\mathrm{grad}_{R^{2}g}(f))=R^{-2}g(\mathrm{grad}_{g}(f),\mathrm{grad}_{g}(f))=R^{-2}|\mathrm{d}f|_{g}^{2}.

If
(e_{i})_{i}
is a local orthonormal frame for
g
, then
(\bar{e}_{i}=R^{-1}e_{i})_{i}
is a local orthonormal frame for
R^{2}g
.
For each bilinear form
b
, this gives

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}_{R^{2}g}(b)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b(\bar{e}_{i},\bar{e_{i}})=R^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b(e_{i},e_{i})=R^{-2}\mathrm{tr}_{g}(b).

This result carries over to
\mathrm{tr}(b^{\mathrm{op}})
, because of the relation
b^{op,R^{2}g}=R^{-2}b^{op,g}
.

The metrics
g
and
R^{2}g
have the same Levi-Cevita connection
\nabla
because
\nabla
is a metric connection with respect to both metrics and being torsion-free is a metric independent condition.
This implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hess}_{R^{2}g}(f)(\bar{e_{i}},\bar{e_{i}})

\displaystyle=R^{2}g(\nabla_{\bar{e_{i}}}\mathrm{grad}_{R^{2}g}(f),\bar{e_{i}})

\displaystyle=g(\nabla_{{e_{i}}}\mathrm{grad}_{R^{2}g}(f),e_{i})

\displaystyle=R^{-2}g(\nabla_{{e_{i}}}\mathrm{grad}_{g}(f),e_{i})=R^{-2}\mathrm{Hess}_{g}(f)(e_{i},e_{i}).

In particular,

\Delta_{R^{2}g}(f)=R^{-2}\Delta_{g}(f).

Finally, we observe
\partial_{t}({}_{R}h)=R^{-1}\cdot{}_{R}(\partial_{t}h)
and recall that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(R^{2}h)=R^{-2}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h)
.

With these information, we derive

\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(R^{2}{}_{R}h+{}_{R}f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})=R^{-2}\cdot{}_{R}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h+f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2}).

The assumption on the
2
-jet implies that
\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h+f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})
is uniformly bounded, so the first statement follows.

The second statement follows from the rescaling behaviour and the previous lemma because the differential of
f
“vanishes in
M
-direction”:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}({}_{R}h

\displaystyle+{}_{R}f^{2}\mathrm{d}t^{2})-{}_{R}\mathord{\mathrm{scal}}(h)

\displaystyle=R^{-2}\cdot{}_{R}\left(f^{-2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{tr}((\dot{h}^{\mathrm{op}})^{2})-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})^{2}-\mathrm{tr}(\ddot{h})+f^{-1}\dot{f}\mathrm{tr}(\dot{h})\right)\right).

Since the
2
-jets of
f
and
h
are uniformly bounded, the right hand side converges to zero.

The final statement follows the second statement applied to the special case
f=1
.
∎
